the minecraft moba originated as a volleyball moba concept - can you recall?
Yes. I was able to recover an earlier description of the volleyball MOBA, although the original design documents and the precise transition into Minecraft MOBA aren't fully recovered.

The original concept appears to have been a volleyball game structured around MOBA roles, abilities, and strategic specialization, rather than a conventional MOBA with volleyball aesthetics.

What I can recover
1. The rally was the teamfight.

Instead of fighting over health bars, players competed for control of the rally. Pressure replaced conventional damage, while positioning, timing, deception, and reading opponents became the primary expressions of skill.

2. Volleyball positions corresponded to MOBA roles.

Volleyball position	MOBA interpretation
Setter	Controller / Playmaker
Outside Hitter	Generalist Carry
Opposite	Primary Damage Dealer
Middle Blocker	Initiator / Space Controller
Libero	Defensive Support
3. Abilities exaggerated actual volleyball techniques.

Rather than introducing arbitrary magical powers, abilities enhanced the possibilities of volleyball: deceptive sets, unusual attacks, defensive reads, timing manipulation, and positioning.

4. Progression emphasized specialization.

The recovered description mentions partial starting kits, branching technique upgrades, and improvements to information or opponent-reading capabilities.

Stamina, focus, composure, and momentum were considered as alternatives to conventional health-based combat.

5. The court replaced the MOBA map.

Court zones and rotation-based opportunities created strategic geography. Winning rallies and sets replaced destroying towers and bases.

What I can't establish yet
I can identify earlier references to a project called Voli, and a separate designation VWM, but I haven't recovered enough to establish their exact relationship or the original roster, ability designs, and detailed rules.

More importantly, I cannot yet substantiate how the volleyball concept became Minecraft MOBA. The retrieved history establishes that both projects existed, but not the sequence of design decisions connecting them.

An interesting continuity
The strongest conceptual connection is the idea that a MOBA doesn't necessarily need conventional combat to support meaningful roles.

In the volleyball concept, setting, blocking, attacking, and receiving could each become a distinct avenue of strategic contribution.

In Dimension Clash, something similar happens with Mining, Construction, Development, Exploration, Production, and Combat: different activities can contribute to winning without all being reducible to dealing damage.

That's an interpretation of the two designs, rather than a recovered statement of your original intentions.

One question: Are you interested in recovering the volleyball MOBA as its own project, or specifically examining how its design philosophy carried into Dimension Clash?

Hitting Lanes: Teams of 5 work to gain points for their team across 3 different hitting lanes. They may do drills to accumulate experience and Hitting Power, to eventually perform a Serve at a Net, a key location within each lane. Opposing players may be present at the Net during the Serve to participate in the Rally; otherwise, the serving team earns a point. 

Rallies: During a Rally, players lose Hitting Power for every contact they make with the ball, losing less depending on how their contact corresponds with their position. A player may contact the ball multiple times in a row, but successive contacts greatly reduce their Hitting Power. Once a Rally ends by a volleyball fault (besides multiple contacts), the awarded team gains a point. 

Positions and Hitting Power: The 5 positions are OH, OP, S, MB, and L. S lose no HP when they set the ball on their team's second contact; L lose a smaller amount. OH lose slightly more, and MB and OP lose the most. L lose no HP on digs; OH and OP lose slightly less; other positions lose the most. OH/OP/MB lose no HP for hitting; S lose more; L loses the most. MB loses no HP for blocking, L loses the most, other pos lose less. 

HP modifies the abilities of players depending on their abilities, most commonly affecting their movement speed, accuracy, endurance, and power.

Different positions will generally occupy different sections of the map and stay near other positions: MB takes the middle lane and OP takes top. L is the jungler, S is bottom support and OH is the bottom carry.

Player Differentiation: Players choose their position and create an initial build at the start of the game. Players choose their Height, which affects factors like their power, receiving ability, jumping reach, limb proportion, and stamina. They also choose some number of Traits, which can range from something like "Giant: ++Height", "Pancake: Unlocks Pancake active ability", "Shoot Hitter: +Power on faster sets", etc.
…
This establishes a much more concrete version of the original volleyball MOBA. It also corrects an important part of my earlier reconstruction: the game wasn't simply volleyball with MOBA-inspired roles. It had a three-lane MOBA map, a jungle role, an experience economy, and volleyball rallies functioning as the mechanism for scoring objectives.

The particularly interesting part is that Hitting Power (HP) was both a resource spent during rallies and a determinant of player effectiveness. That creates a connection between preparation, specialization, and execution.

The core gameplay loop
Drills and development

Gain experience and accumulate Hitting Power

Serve at a lane's Net

Initiate a scoring opportunity

Uncontested

Serving team earns a point

Contested

Rally determines which team scores

Points toward victory

Return to development and contest future serves

Positional specialization
Your Hitting Power rules make the five positions mechanically distinct without prohibiting any of them from performing ordinary volleyball actions.

Position	Primary efficiency	Map assignment
OH	Hitting; relatively efficient digs and sets	Bottom carry
OP	Hitting; relatively efficient digs	Top
S	Second-contact setting	Bottom support
MB	Blocking and hitting	Middle
L	Digging; relatively efficient setting	Jungle
This has an important consequence: a Setter can attack, and a Middle Blocker can dig, but the opportunity cost is different. Positional identity emerges from the economics of contact rather than exclusive permissions.

There is also a meaningful tension in the repeated-contact rule. Removing the conventional consecutive-contact fault creates opportunities for individual plays, but rapidly escalating HP costs discourage players from monopolizing rallies.

Player differentiation
The Height and Trait system suggests that the original game separated three dimensions of identity:

Position: Determines relative efficiency across volleyball actions.
Height: Changes physical strengths and weaknesses, including reach, stamina, receiving, and power.
Traits: Introduce specialized capabilities, statistical advantages, and potentially entirely new actions.
Unlike Dimension Clash's authored classes, this system appears to have allowed players to assemble their own archetypes.

That distinction is worth preserving if we revisit the concept.

What I'd particularly want to investigate
The biggest unresolved design question is how rallies interacted with the wider map economy.

For example, a team might have a stronger OH and Setter in the bottom lane, but if the opposing team sends its Middle Blocker or Libero to contest a Serve, the rally becomes a different kind of engagement.

This suggests that rotations, numerical advantages, and the decision to abandon drills to defend a Net could have been just as strategically important as volleyball execution.

I also wouldn't automatically import Dimension Clash's design solutions back into this project. The original system already has a distinctive foundation: three lanes, specialized volleyball positions, player-authored builds, and a resource economy directly connected to ball contacts.

Are these notes the original established design, or are you reconstructing it from memory? That distinction matters if we're going to recover the volleyball MOBA accurately rather than inadvertently redesign it.

reconstructing newly, not recall. 

as players do drills, they gain XP aswell, which allows them to choose new traits (itemization)
That clarifies the progression model. We're reconstructing the volleyball MOBA as a new design, rather than treating these mechanics as recovered canon.

Drills produce two forms of progression:

Hitting Power (HP): A replenishable resource that determines a player's immediate effectiveness and is expended during rallies.
Experience (XP): A progression resource that unlocks additional Traits, functioning as the game's equivalent of MOBA itemization.
This creates two distinct incentives for doing drills. A player might need to restore HP before contesting a Net, or continue drilling to reach an XP breakpoint and acquire a new Trait.

The distinction between initial and acquired Traits is particularly interesting. Players begin with a build defined by their Position, Height, and starting Traits, then develop that build throughout the match by selecting additional Traits.

Unlike conventional MOBA items, Traits could modify volleyball mechanics directly: changing how a player receives, attacks, moves, jumps, or interacts with particular kinds of sets.

One design question worth resolving: Does XP progression offer Traits from a shared pool available to every position, or do positions have their own exclusive Trait selections?

I'd lean toward a largely shared pool with some position-specific options. That would preserve the flexibility of player-authored builds while allowing each position to develop distinctive specializations.

yes, largely shared. a libero that invests until reaching Giant unlock should be powerful given that it actually is able to be leveraged
Agreed. That suggests an important principle for the Trait system: positions determine natural efficiencies, but shouldn't restrict access to unconventional builds.

A Libero investing heavily enough to unlock Giant is a useful example. Height naturally benefits hitting, blocking, and reach, while potentially compromising receiving, agility, and stamina. Those tradeoffs are particularly consequential for a Libero, whose baseline specialization favors defensive contacts.

But if that Libero deliberately invests toward Giant, they should gain the full mechanical benefits of their new height. They might become an unexpectedly effective blocker, gain offensive reach, or develop a very different approach to defending the court.

The investment should be rewarded because it creates a genuine new capability, not because the game grants a special bonus for an unusual combination.

I'd distinguish three principles:

Shared access: Most Traits are available regardless of position.
Mechanical consistency: Giant provides the same underlying height modification to every position. Its consequences differ because positions have different contact efficiencies and responsibilities.
Meaningful investment: Powerful Traits can require prerequisites or substantial XP investment, creating build trajectories rather than isolated purchases.
One caution: Giant shouldn't erase the Libero's positional disadvantages. A Giant Libero might gain tremendous blocking reach but still expend more HP blocking than a Middle Blocker. That makes the build unusual without invalidating the position system.

This also suggests that Traits should not all be straightforward statistical improvements. Some should fundamentally alter what a player can accomplish, as Pancake does by unlocking a new active technique.

The emerging progression identity is quite strong: Position establishes your economic specialization, Height establishes your physical profile, and Traits let you develop or deliberately subvert both.

Drills and Stamina:
On the map, recurring Drills of various types appear in preset locations. For example, a Spike Trainer Drill is best completed by a hitting position player, draining the least amount of Stamina to complete. Certain Drills may require more players or Stamina: a Transition Set Drill requires one player to receive, and another player to accurately set the receive into a goal. Another drill found in the "jungle" may be a demanding Serve Receive Drill that requires great defensive Endurance - a non-Libero player would likely run out of Stamina if they were to attempt it at base level. 
If a player runs out of Stamina, they temporarily lose movement speed, gain Hitting Points less efficiently, and lose more Hitting Points during rallies until their Stamina recovers at least halfway.
This gives the volleyball MOBA a much clearer economy. Drills are not simply a means of accumulating XP and Hitting Power; they are recurring map resources whose accessibility depends on positional specialization, physical attributes, and Stamina management.

Importantly, a player's suitability for a Drill isn't necessarily determined by an explicit position requirement. It emerges from how efficiently their build can complete the exercise.

The three-resource economy
Resource	Function
Hitting Power (HP)	Determines performance during rallies; consumed by ball contacts
Experience (XP)	Earned through Drills; unlocks new Traits
Stamina	Determines how much physical activity a player can sustain before suffering exhaustion
These resources interact without being interchangeable.

For example, a player can accumulate substantial HP but exhaust their Stamina while drilling. They might arrive at a Net with plenty of Hitting Power but perform poorly because they're exhausted.

Conversely, a player who conserves Stamina might be better prepared for a rally but have accumulated less HP or XP.

Drill categories
Spike Trainer

A hitting-focused Drill, efficiently completed by OH, OP, and MB players. Other positions can participate but may incur greater Stamina costs or have difficulty meeting its requirements.

Transition Set

A cooperative Drill requiring a receive followed by an accurate set into a target. Rewards coordination between players and may create reasons for teammates to converge on the same location.

Demanding Serve Receive

A defensive-endurance Drill in the jungle. A base-level Libero is naturally suited to it, while other positions risk exhausting themselves before completing it.

Exhaustion as a meaningful constraint
Your proposed exhaustion threshold is particularly important.

Illustrative Stamina states

Normal performance Above 0%

Stamina reaches zero

Exhausted (Recovery required)

Reduced movement speed, less efficient HP gains, and increased HP expenditure during rallies.

Exhaustion persists until Stamina recovers to at least 50%.

The 50% recovery requirement prevents players from repeatedly crossing between exhausted and normal states as their Stamina fluctuates near zero.

It also makes exhausting oneself a consequential decision. A player might finish a valuable Drill, but doing so could leave them unable to contest an approaching Serve effectively.

The most important design consequence
This introduces something analogous to jungle camp efficiency and lane resource allocation, without simply assigning rewards by position.

A Libero is the natural jungler because their defensive Endurance makes certain jungle Drills economically viable. But a different position could eventually specialize into those Drills through Height and Trait choices.

Likewise, a Libero who invests in offensive Traits might begin taking hitting Drills that would ordinarily be inefficient for them.

This is a strong foundation for emergent builds: players aren't choosing a separate economic specialization from a menu. Their physical attributes, position, and Traits collectively determine which economic opportunities they can exploit.

One issue I'd flag for later is whether Stamina is consumed by ordinary rally actions as well as Drills. If it is, Stamina management becomes a continuous consideration during combat. If it isn't, it primarily regulates preparation and the consequences of overtraining. Both models are plausible, but they produce substantially different incentives.

Controls In and Out of Rallies:
While not in a Rally, players still have access to many of their controls. They may still move freely, use their abilities, and even Sprint. (The HP cost of ability usage depends on the ability being used, and its cooldown is lessened compared to when it is used in a Rally; Sprinting consumes less HP compared to during a Rally as well.) In addition, when not at a Net, their Serve action is replaced by a Pepper action. This skillshot is available to all players and is modified (as Serving is, as well) by the initiating player's stats. When an opposing player is struck by a Pepper, they must receive the ball or lose HP. If successful, the involved players then engage in a cycle of bump-set-spike, with the eventual erroneous player losing some HP and the winning player gaining some XP. Some rules and controls during a Pepper are adjusted compared to during a Rally.

Rally Controls:
All players have access to a number of inputs. Players may Receive, Set, and Attack, as well as Jump. The three main actions can all be modified by Jumping first: a Receive turns into a Dive, a Set turns into a Jump Set, and an Attack turns into a Spike. Actions can be modified further by Traits: a Dive can become a Pancake or a Rolling Receive, a Jump Set can become a Dump, and a Spike can become a variety of attacking options like Roll Shot, Cut Shot, Tool, Feint, etc.
Action inputs have two steps. Players must first charge or tap the action in order to set its power. A tapped action's power is determined entirely by the player's stats, while a charged action's power is determined by the level of charge. After a tap or charge is released, a second input performs the action. Success is determined by how closely the second input's timing matches the incoming ball. An Attack, Serve, or Spike is most successfully Received when its charge level matches the attack itself. 

review. then: anything missing?
…
I think this is one of the more important additions to the volleyball MOBA so far. It establishes that volleyball is not merely the game's scoring mechanism; it is also its system of combat. Rallies are the equivalent of major objective fights, while Peppers create smaller engagements throughout the map.

More importantly, the controls appear to support a common mechanical language across both. A player who becomes proficient at receiving, setting, and attacking during Peppers develops skills directly applicable to contesting Nets.

There are several particularly strong design decisions here, but also a few mechanical gaps that I think need resolving before the control system is fully coherent.

1. Review of the established design
The action vocabulary
Your current controls create a fairly elegant hierarchy.

Additional universal actions

This works because players don't need to learn entirely different controls for each build. They learn a handful of basic actions, then unlock increasingly specialized expressions of those actions.

A Pancake, for instance, isn't necessarily an additional button. It's a modified Dive. Likewise, a Dump can emerge from the Jump Set input.

The main strength is that complexity is acquired through Traits rather than imposed universally. A beginning player can participate with Receive, Set, Attack, and Jump, while an experienced player can construct a much more technical playstyle.

One caveat: the examples don't all occupy the same mechanical category. Pancake is a defensive technique, Dump is a change in offensive intent, and Tool describes an attack's interaction with an opposing blocker. They might not all be implemented as equivalent input modifiers.

That's not a problem yet, but it matters for the eventual control scheme.

The two-stage input system
Example: receiving an incoming Spike

1. Prepare

Tap or charge Receive to establish power

2. Time

Read the approaching ball

3. Contact

Second input determines contact timing

I like the separation between power selection and contact timing.

It gives each volleyball action two independently meaningful skill tests. Players must judge the appropriate amount of force and then execute at the correct moment.

The tap mechanic is also useful. It gives players a stat-dependent baseline that doesn't require precise charge management every time, allowing less mechanically intensive builds to remain viable.

The most interesting part is the receive-matching rule. It means defending against a powerful Spike isn't necessarily just a matter of pressing Receive at the right time; the defender must also interpret the incoming attack's strength.

This creates room for deception, particularly through Traits that change the relationship between apparent and actual attack power.

However, the exact relationship between charge and stats needs clarification. If charging entirely overrides a player's stats, it could undermine the importance of Power, Accuracy, and other attributes. I'd expect stats to continue influencing some part of execution even when the player manually charges an action.

2. Peppers are especially promising
Peppers solve a problem that might otherwise arise from the three-lane structure: what do players do when they encounter an opponent away from a Net?

The answer is that they can challenge them directly, without needing to initiate a full Rally.

The consequences are nicely differentiated:

Outcome	Consequence
Pepper strikes an opponent who fails to receive	Opponent loses HP
Opponent successfully receives	A Pepper exchange begins
Player makes the eventual error	That player loses HP
Player wins the exchange	Winner gains XP
This makes Peppers a form of economic harassment and small-scale combat.

A player can interfere with an opponent's drilling, force them to spend Hitting Power, or potentially exhaust them before a major Net contest.

The XP reward creates an offensive incentive, while the HP penalty creates a defensive incentive.

One thing I would preserve is the distinction between these engagements and formal Rallies. Peppers should probably remain less consequential than winning a point at a Net, even if they're mechanically demanding.

Otherwise, teams might find that repeatedly fighting Peppers is more productive than contesting objectives.

3. What's missing?
I see seven areas that need definition. Not all require entirely new mechanics; several are simply missing rules connecting systems you've already established.

A. Ball movement and targeting
This is the biggest omission.

We know how a player determines power and contact timing, but not how they determine the ball's destination.

Questions include whether players aim using their camera or cursor, whether the ball follows a calculated trajectory, whether a Set targets a teammate or a location, and how Accuracy influences the result.

This matters because aiming is arguably the third major component of volleyball execution, alongside power and timing.

I would strongly prefer a shared aiming model for Receive, Set, Attack, Serve, and Pepper, with different actions producing different trajectories.

B. Contact timing and failed actions
What actually happens when a player mistimes their second input?

There are several plausible consequences: missing the ball entirely, making a weak contact, sending the ball inaccurately, or committing a volleyball fault.

These should probably not all be equivalent.

A slightly mistimed Receive might produce a poor pass, while a dramatically mistimed Receive could miss altogether.

That creates a continuous skill gradient rather than a binary success/failure test.

C. Ball possession and contact sequences
This is particularly important because you've removed the prohibition against consecutive contacts.

During a Rally, what determines when the ball is eligible for another contact? Can players use Receive twice, then Spike? Can one player perform an entire Receive–Set–Spike sequence?

Your HP penalties already discourage repeated individual contacts, which is a good foundation.

But we still need to establish whether the three-contact team limit exists, and how the system distinguishes ordinary ball contacts from actions that count as volleyball faults.

Peppers also need a precise definition of their modified contact rules.

D. Defensive blocking
Blocking is conspicuously absent from the control vocabulary.

You've already established that Middle Blockers expend no HP on blocks, meaning blocking is intended to be a major mechanic.

It could be an Attack input while jumping near the Net, a separate contextual action, or a modified Receive.

I would be cautious about introducing another universal button before investigating contextual solutions.

Blocking also introduces the question of whether a successful block counts toward a team's allowed contacts.

E. The role of movement during preparation
Can a player continue moving normally while charging Receive, Set, or Attack?

Can they Sprint while charging? Can they jump after beginning a charge? Does a Dive need to be initiated before or after the ball enters a particular range?

These details substantially affect the feel of the game.

In particular, the Jump modifier needs to work cleanly with the two-stage input system. If players must charge, jump, and then execute contact, that is already a reasonably demanding sequence before Traits add further complexity.

F. HP and Stamina costs during engagements
You have defined HP costs for positional contacts, ability use, and Sprinting, with reduced costs outside Rallies.

But the precise relationship between HP and Stamina is still incomplete.

For instance, does a Dive consume Stamina, HP, or both? Does Sprinting expend HP directly, or does it consume Stamina and indirectly threaten HP efficiency through exhaustion?

I think the distinction is worth protecting:

HP measures the player's accumulated volleyball effectiveness.
Stamina measures their capacity to continue exerting themselves.
If both resources are consumed identically by most actions, one risks becoming redundant.

G. Trait input conflicts
A player might eventually unlock Pancake, Rolling Receive, Dump, Roll Shot, Cut Shot, Tool, and Feint.

How do they select among multiple modifications of the same action?

This is the most significant scalability concern with a shared Trait pool.

There are a few possible approaches: mutually exclusive modifications, contextual activation, or a limited number of equipped active techniques.

I wouldn't choose one yet. The answer depends on how much mechanical complexity you want a fully developed player to manage.

4. Two broader concerns
First: the relationship between player skill and character progression.

A two-stage timing-and-power system already rewards mechanical proficiency. Height, Traits, position efficiencies, and HP then modify the outcome.

The important balance question is whether an exceptionally skilled, low-level player can meaningfully outperform a poorly skilled, highly developed player.

I'd want the answer to be yes in at least some circumstances. Otherwise, rallies could become predetermined by the drilling economy rather than meaningfully contested through volleyball execution.

Second: whether the game has enough team interaction during Rallies.

Your mechanics clearly support passing, setting, attacking, and defending. But the specific incentives for involving teammates need attention.

If successive individual contacts are merely more expensive, a sufficiently developed attacker might still find it optimal to handle the entire rally alone.

The positional HP discounts help address this, particularly the Setter's second-contact efficiency. I'd test those incentives before adding explicit team-combination bonuses.

5. My suggested next priority
I wouldn't design more Traits yet.

The highest-value next step is to define one complete ball interaction, from initiation to conclusion, using only universal controls.

For example:

An OH initiates a Serve.
An opposing Libero prepares and executes a Receive.
The Setter moves into position and performs a Set.
The Opposite jumps, charges, and executes a Spike.
A Middle Blocker attempts to block.
The Rally continues or ends in a fault.
For each action, we would establish aiming, timing, ball trajectory, contact validity, and HP expenditure.

That single sequence would expose most remaining mechanical ambiguities without requiring us to invent additional systems.

Overall assessment: The control design has a strong foundation, particularly the shared action vocabulary and two-stage execution. The most urgent missing pieces are ball targeting, blocking, contact sequencing, and Trait-selection inputs. Those are more fundamental than additional content or numerical balance, and I would resolve them before expanding the roster of available techniques.


let's take 6'0 or 181cm as an "average" athlete height for the in game universe. heights in inches around that, from 5'10 to 6'2, are freely selectable without AP investment, but still do change the more granular stat profile - speed, stamina, power, are all affected. [so should players be able to granularly adjust their individual statistics? or just the overall attributes? leaning towards only attributes in gameplay, but while building the initial athlete then individual statistics within the range allocated by the attribute value.]
I agree with that distinction. Players should allocate individual statistics during Base Athlete creation, but only the four overarching Attributes during a match.

That gives us two different kinds of customization:

Base Athlete Build: Defines what kind of athlete the character physically is, including height, proportions, and specific athletic strengths.
In-match development: Improves the athlete's broader capabilities through POW, ACC, SPD, and END.
This also gives VWM's granular statistical model a meaningful purpose without making in-match leveling excessively complicated.

1. Height as a free physical tradeoff
I'd establish:

Reference height: 6'0" (182.9 cm).

A small correction: 181 cm is approximately 5'11¼", so I'd use 183 cm as the reference if we want exactly 6'0".

Height	Metric	AP cost
5'10"	178 cm	Free
5'11"	180 cm	Free
6'0"	183 cm	Free
6'1"	185 cm	Free
6'2"	188 cm	Free
All five heights should be competitively viable, but they need not be equally suited to every role.

The crucial point is that height shouldn't directly change POW, SPD, or END ratings. Instead, it should modify the underlying physical statistics those Attributes govern.

For example:

Physical characteristic	Likely benefit	Likely tradeoff
Taller	Standing reach, interception height	Potentially slower acceleration, higher movement energy expenditure
Shorter	Lower center of mass, easier directional changes	Lower standing reach, potentially greater jumping demands
Longer arms	Contact reach, Blocking coverage	Potential arm-speed or repositioning tradeoff
Longer legs	Stride length, approach coverage	Potentially different acceleration and jumping characteristics
These should be relatively modest effects within the free height range.

Otherwise, a nominally free choice becomes a disguised mandatory optimization.

2. Four Attributes, multiple underlying statistics
I would treat the four Attributes as development categories, not as indivisible physical properties.

A possible initial decomposition:

Attribute	Underlying statistics
POW	Jump explosiveness, hitting force, upper-body strength
ACC	Contact precision, ball control, targeting precision
SPD	Acceleration, maximum running speed, agility, reaction speed
END	Stamina capacity, Stamina recovery, exertion efficiency, impact resistance
These are candidate categories rather than a finalized VWM-derived list.

They immediately explain why two Athletes with identical Attributes could perform differently.

For instance, two Athletes with 70 SPD might have:

Athlete A — Explosive

Acceleration: 76
Maximum speed: 66
Agility: 72
Reaction speed: 66
Athlete B — Mobile

Acceleration: 66
Maximum speed: 76
Agility: 68
Reaction speed: 70
Both average 70 SPD, but one excels at short repositioning while the other covers longer distances.

That is particularly interesting for volleyball because court movement is not simply running speed.

3. How granular customization should work
I'd avoid giving players two completely independent sets of AP.

Instead, use a hierarchical allocation system.

Step A — Allocate broad Attributes
The player spends Base Athlete AP on POW, ACC, SPD, and END.

For example:

Attribute	Rating
POW	65
ACC	63
SPD	70
END	62
Average: 65 OVR.

Step B — Distribute each Attribute internally
The player can redistribute a limited amount of that Attribute's value among its underlying statistics.

For example, with 70 SPD:

Statistic	Balanced	Acceleration-focused
Acceleration	70	76
Maximum speed	70	66
Agility	70	72
Reaction speed	70	66
Average	70	70
This preserves the total Attribute budget while allowing different expressions of the same rating.

I'd initially limit these redistributions to approximately ±5–8 points, preventing an Athlete with 65 SPD from obtaining a 95-rated acceleration statistic through extreme min-maxing.

The exact limits can be calibrated later.

Step C — Apply physical morphology
Height, arm length, and leg length then influence actual performance.

A 6'2" Athlete with 70 SPD might have the same underlying acceleration statistic as a 5'10" Athlete, but slightly different realized acceleration because of body geometry.

This creates three distinct quantities:

Attribute rating → underlying statistic → realized physical performance

That separation is useful for simulation and balancing.

4. What happens when the Athlete levels up?
This is where I'd make the system particularly simple.

In-match Attribute gains should preserve the Athlete's underlying statistical profile.

Suppose an Athlete starts with:

SPD: 70
Acceleration: 76
Maximum speed: 66
Agility: 72
Reaction speed: 66
The player invests +5 SPD during the match.

The resulting profile becomes approximately:

SPD: 75
Acceleration: 81
Maximum speed: 71
Agility: 77
Reaction speed: 71
In other words, the underlying statistics retain their relative offsets.

This means a pre-match acceleration specialist remains an acceleration specialist as they develop.

I would not let players redistribute those offsets during a match. Doing so would introduce an entirely separate optimization system on top of Attributes, Innates, Traits, and position-specific growth.

One refinement: at high ratings, offsets should compress near the 99 cap rather than allowing underlying statistics to exceed it.

5. An important issue: how granular statistics interact with Traits
This is where I'd draw a firm boundary.

Ability Traits should primarily require overarching Attributes, not individual underlying statistics.

For example:

A movement Trait requires 70 SPD.
A jumping Trait requires 75 POW.
A defensive endurance Trait requires 75 END.
Two Athletes with identical SPD but different acceleration distributions can qualify for the same movement Trait.

Their physical profiles may cause them to use it differently, but neither gets access to an entirely different ability tree solely because they adjusted a hidden substat.

Otherwise, Base Athlete creation becomes a complicated exercise in optimizing dozens of thresholds.

There could eventually be exceptions for particularly distinctive physical Traits, but I'd avoid them initially.

6. How this affects jumping economy
This is perhaps the strongest reason to use granular statistics.

Consider two Level 1 Athletes, both with 65 POW and 65 END.

Athlete A — Explosive jumper

Higher jump explosiveness.
Lower exertion efficiency.
Excellent initial vertical performance.
Repeated jumping becomes expensive.
Athlete B — Sustainable jumper

Lower jump explosiveness.
Higher exertion efficiency.
Slightly lower maximum vertical performance.
Can sustain repeated jumps more comfortably.
Neither has higher overall POW or END.

Their differences emerge from how they distributed the underlying statistics.

Now introduce height.

A taller Athlete might need less vertical displacement to reach a particular contact height, but could incur different physical costs when jumping.

A shorter Athlete might have superior acceleration and directional responsiveness, yet need a more demanding jump to contest the same ball.

These are meaningful differences even before players begin investing AP during the match.

This does introduce one calibration requirement: our previous target of 4–5 fresh jumps for an ordinary Level 1 Athlete should apply to a reference physical profile, not every possible Athlete.

A particularly efficient jumper might sustain six, while an explosive but inefficient Athlete might sustain four.

The MB's reduced Block Jump cost would then apply on top of that physical variation.

7. One caution: the risk of false complexity
The biggest danger is giving players many underlying statistics that don't produce perceptible differences.

For example, if acceleration, agility, and reaction speed all effectively determine how quickly a player reaches a ball, separating them might not add much.

I'd only include a granular statistic when it influences a distinguishable part of gameplay.

For SPD, that might mean:

Acceleration: How quickly movement speed builds.
Top Speed: Maximum sustained movement velocity.
Agility: How quickly movement direction changes.
Reaction: How quickly the Athlete responds to a ball or initiates movement.
Each has a separate mechanical responsibility.

Similarly, END's capacity, recovery, and exertion efficiency need to affect different aspects of the Stamina economy rather than all functioning as interchangeable Stamina multipliers.

My recommendation
I'd provisionally establish the following:

Base Athlete

Four overarching Attributes determine the sports-card rating.
The player allocates Base Athlete AP among those Attributes.
The player can redistribute a limited amount of each Attribute's value among its underlying statistics.
Height from 5'10" to 6'2" is free.
Height and body proportions influence physical performance without directly changing displayed Attribute ratings.
Additional Innates consume Base Athlete AP.
Position grants its own Innate for free.
During matches

Players allocate AP only to POW, ACC, SPD, and END.
Underlying statistics improve alongside their parent Attribute, preserving the pre-match distribution.
Ability Traits are primarily gated by overarching Attributes and Athlete level.
Morphology and underlying statistical specialization remain fixed.
The next thing I'd establish is the actual granular statistic roster, beginning with POW and END, because those determine jumping height, jump Stamina costs, and recovery.

One additional design question matters here: should height and limb proportions be independently selectable, or should selecting height generate a plausible default body geometry that players can adjust within a limited range?

I'd favor the latter. It allows meaningful body-type customization without producing implausible combinations or turning character creation into an unrestricted reach-maximization problem.

yes, the latter, as VWM has. please reference the repo https://github.com/iracarranza/volley-world-manager 


i think allocating attribute points could also further allow manual allocation of those points into the granular attributes even during the game? something that looks like this:


A player levels up -> they gain their free AP -> they allocate it into a stat -> that stat increases -> that increase grants statistical points -> can be auto-spent or manually spent?


the UI could look like this:
(Position) | Level 5 | 2 Points | Power -A+ | Speed -B+ | Accuracy -C+ | Endurance -D+ | Trait Tree
(each attribute's governed statistics listed tree-style below. the Trait Tree is a submenu that can be opened:)
Trait Tree | Power A | Speed B | Accuracy C | Endurance D
(with branching traits greyed or highlighted depending on availability and investment)


does this make sense? or weak?

…

Worked for 13s
The proposed progression system is stronger than automatically distributing granular statistics. It gives players two meaningful decisions at each level: which broad capability to improve, and how to express that improvement.

I would retain both decisions, with automatic allocation as the default and manual allocation as an optional expansion.

The main risk isn't balance complexity alone. It's decision frequency: a player should not have to navigate two allocation screens every time they level up while managing a rally, a Drill, or an approaching opponent.

Your tree-style UI is a good solution because it can make the second decision available without making it compulsory.

1. The progression structure
I'd use three layers:

Layer	Player decision	Purpose
Attribute Points (AP)	Invest in POW, SPD, ACC, END	Determines overall development and Trait eligibility
Statistical Points (SP)	Distribute within the upgraded Attribute	Determines the specific physical or technical specialization
Trait claims	Choose an available Trait	Adds new mechanics or changes existing ones
The distinction between AP and SP is important: SP should be generated by investing AP, not earned as an independent resource.

For example, a player receives 3 AP and invests one in END. Their END rating increases by one, and they receive a corresponding increment to distribute among END's underlying statistics.

They can accept the recommended distribution immediately or expand END to customize it.

That preserves the simplicity of the four-Attribute system while supporting athletes who specialize in different kinds of endurance.

2. What VWM contributes
I accessed volley-world-manager and found several directly relevant design documents.

The most important finding is that VWM already distinguishes physique from trainable skill. Its attribute-first generation design explicitly separates body geometry from the skill budget. Height influences mass, wingspan, and stride, while skill specialization comes from distributing a finite budget.

That supports the Base Athlete approach we're developing.

VWM also has a much more granular vocabulary than the provisional four-stat breakdown we were using.

MOBA Attribute	Relevant VWM statistics	Possible MOBA interpretation
Power	jump_reach, explosiveness, arm_speed, attack_power	Jump height, takeoff speed, striking force
Speed	acceleration, lateral_speed, transition_speed	Initial acceleration, lateral coverage, forward/backward movement
Accuracy	hand_control, ball_control, set_accuracy, attack_accuracy, reception_stability	Contact precision and consistency across actions
Endurance	stamina, plus recovery and fatigue mechanics	Stamina capacity, repeated-exertion efficiency, recovery
VWM's Granular Attribute Breakdown also distinguishes jump displacement from takeoff time and models approach quality separately.

That distinction is valuable. An Athlete can be capable of jumping high without necessarily being capable of jumping quickly.

One caution: VWM's documentation contains both implemented mechanics and proposed refinements. We should reuse its underlying distinctions, not assume every documented formula is established or suitable for the MOBA.

Sources: Attribute-First Generation, Player Attributes Ledger.

3. I would slightly change how Statistical Points work
The potential weakness in your proposal is that an Attribute increase and its granular distribution could become mathematically redundant.

Suppose SPD has three equally weighted statistics:

Acceleration
Lateral Speed
Transition Speed
If SPD increases from 65 to 66, and the player receives one statistical point, raising Acceleration from 65 to 66 doesn't actually produce an underlying average of 66.

The simplest solution is to define an Attribute rating as the budget governing its statistics, rather than requiring it to equal their literal arithmetic mean at every moment.

For example, increasing SPD by one could grant three Statistical Points:

Statistic	Before	Automatic	Manual
Acceleration	65	66	68
Lateral Speed	65	66	65
Transition Speed	65	66	65
SPD	65	66	66
Both distributions consume three SP. The parent SPD rating rises by one in either case.

This is an illustrative allocation rule, not yet a calibrated one. If the underlying statistics have unequal gameplay value, their SP costs or permitted ranges may need adjustment.

Most importantly, spending SP should never change the parent Attribute's value or Trait eligibility. Otherwise players are effectively being charged twice for specialization.

Recommended allocation behavior
I'd make each Attribute investment generate a small SP budget, then offer three options:

Automatic: Distribute SP according to the Athlete's existing profile.

Balanced: Distribute SP evenly among the Attribute's statistics.

Manual: Let the player choose where each SP goes.

Automatic should preserve the specialization selected during Base Athlete creation. Balanced would be an explicit choice to move toward a more rounded profile.

This is a meaningful distinction: a player who created an explosive jumper can continue developing explosiveness automatically, without repeatedly opening the granular allocation interface.

4. The UI is conceptually sound
Your proposed layout could work as follows:

MB  |  LEVEL 5  |  2 AP AVAILABLE

POWER       67  [-] [+]
SPEED       63  [-] [+]
ACCURACY    61  [-] [+]
ENDURANCE   72  [-] [+]

TRAIT TREE
Selecting or expanding END could reveal:

ENDURANCE 72 → 73

3 STATISTICAL POINTS

  Stamina Capacity       76  [+]
  Exertion Efficiency    70  [+]
  Stamina Recovery       70  [+]

[AUTO]  [BALANCED]  [CONFIRM]
And the Trait Tree:

TRAIT TREE

POWER       67
SPEED       63
ACCURACY    61
ENDURANCE   73

Available Traits
  ● Trait A — Requirements met
  ● Trait B — Requirements met

Locked Traits
  ○ Trait C — Requires END 80
  ○ Trait D — Requires POW 75
The key UI improvement I'd make is keeping Attribute allocation and Trait selection separate, but accessible from the same screen.

The Trait Tree should always show current Attribute ratings, available claims, and the effects of any pending allocations.

For example, hovering over +1 END could highlight newly available Traits before the player commits.

That makes leveling a strategic decision rather than a sequence of disconnected menus.

5. The bigger concern: specialization may undermine Attribute identity
Consider two Athletes:

Athlete A	Athlete B
END	80	80
Stamina Capacity	90	70
Exertion Efficiency	70	90
Stamina Recovery	80	80
These athletes have the same END rating but different Stamina economies.

That's desirable.

However, suppose Athlete B can jump significantly more often because Exertion Efficiency is overwhelmingly more valuable than Capacity.

Then END becomes a misleading indicator of jumping sustainability. The actual relevant statistic is Exertion Efficiency, and rational players may allocate nearly everything into it.

This is the principal balancing risk of granular allocation.

I would address it in two ways.

First, use bounded specialization. Individual statistics can deviate from their parent Attribute, but only within a defined range. For example, ±10 points initially.

Second, make the statistics solve genuinely different problems.

For END:

Statistic	Main gameplay responsibility
Stamina Capacity	How much exertion the Athlete can perform before Exhaustion
Exertion Efficiency	How much Stamina each physical action consumes
Stamina Recovery	How quickly Stamina returns between actions
This creates a distinction between burst capacity, sustained efficiency, and recovery.

An MB who frequently jumps several times in rapid succession might value Capacity and Efficiency. A roaming L who repeatedly performs Drills between Rallies might value Recovery.

The precise relationship needs testing because Capacity and Efficiency can easily become mathematically interchangeable over short time horizons.

That is exactly the kind of problem our jumping-economy calibration should investigate.

6. How this interacts with positional Innates
The proposed system also strengthens our earlier decision to make Innates drive positional development.

An MB's Innate can grant END-oriented growth, while the player determines whether that growth becomes Capacity, Efficiency, or Recovery.

Likewise:

S: ACC growth, customized toward Setting precision, tempo control, or other technical specializations.
L: SPD growth, customized toward acceleration, lateral coverage, or transition movement.
OH: Broad Attribute growth from its Innate, with flexible statistical development.
OP: Greater discretionary AP, allowing unusual Attribute combinations and their corresponding granular specializations.
This creates two independent forms of specialization.

A player chooses which Attributes develop, subject to their position's growth profile, and then chooses how those Attributes manifest.

It also makes the distinction between OH and OP more substantial.

OH receives broad, relatively reliable development. OP has more freedom to concentrate growth in particular capabilities, including unconventional defensive or utility builds.

Neither needs to be explicitly forbidden from developing in another position's specialty.

7. My preferred final structure
I would adopt your proposal with one significant simplification:

Do not require players to spend Statistical Points individually after every level-up.

Instead:

Leveling grants AP according to the position and Innate.
Players allocate AP among the four Attributes.
Each Attribute increase automatically generates and distributes SP according to the Athlete's current statistical development preference.
Players can expand any Attribute and manually adjust that distribution before confirming.
The Trait Tree updates to reflect the resulting Attribute ratings.
This retains the full depth of your proposal but makes the normal leveling interaction only a few clicks.

There is one additional option worth considering: let players define an automatic statistical growth preference for each Attribute during Base Athlete creation. That preference can then be changed between rallies.

An Athlete's initial physical profile and their subsequent training emphasis would consequently be distinct. A naturally explosive jumper could choose to develop Stamina Efficiency rather than continuing to maximize Explosiveness.

Verdict
Strong design direction, with moderate UI and balancing risks.

The granular layer adds genuine strategic depth because volleyball performance depends on specific physical capabilities, not just four generic ratings. VWM provides a useful foundation for modeling those distinctions.

I would now establish the complete granular statistics governed by each Attribute, using VWM's existing vocabulary as the starting point, before deciding how many SP each AP should generate.

In particular, we should settle whether jump height belongs primarily to POW while jump economy belongs to END, and how those two Attributes interact with the Athlete's fixed height, wingspan, and body proportions. That will determine whether the system produces meaningful physical specialization or merely different numerical routes to the same optimal build.


the projection shows the full extent of a fully charged jump along the floor, not accounting for aerial displacement and landing position. a player can release jump earlier if they wish to jump before reaching the full extent of the projection
Understood. This establishes a simpler relationship between the projection, Jump Charge, and takeoff.

The floor projection represents the full ground approach available to the Athlete. Jump Charge determines how far along that approach the Athlete travels before taking off.

The projection does not represent the Athlete's airborne trajectory or eventual landing position.

Scenario: Full versus partial approach
A hitter prepares an Attack and begins charging Jump toward a high Set.

A projected approach appears on the floor, extending to the position where the Athlete would take off if Jump were fully charged.

As the Athlete advances, the player can steer the projected path through movement inputs and camera rotation. The projection updates according to the Athlete's movement capabilities.

The player has two options:

Full approach: Continue charging Jump until the Athlete reaches the projected endpoint, then take off.

Partial approach: Release Jump before reaching the endpoint, immediately transitioning into takeoff from the Athlete's current position.

This means the player isn't selecting an approach length in advance. They are continuously deciding when to end an approach already in progress.

That is a meaningful distinction.

Scenario: Approaching too close to the net
A hitter begins charging Jump toward a Set near the net.

The full projection extends dangerously close to the net. The player recognizes that completing the approach may create an unfavorable takeoff position.

They release Jump early.

The Athlete takes off before reaching the projected endpoint, sacrificing some approach preparation but preserving a safer contact position.

Alternatively, the player continues charging, reaches the full endpoint, and jumps. Their forward momentum may carry them into the net.

The projection communicates the ground approach, but the player must still account for airborne movement and contact geometry.

This preserves the judgment involved in avoiding net faults without requiring the interface to predict every consequence.

One important consequence
I would no longer describe Jump Charge as selecting an intended approach length.

More precisely:

Charging Jump initiates an approach sequence and displays its full projected ground trajectory. The Athlete advances along this trajectory as the player maintains the charge, with movement inputs and camera steering continuously adjusting its course within the Athlete's physical capabilities. Releasing Jump initiates takeoff at the Athlete's current position, allowing the player to perform a partial approach rather than completing the full projected sequence. The projection does not include airborne displacement or landing position.

This also clarifies the relationship between Explosiveness and Jump Power.

Explosiveness can govern how quickly the Athlete progresses through the approach and prepares for takeoff. Jump Power determines their available vertical displacement, while the completed portion and quality of the approach determine how much of that capability they realize.

One detail remains worth establishing: If a player continues holding Jump after reaching the full projected endpoint, should takeoff occur automatically, or should the Athlete remain at the endpoint until Jump is released?

I favor automatic takeoff. Otherwise, players could exploit holding the charge indefinitely, and the approach would no longer function as a predictable movement commitment.

automatic takeoff yes
Agreed. Automatic takeoff at the end of the projected approach is now established.

The Jump sequence has three possible outcomes:

Early release: Releasing Jump before completing the approach immediately initiates takeoff from the Athlete's current position.
Full charge: Reaching the end of the projected approach automatically initiates takeoff, even if Jump remains held.
Interrupted approach: If the Athlete's approach is disrupted before takeoff, the resulting behavior remains to be defined.
The floor projection shows the full available ground approach, updates with steering inputs, and excludes airborne displacement and landing position.

This gives Jump Charge a clear function: the player controls when to take off, up to the maximum approach length permitted by the Athlete's physical capabilities.

The next useful question is what happens when an approach is interrupted—for example, by contact with another Athlete or an obstruction—before takeoff.


yes agree with B. and additionally, a ball's Spike Point IS determined by the ball's trajectory AND the player's attributes - but it will always assume ideal conditions, like a full Jump and time to reach the point of contact. In this case, if the botched receive was somewhat to the right and slightly low, the ball's Spike Point may simply be the top of the receive's arc, regardless of whether or not the Spike Point is actually reachable from the Athlete's current position
Agreed. This establishes an important distinction: the Spike Point is an idealized contact target, not a prediction of where the Athlete will actually make contact.

It is calculated from the ball's trajectory and the Athlete's physical capabilities, assuming the Athlete can arrive properly prepared.

That means a Spike Point can be perfectly valid as a theoretical target while being completely inaccessible in the current rally.

1. Spike Point versus actual contact
We now have three separate locations:

Location	Meaning	Determined by
Spike Point	Ideal location for this Athlete to strike the incoming ball	Ball trajectory, Athlete's attributes and physique, ideal preparation
Reachable contact opportunity	Where the Athlete can realistically intercept the ball from their current state	Current position, movement, available time, jump preparation
Actual contact point	Where the Athlete ultimately touches the ball	Player inputs, physical motion, ACC-based corrections
The Spike Point assumes ideal conditions, including a full Jump and sufficient time to reach the required position.

It does not account for whether the Athlete can get there from their present location.

This makes it a reference point rather than an instruction the simulation must fulfill.

2. Revisiting the botched self-set
Suppose the hitter attempts to bump-set the ball upward.

Their Receive produces a trajectory that is too low and somewhat to the right.

The game calculates the Athlete's ideal Spike Point along that trajectory.

Because the ball never rises very high, the Spike Point might simply be at the apex of the ball's arc.

That point is still determined by the Athlete's capabilities. Another Athlete with different height, reach, or Jump Power could have a different ideal point along the same ball trajectory.

However, our hitter cannot reach the calculated Spike Point in time.

Perhaps it requires an approach that is too long, an impractical change in direction, or a jump that would have needed to begin earlier.

The player recognizes the poor Set and attempts a rescue.

They move toward the ball, jump from a compromised position, and confirm Attack as the ball passes near their reachable contact envelope.

The Spike Point remains where it was calculated. The game does not relocate it to accommodate the player's poor position.

Low-ACC outcome
The Athlete makes a marginal contact near the edge of their reach.

They can only brush the ball with their fingertips, producing a weak Attack.

High-ACC outcome
With identical movement, Jump, and Attack inputs, the Athlete makes a more effective physical correction.

They adjust their body configuration and, through Timing, can shift the precise contact moment slightly around the player's confirmation input.

This produces a better contact.

Neither Athlete reaches the ideal Spike Point. The high-ACC Athlete simply extracts more useful performance from the compromised opportunity.

That is the intended distinction.

3. Why the ideal Spike Point should remain independent of current positioning
I think this is valuable for three reasons.

First, it preserves the consequences of earlier actions. A poor Receive creates a poor Set trajectory. The game does not automatically redefine the ideal Attack around the hitter's current position.

Second, it makes physical limitations meaningful. A player might identify the correct Spike Point but lack the SPD, Jump Power, or available time to reach it.

Third, it gives ACC a clear responsibility. Accuracy helps the Athlete perform under imperfect circumstances, rather than making those circumstances disappear.

This also prevents an undesirable feedback loop in which the Spike Point continually moves toward the Athlete whenever they fall behind their approach.

The ideal target can remain stable while the actual opportunities for contact change.

4. One nuance: the Spike Point should be Athlete-specific, not necessarily action-specific
A Spike Point calculated for a 6'7" OH with exceptional Jump Power could differ substantially from one calculated for a 5'6" Libero.

However, I wouldn't have ACC move the Spike Point toward a more convenient location.

Otherwise, high ACC would effectively improve the Athlete's ideal geometry, rather than their ability to execute within that geometry.

Instead:

Physique and physical Attributes establish the ideal Spike Point.
Current movement and preparation determine whether it can be reached.
ACC determines how well the Athlete performs when the actual contact differs from ideal.
One qualification: if the Spike Point calculation uses the Athlete's ideal striking mechanics, we should decide whether it incorporates their maximum Swing Power or simply assumes an ideal contact position for a standard Attack. Those could produce different ideal points.

5. A useful consequence for the interface
The Spike Point should not need to indicate whether it is currently reachable.

A player might see it positioned well beyond their realistic approach path.

That's useful information in itself: the Set is poor, and they need to improvise.

The player can then use the ball's visible trajectory, their movement controls, and their understanding of their Athlete to attempt a rescue.

We don't need an additional system that labels the situation unreachable, suboptimal, or difficult.

That keeps the interface consistent with your broader direction of avoiding excessive explanatory feedback.

Revised rule
Spike Point: An Athlete-specific ideal contact location calculated from the incoming ball trajectory and the Athlete's physical capabilities, assuming a fully prepared Jump and sufficient time to reach the contact location. The Spike Point is independent of the Athlete's current position and does not guarantee reachability. Actual contact is determined by the Athlete's movement, Jump, confirmation timing, and ACC-mediated physical corrections.

This also confirms the earlier Timing decision: the player's confirmation establishes the intended contact moment, but the Athlete can make a limited physical timing correction around it.

One question follows naturally: Should the Spike Point represent the single best point for maximizing attacking power, or the best compromise between power, available attacking angles, and net clearance?

Those can differ, especially on a low Set.

the Spike Point represents the highest point at which a hitter's attributes allow them to make the best contact with the ball. a high set will not have its Spike Point be the peak of the arc; instead, there may be TWO spike points where the trajectory of the ball is at its highest intersection with the Athlete's highest jump and arm reach.
That clarifies the Spike Point considerably. It is the highest point along the ball's trajectory at which the Athlete can make an ideal attacking contact, assuming a fully realized Jump and ideal positioning.

Crucially, a ball trajectory can have zero, one, or two Spike Points, depending on its relationship to the Athlete's maximum effective attacking height.

1. Spike Points as intersections
Consider two functions:

Ball trajectory: The ball's position in three-dimensional space over time.
Maximum ideal attacking height: The highest ball-contact height at which this Athlete can execute an ideal Spike, given their physique, Jump Power, and arm reach.
For a sufficiently high Set, the ball rises above the Athlete's maximum ideal attacking height.

It therefore intersects that height twice:

Ascending Spike Point: The ball first reaches the Athlete's maximum ideal attacking height while rising.
Descending Spike Point: The ball returns to that height while falling.
Both are theoretically ideal contact opportunities.

Neither is necessarily reachable from the Athlete's current position.

Example: High Set
Suppose an Athlete's maximum ideal attacking contact height is 3.20 m.

The Set reaches a peak of 3.80 m.

Ball position	Height	Interpretation
Ascending intersection	3.20 m	First Spike Point
Apex	3.80 m	Above the Athlete's ideal attacking reach
Descending intersection	3.20 m	Second Spike Point
The two Spike Points occupy different positions in space and occur at different times.

That distinction matters because the Athlete may be able to approach one but not the other.

It also means that an early Attack and a delayed Attack can target different positions along the same Set without changing the Athlete's maximum attacking height.

2. Low Set: Spike Point at the apex
Now consider the botched self-set.

The ball only rises to 2.85 m, below the Athlete's maximum ideal attacking contact height of 3.20 m.

There is no intersection with the maximum-height boundary.

Instead, the highest possible ideal contact along the trajectory occurs at the ball's apex.

The apex becomes the single Spike Point.

This matches your rescue scenario.

Even though the Athlete could theoretically reach higher, the ball never reaches that height.

And even though the Spike Point exists, the Athlete might lack the time or movement capacity to reach it.

3. The three cases
Ball trajectory	Spike Points	Reason
Apex below maximum ideal attacking height	One	The apex is the highest available contact
Apex exactly at maximum ideal attacking height	One	The apex touches the maximum-height boundary
Apex above maximum ideal attacking height	Two	Ascending and descending intersections
There is one physical qualification: a trajectory could provide zero valid Spike Points if no portion of it permits an ideal attacking contact—for example, because the relevant contact locations are obstructed or otherwise illegal.

That is distinct from an Athlete simply being unable to reach an otherwise valid Spike Point in time.

4. Why two Spike Points matter
Consider a high Set traveling toward the left pin.

The ascending Spike Point might be closer to the Setter, while the descending Spike Point is farther toward the antenna.

An OH preparing an approach may prefer the descending point because it provides more time to arrive.

But another Athlete with exceptional movement and preparation might attack at the ascending point.

Importantly, the system shouldn't automatically choose whichever point is easiest to reach.

Both are idealized geometric targets.

The player decides which contact opportunity to pursue.

This gives high Sets an additional tactical property: they can create multiple ideal contact opportunities for the same Athlete.

A quick Set, by contrast, may provide only a very narrow opportunity to reach its Spike Point.

5. How this affects ACC
This also reinforces the simplified Accuracy model.

ACC does not determine where the Spike Points are.

Instead, it determines how well the Athlete can compensate when actual contact occurs away from them.

For example, a hitter might jump too early to reach the descending Spike Point.

A low-ACC Athlete makes a poor contact.

A high-ACC Athlete adjusts their arm position and contact timing, preserving more of the intended Attack.

Neither Athlete gains a different theoretical maximum attacking height through ACC.

Formalized rule
Spike Point: The highest ideal attacking contact location or locations along an incoming ball trajectory, determined by the Athlete's physique, Jump Power, and arm reach under ideal Jump and positioning conditions. When the ball's apex exceeds the Athlete's maximum ideal attacking contact height, two Spike Points exist at the ascending and descending intersections. Otherwise, the trajectory's apex is the single Spike Point, provided that an ideal attacking contact is physically valid there. Spike Points do not account for whether the Athlete can reach them from their current position.

One implementation nuance: these intersections must be calculated in three-dimensional space, not merely by comparing heights. The ascending and descending Spike Points may have the same height but very different horizontal positions, and those positions are essential to approach planning.

Spike Points also do not correct for a player's approach decisions- a Spike Point is always physically reachable given ideal conditions, but oftentimes a player will be slightly out of position to approach perfectly towards the Spike Point, or they may choose an approach path that deviates their own body away from that point.
Agreed. This establishes that Spike Points are independent of the player's approach decisions, including their current positioning, chosen approach trajectory, and eventual takeoff location.

A Spike Point represents what the Athlete could achieve under ideal conditions—not what their current approach will allow them to achieve.

1. Three distinct components
Component	Determined by	Responds to player movement?
Spike Point	Ball trajectory, Athlete physique, Jump Power, and ideal attacking reach	No
Approach trajectory	Movement inputs, camera steering, SPD, physical movement constraints	Yes
Actual attacking contact	Resulting jump, body position, confirmation timing, ACC corrections	Yes
The Spike Point remains fixed relative to the predicted ball trajectory, even when the player makes a poor approach.

It can move if the predicted ball trajectory itself changes, but it does not move to accommodate the Athlete.

2. Scenario: Slightly misaligned approach
An OH receives a high Set toward the left pin.

The descending Spike Point is located at a height and position the Athlete could reach with a full Jump and an appropriately aligned approach.

The player begins charging Jump.

Their projected ground approach curves toward the ball, but their steering leaves them slightly too far inside the court.

The Spike Point remains unchanged.

At takeoff, the Athlete is displaced from the ideal location.

They jump and attempt to Attack.

Low ACC: The Athlete struggles to accommodate the lateral displacement. Contact occurs farther from the ideal striking position, reducing the quality of the Attack.

High ACC: The Athlete adjusts their torso, shoulder, arm, and contact timing to compensate. They achieve a better contact from the same compromised position.

Neither Athlete is automatically repositioned toward the Spike Point.

This preserves the consequences of the player's approach.

3. Scenario: Deliberately deviating from the Spike Point
This distinction also matters when the deviation is intentional.

Suppose an attacker sees the ideal Spike Point near the antenna but deliberately chooses an approach that positions them farther inside the court.

They might want to avoid a blocker, preserve a particular facing angle, or reduce the risk of a net fault.

The system should not interpret this as a request to relocate the Spike Point.

Instead, the player accepts a less favorable contact geometry in exchange for another tactical advantage.

Their ACC determines how effectively they can execute from that compromised position.

The Spike Point is a reference for optimal contact, not a mandatory destination.

4. Spike Points and physical reachability
Your statement that a Spike Point is always physically reachable under ideal conditions is worth formalizing carefully.

It means the calculation assumes:

The Athlete can begin from an appropriate location.
Sufficient time exists to prepare and execute a full Jump.
The Athlete can align their body appropriately for the contact.
The contact is within their maximum ideal attacking envelope.
It does not guarantee that the Athlete can reach the point from their current location, nor that their chosen approach will bring them there.

This distinction prevents the Spike Point system from becoming a dynamic pathfinding or interception assistant.

The game identifies the ideal contact location. The player is responsible for getting the Athlete there.

5. A consequence for the approach visualization
The projected ground approach and the Spike Point should remain separate visual elements.

The approach projection shows where the Athlete will move along the ground if they continue the current Jump Charge.

The Spike Point shows the ideal location of ball contact.

Neither projection needs to automatically correct the other.

This is particularly important because the approach projection intentionally excludes airborne displacement.

A player might steer their projected approach to end short of the Spike Point's horizontal position, correctly anticipating that their airborne movement will carry them toward it.

Conversely, a projected approach that appears to end directly beneath the Spike Point might produce an overshoot after takeoff.

That relationship is something the player must learn to judge.

Consolidated definition
Spike Point: An Athlete-specific, idealized location along the incoming ball trajectory at which the Athlete can make their highest ideal attacking contact, assuming sufficient preparation, optimal positioning, and a fully realized Jump. A trajectory may produce one or two Spike Points depending on its maximum height relative to the Athlete's ideal attacking reach. Spike Points are independent of the Athlete's current position, approach trajectory, takeoff location, and actual contact execution. They remain fixed unless the underlying ball trajectory changes.

This creates a clean division of responsibility:

The Spike Point tells the player where ideal contact is possible. The approach determines where the Athlete actually goes. ACC determines how effectively the Athlete handles any discrepancy.

the scenario for 3 that makes it clearer is more like this: the Spike Point actually occurs BEFORE the antenna, but the player wants to hit closer to the antenna instead. Despite the Athlete being capable of reaching the height of the Set at the Spike Point, the player makes their approach to line up with a lower point of the Set's trajectory.
That is a clearer example because the player isn't necessarily making a worse approach. They're deliberately choosing a different contact point along the Set's trajectory.

The Spike Point identifies the highest ideal contact available to the Athlete, but it does not prescribe where they should attack.

Scenario: Attacking closer to the antenna
A Setter delivers a high Set toward the left pin.

The descending Spike Point occurs somewhat inside the antenna, where the ball crosses the Athlete's maximum ideal attacking height.

However, the ball continues traveling outward and downward toward the antenna.

The hitter has two possible approaches:

Approach A — Toward the Spike Point

The player aims their approach to intercept the ball at the Spike Point.

The Athlete can contact the ball at their maximum ideal attacking height.
The player can exploit their full vertical attacking potential.
The contact occurs farther inside the court.
Approach B — Toward the antenna

The player deliberately directs their approach farther outside.

The ball has descended below the Spike Point's height by the time it reaches the intended contact location.
The Athlete can still make a good attacking contact, but at a lower height.
The more outward contact position may create different attacking angles relative to the net and opposing blockers.
Neither approach is inherently incorrect.

Approach B trades contact height for contact location. That can be tactically advantageous, even though the Athlete could have contacted the ball higher at the earlier Spike Point.

What this establishes
We should distinguish maximum-height contact from tactically desirable contact.

Concept	Meaning
Spike Point	Highest ideal attacking contact location along the ball trajectory, assuming ideal conditions
Chosen contact location	Where the player intends to intercept the ball, based on their approach and timing
Actual contact location	Where the Athlete physically contacts the ball after executing those inputs
The Spike Point remains unchanged when the player chooses Approach B.

Importantly, contacting the ball below the Spike Point does not automatically impose an ACC penalty.

If the hitter deliberately approaches the lower contact location and aligns their body appropriately, the resulting contact can still be clean and controlled.

The lower height changes the physical attacking possibilities—particularly the available downward angles and net clearance—but does not inherently indicate poor execution.

ACC becomes relevant if the Athlete's actual contact differs from the contact they were attempting to make.

Revised principle
The Spike Point is a maximum-height reference, not a universal optimum for attacking.

Players can deliberately target other points along the Set's trajectory to obtain different attacking positions, angles, and timing opportunities. Those contacts may be lower without being less accurate or poorly executed.

This also means that a successful approach should not be evaluated primarily by its proximity to the Spike Point. It should be evaluated by how well the Athlete executes the contact the player actually pursued.

then additionally, it is the Setter's implicit responsibility to understand their hitters' plausible Spike Points and measure their trajectories and charge levels to idealize these Spike Points versus the offense they want to run
Exactly. This gives the Setter a more demanding responsibility than simply delivering the ball to a particular hitter or location.

The Setter is implicitly designing the hitter's contact opportunities through the Set's trajectory and Charge. To do that effectively, they must understand the receiving hitter's physical capabilities and the offense they intend to run.

Importantly, the Setter isn't directly choosing the hitter's Spike Point. They're choosing a ball trajectory that produces particular Spike Points for that Athlete.

1. Setting as the construction of attacking opportunities
Consider two hitters:

Hitter A	Hitter B
Height	6'7"	6'1"
Jump Power	Moderate	Exceptional
Arm reach	Long	Shorter
SPD	Moderate	High
Preferred attack	High outside Attack	Fast, aggressive Attack
The same Set can produce different Spike Points for these Athletes.

A Set that creates an excellent descending Spike Point for Hitter A might create a poorly positioned or mistimed opportunity for Hitter B.

Conversely, a faster, flatter Set could suit Hitter B's movement and jumping capabilities while giving Hitter A insufficient time to establish a full approach.

Neither Set is universally better.

Its quality depends on the intended hitter and offensive purpose.

2. Scenario: Setting for maximum contact height
A Setter wants their OH to attack at their maximum available height.

They know the OH has substantial Jump Power and arm reach.

The Setter selects a high Set whose descending trajectory crosses the OH's maximum ideal attacking height at a desirable location near the left pin.

This creates a Spike Point the OH can plausibly approach.

The Setter must consider several things simultaneously:

Set Charge: Sufficient to produce the desired height and distance.
