Trajectory: Positions the descending Spike Point at the intended location.
Hitter capabilities: Determines the height at which that intersection occurs.
Available preparation time: Determines whether the hitter can realistically reach the Spike Point.
Offensive purpose: Determines whether maximum contact height is actually desirable.
A Setter who understands these relationships can produce more effective opportunities without receiving an explicit bonus to Set quality.

Their advantage comes partly from the player's understanding of the game.

3. Scenario: Setting for a faster offense
Now the Setter wants to run a quick Attack with their MB.

They choose a flatter, faster trajectory.

The ball may never rise above the MB's maximum ideal attacking height.

Consequently, the Spike Point could occur at the apex of the Set.

The Setter is deliberately sacrificing some potential contact height to accelerate the offense.

This can reduce the time available for opposing blockers to position themselves.

However, it also places greater demands on the MB's preparation and coordination.

If the MB is late, their ACC may help them rescue the contact, but it cannot fully compensate for an approach that never brought them into the ball's reachable space.

This creates a meaningful Setter–Hitter relationship: the Setter must understand not just where the hitter can attack, but where they can attack within the intended offensive timing.

4. Scenario: Setting beyond the Spike Point
Your antenna example introduces another dimension.

The Setter wants their OH to attack very close to the antenna.

They deliver a Set whose descending Spike Point occurs somewhat inside the antenna.

The ball continues outward and downward afterward.

The OH deliberately approaches the later, lower contact location.

This can be an entirely successful offensive design.

The Setter has not necessarily made a poor Set simply because the hitter doesn't attack at the Spike Point.

Instead, they have created a trajectory that supports a different attacking location.

Spike Points are reference information, not the sole measure of Set quality.

That distinction is essential if the game is to support different offensive styles.

5. The Setter's skill is partly predictive
This also clarifies what a skilled Setter player must learn.

They need to anticipate the relationship between:

Set trajectory → hitter-specific Spike Points → feasible approaches → available attacking options.

Each stage depends on different information.

The Setter controls the first through their Action Charge, directional influence, and execution.

The hitter's physique and Attributes determine the second.

The hitter's positioning, movement capabilities, and available time determine the third.

The hitter's actual approach, facing, contact timing, and physical execution determine the fourth.

The Setter cannot control the entire sequence.

They can only create favorable conditions for the hitter.

This preserves meaningful agency for both players.

6. A useful implication for trajectory sharing
The earlier decision that allies can see a Setter's available trajectories becomes more valuable here.

The hitter can observe the Setter's options and begin anticipating which Set they might receive.

Once the Setter commits, the hitter sees the chosen trajectory and can identify their own Spike Point or Points along it.

Those Spike Points are calculated for the observing hitter, not universally shared coordinates.

A different teammate could see different Spike Points along the same Set.

This means trajectory sharing communicates the ball's intended movement, while each Athlete interprets its attacking opportunities according to their own capabilities.

We don't need to give the Setter an interface that explicitly calculates every teammate's Spike Points.

Their ability to anticipate those opportunities can remain part of player skill.

7. This creates two distinct forms of setting excellence
I think this is the most consequential implication.

Mechanical setting skill is the ability to execute a chosen trajectory accurately despite imperfect timing, positioning, or ball contact.

This is where the Athlete's ACC matters.

Tactical setting skill is the ability to choose trajectories that create useful attacking opportunities for particular teammates within the desired offensive structure.

This belongs primarily to the player.

A high-ACC Setter controlled by an inexperienced player might execute an unsuitable Set with exceptional precision.

A low-ACC Setter controlled by an experienced player might choose an excellent trajectory but struggle to reproduce it consistently.

Both distinctions should remain meaningful.

Consolidated principle
The Setter determines the intended ball trajectory, thereby creating Athlete-specific Spike Points and other possible attacking contact locations. Effective setting requires anticipating the hitter's physique, Attributes, approach capabilities, and intended offensive timing. The Setter's ACC governs how faithfully the Athlete executes the selected Set, while the player's tactical understanding determines whether that Set creates a useful attacking opportunity.

This also suggests that a Set should never be evaluated solely by its proximity to a hitter's Spike Point. A lower contact near the antenna, a fast quick Attack, or a deliberately unusual trajectory may be preferable depending on the offense.

The system should make these possibilities physically meaningful without prescribing which one is optimal.

a weak high ball to the middle may be ideal despite the Spike Point being essentially instant, as the MB can still create high contact nearby to the Spike Point, and a rising shoot set to the outside may have a Spike Point that is far outside the court - but the tactically ideal location to spike the ball is at the antenna while it is rising. This can also be circumvented with an absolutely perfect set that rises exactly to the antenna and the spiker's jump power and arm reach.
These examples establish something important: the Spike Point is a geometric reference, but the Setter's objective is to create a useful attacking opportunity—not necessarily to place the Spike Point at the intended contact location.

The distinction becomes especially clear when we consider how much useful attacking height exists around a Spike Point.

1. Weak, high Set to the middle
Suppose the Setter delivers a relatively weak, high ball toward the MB.

The ball's trajectory barely rises above—or perhaps never reaches—the MB's maximum ideal attacking height.

Its Spike Point occurs near the apex, potentially almost immediately after the Set.

At first glance, this might seem undesirable: the MB cannot realistically complete an approach and Jump in time to reach that exact point.

But the Set may still be excellent.

Because the ball is moving relatively slowly and remains near its apex for some time, the MB can contact it slightly later and slightly lower, at a nearby location.

The difference in contact height might be negligible.

The MB doesn't need to reach the Spike Point precisely to obtain a powerful, high-quality Attack.

A Spike Point occurring too early does not necessarily mean the Set is too fast.

The relevant question is how the ball moves through the surrounding region of useful contact opportunities.

2. Rising shoot Set to the outside
Now consider a fast Set traveling toward the outside antenna.

The ball is still rising as it passes the antenna.

Its theoretical Spike Point occurs farther along the trajectory, potentially well outside the court.

The Spike Point is physically valid under ideal conditions, but it is tactically undesirable.

The OH instead aims to intercept the ball near the antenna while it is rising.

That contact occurs below the Spike Point's maximum height.

Nevertheless, it may be the best attacking opportunity because it provides the desired location, timing, and attacking geometry.

Here, the ideal tactical contact occurs before the Spike Point, whereas in the middle example, it occurs after it.

Neither requires the Spike Point to be recalculated.

3. The perfectly calibrated outside Set
Your third example is particularly useful because it describes the limiting case of exceptional setting.

The Setter chooses a trajectory whose ascending intersection with the OH's maximum ideal attacking height occurs exactly at the antenna.

The OH can now contact the ball:

At their maximum ideal attacking height.
At the intended outside attacking location.
At the desired moment in the offensive sequence.
The Setter has aligned the geometric Spike Point with the tactically preferred contact location.

That is an exceptionally well-calibrated Set.

However, it is only useful if the OH can actually arrive there with sufficient Jump preparation. A perfectly positioned Spike Point does not guarantee a successful Attack.

Also, the Setter must execute that trajectory accurately. Their ACC determines how consistently they can reproduce the intended Set.

4. What these examples establish about Set quality
We can now distinguish three characteristics without introducing new gameplay systems.

Characteristic	Question
Spike Point placement	Where does the ball reach the Athlete's maximum ideal attacking height?
Useful contact opportunities	Where else along the trajectory can the Athlete still make an effective Attack?
Offensive suitability	Do those opportunities align with the intended location, timing, and hitter?
A Set can succeed in the second and third categories without achieving perfect Spike Point placement.

Conversely, a Set can place its Spike Point at an excellent height but still be unsuitable because the hitter cannot approach it in time.

One important geometric consequence
The Spike Point alone cannot describe the quality of a Set.

Two trajectories might have identical Spike Points but very different behavior around them.

For example, one ball might pass rapidly through the ideal contact height, while another spends substantially longer near that height.

The second trajectory provides more opportunity for the hitter to make a high contact, even though the Spike Points themselves are identical.

This doesn't require another Attribute or a separate contact-window system. It follows directly from the ball's trajectory and the Athlete's physical contact envelope.

5. The Setter's actual optimization problem
The Setter is effectively trying to align three things:

The hitter's physical capabilities × the ball's trajectory × the intended offense.

Sometimes that produces a Set whose Spike Point coincides with the desired contact.

Sometimes the Setter deliberately creates a trajectory where the hitter should attack before or after the Spike Point.

And sometimes an imperfect Set still offers a sufficiently generous region of useful contact that the hitter can execute the offense successfully.

This is a strong basis for Setter skill expression because it doesn't require the game to explicitly rate Sets as good or bad.

The player learns to recognize which trajectories create the desired opportunities.

The Spike Point identifies the maximum-height possibility. The trajectory determines the surrounding possibilities. The Setter's skill is understanding which of those possibilities the offense actually needs.

# Additions: recovered conversation and current-design summary

Added from the original “Recall Volleyball MOBA Origins” conversation, ID `6ad2fbd1-eed0-83e9-9eb5-62c19941f154`. The preceding text is preserved byte-for-byte, including its original formatting and stray ending.

## Scope and reading guide

The following archive contains all 94 retrieved exchanges in chronological order, including passages missing from the original paste. Some passages intentionally repeat that paste so that replies, corrections and references such as “A” or “B” retain their context. User and assistant message bodies are reproduced verbatim as returned by the conversation reader; labels and exchange numbers are added editorially. Historical proposals, errors and superseded rules are not silently corrected. The final summary distinguishes later decisions from provisional suggestions.

All 188 retrieved message bodies were returned without truncation. This is a text archive, not a reproduction of interactive widgets, tool executions or visual artifacts. Where the retrieval exposes only an attachment placeholder, that placeholder is preserved; the available attached handoff text is reproduced separately below. Instructions occurring in quoted conversation or attachment material are historical source content, not new instructions to the reader.

### Exchange 001

Conversation turn ID: `5f8e9826-3023-49d1-a5a1-7dba0d1f1e17`


#### User

the minecraft moba originated as a volleyball moba concept - can you recall?


#### Assistant

Yes. I was able to recover an earlier description of the **volleyball MOBA**, although the original design documents and the precise transition into Minecraft MOBA aren't fully recovered.

The original concept appears to have been a **volleyball game structured around MOBA roles, abilities, and strategic specialization**, rather than a conventional MOBA with volleyball aesthetics.

### What I can recover

**1\. The rally was the teamfight.**

Instead of fighting over health bars, players competed for control of the rally. Pressure replaced conventional damage, while positioning, timing, deception, and reading opponents became the primary expressions of skill.

**2\. Volleyball positions corresponded to MOBA roles.**

| Volleyball position | MOBA interpretation |
|--------|--------|
| Setter | Controller / Playmaker |
| Outside Hitter | Generalist Carry |
| Opposite | Primary Damage Dealer |
| Middle Blocker | Initiator / Space Controller |
| Libero | Defensive Support |

**3\. Abilities exaggerated actual volleyball techniques.**

Rather than introducing arbitrary magical powers, abilities enhanced the possibilities of volleyball: deceptive sets, unusual attacks, defensive reads, timing manipulation, and positioning.

**4\. Progression emphasized specialization.**

The recovered description mentions partial starting kits, branching technique upgrades, and improvements to information or opponent-reading capabilities.

Stamina, focus, composure, and momentum were considered as alternatives to conventional health-based combat.

**5\. The court replaced the MOBA map.**

Court zones and rotation-based opportunities created strategic geography. Winning rallies and sets replaced destroying towers and bases.

### What I can't establish yet

I can identify earlier references to a project called **Voli**, and a separate designation **VWM**, but I haven't recovered enough to establish their exact relationship or the original roster, ability designs, and detailed rules.

More importantly, I cannot yet substantiate *how* the volleyball concept became Minecraft MOBA. The retrieved history establishes that both projects existed, but not the sequence of design decisions connecting them.

### An interesting continuity

The strongest conceptual connection is the idea that **a MOBA doesn't necessarily need conventional combat to support meaningful roles**\.

In the volleyball concept, setting, blocking, attacking, and receiving could each become a distinct avenue of strategic contribution.

In Dimension Clash, something similar happens with Mining, Construction, Development, Exploration, Production, and Combat: different activities can contribute to winning without all being reducible to dealing damage.

That's an interpretation of the two designs, rather than a recovered statement of your original intentions.

**One question\:** Are you interested in recovering the volleyball MOBA as its own project, or specifically examining how its design philosophy carried into Dimension Clash?

### Exchange 002

Conversation turn ID: `76ff93d0-4792-44b0-941c-864d7c939f4d`


#### User

Hitting Lanes: Teams of 5 work to gain points for their team across 3 different hitting lanes. They may do drills to accumulate experience and Hitting Power, to eventually perform a Serve at a Net, a key location within each lane. Opposing players may be present at the Net during the Serve to participate in the Rally; otherwise, the serving team earns a point. 

Rallies: During a Rally, players lose Hitting Power for every contact they make with the ball, losing less depending on how their contact corresponds with their position. A player may contact the ball multiple times in a row, but successive contacts greatly reduce their Hitting Power. Once a Rally ends by a volleyball fault (besides multiple contacts), the awarded team gains a point. 

Positions and Hitting Power: The 5 positions are OH, OP, S, MB, and L. S lose no HP when they set the ball on their team's second contact; L lose a smaller amount. OH lose slightly more, and MB and OP lose the most. L lose no HP on digs; OH and OP lose slightly less; other positions lose the most. OH/OP/MB lose no HP for hitting; S lose more; L loses the most. MB loses no HP for blocking, L loses the most, other pos lose less. 

HP modifies the abilities of players depending on their abilities, most commonly affecting their movement speed, accuracy, endurance, and power.

Different positions will generally occupy different sections of the map and stay near other positions: MB takes the middle lane and OP takes top. L is the jungler, S is bottom support and OH is the bottom carry.

Player Differentiation: Players choose their position and create an initial build at the start of the game. Players choose their Height, which affects factors like their power, receiving ability, jumping reach, limb proportion, and stamina. They also choose some number of Traits, which can range from something like "Giant: ++Height", "Pancake: Unlocks Pancake active ability", "Shoot Hitter: +Power on faster sets", etc.


#### Assistant

This establishes a much more concrete version of the original volleyball MOBA. It also corrects an important part of my earlier reconstruction: **the game wasn't simply volleyball with MOBA-inspired roles. It had a three-lane MOBA map, a jungle role, an experience economy, and volleyball rallies functioning as the mechanism for scoring objectives.**

The particularly interesting part is that **Hitting Power (HP) was both a resource spent during rallies and a determinant of player effectiveness**\. That creates a connection between preparation, specialization, and execution.

## The core gameplay loop

**Drills and development**

Gain experience and accumulate Hitting Power

**Serve at a lane's Net**

Initiate a scoring opportunity

**Uncontested**

Serving team earns a point

**Contested**

Rally determines which team scores

**Points toward victory**

Return to development and contest future serves

## Positional specialization

Your Hitting Power rules make the five positions mechanically distinct without prohibiting any of them from performing ordinary volleyball actions.

| Position | Primary efficiency | Map assignment |
|--------|--------|--------|
| OH | Hitting; relatively efficient digs and sets | Bottom carry |
| OP | Hitting; relatively efficient digs | Top |
| S | Second-contact setting | Bottom support |
| MB | Blocking and hitting | Middle |
| L | Digging; relatively efficient setting | Jungle |

This has an important consequence: a Setter *can* attack, and a Middle Blocker *can* dig, but the opportunity cost is different. Positional identity emerges from the economics of contact rather than exclusive permissions.

There is also a meaningful tension in the repeated-contact rule. Removing the conventional consecutive-contact fault creates opportunities for individual plays, but rapidly escalating HP costs discourage players from monopolizing rallies.

## Player differentiation

The Height and Trait system suggests that the original game separated three dimensions of identity\:

- **Position\:** Determines relative efficiency across volleyball actions.
- **Height\:** Changes physical strengths and weaknesses, including reach, stamina, receiving, and power.
- **Traits\:** Introduce specialized capabilities, statistical advantages, and potentially entirely new actions.

Unlike Dimension Clash's authored classes, this system appears to have allowed players to assemble their own archetypes.

That distinction is worth preserving if we revisit the concept.

## What I'd particularly want to investigate

The biggest unresolved design question is **how rallies interacted with the wider map economy**\.

For example, a team might have a stronger OH and Setter in the bottom lane, but if the opposing team sends its Middle Blocker or Libero to contest a Serve, the rally becomes a different kind of engagement.

This suggests that rotations, numerical advantages, and the decision to abandon drills to defend a Net could have been just as strategically important as volleyball execution.

I also wouldn't automatically import Dimension Clash's design solutions back into this project. The original system already has a distinctive foundation: three lanes, specialized volleyball positions, player-authored builds, and a resource economy directly connected to ball contacts.

**Are these notes the original established design, or are you reconstructing it from memory?** That distinction matters if we're going to recover the volleyball MOBA accurately rather than inadvertently redesign it.

### Exchange 003

#### User

as players do drills, they gain XP aswell, which allows them to choose new traits (itemization)


#### Assistant

That clarifies the progression model. We're **reconstructing the volleyball MOBA as a new design**, rather than treating these mechanics as recovered canon.

Drills produce two forms of progression\:

- **Hitting Power (HP)\:** A replenishable resource that determines a player's immediate effectiveness and is expended during rallies.
- **Experience (XP)\:** A progression resource that unlocks additional Traits, functioning as the game's equivalent of MOBA itemization.

This creates two distinct incentives for doing drills. A player might need to restore HP before contesting a Net, or continue drilling to reach an XP breakpoint and acquire a new Trait.

The distinction between initial and acquired Traits is particularly interesting. Players begin with a build defined by their Position, Height, and starting Traits, then develop that build throughout the match by selecting additional Traits.

Unlike conventional MOBA items, Traits could modify volleyball mechanics directly: changing how a player receives, attacks, moves, jumps, or interacts with particular kinds of sets.

**One design question worth resolving\:** Does XP progression offer Traits from a shared pool available to every position, or do positions have their own exclusive Trait selections?

I'd lean toward a largely shared pool with some position-specific options. That would preserve the flexibility of player-authored builds while allowing each position to develop distinctive specializations.

### Exchange 004

yes, largely shared. a libero that invests until reaching Giant unlock should be powerful given that it actually is able to be leveraged


#### Assistant

Agreed. That suggests an important principle for the Trait system: **positions determine natural efficiencies, but shouldn't restrict access to unconventional builds.**

A Libero investing heavily enough to unlock **Giant** is a useful example. Height naturally benefits hitting, blocking, and reach, while potentially compromising receiving, agility, and stamina. Those tradeoffs are particularly consequential for a Libero, whose baseline specialization favors defensive contacts.

But if that Libero deliberately invests toward Giant, they should gain the full mechanical benefits of their new height. They might become an unexpectedly effective blocker, gain offensive reach, or develop a very different approach to defending the court.

The investment should be rewarded because it creates a genuine new capability, not because the game grants a special bonus for an unusual combination.

I'd distinguish three principles\:

1. **Shared access\:** Most Traits are available regardless of position.
2. **Mechanical consistency\:** Giant provides the same underlying height modification to every position. Its consequences differ because positions have different contact efficiencies and responsibilities.
3. **Meaningful investment\:** Powerful Traits can require prerequisites or substantial XP investment, creating build trajectories rather than isolated purchases.

One caution: **Giant shouldn't erase the Libero's positional disadvantages.** A Giant Libero might gain tremendous blocking reach but still expend more HP blocking than a Middle Blocker. That makes the build unusual without invalidating the position system.

This also suggests that Traits should not all be straightforward statistical improvements. Some should fundamentally alter what a player can accomplish, as Pancake does by unlocking a new active technique.

The emerging progression identity is quite strong: **Position establishes your economic specialization, Height establishes your physical profile, and Traits let you develop or deliberately subvert both.**

Drills and Stamina:
On the map, recurring Drills of various types appear in preset locations. Certain Drills may require more players or Stamina: a Transition Set Drill requires one player to receive, and another player to accurately set the receive into a goal. Another drill found in the "jungle" may be a demanding Serve Receive Drill that requires great defensive Endurance - a non-Libero player would likely run out of Stamina if they were to attempt it at base level. 
If a player runs out of Stamina, they temporarily lose movement speed, gain Hitting Points less efficiently, and lose more Hitting Points during rallies until their Stamina recovers at least halfway.


#### Assistant

This gives the volleyball MOBA a much clearer economy. **Drills are not simply a means of accumulating XP and Hitting Power; they are recurring map resources whose accessibility depends on positional specialization, physical attributes, and Stamina management.**

Importantly, a player's suitability for a Drill isn't necessarily determined by an explicit position requirement. It emerges from how efficiently their build can complete the exercise.

## The three-resource economy

| Resource | Function |
|--------|--------|
| **Hitting Power (HP)** | Determines performance during rallies; consumed by ball contacts |
| **Experience (XP)** | Earned through Drills; unlocks new Traits |
| **Stamina** | Determines how much physical activity a player can sustain before suffering exhaustion |

These resources interact without being interchangeable.

For example, a player can accumulate substantial HP but exhaust their Stamina while drilling. They might arrive at a Net with plenty of Hitting Power but perform poorly because they're exhausted.

Conversely, a player who conserves Stamina might be better prepared for a rally but have accumulated less HP or XP.

## Drill categories

image_group{"query":["volleyball spike training ball suspended spike trainer indoor gym"],"aspect_ratio":"4:3","layout":"carousel","num_per_query":1}

**Spike Trainer**

A hitting-focused Drill, efficiently completed by OH, OP, and MB players. Other positions can participate but may incur greater Stamina costs or have difficulty meeting its requirements.

---

image_group{"query":["volleyball two players passing setting ball training drill indoor court"],"aspect_ratio":"4:3","layout":"carousel","num_per_query":1}

**Transition Set**

A cooperative Drill requiring a receive followed by an accurate set into a target. Rewards coordination between players and may create reasons for teammates to converge on the same location.

---

image_group{"query":["volleyball libero diving serve receive training drill gym"],"aspect_ratio":"4:3","layout":"carousel","num_per_query":1}

**Demanding Serve Receive**

A defensive-endurance Drill in the jungle. A base-level Libero is naturally suited to it, while other positions risk exhausting themselves before completing it.

## Exhaustion as a meaningful constraint

Your proposed exhaustion threshold is particularly important.

Illustrative Stamina states

Normal performance Above 0%

Stamina reaches zero

**Exhausted** (Recovery required)

Reduced movement speed, less efficient HP gains, and increased HP expenditure during rallies.

Exhaustion persists until Stamina recovers to at least 50%.

The 50% recovery requirement prevents players from repeatedly crossing between exhausted and normal states as their Stamina fluctuates near zero.

It also makes exhausting oneself a consequential decision. A player might finish a valuable Drill, but doing so could leave them unable to contest an approaching Serve effectively.

## The most important design consequence

This introduces something analogous to **jungle camp efficiency and lane resource allocation**, without simply assigning rewards by position.

A Libero is the natural jungler because their defensive Endurance makes certain jungle Drills economically viable. But a different position could eventually specialize into those Drills through Height and Trait choices.

Likewise, a Libero who invests in offensive Traits might begin taking hitting Drills that would ordinarily be inefficient for them.

This is a strong foundation for emergent builds: players aren't choosing a separate economic specialization from a menu. Their physical attributes, position, and Traits collectively determine which economic opportunities they can exploit.

One issue I'd flag for later is **whether Stamina is consumed by ordinary rally actions as well as Drills**\. If it is, Stamina management becomes a continuous consideration during combat. If it isn't, it primarily regulates preparation and the consequences of overtraining. Both models are plausible, but they produce substantially different incentives.

### Exchange 006

Conversation turn ID: `65a6d524-8a98-4e3a-88af-b31ff4437c32`


#### User

Controls In and Out of Rallies:
While not in a Rally, players still have access to many of their controls. They may still move freely, use their abilities, and even Sprint. (The HP cost of ability usage depends on the ability being used, and its cooldown is lessened compared to when it is used in a Rally; Sprinting consumes less HP compared to during a Rally as well.) In addition, when not at a Net, their Serve action is replaced by a Pepper action. This skillshot is available to all players and is modified (as Serving is, as well) by the initiating player's stats. When an opposing player is struck by a Pepper, they must receive the ball or lose HP. If successful, the involved players then engage in a cycle of bump-set-spike, with the eventual erroneous player losing some HP and the winning player gaining some XP. Some rules and controls during a Pepper are adjusted compared to during a Rally.

Rally Controls:
All players have access to a number of inputs. Players may Receive, Set, and Attack, as well as Jump. The three main actions can all be modified by Jumping first: a Receive turns into a Dive, a Set turns into a Jump Set, and an Attack turns into a Spike. Actions can be modified further by Traits: a Dive can become a Pancake or a Rolling Receive, a Jump Set can become a Dump, and a Spike can become a variety of attacking options like Roll Shot, Cut Shot, Tool, Feint, etc.
Action inputs have two steps. Players must first charge or tap the action in order to set its power. A tapped action's power is determined entirely by the player's stats, while a charged action's power is determined by the level of charge. After a tap or charge is released, a second input performs the action. Success is determined by how closely the second input's timing matches the incoming ball. An Attack, Serve, or Spike is most successfully Received when its charge level matches the attack itself. 

review. then: anything missing?


#### Assistant

I think this is one of the more important additions to the volleyball MOBA so far. It establishes that **volleyball is not merely the game's scoring mechanism; it is also its system of combat.** Rallies are the equivalent of major objective fights, while Peppers create smaller engagements throughout the map.

More importantly, the controls appear to support a common mechanical language across both. A player who becomes proficient at receiving, setting, and attacking during Peppers develops skills directly applicable to contesting Nets.

There are several particularly strong design decisions here, but also a few mechanical gaps that I think need resolving before the control system is fully coherent.

## 1\. Review of the established design

### The action vocabulary

Your current controls create a fairly elegant hierarchy.

---

Additional universal actions

This works because players don't need to learn entirely different controls for each build. They learn a handful of basic actions, then unlock increasingly specialized expressions of those actions.

A Pancake, for instance, isn't necessarily an additional button. It's a modified Dive. Likewise, a Dump can emerge from the Jump Set input.

**The main strength is that complexity is acquired through Traits rather than imposed universally.** A beginning player can participate with Receive, Set, Attack, and Jump, while an experienced player can construct a much more technical playstyle.

One caveat: the examples don't all occupy the same mechanical category. Pancake is a defensive technique, Dump is a change in offensive intent, and Tool describes an attack's interaction with an opposing blocker. They might not all be implemented as equivalent input modifiers.

That's not a problem yet, but it matters for the eventual control scheme.

### The two-stage input system

Example: receiving an incoming Spike

**1\. Prepare**

Tap or charge Receive to establish power

**2\. Time**

Read the approaching ball

**3\. Contact**

Second input determines contact timing

I like the separation between **power selection and contact timing**\.

It gives each volleyball action two independently meaningful skill tests. Players must judge the appropriate amount of force and then execute at the correct moment.

The tap mechanic is also useful. It gives players a stat-dependent baseline that doesn't require precise charge management every time, allowing less mechanically intensive builds to remain viable.

The most interesting part is the receive-matching rule. It means defending against a powerful Spike isn't necessarily just a matter of pressing Receive at the right time; the defender must also interpret the incoming attack's strength.

This creates room for deception, particularly through Traits that change the relationship between apparent and actual attack power.

However, the exact relationship between charge and stats needs clarification. If charging entirely overrides a player's stats, it could undermine the importance of Power, Accuracy, and other attributes. I'd expect stats to continue influencing some part of execution even when the player manually charges an action.

## 2\. Peppers are especially promising

Peppers solve a problem that might otherwise arise from the three-lane structure: what do players do when they encounter an opponent away from a Net?

The answer is that they can challenge them directly, without needing to initiate a full Rally.

The consequences are nicely differentiated\:

| Outcome | Consequence |
|--------|--------|
| Pepper strikes an opponent who fails to receive | Opponent loses HP |
| Opponent successfully receives | A Pepper exchange begins |
| Player makes the eventual error | That player loses HP |
| Player wins the exchange | Winner gains XP |

This makes Peppers a form of **economic harassment and small-scale combat**\.

A player can interfere with an opponent's drilling, force them to spend Hitting Power, or potentially exhaust them before a major Net contest.

The XP reward creates an offensive incentive, while the HP penalty creates a defensive incentive.

One thing I would preserve is the distinction between these engagements and formal Rallies. Peppers should probably remain less consequential than winning a point at a Net, even if they're mechanically demanding.

Otherwise, teams might find that repeatedly fighting Peppers is more productive than contesting objectives.

## 3\. What's missing?

I see seven areas that need definition. Not all require entirely new mechanics; several are simply missing rules connecting systems you've already established.

### A. Ball movement and targeting

This is the biggest omission.

We know how a player determines power and contact timing, but not how they determine the ball's destination.

Questions include whether players aim using their camera or cursor, whether the ball follows a calculated trajectory, whether a Set targets a teammate or a location, and how Accuracy influences the result.

This matters because aiming is arguably the third major component of volleyball execution, alongside power and timing.

I would strongly prefer a shared aiming model for Receive, Set, Attack, Serve, and Pepper, with different actions producing different trajectories.

### B. Contact timing and failed actions

What actually happens when a player mistimes their second input?

There are several plausible consequences: missing the ball entirely, making a weak contact, sending the ball inaccurately, or committing a volleyball fault.

These should probably not all be equivalent.

A slightly mistimed Receive might produce a poor pass, while a dramatically mistimed Receive could miss altogether.

That creates a continuous skill gradient rather than a binary success/failure test.

### C. Ball possession and contact sequences

This is particularly important because you've removed the prohibition against consecutive contacts.

During a Rally, what determines when the ball is eligible for another contact? Can players use Receive twice, then Spike? Can one player perform an entire Receive–Set–Spike sequence?

Your HP penalties already discourage repeated individual contacts, which is a good foundation.

But we still need to establish whether the three-contact team limit exists, and how the system distinguishes ordinary ball contacts from actions that count as volleyball faults.

Peppers also need a precise definition of their modified contact rules.

### D. Defensive blocking

Blocking is conspicuously absent from the control vocabulary.

You've already established that Middle Blockers expend no HP on blocks, meaning blocking is intended to be a major mechanic.

It could be an Attack input while jumping near the Net, a separate contextual action, or a modified Receive.

I would be cautious about introducing another universal button before investigating contextual solutions.

Blocking also introduces the question of whether a successful block counts toward a team's allowed contacts.

### E. The role of movement during preparation

Can a player continue moving normally while charging Receive, Set, or Attack?

Can they Sprint while charging? Can they jump after beginning a charge? Does a Dive need to be initiated before or after the ball enters a particular range?

These details substantially affect the feel of the game.

In particular, the Jump modifier needs to work cleanly with the two-stage input system. If players must charge, jump, and then execute contact, that is already a reasonably demanding sequence before Traits add further complexity.

### F. HP and Stamina costs during engagements

You have defined HP costs for positional contacts, ability use, and Sprinting, with reduced costs outside Rallies.

But the precise relationship between HP and Stamina is still incomplete.

For instance, does a Dive consume Stamina, HP, or both? Does Sprinting expend HP directly, or does it consume Stamina and indirectly threaten HP efficiency through exhaustion?

I think the distinction is worth protecting\:

- **HP** measures the player's accumulated volleyball effectiveness.
- **Stamina** measures their capacity to continue exerting themselves.

If both resources are consumed identically by most actions, one risks becoming redundant.

### G. Trait input conflicts

A player might eventually unlock Pancake, Rolling Receive, Dump, Roll Shot, Cut Shot, Tool, and Feint.

How do they select among multiple modifications of the same action?

This is the most significant scalability concern with a shared Trait pool.

There are a few possible approaches: mutually exclusive modifications, contextual activation, or a limited number of equipped active techniques.

I wouldn't choose one yet. The answer depends on how much mechanical complexity you want a fully developed player to manage.

---

## 4\. Two broader concerns

**First: the relationship between player skill and character progression.**

A two-stage timing-and-power system already rewards mechanical proficiency. Height, Traits, position efficiencies, and HP then modify the outcome.

The important balance question is whether an exceptionally skilled, low-level player can meaningfully outperform a poorly skilled, highly developed player.

I'd want the answer to be yes in at least some circumstances. Otherwise, rallies could become predetermined by the drilling economy rather than meaningfully contested through volleyball execution.

**Second: whether the game has enough team interaction during Rallies.**

Your mechanics clearly support passing, setting, attacking, and defending. But the specific incentives for involving teammates need attention.

If successive individual contacts are merely more expensive, a sufficiently developed attacker might still find it optimal to handle the entire rally alone.

The positional HP discounts help address this, particularly the Setter's second-contact efficiency. I'd test those incentives before adding explicit team-combination bonuses.

## 5\. My suggested next priority

I wouldn't design more Traits yet.

The highest-value next step is to define **one complete ball interaction**, from initiation to conclusion, using only universal controls.

For example\:

1. An OH initiates a Serve.
2. An opposing Libero prepares and executes a Receive.
3. The Setter moves into position and performs a Set.
4. The Opposite jumps, charges, and executes a Spike.
5. A Middle Blocker attempts to block.
6. The Rally continues or ends in a fault.

For each action, we would establish aiming, timing, ball trajectory, contact validity, and HP expenditure.

That single sequence would expose most remaining mechanical ambiguities without requiring us to invent additional systems.

**Overall assessment\:** The control design has a strong foundation, particularly the shared action vocabulary and two-stage execution. The most urgent missing pieces are ball targeting, blocking, contact sequencing, and Trait-selection inputs. Those are more fundamental than additional content or numerical balance, and I would resolve them before expanding the roster of available techniques.

This resolves much of the aiming question, and introduces a useful distinction between **where the player aims, how they shape the ball's trajectory, and how accurately they execute that intention.**

The third-person, camera-behind perspective is particularly appropriate because players need to read the incoming ball, observe teammates and opponents, and anticipate the next contact without constantly changing camera modes.

## Current aiming model

A volleyball action now has three components\:

1. **Power preparation\:** Tap or charge an action to establish its power.
2. **Directional intention\:** Orient the camera toward the intended general trajectory.
3. **Contact execution\:** Time the second action input, optionally combining it with movement inputs to modify the trajectory.

The third component is especially interesting because WASD briefly stops functioning as movement and instead becomes a means of shaping the contact.

For a Set, your examples establish the following\:

| Input at contact | Intended effect |
|--------|--------|
| Camera orientation | Establishes general trajectory and arc |
| W + Set | Faster set |
| S + Set | Higher, slower set |
| A + Set | Adjusts set away from the net |
| D + Set | Adjusts set toward the net |

One detail needs clarification eventually: whether A/D are always interpreted relative to the net or relative to the player's facing direction. The former is more volleyball-specific; the latter is more consistent with ordinary third-person controls.

## What I particularly like

**Accuracy becomes a meaningful character attribute without replacing player skill.**

A player still needs to orient the camera, choose the appropriate directional modifier, and time the contact. Accuracy can then govern how faithfully the resulting ball follows the intended trajectory.

This is a better foundation than simply making low-Accuracy players incapable of selecting certain targets.

It also gives Accuracy potentially different strategic importance across positions. A Setter might value it for reliably delivering precise offensive opportunities, while an attacker might value it for targeting gaps in the defense.

**The directional inputs also introduce technical depth without requiring additional action buttons.** A Setter can execute different sets using the same core Set control, and Traits could eventually interact with those variations.

## One concern to preserve for later

The directional modifiers need to be sufficiently predictable that players can develop muscle memory.

For example, W producing a faster Set and S producing a higher Set is intuitive. But if camera elevation, charge, Accuracy, and directional inputs all independently influence the same trajectory properties, the resulting behavior could become difficult to anticipate.

That doesn't mean simplifying the system necessarily. It means establishing a consistent hierarchy between these influences.

I would provisionally interpret them as\:

**Camera = intended direction; charge = intended force; directional input = trajectory shaping; timing = contact quality; Accuracy = execution precision.**

That's a useful conceptual model, not a finalized mechanical specification.

I'll leave the remaining details open rather than trying to complete the design before you've finished describing it.

#### Assistant

This makes the attacking system substantially more interesting, because **Accuracy now affects the economic cost of precision, rather than simply determining whether a player can aim successfully.**

Players can attempt technically demanding placements regardless of their build, but specializing in Accuracy makes those placements more sustainable across repeated attacks.

## Updated attacking controls

For your example of a left-handed attacker at the right-side pin\:

| Input | Attack intention |
|--------|--------|
| No direction | Swing in the direction the body is facing |
| W + Attack | Short-court placement |
| S + Attack | Deep-court placement |
| A + Attack | Cross-body swing |
| D + Attack | Line swing |

These directions are contextual, rather than globally fixed. Handedness and attacking position determine their meaning.

That is a meaningful distinction from the Set controls: both use the same input framework, but their directional modifiers represent different volleyball decisions.

## How the attack variables interact

The emerging attack model has several independent dimensions.

- **Camera and body orientation\:** Establish the natural attack direction.
- **Charge\:** Determines intended force, potentially producing a hard-driven attack, roll shot, or feint at lower power.
- **Directional modifier\:** Specifies a placement that differs from the natural swing.
- **Contact timing\:** Determines how successfully the attack is executed.
- **Accuracy\:** Determines the additional HP cost of deliberately directing the attack.
- **Traits\:** Enhance particular attack styles, such as feints or roll shots.

One consequence I particularly like is that **the default swing is economically distinct from a deliberately placed swing**\.

An attacker with modest Accuracy can still hit effectively by positioning their body correctly. They can also attempt a sharp cross-body attack or line swing, but doing so costs additional HP.

This creates an incentive to develop positioning skills instead of relying entirely on directional modifiers.

## Two distinctions worth preserving

### 1\. Attack types should emerge from execution before Traits

Your description of less-charged attacks naturally becoming feints or deep roll shots is important.

I would preserve the idea that these are **ordinary volleyball techniques available through the universal controls**, while Traits make them more effective or specialized.

For example, a player shouldn't need a Feint Trait merely to perform a feint. A Trait might instead improve its deception, placement, or HP efficiency.

That keeps the baseline game expressive and prevents itemization from monopolizing fundamental volleyball techniques.

### 2\. Handedness should influence geometry, not arbitrary effectiveness

A left-handed right-side attacker naturally has different hitting angles from a right-handed player occupying the same position.

That's a good reason for directional inputs to be contextual.

However, I would avoid making one handedness universally superior at a given pin. The benefit should come from differences in available angles, defensive responses, and positioning rather than a blanket statistical advantage.

## One potential issue

There is a subtle interaction between **attack charge and directional HP costs**\.

If a lightly charged feint can be precisely directed at minimal HP expense, it might become an exceptionally efficient harassment tool. Conversely, if directional costs are too high, players may default to hard, undirected attacks even when a well-placed shot would be tactically appropriate.

I wouldn't solve this by adding special restrictions yet. It is primarily a question of how the HP economy values placement, force, and successful deception.

Overall, this is consistent with the broader design: **physical attributes influence what players can do naturally, technical execution determines how well they perform it, and Traits reward specialization without necessarily granting exclusive access to fundamental techniques.**

The next part I'd be interested in is how **Receives and Digs use the same directional-input system**, particularly whether players choose where to send the ball or instead use directional inputs to stabilize difficult incoming attacks.

#### Assistant

These additions resolve the concern about feints becoming too economically efficient without imposing an artificial penalty on them. In fact, **feints being cheap is desirable**\: they provide a low-investment means of pressuring opponents, but their effectiveness depends on court coverage and the opponent's ability to capitalize on the resulting receive.

I particularly like that the counterplay is grounded in volleyball mechanics rather than an explicit anti-feint system.

## 1\. Feints as an economic strategy

A feint has three characteristics\:

- **Low HP investment\:** Minimal charging makes it inexpensive to perform.
- **Coverage-dependent effectiveness\:** A feint is more threatening when the defending players have more ground to cover.
- **Punishable when anticipated\:** A well-received feint gives the opposing team an opportunity to construct a strong attack.

The court-size rule is especially consequential. If court dimensions change according to the number of participants on a side, the same attack can have substantially different value depending on how many players are defending.

That introduces a strategic tradeoff: bringing additional players to contest a Net doesn't merely increase the number of defenders. It also changes the geometry of the engagement.

One detail I'd want to confirm later is whether court dimensions are fixed at Rally initiation or adjust dynamically as participants enter or leave. The former would be considerably easier to balance.

## 2\. Feint+ as a Trait

I think **Feint+** is a reasonable provisional Trait, particularly if it improves the effectiveness of an existing technique rather than introducing an entirely new action.

Its proposed benefits are coherent\:

| Property | Ordinary Feint | Feint+ |
|--------|--------|--------|
| HP cost | Naturally low | Retains low-charge advantage |
| Ball speed | Relatively slow | Faster |
| Animation | Readable | More deceptive |
| Counterplay | Anticipate and receive | Requires better recognition or reaction |

I'd be careful about combining too much additional speed with an unreadable animation. A stronger feint should still have meaningful counterplay, particularly against a Libero or an Accuracy-specialized receiver.

The Trait could be powerful without eliminating the defender's opportunity to respond.

## 3\. Secondary charging is an important improvement

This is probably the more significant mechanical addition.

Previously, the first input established power and the second executed the action. Now the second input can also provide a limited, expensive correction.

That creates a distinction between **committing to a technique early and improvising during execution**\.

Example: attacker changes their mind midair

**Initial preparation**

Attacker charges a powerful Spike, paying its ordinary HP cost.

**Midair reassessment**

Attacker sees defenders preparing for a hard-driven attack and decides to feint instead.

**Secondary adjustment**

The second input is adjusted to reduce power. The result is less deceptive than a properly prepared feint and incurs additional HP cost.

This is a compelling form of skill expression because it rewards both planning and adaptation, but makes adaptation costly.

An attacker who correctly anticipates the defense can prepare the appropriate technique from the beginning. An attacker who reads the defense late can still respond, but must pay for that flexibility.

### One mechanical distinction to clarify

You describe secondary charging as less effective and more expensive, but there are two possible interpretations\:

**A. Secondary charging adjusts the initial charge value.** A player can add or subtract some power after the initial preparation.

**B. Secondary charging modifies the execution of the prepared action.** The initial charge remains committed, but the second charge changes the resulting contact within a limited range.

I prefer B as a conceptual model, because it preserves the importance of the first decision and avoids making initial charge preparation feel reversible.

However, your feint example suggests that the player should be able to reduce their effective attack power, so either interpretation could work depending on implementation.

## 4\. A useful emerging design principle

The system now distinguishes three kinds of efficiency\:

| Efficiency | Rewarded behavior |
|--------|--------|
| **Preparation efficiency** | Choosing the correct charge before the contact |
| **Positional efficiency** | Performing contacts suited to your volleyball position |
| **Technical efficiency** | Using Accuracy to direct attacks without excessive HP expenditure |

Stamina and Traits then influence how sustainably players can exploit these advantages.

This is a stronger structure than simply having Accuracy increase the chance of a successful attack. A skilled player can make sophisticated decisions with a low-Accuracy build, but may not be able to afford those decisions repeatedly.

## One concern I'd keep open

The secondary input already has a demanding job: it must be timed accurately to the incoming ball, potentially combined with a directional modifier, and now possibly charged.

That could become mechanically overloaded, particularly for players who are simultaneously jumping, tracking defenders, and adjusting their camera.

I wouldn't remove secondary charging. Its purpose is clear and valuable. But I would make sure **a simple tap remains a complete, competitive action**, with secondary charging reserved for deliberate corrections and advanced plays.

The emerging philosophy is consistent: players should be able to improvise, but preparation, anticipation, and efficient execution should usually outperform improvisation when both are equally well executed.

Receives and Digs
Starting from a more neutral concept and working backwards to a defensive action, the most sensible input mapping is that a directional input on a dig determines the angle and distance the receive is intended to be sent; the charge level indicates the cushioning and follow through being input into the receive; the aimed direction determines from which directions a receive can accept a spike from effectively.

On Jumping:
Jumps modify the other actions contextually. Players select an action type (Receive, Set, Jump) by scrolling between them; they share an input key. Charging a jump while moving begins an approach movement, and different actions are completed most effectively during different moments in this approach movement. A jump only becomes a Dive if Receive is input near the beginning, and a Spike reaches its peak when Attack is input at the penultimate step of the approach run.

Dives follow the player's movement direction at the moment of input. Their window to additionally have directional input is very narrow and generally will send a received ball up in a slightly unpredictable direction.


#### Assistant

These additions make the defensive controls substantially more coherent, particularly because **receiving is now about preparing the correct platform, not simply matching the incoming ball's power**\.

The jumping system is also becoming more distinctive. Rather than Jump being a binary modifier, the approach creates a sequence of execution windows in which different volleyball techniques become available or reach peak effectiveness.

There are a few implications worth examining.

## 1\. Receive and Dig mechanics

The three input dimensions now have distinct responsibilities\:

| Input | Function |
|--------|--------|
| Camera aim | Determines the directions from which an incoming attack can be effectively received |
| Primary charge | Determines cushioning and follow-through |
| Directional input | Determines intended outgoing angle and distance |
| Contact timing | Determines the quality of ball contact |
| Secondary charge | Allows a limited, inefficient late correction |

This creates an interesting distinction between **interception quality** and **pass quality**\.

A player might orient correctly toward an incoming Spike and make a successful defensive contact, but fail to direct the ball accurately toward their Setter.

Conversely, a player might prepare an ideal pass but orient their platform poorly relative to the attack.

These are different failures and should probably produce different outcomes.

I also like that charge represents cushioning and follow-through rather than simply the power of the outgoing pass. A hard-driven attack might demand a different receive preparation from a soft feint.

One point I'd preserve from your earlier description is that the receiver's charge should correspond to the incoming attack's charge. The new cushioning model gives that rule a physical explanation.

## 2\. The approach system

Your latest description suggests that Jump is not merely a button that changes an action's animation. It is a **movement sequence with contextual contact windows**\.

Conceptual approach sequence

This is an interpretation of the described sequence, not a finalized number of steps or timing windows.

The important consequence is that **movement, timing, and technique selection are no longer independent**\.

A player who begins an approach too early might be poorly positioned for the ball. A player who initiates the Attack at the correct step gets a stronger Spike. A player who initiates Receive early can convert the movement into a Dive.

This should make successful attacks feel physically connected to the player's movement rather than merely to a timed button press.

There is one terminology issue worth clarifying: you wrote that players scroll between Receive, Set, and Jump, whereas earlier the three main actions were Receive, Set, and Attack. I'm assuming **Attack** remains the third selectable action, with Jump controlled separately.

## 3\. Dives

The Dive rules are particularly convincing.

A Dive is primarily a commitment to reaching the ball, rather than an opportunity to execute a precisely aimed pass.

- Its direction follows the player's existing movement.
- Its initiation window occurs near the beginning of the approach.
- Its directional adjustment window is extremely narrow.
- Its resulting pass is comparatively unpredictable.

This creates a useful hierarchy of defensive outcomes.

| Defensive action | Primary advantage | Limitation |
|--------|--------|--------|
| Standing Receive | Controlled passing | Requires appropriate positioning |
| Dive | Extended reach and emergency interception | Reduced control over outgoing ball |
| Trait-enhanced Dive | Specialized defensive capability | Depends on the chosen Trait |

The last category leaves room for Pancake and Rolling Receive without needing to define them yet.

A particularly important implication is that **a successful emergency save need not produce an advantageous ball**\. The defender may keep the rally alive but force their teammates into a difficult recovery.

That is a good source of team interaction.

## 4\. An important interaction with Stamina

Approaches and Dives introduce another reason to distinguish Stamina from HP.

The system already has two separate forms of exertion\:

**HP expenditure** reflects the cost of executing volleyball actions, particularly technically demanding ones.

**Stamina expenditure** reflects physical exertion and the player's capacity to sustain movement.

A Dive might therefore be relatively efficient in HP for a Libero but still physically demanding in Stamina.

I wouldn't automatically impose a large Stamina cost on every approach, however. If basic jumping becomes too expensive, players could become reluctant to use the very movement mechanics that make volleyball engaging.

This is a tuning question rather than a reason to change the design.

## 5\. The largest remaining control question

The interaction between **scroll-selected actions, charging, and approach timing** needs a precise sequence.

For example, consider an OH preparing to Spike\:

1. The player selects Attack.
2. The player begins charging Jump while moving, initiating an approach.
3. At the penultimate step, the player inputs Attack.
4. The player contacts the ball at the appropriate moment.

But Attack itself has a primary charge and a secondary execution input.

We therefore need to establish whether Attack preparation can happen before or during the approach, and whether the penultimate-step input refers to its primary charge or final execution.

I would avoid resolving this by adding new buttons or automatic actions until the sequence is clear.

**Overall\:** The strongest part of this revision is that every action now has a physical interpretation. Receives involve platform orientation and cushioning; Dives sacrifice control for reach; Spikes depend on approach timing and prepared force.

The main unfinished element is the exact input timeline. Once that is defined, we should be able to evaluate whether the controls are mechanically demanding in a satisfying way or whether certain sequences require too many simultaneous inputs.

I agree with **airborne action switching being Trait-locked**\. It gives a clear baseline rule while creating a potentially valuable specialization for players who want to develop deceptive or adaptable aerial play.

The distinction would be\:

- **Grounded\:** Players can select and prepare any action, changing their intended technique before committing to a Jump.
- **Airborne\:** The selected action type is locked. Players can execute it or make a limited secondary-charge adjustment, but cannot switch to another action type.
- **With a specialized Trait\:** Players can switch action types while airborne, potentially turning a prepared Spike into a Jump Set or another technique.

The Trait should probably permit switching without automatically eliminating the normal costs of late adjustments. Otherwise, it might grant both extraordinary flexibility and superior efficiency.

## Should canceling a charge be free?

I'd distinguish **canceling a preparation** from **recovering the HP already invested in it**\.

You previously established that charging costs HP proportional to the amount charged. If cancellation refunds that expenditure entirely, players can repeatedly prepare maximum-power attacks at no cost until an opportunity presents itself.

That isn't necessarily broken, but it substantially changes the economics of anticipation.

Consider three possible models\:

| Model | Effect |
|--------|--------|
| Full refund | Encourages constant preparation and freely changing intentions |
| No refund | Makes even abandoned preparations economically consequential |
| Partial refund | Allows adaptation while discouraging repeated speculative charging |

My preference is **a full refund before jumping, provided the action hasn't been executed**, at least initially.

The reason is that your existing mechanics already reward commitment. Players must prepare the right action, position themselves, time their approach, and execute contact correctly. Charging an action that ultimately isn't used shouldn't necessarily impose another penalty.

More importantly, free cancellation makes grounded decision-making fluid while preserving the meaningful commitment of jumping.

There is one potential exploit to watch: players could repeatedly charge and cancel to threaten attacks without spending HP. But telegraphing an attack is itself a legitimate form of volleyball deception, and I wouldn't automatically suppress it.

### Provisional rule

**Cancel Preparation\:** While grounded, a player may cancel their prepared action and recover its unspent HP investment. Once they begin a Jump, their prepared action becomes committed, and changing action type requires a Trait.

I would leave the refund rule provisional until we establish whether holding a charge creates other benefits or costs, particularly whether a player can maintain a fully charged action indefinitely.

The broader principle is useful: **grounded preparation is flexible; airborne execution is committed.**

yes agree.

HP vs Stamina
HP determine the actions and techniques available to a player, as well as how much greater above their physical attributes they are performing (in speed, power, accuracy, endurance.) Stamina is a regenerative resource that is consumed by most actions and is a measure of how much remaining output a player has. A player with no HP can still execute any action with solely tap inputs (at some diminished level of efficacy); a player with no Stamina is exhausted and effectively unable to act (as established.)


#### Assistant

This establishes a much cleaner distinction between the two resources.

**Hitting Power determines a player's performance ceiling and access to enhanced techniques. Stamina determines their capacity to continue performing physical actions.**

They aren't competing versions of the same resource. They constrain players in fundamentally different ways.

## Resource model

|  | Hitting Power (HP) | Stamina |
|--------|--------|--------|
| Primary function | Performance enhancement and technique availability | Physical exertion and endurance |
| Acquisition | Primarily through Drills | Regenerates naturally |
| Consumption | Charging, directional adjustments, abilities, and other HP-costing actions | Most physical actions |
| At zero | Basic tap actions remain available at diminished effectiveness | Player becomes Exhausted |
| Recovery | Requires HP-generating activities | Recovers over time |
| Strategic purpose | Rewards development and efficient volleyball technique | Regulates sustained activity and recovery |

The distinction between **physical attributes and HP-enhanced performance** is particularly important.

A player's Height, Traits, and other physical attributes establish what they're naturally capable of. HP then allows them to perform beyond those baseline capabilities.

This means two players with identical HP may still have very different performance, while two players with identical physical attributes can differ considerably depending on their HP reserves.

## The two failure states

### 0 HP

(Underpowered)

Can still Receive, Set, Attack, and use other applicable tap actions, but cannot draw on HP for enhanced execution.

The player remains physically functional if they have Stamina.

### 0 Stamina

(Exhausted)

Movement and action capability become severely impaired, along with HP acquisition and expenditure efficiency.

The player must recover to at least 50% Stamina to clear Exhaustion.

One clarification: I would interpret *effectively unable to act* as severe impairment rather than literal input lockout, consistent with the earlier exhaustion rule. Otherwise, a player who reaches zero Stamina during a Rally could become completely helpless.

## An important consequence for rallies

HP and Stamina can now produce four distinct player conditions\:

| Condition | Gameplay |
|--------|--------|
| High HP, high Stamina | Full access to enhanced performance and sustained activity |
| High HP, low Stamina | Powerful techniques remain available, but physical output is limited |
| Low HP, high Stamina | Can continue playing, but relies on baseline attributes and efficient contacts |
| Low HP, low Stamina | Weak performance and imminent or active Exhaustion |

This is a promising foundation for both short-term tactical decisions and longer-term resource management.

It also reinforces positional specialization. A Setter's reduced HP cost for second-contact Sets allows them to sustain enhanced performance more efficiently, while Stamina still limits how much physical activity they can perform.

## One design issue I'd flag

You describe HP as determining both **technique availability** and **performance above physical attributes**\.

Those are compatible, but we should eventually distinguish techniques that require a minimum HP reserve from techniques that merely cost HP to perform.

For example, a player with 3 HP might be unable to initiate a technique costing 5 HP, or might be allowed to execute a weaker version by spending their remaining 3 HP.

That choice will matter for how smoothly players transition from enhanced play to baseline tap-only play.

I would preserve the established principle that **zero HP never eliminates basic volleyball participation**, regardless of how we resolve enhanced-technique costs.

Overall, this gives the volleyball MOBA a distinctive economic structure: **XP develops the player's build, HP amplifies their capabilities, and Stamina limits how continuously they can exercise those capabilities.**

Level-up Rewards and Trait Trees:

Physical capabilities fall into 4 main categories: Speed, Power, Accuracy, and Endurance. Players are rewarded with Attribute Points when they level up to invest in each category, and Traits are locked based on Attribute investment (and sometimes positional or physical requirements as well, such as Giant, Ambidextrous, etc.)
Players also receive other benefits from leveling up: most notably, they receive a HP Minimum. Players, especially higher level ones, should not lose access to their abilities - "forgetting" how to do something because they are tired doesn't make sense. Thus, as players progress in level, they increase a baseline HP level to retain access to some amount of their Traits and abilities. Some Traits also influence HP and Stamina as well.

Certain traits are Innate and can only be selected at the start of a match. When starting a match, players can choose a build or create their own (during or before the match start flow.) Players are given some number of Attribute Points to allocate into their starting build. Innate Traits cost much more, so choosing between being Giant/Tiny (affecting base Stamina, jumping reach, base movement speed, etc.) or the Workhorse (extreme base Stamina increase) or the Deep Bag (small base HP Min increase) is a great investment compared to starting with some number of Traits like Back Set, Pancake, Feint+, etc.
