If a player is choosing Tiny, they don't also have to pay for choosing a Tiny height.)


#### Assistant

This is a substantial improvement to the progression model, particularly the introduction of **HP Minimum** and the separation between Innate Traits and ordinary Traits.

The system now has three distinct layers of character development\:

1. **Innate build\:** Establishes physical identity and starting specialization.
2. **Attribute progression\:** Improves general capabilities and unlocks Trait prerequisites.
3. **Trait progression\:** Converts Attribute investments into specialized techniques and playstyles.

Crucially, this is not simply a skill tree where players unlock stronger versions of existing actions. It's a system where a player's physical characteristics determine what techniques are practical, and their investment choices determine which techniques become efficient.

## 1\. The four Attributes

| Attribute | Primary applications |
|--------|--------|
| **Speed** | Movement, Sprinting, approach speed, rapid repositioning |
| **Power** | Serving, attacking force, jump-related physical output |
| **Accuracy** | Aiming, setting, placement, precision, HP efficiency |
| **Endurance** | Stamina capacity, sustained exertion, recovery-related performance |

The particularly valuable design choice is that actions use different combinations of Attributes.

A Setter specializing in Accuracy will naturally improve their sets, but that investment may also make them more efficient at placing attacks.

A Power-focused Opposite might excel at hard-driven Spikes while struggling with more technical shots.

An Endurance-focused Libero could sustain defensive engagements and demanding jungle Drills for longer.

These are natural incentives rather than rigid role restrictions.

## 2\. HP Minimum solves an important progression problem

Previously, HP was both a consumable resource and a requirement for enhanced techniques. That creates a potential contradiction: a player could learn a technique through XP progression, then lose access to it simply by spending HP.

Your HP Minimum addresses that.

Illustrative HP reserve

Current HP 65 / 100

30 HP Minimum 35 expendable HP

Illustrative values only. This assumes HP Minimum acts as a protected floor rather than a replenishment target.

There is one mechanical ambiguity here that matters enormously.

**Does HP Minimum mean HP cannot fall below that value, or does it mean the player's effective HP is never treated as lower than that value for ability access?**

I lean toward the second interpretation.

If HP Minimum is a protected floor, sufficiently high-level players might eventually become incapable of suffering meaningful HP losses during rallies or Peppers.

If it's an effective baseline, players can still spend down their accumulated HP, but retain the fundamental capability to use learned techniques.

That seems more consistent with your stated intention: players shouldn't forget techniques because they're tired.

I would not finalize either interpretation without your confirmation.

## 3\. Innate Traits create meaningful starting-build tradeoffs

The distinction between Innate and ordinary Traits is particularly strong.

**Giant / Tiny**

Extreme physical profiles that change Height and consequently reach, movement, and Stamina. Their associated extreme heights are included in their AP cost.

---

**Workhorse**

Provides a substantial increase to base Stamina, favoring sustained drilling, movement, and repeated physical effort.

---

**Deep Bag**

Increases starting HP Minimum, improving access to enhanced capabilities even when accumulated HP is depleted.

The interesting part is that these Traits compete directly with more immediate technical investments.

A Giant may have extraordinary physical advantages but enter the match with relatively few specialized techniques.

A smaller player with several ordinary Traits might already possess Back Set, Pancake, or Feint+, but lack the long-term physical potential of a Giant.

That is a meaningful build choice rather than a straightforward comparison of starting power.

## 4\. Trait trees should be permissive, not prescriptive

I would preserve your largely shared Trait pool.

The Attribute requirements already create natural specialization without needing to make most Traits exclusive to positions.

For example, the following could be plausible prerequisite structures, without fixing numerical thresholds\:

| Trait | Possible prerequisite |
|--------|--------|
| Pancake | Accuracy and/or Endurance |
| Feint+ | Accuracy |
| Back Set | Accuracy |
| Workhorse | Innate-only AP investment |
| Ambidextrous | Innate or physical prerequisite |
| Giant | Innate investment and extreme Height |

One important distinction: **Attribute prerequisites should generally be checked against permanent investment, not current HP-enhanced performance.**

Otherwise, a player could unlock a Trait when their HP is high and lose access to it when their HP falls. That would recreate the problem HP Minimum is intended to solve.

## 5\. Height and Innate cost accounting

Your no-double-counting rule is essential.

If Giant requires an extreme height, purchasing Giant should include that height's AP cost. The player shouldn't be charged twice for what is effectively the same physical investment.

I would model the starting build as a single AP budget that can be spent on\:

- Attribute investments.
- Ordinary starting Traits.
- Innate Traits.
- Nonstandard Height selections not already included in an Innate Trait.

This also suggests that starting-build validation should consider the final configuration, rather than the order in which the player selects its components.

A player should be able to choose Giant first and then its associated Height, or choose the Height first and then Giant, without producing different total costs.

## 6\. The biggest balance consideration

**Innate Traits need to justify their substantial upfront cost without becoming mandatory long-term investments.**

Giant, Tiny, Workhorse, and Deep Bag are particularly interesting because they affect foundational physical properties.

A player who spends heavily on Giant should feel the benefit throughout the match. But a player who instead purchases several technical Traits and Attributes should have a comparably viable development path.

I would avoid making Innate Traits categorically stronger than ordinary Traits. They should offer *different kinds of value*\: permanent physical advantages versus broader technical capabilities.

This also raises a useful question about whether ordinary Traits can be acquired later but Innate Traits cannot. Your description strongly suggests that distinction, and I think it should remain firm.

---

## 7\. Two questions I'd resolve next

**First: the precise meaning of HP Minimum.** Is it a protected HP floor, or a baseline of effective performance and ability access even when expendable HP reaches zero?

**Second: whether leveling grants both Attribute Points and separate Trait selections, or whether Traits themselves are purchased using AP.**

Your description suggests AP is the common currency for both Attributes and Traits, including starting Traits, but doesn't explicitly establish whether that remains true after leveling.

That decision will strongly affect the shape of the Trait trees and how quickly players can diversify.

The most promising aspect of the overall system is that **Position, Height, Attributes, Traits, HP, and Stamina now have distinct purposes**\. None of them needs to be collapsed into another, and together they offer considerably more build flexibility than a conventional fixed-character MOBA.

## 1\. HP Minimum: a protected performance floor

## 2\. Attribute Points and Traits

The revised model is more interesting than a conventional progression tree because **Attributes serve both as improvements and as prerequisites for technical development**\.

However, I want to distinguish two interpretations that your explanation still leaves slightly open.

## 3\. One important balance implication

Because AP investments directly improve Attributes, a player pursuing a high-requirement Trait isn't necessarily sacrificing immediate effectiveness while progressing toward it.

For example, an Accuracy-focused Setter becomes better at setting with every Accuracy investment, even before unlocking an advanced setting technique.

This is desirable, but it creates a risk that stacking one Attribute becomes more efficient than building a balanced character.

I wouldn't introduce diminishing returns or Attribute caps yet. The different demands of serving, receiving, movement, and attacking may naturally reward diversification.

The stronger design principle is that **a highly specialized build should excel in its chosen activities while retaining meaningful weaknesses elsewhere**\.

## 4\. What I would preserve

I would treat the following as established\:

**Innates** are expensive, match-start-only investments that fundamentally alter a character's physical profile or starting resources.

**Attributes** are universally relevant statistical investments that modify physical performance and unlock access to Traits.

**Ability Traits** expand or specialize the player's volleyball techniques and may require particular Attribute investments.

**HP Minimum** increases through leveling, protecting an increasingly capable baseline while leaving the most expensive techniques dependent on accumulated HP.

There is one question I'd resolve before designing actual Trait trees\:

**When a player meets a Trait's Attribute requirements, do they receive that Trait automatically, choose one Trait from several available options, or spend an additional resource to acquire it?**

That determines whether Attribute thresholds function as a complete progression tree or merely as eligibility requirements for a separate selection system.

#### Assistant

This strengthens the map design considerably. The important development is that **the map's geography, its economic opportunities, and the player's physical build are all connected through the same movement and volleyball mechanics**\.

The Box Jump and Serve Receive Drills are particularly useful examples because neither requires a separate minigame system. They test capabilities that players already use in Rallies, Peppers, and ordinary traversal.

## 1\. The map as a physical skill environment

Walls, dividers, and elevation changes create meaningful differences between players without necessarily making routes exclusive.

A tall, Power-invested player might clear a divider with a single jump. A smaller, Speed-invested player might navigate around it faster. A player with a mobility-enhancing Trait might discover a third route.

This gives Height, Speed, Power, and Endurance value outside formal volleyball engagements.

It also creates a potentially interesting distinction between **geometric accessibility and practical accessibility**\.

A route might technically be available to every player but only be efficient for certain builds. Likewise, a Drill might be physically reachable but too demanding to complete reliably without sufficient Attributes, Stamina, or mechanical proficiency.

That is preferable to simply restricting access by position.

## 2\. Drills as recurring economic encounters

image_group{"query":["indoor volleyball training gym wooden plyometric jump boxes obstacle course"],"aspect_ratio":"4:3","layout":"carousel","num_per_query":1}

**Box Jump Parkour — Top lane**

A winding vertical course requiring repeated demanding jumps. Players must manage approach timing, jump reach, and Stamina to reach the reward at the top.

Likely strengths: jumping capability, Power, Endurance, movement execution.

---

image_group{"query":["volleyball libero serve receive practice indoor court ball passing"],"aspect_ratio":"4:3","layout":"carousel","num_per_query":1}

**Serve Receive — Central jungle sectors**

A sequence of distant serves that must all be received successfully. It tests defensive positioning, platform orientation, timing, and the ability to sustain repeated contacts.

Likely strengths: Accuracy, Endurance, defensive technique, consistent execution.

These Drills produce different kinds of economic specialization.

Box Jump rewards a player who can translate their physical jumping capability into repeated successful traversal. Serve Receive rewards defensive consistency and stamina management.

Importantly, neither needs to guarantee that its traditionally favored position is always the optimal participant.

A Giant Libero might excel at Box Jump. An Accuracy- and Endurance-invested Setter might eventually become highly efficient at Serve Receive.

That is exactly the kind of unconventional build your shared Trait system should support.

## 3\. Energy Drinks introduce another map incentive

Energy Drinks appear to be a means of restoring HP and Stamina outside the ordinary Drill economy.

That gives them a potentially different strategic role: **Drills develop a player, while Energy Drinks help sustain or restore their immediate readiness.**

If Energy Drinks are recurring contested resources, they could become especially important immediately before a Serve or after a demanding Pepper exchange.

I wouldn't yet assume whether they're consumable inventory items, fixed-location pickups, or interactable stations. Those implementations would create different incentives.

## 4\. Skill and character capability

I think your last paragraph articulates the most important balancing principle established so far\:

**Player skill and character capability are complementary, not interchangeable.**

That implies two requirements.

First, progression must matter. A player who invests in Power, Height, and jumping Traits should accomplish things that a baseline character genuinely cannot, regardless of execution skill.

Second, execution must matter. A highly developed character should not automatically complete difficult Drills or win Rallies merely because their Attributes are high.

These can coexist if challenges have both physical demands and execution demands.

For example, consider the Box Jump course\:

- A low-level player with excellent timing might complete early sections but lack the reach or Stamina to finish.
- A powerful Giant might have sufficient reach but fail because of poor approach timing.
- A specialized player with strong execution might complete it quickly and economically.

The same logic applies to Rallies. A lower-level player can outread an opponent and produce a better contact, but may lack the attack power, reach, or technique selection needed to capitalize on every opportunity.

## 5\. Two map-design concerns

**The first is whether Drills are exclusive or shareable.**

If a Serve Receive Drill is available to only one player at a time, the jungle becomes a resource-allocation problem. If several players can use it simultaneously, the economy behaves differently.

The second is whether difficult Drills have partial rewards.

Your Box Jump example rewards reaching the top, while Serve Receive requires completing every receive in sequence. Those sound like completion-based rewards.

That creates high-risk opportunities for underdeveloped players: they may expend considerable Stamina and gain nothing.

This can be desirable, provided the difficulty is sufficiently legible that players can make informed decisions before attempting a Drill.

## 6\. What this contributes to the overall design

The volleyball MOBA is now developing a fairly consistent relationship between its systems\:

| System | Strategic question |
|--------|--------|
| Drills | Which activities can my build complete efficiently? |
| Map traversal | Which routes can my physical capabilities exploit? |
| Peppers | Can I outplay an opponent economically? |
| Nets and Rallies | Can my team convert preparation into points? |
| Attributes and Traits | Which capabilities should I develop next? |
| HP and Stamina | How much performance can I sustain or afford? |

The map is particularly important because it prevents the volleyball mechanics from existing only during Rallies. Jumping, receiving, movement, and positioning all have economic applications elsewhere.

One principle I'd formalize is\:

**The map should reward physical specialization through differences in efficiency and opportunity, while preserving meaningful routes to success for mechanically skilled players using less specialized builds.**

I would not interpret that as requiring every Drill to be completable by every build. Some opportunities should legitimately demand capabilities that particular players lack. That is part of what makes specialization valuable.

### Exchange 020

some drills are exclusive and others are shareable. in particular, opposing players may attempt to invade and complete the other team's drills - either side can interrupt the other with a Pepper.


#### Assistant

This establishes **Drill invasion as a central form of economic contest**, with Peppers providing the mechanism for direct interference.

It also makes the distinction between exclusive and shareable Drills strategically meaningful.

| Drill type | Economic implications |
|--------|--------|
| **Exclusive** | Only one player can complete or occupy the Drill at a time. Creates competition over access and completion rewards. |
| **Shareable** | Multiple players can participate, allowing cooperative development or simultaneous use. |
| **Invasion** | Opponents can enter enemy territory and attempt to claim Drill rewards themselves. |
| **Pepper interference** | Players can interrupt opposing Drills, potentially draining HP and Stamina or forcing an opponent to abandon an attempt. |

The important distinction is that an invader doesn't necessarily need to defeat an opponent. They can profit by completing a Drill the defending team would otherwise have used.

Likewise, a defender doesn't necessarily need to win a Pepper outright. Forcing an invader to miss a timed Drill or exhaust their Stamina might already be sufficient.

## Three strategic consequences

**1\. The Libero's jungle role becomes more than efficient resource collection.**

The Libero may naturally excel at jungle Serve Receive Drills, but also has reasons to patrol, contest opposing junglers, and protect teammates completing vulnerable Drills.

Their defensive specialization could make them particularly effective at surviving Pepper harassment without sacrificing too much HP.

**2\. Drill completion becomes a commitment.**

A player attempting Box Jump Parkour might be halfway through the course when an opponent arrives. They must decide whether to continue, evade, or engage in a Pepper.

This creates risk around valuable Drills without requiring artificial enemy mobs or scripted combat encounters.

**3\. Map control emerges from economic opportunity.**

A team controlling a region can access its Drills more reliably, deny those opportunities to opponents, and threaten nearby Nets.

That creates a recognizable MOBA relationship between resource control, territorial pressure, and objective scoring.

## One mechanic I would be careful about

**Pepper interruption should not necessarily erase all Drill progress.**

For some Drills, interruption naturally causes failure. A Serve Receive sequence requiring consecutive successful receives might reasonably reset if the player misses a ball.

But a Box Jump course need not reset simply because an opponent initiates a Pepper. The invader could interfere through positioning, Stamina pressure, or forcing a missed jump instead.

I would let the physical nature of each Drill determine what interruption means, rather than imposing a universal reset rule.

The next major question is **how players detect and respond to invasions**\. If Drill access is valuable and Pepper harassment is possible, visibility, map information, and travel time will substantially determine whether defending territory is practical.

That seems like the natural next area to establish before adding more Drill types.

#### Assistant

This gives the map a **primarily geometry-based vision system**, rather than the conventional MOBA model of widespread concealment through bushes or fog of war.

It also gives Peppers an important counterplay mechanism: players receive advance warning of a potential attack, but the quality of that warning depends on their awareness and the attacker's position.

## Vision and Pepper telegraphing

| Mechanic | Established behavior |
|--------|--------|
| Map visibility | Generally open, with relatively few dedicated concealment areas |
| Natural cover | Walls, corners, and elevation can obstruct vision |
| Pepper preparation | Alerts nearby susceptible opponents |
| Alert direction | Indicates the direction of the Pepper source |
| Alert presentation | Changes in size and visibility according to source distance and visibility |

The strongest aspect is that **concealment doesn't necessarily eliminate warning**\. An opponent behind a wall might still generate an alert, but the defender may receive less useful information than if the attacker were clearly visible.

That gives walls two roles: they obstruct ordinary vision and affect how easily players can interpret an incoming threat.

## Why this works with the existing mechanics

Pepper preparation already involves charging an action, so there is a natural period during which an opponent can anticipate an attack.

The warning system makes that preparation meaningful even when the defender isn't directly watching the attacker.

It also preserves the importance of player skill. The alert communicates danger, but the defender must still orient toward the source, prepare an appropriate Receive, and time their contact.

An experienced defender might interpret a faint directional warning, reposition, and prepare a Receive before the Pepper arrives. A less experienced player might recognize the threat too late.

Neither response needs to be determined entirely by Attributes.

## One important design distinction

I would distinguish **warning that a Pepper is being prepared** from **warning that the Pepper has been released**\.

Your current rule establishes the former. I wouldn't automatically add a second alert.

Preparation warnings create an interesting possibility: an attacker could charge a Pepper to pressure an opponent into interrupting a Drill, even without actually releasing it.

Because grounded charge cancellation is free, this could become a legitimate form of bluffing.

That is potentially desirable. A player threatening a Pepper might force an opponent to abandon a demanding Serve Receive Drill or reposition away from an important resource.

However, it also creates a balance consideration: if alerts are too prominent, repeatedly threatening Peppers could become more effective than actually performing them.

## A question about susceptibility

The term **susceptible players** deserves a precise definition.

Does the warning appear for every opposing player within a certain distance of a prepared Pepper, or only for players whom the attacker could plausibly hit based on their current aim and the map geometry?

I lean toward proximity-based warnings, modified by visibility, rather than requiring an exact predicted trajectory. That would make the warning system more robust and prevent it from becoming an unintended targeting detector.

More broadly, the emerging map design now has three connected forms of spatial skill: **navigating geometry, controlling Drill territory, and reading potential Pepper threats**\. That should make movement and awareness meaningful even outside formal Rallies.

One distinction to retain: **a missed Pepper initiation is not necessarily the same as losing a Pepper exchange**\. Missing the initial skillshot and making an error after an opponent successfully receives it are different outcomes and may deserve different consequences.

This also corrects my earlier suggestion about charge-cancel bluffing: free cancellation applies to ordinary grounded preparations, **not Peppers**\.

both Peppers and Serves are not cancellable. Also a good time to introduce blocking as an action type. Blocking is another action type that can be chosen. When a Block is charged, it replaces part of the Jump charge requirement. A Block can be directionally influenced to swing hands, press in, or pull back.


#### Assistant

Blocking fits naturally into the existing control system, and I think making it a **fourth selectable action type** is preferable to treating it as a contextual version of Receive or Attack.

It has a distinct purpose, preparation process, and set of directional decisions.

## Updated action vocabulary

| Action | Primary preparation | Jump interaction |
|--------|--------|--------|
| Receive | Cushioning and follow-through | Dive |
| Set | Set force and trajectory | Jump Set |
| Attack | Attack power | Spike |
| **Block** | Blocking preparation | Replaces part of Jump charge |
| Serve / Pepper | Initiation power | Separate committed actions |

Both **Serves and Peppers are non-cancellable**, unlike ordinary grounded preparations. Their initiation therefore carries a commitment even before the ball is released.

## Blocking mechanics

The interesting part of your proposal is that charging Block partially substitutes for charging Jump.

That gives blocking a different physical rhythm from attacking.

A hitter generally wants to develop an approach and execute their Spike at the appropriate step. A blocker instead prepares to jump and establish a defensive position, potentially with less need for a conventional approach.

The exact substitution ratio can remain unspecified.

### Directional blocking inputs

You have established three techniques that directional inputs should support\:

- **Swing hands\:** Redirect the blocked ball toward a chosen area.
- **Press in\:** Reach across the net to intercept an attack more aggressively.
- **Pull back\:** Keep the hands farther from the opponent's court, potentially changing the rebound or avoiding an unfavorable interaction.

These are different defensive intentions rather than simply stronger and weaker blocks.

I'd avoid assigning WASD mappings until we establish whether blocking directions should be camera-relative, net-relative, or dependent on which side of the court the player occupies.

## Why Block deserves its own action

This also completes the positional contact economy more cleanly.

The Middle Blocker's zero-HP blocking efficiency now corresponds to a dedicated technique that players can develop, rather than an incidental defensive interaction.

Other positions can still Block, but must consider the greater HP expense and whether their Height, Power, and jumping capability make the attempt worthwhile.

A Giant Libero, for example, could develop a credible blocking presence while retaining the Libero's unfavorable positional HP cost for blocking. That's exactly the sort of unconventional build the system should permit.

## Two mechanical questions worth preserving

**First, does charging Block substitute for Jump preparation only, or does it also improve the resulting block?**

I think it should do both to some extent. Otherwise, blocking preparation might feel like merely paying a different cost for the same jump.

**Second, what determines a successful block?**

There are at least three distinct dimensions: reaching the ball, positioning the hands correctly, and controlling the rebound. Your directional controls already provide a foundation for the latter two.

I wouldn't add a separate blocking minigame. Ideally, the existing camera, charge, timing, and directional systems should handle all three.

One especially useful possibility is that **a block can succeed defensively without producing an advantageous rebound**\. A poorly controlled block might deflect the ball back into play, while a precisely timed and directed block could produce an immediate point or a difficult recovery.

That would give blocking the same graduated outcomes as Receiving, Setting, and Attacking, rather than reducing it to a binary success or failure.

Overall, this is a good addition. The universal control system now supports the four essential volleyball actions while giving each a distinct mechanical identity.

The three blocking techniques are therefore\:

- **Swing Hands\:** Move the arms left or right to intercept a different attacking angle or course, without necessarily repositioning the player's body.
- **Press In\:** Extend the hands farther over the net, attempting to intercept the attack earlier and more aggressively.
- **Pull Back\:** Withdraw the hands to change the block's depth and interaction with the incoming ball.

This gives blocking three distinct spatial considerations: **lateral coverage, forward reach, and depth control**\.

It also creates a meaningful distinction between footwork and hand positioning. A blocker who anticipates the wrong attacking lane may still correct with Swing Hands, but their ability to do so effectively should depend on their preparation, timing, and physical capabilities.

I would preserve Swing Hands as a normal directional blocking option, not a Trait-exclusive technique. Traits can subsequently improve its range, speed, or efficiency.

The key distinction is that **directional blocking inputs modify the geometry of the interception itself**, whereas directional attacking inputs modify the intended destination of the ball.

Double Contacts and Setting:
Players are permitted to contact the ball up to three times (retaining the three contact rule), with the only penalty being increasing HP cost for each contact. This is to accommodate for solo laners entering a Rally.

So a Rally between two MBs may look like many different things:
a. Both MBs take turns Receiving high and attempting to swing at the received ball, to lower the HP cost of both having 3 contacts AND setting as a MB. The other MB may Block (at no HP cost), but potentially forfeit their opportunity to Receive if their Block is unsuccessful.
b. One or both MBs choose to Set during a Rally, creating deception in the actual process. One of them might mistakenly try to block the second contact and be unable to stop the third. c. The Libero may appear for one or both sides, increasing that side's court area and allowing for more diverse Rallies.


#### Assistant

This is an important resolution of the solo-lane problem. **The three-contact rule remains, but the prohibition on consecutive contacts is replaced by an escalating HP cost.** As a result, a solo player can participate in a complete Rally without making conventional team volleyball obsolete.

I particularly like the Middle Blocker example because it demonstrates that the optimal sequence isn't predetermined by position. Two players with identical positional efficiencies can make very different tactical decisions.

## 1\. The contact economy

There are now three separate factors influencing the HP cost and effectiveness of an attack\:

| Factor | Effect |
|--------|--------|
| Positional efficiency | MB, OH, and OP attack with favorable baseline HP costs |
| Consecutive contacts | Repeated contacts by the same player become increasingly expensive |
| Setting | Improves attack positioning, reduces attack HP cost, and slightly increases power |

The crucial interaction is that **Setting offers a benefit even when the Setter and attacker are the same player**, provided the player can afford the additional contact.

That means Setting is not merely a support action. It is a means of preparing an offensive opportunity.

A Middle Blocker might reasonably skip Setting because their second contact would be expensive. Another might Set anyway because the enhanced third-contact attack justifies the investment.

Neither decision should be universally correct.

## 2\. The solo Middle Blocker matchup

Your examples reveal several viable strategies.

## A

**Receive → Attack**

The MB uses a high Receive to create an attacking opportunity without paying for an additional Set.

This saves HP and reduces exposure to escalating consecutive-contact costs, but produces a less advantageous attack than a well-executed Set.

---

## B

**Receive → Set → Attack**

The MB invests in a third contact to improve the eventual attack.

This can create a stronger or more deceptive offensive sequence, but incurs additional HP costs and gives the opponent more time to anticipate the attack.

---

## C

**Block → Transition**

The MB attempts to intercept the opponent's attack directly, potentially winning the exchange without needing a conventional Receive.

A failed block can leave them poorly positioned or unable to receive the ball afterward.

The strongest part is that these strategies interact.

An MB who repeatedly attacks on their second contact may encourage the opponent to prepare an early block. That expectation makes a third-contact attack more threatening.

Conversely, an MB who habitually Sets may give the opponent opportunities to pressure them before the eventual attack.

This is exactly the sort of strategic variation that should emerge from the controls rather than from explicitly prescribed combos.

## 3\. Blocking introduces a commitment decision

Your example of an MB attempting to block the second contact is particularly valuable.

The defender has to decide whether the opponent is preparing an immediate attack or extending the sequence with a Set.

Because Block requires preparation and a Jump commitment, guessing incorrectly can be consequential.

That gives Setting an additional benefit beyond its numerical bonuses: **it can manipulate the opponent's defensive timing**\.

I would preserve this distinction rather than adding artificial deception bonuses to ordinary Sets.

A well-executed Set can already be deceptive because it changes when and where the attack becomes possible.

## 4\. Adding a Libero changes the encounter

The court-size rule makes this especially interesting.

A Libero joining the MB doesn't simply provide another defensive player. The team's court area also increases, changing the spatial demands of the Rally.

The Libero contributes efficient Receives and potentially Sets, while the MB remains the more efficient hitter and blocker.

That creates a natural division of labor, but it isn't free: the larger court creates additional space to defend.

This is a good mechanism for preventing numerical advantages from being entirely straightforward.

One caveat: the value of adding a player will depend heavily on how court area scales. If two players can cover the enlarged court substantially more efficiently than one player covers the smaller court, additional participants may still be overwhelmingly advantageous.

That is primarily a balance question.

## 5\. Two rules that now deserve clarification

**First: does a Block count as one of the team's three contacts?**

In conventional volleyball, a block touch does not count toward the three-contact limit.

I'd favor retaining that rule. It would make Blocking distinct from Receiving and allow a successful block that remains on the defending side to transition into a normal offensive sequence.

**Second: does the consecutive-contact HP penalty apply to every additional contact by the same player, or only to uninterrupted contacts?**

For example\:

- MB Receives → MB Sets → MB Attacks: escalating penalties.
- MB Receives → Libero Sets → MB Attacks: does the MB's third-contact attack return to its normal positional HP cost?

I would favor resetting the consecutive-contact penalty when a teammate contacts the ball. That rewards cooperation without requiring an additional explicit team-combination bonus.

## 6\. Overall assessment

This is one of the strongest parts of the design so far because it reconciles three requirements that could easily conflict\:

- Solo players must be able to contest Nets meaningfully.
- Team volleyball should remain more efficient and tactically diverse.
- Positional specialization should matter without restricting which actions players can perform.

The system accomplishes this through the existing HP economy, contact rules, and Setting bonuses.

I would avoid adding further explicit teamplay rewards until these mechanics are tested. They already create substantial incentives to cooperate.

**The next critical issue is the interaction between court size, participant count, and when players are permitted to enter or leave a Rally.** That will determine how reliably the solo and cooperative scenarios you've described can coexist.

It also creates an unusual MOBA mechanic: bringing more players to a Net increases the physical demands placed on *both* teams, but the larger team is better equipped to handle those demands.

## Court scaling rules

| Property | Established rule |
|--------|--------|
| Maximum participants | 6 players per side |
| Court dimensions | Determined by the highest participant count on either side |
| Court expansion | Applies equally to both sides |
| Serving distance | Fixed relative to the Net |
| Joining | Players can enter an ongoing Rally by walking into its bounds |
| Leaving | Players can leave, but court dimensions do not shrink until the Rally ends |
| Reset | Court size can be recalculated for the next Rally |

The most consequential rule is that **court expansion is persistent within a Rally**\.

A team cannot bring several players to expand the court, then withdraw them and expect the geometry to contract again.

## Why the numerical advantage is so powerful

Consider three examples.

Illustrative court scaling

- 1v1
- 3v1

Current selection unavailable.

Conceptual illustration only. Actual court dimensions and scaling ratios remain undefined.

This is a particularly effective way of making rotations meaningful.

A solo laner can participate in a Rally because the court is small enough for individual coverage. But if several opponents arrive, the same player can become dramatically disadvantaged without any change to their own Attributes.

That makes map awareness and timely reinforcement critical.

## Joining and leaving create interesting tactics

Because players can join mid-Rally, a team might initiate a Serve against a lone defender and then bring additional players into the court.

However, the expanding court would also affect their own defensive responsibilities.

Leaving is even more interesting.

Suppose a team expands the court to accommodate four players, then three leave. The remaining player must continue defending the enlarged area until the Rally concludes.

This creates a meaningful commitment to numerical escalation without artificially preventing players from moving elsewhere.

It also means that a player who enters briefly can permanently change the conditions of that Rally.

## A design concern: late entry

I would specifically test **whether entering a Rally immediately before an opponent's attack creates an excessively strong tactic**\.

For example, a player might wait just outside the boundary, then step in as the opponent prepares to Spike. If their entry instantly expands the court, the defender could suddenly face a much larger target area with almost no time to reposition.

That could become a powerful form of surprise engagement, particularly if players deliberately manipulate the boundary.

I wouldn't prohibit it outright. Sudden reinforcements are an important part of MOBA gameplay.

But the court expansion should be clearly communicated and its timing predictable.

## One remaining rule to establish

**What happens when the Rally ends and some participants are still inside the court?**

Does the next Rally automatically include those players, or must they explicitly rejoin?

I'd lean toward treating physical presence within the Rally bounds as participation when the next Serve begins. That would make defending a Net intuitive and avoid unnecessary confirmation inputs.

The overall structure is now clear: **solo Rallies are mechanically supported, but grouping is a major strategic advantage.** The cost of grouping comes from abandoning other lanes, Drills, and map resources—not from an artificial penalty imposed on the larger team.

That is a strong MOBA-like incentive structure.

#### Assistant

These two rules clarify the distinction between **being present at a Net, participating in a Rally, and expanding the court**\.

## Updated Rally entry rules

1. **Rally initiation\:** A Rally begins only after a Serve successfully crosses the Net.
2. **Initial participation\:** Players present in their team's court area when the Rally begins are entered into it.
3. **Late entry\:** Additional players can join only while inside their team's court area and after the opposing team has contacted the ball at least once, including through a Block.
4. **Court expansion\:** The court expands according to the highest participant count on either side, up to six per side.
5. **Persistent dimensions\:** Leaving a Rally does not reduce its court size. Dimensions reset after the Rally concludes.

This prevents players from manipulating court dimensions merely by standing near a Net before a Serve. It also gives late entry a meaningful timing requirement.

## Important consequences

**A Serve is a commitment, not automatically a Rally.** A failed Serve does not create an active Rally. A successful Serve initiates one, allowing the present defenders to participate.

**Reinforcements cannot immediately join just because the Serve crossed the Net.** They must wait until the opposing side has made contact. This creates a predictable initial phase before additional players can enter.

**A Block can open the entry window.** That matters because it means a defensive block can enable reinforcements without requiring a conventional Receive.

There is one detail I would avoid assuming: whether the entry requirement is checked against *any opposing contact since the Serve* or whether it resets after each change of possession. Your wording supports the former more naturally.

I would therefore provisionally treat it as a persistent eligibility condition once the opponent has contacted the ball.

The result is a clearer Rally structure: **successful Serve → initial participants enter → opposing contact permits reinforcements → Rally continues until a fault**\.

That preserves the importance of arriving before a Serve while allowing teammates to rotate into an ongoing engagement.

#### Assistant

## Middle Blocker (MB) — Positional Identity

**Combat analogues\:** Burst Mage / Battlemage / Snarer

**Core identity\:** The Middle Blocker seeks to dominate short, decisive exchanges through explosive attacks and powerful defensive interceptions. Their offense emphasizes height or speed to make attacks difficult to defend, while their blocking threatens to neutralize opposing attacks outright.

The interesting feature is that **MB has both offensive and defensive burst potential**\. They aren't simply a defensive specialist who happens to attack.

### Two expressions of offensive pressure

- **Height\:** Attack from a sufficiently elevated contact point to create steep, difficult-to-receive trajectories, particularly against defenders without adequate blocking reach.
- **Speed\:** Execute attacks with minimal preparation or exceptionally fast ball travel, reducing the opponent's opportunity to react.

These can overlap, but they suggest different Attribute investments and Traits.

### Defensive pressure

Blocking is the other half of the MB's identity. Unlike Receiving, which preserves the Rally and prepares a counterattack, **Blocking threatens to terminate an opponent's offensive sequence immediately**\.

That makes the MB particularly effective at controlling the opponent's options. An attacker facing a strong MB must consider not just where to hit, but whether the MB can reach and intercept the attack at all.

The *snarer* analogy fits because the MB can punish an opponent for committing to a predictable attacking course. This is a form of threat-based control rather than necessarily a literal immobilization mechanic.

### Strategic strengths and weaknesses

| Strengths | Potential weaknesses |
|--------|--------|
| High-impact attacks | Less efficient at sustained multi-contact exchanges |
| Exceptional blocking economy | Vulnerable when committed to an incorrect block |
| Strong at punishing predictable attacks | Setting and Digging are relatively expensive |
| Can create decisive moments without lengthy preparation | Repeated solo contacts rapidly deplete HP |

One particularly useful design implication is that **MB should be dangerous even when they aren't touching the ball**\. Their positioning near the Net and the threat of a block can influence an opponent's attack selection.

I would treat *burst mage / battlemage / snarer* as three possible build expressions within MB, rather than three separate identities that every MB must fulfill equally.

The distinction from the other hitting positions should ultimately be that **MB wins by controlling the opponent's window to attack and defend**, not merely by generating the strongest possible Spike.

#### Assistant

## Opposite (OP) — Positional Identity

**Core identity\:** A sharply specialized, backloaded-impact position that seeks to deliver its greatest value at a decisive moment in the Rally, either by finishing the attack or supplying a critical defensive contribution.

The important distinction from MB is **when and how impact is delivered**\. MB threatens to seize control of an exchange through immediate offensive or blocking pressure. OP is more concerned with ensuring that the exchange ends favorably, often after other players have established the conditions.

### Two principal expressions

**1\. Terminal Hitter**

The OP functions as the team's finishing weapon. They specialize in converting developed attacking opportunities into points, potentially accepting narrower utility or greater setup dependence in exchange for exceptional finishing capability.

This makes them a natural recipient of a Setter's strongest preparations, especially in late-Rally situations where a successful attack must overcome an organized defense.

**2\. Specialized Utility**

Alternatively, the OP invests in a narrower supporting function that becomes particularly valuable when a Rally reaches a critical moment.

This might involve defensive coverage, blocking, emergency reception, or another specialized contribution. The defining feature is not general versatility, but **exceptional performance in a particular situation**\.

An OP need not be the team's primary damage source to justify their place. They need to provide something sufficiently valuable that their specialization is worth building around.

### Relationship to other positions

| Position | Primary distinction from OP |
|--------|--------|
| MB | Frontloaded pressure, interception, and immediate control |
| OH | More flexible participation across different phases of a Rally |
| OP | Sharper specialization and decisive, often late-Rally contribution |

### Design implications

This is a useful identity because it avoids defining OP as merely another hitter with slightly different Attribute efficiencies.

The terminal-hitter and utility paths are also meaningfully different without being contradictory. Both emphasize **concentrating value into a decisive contribution**, rather than spreading it evenly throughout the exchange.

One distinction I'd preserve is that *backloaded impact* describes the OP's preferred contribution, not necessarily an explicit mechanic that makes them stronger on the third contact. Their identity can emerge from Attribute allocation, Trait selection, and Rally economics without requiring a position-specific late-contact bonus.

**Provisional MOBA analogues\:** Hypercarry / Executioner for the terminal-hitter path; Warden / Specialist for defensive utility. These are analogies rather than fixed role assignments.

The emerging contrast is clear: **MB disrupts the opponent's ability to execute; OP capitalizes on the moment when execution matters most.**

#### Assistant

**Yes for the terminal-hitter OP, but only partially for the utility OP.** The earlier contact-type HP economy supports the distinction you're describing, though it doesn't yet strongly differentiate OP from OH.

Here's how the established efficiencies line up\:

| Contact type | MB | OP | OH |
|--------|--------|--------|--------|
| Attack | Efficient | Efficient | Efficient |
| Block | Most efficient | Intermediate | Intermediate |
| Receive/Dig | Expensive | Relatively efficient | Relatively efficient |
| Set | Expensive | Most expensive tier | Intermediate |

### Terminal-hitter OP

This aligns well.

OP has efficient Attacks but expensive Sets, meaning they're naturally better at **finishing an opportunity prepared by someone else** than constructing their own ideal attacking sequence.

Their reasonably efficient Digging also allows them to survive a defensive exchange before delivering a finishing attack.

However, OH also has efficient Attacks, so the HP economy alone doesn't establish OP as the superior terminal hitter. That specialization would have to emerge through builds, Attributes, and Traits.

### Utility OP

This also works, with an important qualification.

OP has two plausible utility contributions already supported by the economy\:

- **Digging\:** They can provide an efficient defensive contact, particularly when an exchange becomes difficult to sustain.
- **Blocking\:** They can assist defensively, although MB retains the strongest blocking economy.

But OP's expensive Setting makes them a poor default choice for a general-purpose facilitator.

That actually reinforces your idea of **sharp specialization**\: an OP can become an exceptional defensive contributor through investment, but isn't naturally efficient at every support function.

### The main discrepancy

The existing HP penalties don't particularly distinguish OP from OH in Attack, Dig, or Block. The most obvious difference is that **OH is a more economical Setter**\.

That is a reasonable starting point, but it means the OP's late-Rally identity currently comes more from intended build behavior than from positional economics.

I wouldn't automatically introduce a late-Rally HP discount. That could turn the identity into a rigid rule rather than an emergent specialization.

Instead, I'd preserve this distinction\:

- **OH\:** Better equipped to participate throughout a Rally, including setting up subsequent attacks.
- **OP\:** Better incentivized to reserve resources for a narrower, decisive contribution.

One point worth checking: earlier, we described OP and OH as having similar Digging efficiency. If the intention is for the utility OP to specialize heavily in defense, that works through Traits, but it may be worth deciding whether their baseline defensive economy should differ at all.

**Verdict\:** The contact penalties are compatible with both OP identities. The especially expensive Set is doing the most work to distinguish OP from OH; the remaining differentiation can reasonably come from build specialization rather than additional positional rules.

the OP has high receiving penalty to establish a role as a "hidden" hitter, but how can that be reconciled with the more defensive historical "universal" opposite? should it be? i think a defensive opposite is important in a system where the middle and outside are spec'd heavily into offense, and additionally where the setter in this game should logically also block for themself (as the duo laner for a hitter.) if the opposite is not a blocker like they are in modern volleyball, and can't be a backcourt defender efficiently due to design constraint, doesn't that kill the utility op?


#### Assistant

**Yes. If OP is penalized heavily for Receiving, isn't an especially efficient Blocker, and has expensive Setting, the utility OP risks becoming a build fantasy that the underlying economy doesn't actually support.**

I think the problem is that we're asking the OP's contact penalties to enforce a particular *offensive pattern*—the hidden hitter—while also expecting the position to accommodate a historically broader defensive identity.

Those goals aren't necessarily incompatible.

## The distinction: Receiving versus Digging

I would first question whether **Serve Receive and defensive Digging should have identical HP efficiencies**\.

They are both performed through the Receive action, but their strategic purposes differ substantially.

- **Serve Receive** is the opening contact that establishes an offense. Penalizing OP here encourages them to remain available as a hitter rather than becoming a primary passer.
- **Digging** is the defensive response to an opponent's attack. Making OP reasonably efficient here would allow them to contribute defensively without undermining their hidden-hitter identity.

This distinction is particularly valuable because the OP can be a poor *primary receiver* while still being a strong *defensive specialist*\.

It also gives you a coherent way to accommodate the historical universal opposite without simply making OP an OH with different positioning.

## What the two OP builds could look like

|  | Terminal OP | Universal/utility OP |
|--------|--------|--------|
| Serve Receive | Poor | Poor to moderate |
| Defensive Digging | Serviceable | Strong |
| Setting | Poor | Situational |
| Attacking | Exceptional specialization | Secondary threat |
| Blocking | Situational | Potential specialization |
| Rally identity | Finishes prepared attacks | Preserves difficult exchanges and creates another opportunity |

The utility OP would therefore specialize in **defensive recovery and emergency contributions**, rather than general offensive construction.

That's a meaningful distinction from OH, which remains more naturally involved in the full receive–set–attack sequence.

## But there's a second problem: Blocking

Your Setter observation is important.

If S is already expected to Block for their lane partner, and MB is the premier Blocker, OP has relatively little unique defensive space unless they can develop an alternative specialization.

I would avoid making OP universally better at Blocking just to rescue the utility build. That risks diluting MB's identity.

Instead, OP could be a **second-tier baseline Blocker with access to builds that make Blocking a primary strength**\.

That preserves the distinction between\:

- MB: Blocking as an inherent positional advantage.
- OP: Blocking as a deliberate specialization.
- S: Blocking as an important practical responsibility in their duo lane.

The OP can become a strong Blocker, but doesn't have to.

## The more fundamental issue

The original contact-type penalties are doing two different jobs\:

1. Establishing which contacts a position naturally performs.
2. Determining which alternative builds remain economically viable.

If those penalties are too severe, the second function disappears. A Trait build cannot realistically rescue a position whose basic defensive actions are prohibitively expensive throughout the match.

This is especially relevant because the OP's utility identity is supposed to be **a legitimate alternative to terminal hitting**, not an intentionally inefficient novelty.

I would therefore revise the OP's defensive economy before designing utility Traits around it.

### Proposed OP efficiency profile

| Action | Proposed baseline |
|--------|--------|
| Attack | Very efficient |
| Serve Receive | Expensive |
| Defensive Dig | Moderately efficient |
| Set | Expensive |
| Block | Moderately efficient |

This would make OP a position that generally avoids initiating offensive sequences through reception but can meaningfully contribute to stopping the opponent's attack.

Importantly, **I would not add a generic late-Rally bonus**\. The OP's backloaded impact should emerge from their role in the contact sequence and the builds they choose.

## Should the universal opposite be preserved?

I think **yes, as a viable specialization, but not as the OP's default identity**\.

The terminal-hitter OP should remain the most immediately recognizable version of the position. However, the universal opposite offers a strategically important alternative when the rest of the team is offensively specialized.

There's also an elegant division of labor here\:

- **OH** offers flexibility across offensive construction and execution.
- **OP** offers specialization in offensive termination or defensive preservation.
- **MB** offers immediate offensive pressure and interception.
- **S** orchestrates offensive opportunities while providing necessary lane defense.
- **L** remains the premier defensive receiver and recovery specialist.

My strongest recommendation is therefore to **separate Serve Receive efficiency from defensive Dig efficiency**, even if they share the same player input and contact mechanics.

That single distinction solves more of the problem than broadly reducing OP's defensive penalties. It preserves the hidden-hitter pattern while making a genuine defensive OP possible.

It also raises a useful broader question: should contact efficiency depend only on *which action is performed*, or partly on *the tactical circumstances in which it is performed*? For OP, that distinction seems particularly consequential.

POW/ACC- Spike
ACC/SPD- Set
SPD/x - Block/Receive
x/x - Block/Receive


#### Assistant

I think this is a better foundation for positional identity than the earlier contact-penalty table.

The key change is that **positions determine a player's starting capabilities and specialization potential, while Attributes determine much of their actual contact efficiency**\. Positional HP modifiers can remain, but they no longer have to enforce a single style of play.

That resolves the OP contradiction without distinguishing Serve Receive from Digging artificially. A defensive OP who invests in the Attributes necessary for Receiving should become genuinely good at Receiving, regardless of whether the incoming ball is a Serve or an attack.

## Reframing OH versus OP

|  | Outside Hitter (OH) | Opposite (OP) |
|--------|--------|--------|
| Fundamental identity | All-rounder | Specialist |
| Starting Attributes | Broad, balanced profile | Lower fixed baseline, greater allocation freedom |
| Attack capability | Naturally strong | Can specialize into exceptional attacking |
| Defensive capability | Naturally competent | Can specialize into exceptional defense |
| Setting | Plausible secondary duty | Requires deliberate investment |
| Build weakness | Less extreme specialization | Greater opportunity cost and possible deficiencies |

The important distinction is that **OH has a higher floor across several responsibilities, while OP has more freedom to concentrate its power**\.

This also means a defensive OP shouldn't necessarily be worse at Receiving than an OH. It should be entirely possible for the OP to outperform the OH in that particular action, having sacrificed other capabilities to do so.

The OP's identity becomes specialization itself, rather than a fixed preference for particular contacts.

## Attribute contributions to contact efficiency

I would start with this provisional mapping, preserving the four established Attributes.

| Contact | Primary Attribute | Secondary Attribute | Rationale |
|--------|--------|--------|--------|
| Attack / Spike | Power | Accuracy | Generate force and direct it effectively |
| Set | Accuracy | Speed | Place the ball precisely and execute quickly |
| Block | Speed | Power | Reach the interception point and establish a strong blocking presence |
| Receive / Dig | Accuracy | Endurance | Control the incoming ball and sustain defensive exchanges |

There are two important uncertainties here.

**Blocking\:** Speed is a convincing primary Attribute because positioning and reaction are essential. Power could govern how effectively the hands withstand or redirect an attack. But if blocking success depends more on hand placement than physical strength, Accuracy might be a better secondary contributor.

**Receiving\:** Accuracy seems difficult to exclude because the player must control both the incoming ball and its outgoing trajectory. Endurance is a plausible secondary Attribute because defensive play involves repeated low-position movements, dives, and recovery. However, Speed could be equally important for reaching the ball.

This suggests that the efficiency formula shouldn't necessarily be the same for every aspect of an action.

For example, a Receive could involve\:

- **Speed\:** Reaching the ball in time.
- **Accuracy\:** Controlling the outgoing pass and reducing directional HP expenditure.
- **Endurance\:** Reducing Stamina expenditure and possibly the HP cost of sustained defensive actions.

That would preserve the relevance of all three Attributes without making them interchangeable.

## Separating physical performance from HP efficiency

One concern with making Attributes govern HP costs is **double rewarding specialization**\.

Power already makes attacks more powerful. If it also dramatically reduces their HP cost, Power becomes doubly valuable to hitters.

This isn't necessarily wrong, but it can create runaway specialization: a powerful hitter both hits harder and can afford more powerful hits.

I would distinguish three outputs\:

\[
\text{Action performance}=f(\text{Attributes, technique, execution})
\]

\[
\text{HP expenditure}=g(\text{action, Attributes, position, charge})
\]

\[
\text{Stamina expenditure}=h(\text{physical effort, Attributes})
\]

Attributes can influence all three, but not necessarily in equal proportions.

For example, Power might strongly influence Spike force but only moderately influence Spike HP efficiency. Accuracy might substantially reduce the cost of directional corrections while only modestly affecting the basic Spike charge.

This gives us more room to balance builds without making Attribute investment feel arbitrary.

## Per-position starting profiles

The Libero example establishes an especially useful principle\:

**Starting Attributes must be calibrated against the actual Drills and actions that each position is expected to perform at level one.**

The L must be capable of completing the first Serve Receive Drill without needing additional levels or Traits. Its starting Speed, Accuracy, Endurance, Stamina, and HP economy must collectively permit that.

The same principle can inform the other positions\:

| Position | Starting-profile priority |
|--------|--------|
| MB | Strong initial Blocking and explosive attacking capability |
| OP | High discretionary allocation, enabling a sharply defined specialty |
| OH | Broad competence in Receiving, attacking, and secondary Setting |
| S | Reliable Setting and enough defensive capability for duo-lane responsibilities |
| L | Strong baseline defensive performance, sufficient for the opening Serve Receive Drill |

These are functional requirements, not proposed numerical distributions.

The question of whether OH gets *more total starting Attributes* while OP gets *more freely allocatable points* deserves care. If the total investment is equal, OP is simply more customizable. If OH has more total points, OP needs some compensating advantage to justify its specialization.

A useful possible structure would be\:

\[
\text{Starting profile}
=
\text{Fixed positional Attributes}
+
\text{Discretionary Attribute Points}
\]

The distribution between those two components varies by position. The overall starting budget could remain equal, at least initially.

## What remains of positional HP modifiers?

I'd retain them, but make them **small enough that Attribute investment can meaningfully overcome them**\.

A defensively specialized OP should be capable of outperforming a typical OH in Receiving. An offensively specialized L might become a credible attacker, but doing so should require considerable investment relative to the natural hitter positions.

The positional modifier would represent an innate familiarity or affinity, not an absolute limitation.

This also lets the earlier contact-economy system continue doing useful work: Setting, Blocking, Attacking, and Receiving can have different costs without permanently prescribing who must perform them.

**My main recommendation\:** establish the Attribute-to-action relationships first, then determine positional starting profiles by testing the minimum functional requirements of each position. Only after that should we calibrate residual positional HP modifiers.

That gives the OP a genuine choice between terminal hitter and defensive specialist, while OH remains the reliable all-rounder.

#### Assistant

I think your proposed arrangement is the strongest of the four-Attribute cycles, particularly if we're mapping **HP efficiency rather than the total physical performance of each action**\.

It creates a closed loop in which each Attribute contributes to exactly two contact types, once as primary and once as secondary.

## Proposed contact–Attribute matrix

| Contact | Primary | Secondary |
|--------|--------|--------|
| Spike | **Power** | Accuracy |
| Set | **Accuracy** | Speed |
| Receive | **Speed** | Endurance |
| Block | **Endurance** | Power |

Attribute cycle

## Why this arrangement works

**Power / Accuracy — Spike\:** Straightforward. Power governs the force behind the attack, while Accuracy supports precise placement and directional control.

**Accuracy / Speed — Set\:** Accuracy is the dominant consideration, while Speed represents getting into position and executing the Set quickly enough.

**Speed / Endurance — Receive\:** Speed supports defensive coverage and reaching difficult balls. Endurance supports repeated defensive efforts and physically demanding Receives, particularly dives.

**Endurance / Power — Block\:** This is the least immediately intuitive pairing, but it has a defensible interpretation. Endurance supports maintaining a strong blocking posture, jumping repeatedly, and sustaining physical resistance. Power supports forceful interception and pressing through an opponent's attack.

The last pairing works especially well if Block HP efficiency reflects the ability to sustain and execute forceful defensive actions, rather than simply how accurately the hands are positioned.

## What this implies for builds

| Attribute emphasis | Naturally efficient contacts | Possible identity |
|--------|--------|--------|
| POW + ACC | Spike, with some Set and Block support | Terminal attacker |
| ACC + SPD | Set, with some Spike and Receive support | Technical facilitator |
| SPD + END | Receive, with some Set and Block support | Defensive specialist |
| END + POW | Block, with some Receive and Spike support | Net defender |

The symmetry is useful because these four pairs form recognizable specializations without requiring rigid position restrictions.

It also helps reconcile OP's two paths. A POW/ACC OP can specialize in terminal hitting, while a SPD/END OP can specialize in defensive reception. An END/POW OP can instead emphasize blocking and net defense.

An OH could start with a more balanced spread, retaining reasonable efficiency across several actions.

## One caveat

I wouldn't let this matrix define *all* of what the Attributes do.

For example, Accuracy should still matter when aiming a Block, even though it isn't one of Block's two principal HP-efficiency Attributes. Likewise, Power may still determine the physical effectiveness of a Receive against a very powerful Spike.

The matrix should determine the **dominant contributors to contact HP efficiency**, not prohibit other Attributes from influencing execution or outcomes.

**I'd provisionally adopt your exact cycle: POW → ACC → SPD → END → POW.** It is mathematically balanced, supports recognizable builds, and creates a meaningful distinction between generalists and specialists without requiring separate Serve Receive and Dig penalties.

Yes. I think there's one alternative worth considering, and it may be more intuitive than the current arrangement.

The weakest association in your original cycle is **Endurance as the primary Attribute for Blocking**\. Blocking is explosive and timing-dependent, whereas Endurance more naturally describes sustaining repeated physical actions.

I'd compare these two arrangements\:

| Contact | Original | Alternative |
|--------|--------|--------|
| Spike | **POW / ACC** | **POW / ACC** |
| Set | **ACC / SPD** | **ACC / END** |
| Receive | **SPD / END** | **END / SPD** |
| Block | **END / POW** | **SPD / POW** |

Both preserve the exact four-Attribute cycle, with each Attribute appearing once as primary and once as secondary.

### Why the alternative might be better

- **Spike — Power / Accuracy\:** Unchanged. The intuitive pairing is already excellent.
- **Set — Accuracy / Endurance\:** Accuracy determines placement, while Endurance represents maintaining control through repeated, technically demanding contacts. This is somewhat less intuitive than Accuracy / Speed, however.
- **Receive — Endurance / Speed\:** Defensive reliability, repeated dives, and sustained rallies favor Endurance, while Speed supports court coverage.
- **Block — Speed / Power\:** Blocking requires rapid reactions, explosive jumps, and physical strength. This feels substantially more natural than Endurance / Power.

The tradeoff is that we improve Blocking's association at the expense of Setting's.

### A third possibility

There's another valid cycle that retains both **POW / ACC Spike** and **ACC / SPD Set**\:

| Contact | Primary | Secondary |
|--------|--------|--------|
| Spike | Power | Accuracy |
| Set | Accuracy | Speed |
| Block | Speed | Endurance |
| Receive | Endurance | Power |

This makes Blocking more intuitive, but Receiving substantially less intuitive. Power is a difficult secondary Attribute to justify for ordinary defensive reception.

## My preference

I would choose the **first alternative**\:

**Spike POW/ACC → Set ACC/END → Receive END/SPD → Block SPD/POW.**

The reason is that all four actions have a reasonably recognizable primary Attribute\:

- Spike: Power
- Set: Accuracy
- Receive: Endurance
- Block: Speed

That's a cleaner association than asking players to understand why Blocking primarily scales with Endurance.

The main concern is Setting's secondary Endurance scaling. But that's easier to justify as sustained technical control than Receiving's secondary Power scaling.

One additional consideration: if Endurance already improves Stamina economy, giving it primary influence over Receive HP efficiency makes defensive specialists especially sustainable. That may be desirable for Libero, but it's worth checking for excessive overlap.

**Verdict\:** The alternative is marginally more intuitive overall, especially for Block and Receive, although your original ACC/SPD Set pairing is better individually.

### Exchange 039

Conversation turn ID: `8e2189fb-8b26-4506-98ee-0f9156ac3531`


#### User

yes, we should calibrate exactly how each attribute affects any and every action on the court. END maybe is best first? ALSO- are you able to reference my VWM project? its stat profile system?


#### Assistant

Yes. I can reference prior discussions of **VWM (Volley World Manager)**, including its stat-profile system. I have recovered several relevant design decisions, although I haven't yet inspected the current repository implementation.

And I agree that **Endurance (END) is probably the best Attribute to calibrate first**\. It intersects with nearly every physical action, both offensive and defensive, and it already has an established relationship with Stamina.

## 1\. What VWM can contribute

VWM used a more granular model of volleyball performance than the MOBA's four Attributes.

Two especially relevant elements are\:

- **Underlying player Attributes\:** Including Strength, Agility, Speed, Stamina, Endurance, and numerous mental or tactical qualities.
- **Functional performance profiles\:** Attack, Blocking, Serve, Serve Receive, Floor Defense, and Setting, with different underlying Attributes contributing to each.

A particularly useful VWM principle was that functional performance wasn't simply the sum of Attributes\:

\[
\text{Functional Contribution}
=
\text{Underlying Ability}
+
\text{Role Utilization}
+
\text{Tactical Fit}
+
\text{Current Form}
+
\text{Context}
\]

VWM also distinguished a player's underlying talent from their current performance.

For the volleyball MOBA, I wouldn't reproduce that entire simulation. Instead, I'd borrow the distinction between **physical capacity, technical effectiveness, and situational execution**\.

The MOBA compresses many VWM qualities into four Attributes, while player-controlled timing, positioning, aiming, and decision-making replace much of VWM's simulated intelligence.

That is a useful starting point for calibration.

## 2\. Calibrating Endurance

I'd begin by defining END as\:

**Endurance is the capacity to withstand physical exertion and impact while maintaining functional performance and recovering from fatigue.**

That gives END three distinct responsibilities\:

1. **Sustained exertion\:** How much physical activity a player can perform before exhausting themselves.
2. **Impact resistance\:** How effectively a player absorbs force during Receives, Blocks, and landings.
3. **Recovery\:** How quickly the player returns to a functional state after strenuous actions.

These should not automatically scale at the same rate.

### Proposed END influence across court actions

The following is a first-pass design hypothesis, not a calibrated numerical model.

| Action | END influence | Proposed effect |
|--------|--------|--------|
| Walking | Low | Minimal influence on ordinary movement |
| Sprinting | High | Lower Stamina drain during sustained sprinting |
| Jumping | Moderate | Reduced Stamina expenditure; better repeated-jump sustainability |
| Approach run | Moderate | Less fatigue from repeated explosive approaches |
| Spike | Low–moderate | Reduced physical exertion cost, not direct attack power |
| Set | Low | Better sustained Setting economy |
| Receive / Dig | High | Impact absorption, reduced Stamina and HP costs |
| Dive / Pancake | High | Impact tolerance, recovery after landing |
| Block | Very high | Force absorption and reduced defensive HP expenditure |
| Serve | Low–moderate | Reduced exertion during powerful Serves |
| Pepper | Contextual | Depends on the actual contact and movement involved |
| Landing | High | Reduced recovery burden after hard landings |
| Exhaustion | Very high | Influences depletion and recovery, without necessarily changing the 50% recovery threshold |

### 3\. The most important distinction: Stamina versus HP

I wouldn't make END simply reduce the costs of everything.

Instead\:

- **Stamina\:** END should have a broad effect on exertion and recovery.
- **HP\:** END should have a concentrated effect on impact-related contacts, especially Blocking and Receiving.
- **Execution\:** END should help preserve performance under physical strain, but not replace player timing or Accuracy.
- **Attributes\:** END should not directly increase jump height, attack power, or movement speed. Those belong primarily to other Attributes.

This makes END useful to everyone without making it universally optimal.

## 4\. The first numerical calibration decision

Before assigning percentages, I think we need to settle one distinction\:

**Does END increase maximum Stamina, reduce Stamina expenditure, accelerate Stamina regeneration, or affect some combination of the three?**

These produce materially different builds.

A larger Stamina pool rewards burst activity followed by rest. Reduced expenditure rewards sustained activity. Faster regeneration rewards intermittent activity and recovery between Rallies.

My provisional preference is to give END a moderate influence on all three, but make **expenditure efficiency its dominant contribution**\. That fits your interpretation of END as physical resilience, and it distinguishes a highly conditioned athlete from one who merely possesses a large energy reserve.

The VWM precedent suggests we should eventually measure the resulting *functional performance*—for example, how many consecutive Blocks or Receives a character can perform before becoming Exhausted—rather than balancing the underlying percentages in isolation.

That would also let us calibrate the starting Libero against the first Serve Receive Drill, then verify that a defensively specialized OP can reach comparable efficiency through investment without receiving an artificial position-specific exemption.

## Why this is especially important for MB

The MB becomes a particularly interesting case because **their preferred actions are individually efficient in HP but physically expensive in Stamina**\.

An MB can Block at zero HP cost, yet still incur substantial Stamina expenditure from jumping.

That separation is essential.

Consider two hypothetical MB builds\:

|  | Explosive MB | Endurance MB |

| Jump height | Potentially higher | Potentially lower |
| Initial attacking threat | Greater | More moderate |

| Extended blocking exchanges | Vulnerable to fatigue | More reliable |
| Preferred Rally | Short, decisive | Repeated pressure and interception |

Both remain legitimate expressions of the same positional identity.

### The paradox you identified

The MB is simultaneously\:

1. Among the positions most incentivized to jump repeatedly.
2. Among the positions most dependent on END to sustain their preferred playstyle.
3. Potentially among the most vulnerable to Stamina depletion when built for maximum offensive explosiveness.

That is a useful source of strategic tension, particularly because the MB's zero-HP Blocking efficiency does not eliminate the Stamina burden.

## An important calibration consequence

I would now distinguish three aspects of jumping\:

| Jump property | Primary influence |
|--------|--------|
| Maximum jump height | Power, potentially modified by height and Traits |
| Takeoff speed / approach execution | Speed |
| Stamina expenditure and repeated-jump sustainability | **Endurance** |

Crucially, I would **not automatically make low END reduce maximum jump height**\. The punishment for insufficient END should primarily be the inability to sustain repeated jumps, eventually culminating in Exhaustion.

Otherwise, END could become mandatory for both jumping effectiveness *and* jumping sustainability, leaving too little room for explosive builds.

There is one question that matters before we start assigning numerical coefficients\:

**Should END primarily reduce the Stamina cost of each jump, or should repeated jumping create an accumulating fatigue burden that END helps resist?**

The former is simpler and works with the existing Stamina system. The latter could represent the MB's physical demands more precisely, but risks introducing a second fatigue mechanic when Stamina and Exhaustion already cover much of that territory.

I'd favor the first approach unless repeated-jump fatigue proves necessary during testing.
