Yes, with one distinction: **HP is the resource for technical exertion and available techniques, while Stamina is the resource for physical exertion.** In MOBA terms, the MB's relationship with Stamina resembles a mana-hungry burst mage, even though HP is not literally health in our existing system.

This suggests that MB should have **high baseline Stamina capacity, high Stamina expenditure, and strong incentives to invest in END**\.

The important part is that these three properties are not contradictory.

A middle might begin with more Stamina than an Outside Hitter but still exhaust themselves faster because their optimal play involves repeated jumps for attacks, blocks, and feints.

That produces a recognizable resource-management identity\:

| Property | MB implication |
|--------|--------|
| High Stamina pool | Can afford several explosive engagements |
| High action costs | Cannot sustain maximum activity indefinitely |
| END investment | Extends the number of jumps and blocks available |
| Recovery opportunities | Rewards choosing when to disengage or remain grounded |
| HP-efficient Blocking | Can preserve technical resources while still exhausting physical resources |

The analogy also helps distinguish **capacity from efficiency**\.

A high maximum Stamina pool lets an MB perform several costly actions in quick succession. END-based expenditure reductions let them perform those actions more sustainably. Stamina regeneration determines how quickly they can prepare for another exchange.

Those are three different balance levers.

I would therefore avoid making END simply synonymous with maximum Stamina. Instead, the MB's **positional starting profile** can provide a relatively large Stamina pool, while END governs how efficiently that pool is used.

One additional implication: this makes the previously established **Workhorse Innate**, which provides very high base Stamina, particularly attractive for MB builds. Workhorse would support explosive sequences through raw capacity, while END investment would improve repeated-action efficiency. They could complement one another without being identical.

The next step is to establish a baseline jump cost and determine how many consecutive jumps an average MB, an END-specialized MB, and a low-END explosive MB can perform before Exhaustion. Those target counts would give us a much more useful basis for numerical calibration than choosing arbitrary percentage reductions.

before continuing, a different direction than by-position stat profiles: by-position Innates instead. MBs Innately consume minimal HP on block and charge Block Jumps more quickly. OPs Innately gain AP more effectively. OHs Innately gain more widespread increases to stats on level up. S Innately consume minimal HP on sets, including when adjusting aim. L Innately consume minimal HP on Receives and Sprint more efficiently.


#### Assistant

I think **position-specific Innates are a better way to establish positional identity than fixed starting stat profiles**, especially given your goal of allowing unconventional builds.

They let positions differ in *how they use and develop their Attributes*, rather than prescribing which Attributes they must have.

There is one significant qualification: the Libero still needs to be capable of completing the opening Serve Receive Drill at base level. An Innate that reduces Receive HP costs doesn't necessarily solve the Stamina or physical-performance requirements of that Drill.

## Proposed positional Innates

| Position | Innate | Gameplay identity |
|--------|--------|--------|
| **MB** | Minimal HP expenditure on Blocks; faster Block Jump charging | Immediate interception and net control |
| **OP** | More effective AP acquisition | Flexible specialization through accelerated development |
| **OH** | Broader Attribute increases upon leveling | Sustained all-round growth |
| **S** | Minimal HP expenditure on Sets, including directional adjustments | Reliable technical facilitation |
| **L** | Minimal HP expenditure on Receives; more efficient Sprinting | Defensive coverage and mobility |

This is a particularly useful distribution because the Innates operate on different parts of the system.

### MB — Blocking specialist

The faster Block Jump charge is more interesting than a simple Blocking bonus.

It improves the MB's ability to react to developing attacks without necessarily increasing jump height or making the Block itself more powerful.

Combined with minimal HP cost, it allows MBs to attempt interceptions frequently. However, **they still pay the Stamina cost of repeated jumping**, preserving the END dependency we just established.

### OP — Specialization through progression

This is the most structurally different Innate.

Rather than improving a specific volleyball action, OP gains greater freedom to develop a focused build.

One distinction needs resolving: does *gaining AP more effectively* mean receiving more AP per level, or earning AP through a separate mechanism?

I'd favor **more discretionary AP per level**, because it directly supports specialization without creating an XP-farming advantage.

It also distinguishes OP from OH\:

- OP accumulates more freely allocatable power.
- OH receives more broadly distributed Attribute growth.

However, the OP must not simply become a superior all-rounder by allocating its extra points evenly. The total effective value of its progression needs to be balanced against OH's broader automatic growth.

### OH — Generalist growth

The OH Innate gives the position a reliable foundation across multiple volleyball duties.

Rather than receiving large increases in one or two Attributes, OH gradually improves in several dimensions.

This naturally supports a player who Receives, attacks, and occasionally Sets without demanding the same degree of specialization as OP.

I would avoid requiring OH players to distribute their discretionary AP evenly. Their Innate should supply breadth automatically, leaving their choices free.

### S — Setting economy

Minimal Set HP costs, including aim adjustment, is especially important because directional inputs ordinarily consume HP based on Accuracy.

The Setter can therefore prepare accurate offensive opportunities repeatedly without exhausting their technical resources.

It also creates a clear distinction between **being capable of Setting** and **being economically suited to Setting**\.

An ACC-specialized OP might execute an excellent Set, but a Setter can perform comparable Sets more efficiently over time.

### L — Defensive mobility

Minimal Receive HP costs and more efficient Sprinting complement one another.

The Libero can reach difficult balls and afford to keep them in play.

I'd make the Sprint bonus primarily a **Stamina-efficiency improvement**, rather than a direct movement-speed increase. That preserves Speed as the principal determinant of movement capability.

## The major advantage: build freedom

Under this system, a POW/ACC Libero can become a credible attacker, but retains an Innate that rewards Receiving and defensive mobility.

An END/POW OP can specialize in Blocking without needing a position-specific Blocking bonus.

An ACC/SPD MB can become a technical facilitator while retaining its fast Block preparation and efficient interceptions.

This seems much closer to the position system you're developing: **positions create persistent comparative advantages, while Attributes and Traits determine the player's actual specialization.**

## One concern: OP and OH progression

These two Innates need to be designed together, not independently.

If OP receives more discretionary AP and OH receives more total Attribute growth, the comparison is meaningful.

If OP simply receives more AP *and* can distribute those points however it likes, including evenly, it may eventually dominate OH's generalist profile.

I'd establish a shared progression budget and distinguish them through allocation\:

- **OP\:** More discretionary AP, fewer automatic Attribute increases.
- **OH\:** Fewer discretionary AP, more automatic increases distributed across Attributes.

That preserves specialization versus generalism without requiring artificial penalties.

**Overall, I would adopt these five Innates provisionally and move starting stat profiles into a secondary balancing role.** The Innates are a clearer expression of positional identity, and they leave much more room for builds that depart from conventional volleyball roles.

## Proposed level-up profiles

| Position | Automatic gains | Freely allocated | Total per level |
|--------|--------|--------|--------|
| MB | \+2 END | \+2 or +3 | 4–5 |
| S | \+2 ACC | \+2 or +3 | 4–5 |
| L | \+2 SPD | \+2 or +3 | 4–5 |
| OH | \+1 POW, SPD, ACC, END | \+1 or +2 | 5–6 |
| OP | \+1 POW | \+4 or +5 | 5–6 |

The updated **Libero Innate** is minimal HP *and* Stamina expenditure on Receives, replacing Sprint efficiency. That is particularly appropriate given the importance of END for defensive impact absorption.

## My preferred initial calibration

Equal total Attribute growth

| **MB** | \+2 END | \+3 free |
|--------|--------|--------|
| **S** | \+2 ACC | \+3 free |
| **L** | \+2 SPD | \+3 free |
| **OH** | \+1 all four | \+1 free |
| **OP** | \+1 POW | \+4 free |

Every position gains 5 total Attribute points per level.

This is the version I would test first.

It establishes three distinct progression patterns\:

- **MB/S/L\:** Guided specialists. Their main Attribute grows automatically, but they retain considerable freedom.
- **OH\:** Broad generalist. Their growth guarantees improvement across every Attribute, regardless of build.
- **OP\:** Open specialist. Nearly all their growth can be concentrated into whichever Attributes they choose.

## One important observation about OH versus OP

At equal total growth, OH doesn't necessarily have an advantage in overall Attribute value. Its advantage is **guaranteed breadth**, which may or may not be beneficial.

An OP can reproduce OH's exact growth pattern\:

- Spend 1 free AP on SPD.
- Spend 1 on ACC.
- Spend 1 on END.
- Spend 1 on POW.

Together with the automatic +1 POW, that produces +2 POW, +1 SPD, +1 ACC, and +1 END—actually a stronger Power profile than OH.

Alternatively, the OP can distribute its points to replicate OH's +1 to every Attribute and place its remaining point wherever it wants.

**This means OP's progression strictly dominates OH's in terms of allocation flexibility.**

That's not automatically fatal, because OH has a different positional Innate and could receive better baseline efficiencies. But under the current proposal, OH's automatic broad growth isn't itself an advantage over OP.

### A possible adjustment

If you want OH's all-rounder identity to be mechanically competitive without requiring a separate innate bonus, I'd consider\:

| Position | Automatic | Free | Total |
|--------|--------|--------|--------|
| OH | \+1 all four | \+2 | **6** |
| OP | \+1 POW | \+4 | **5** |

This creates a meaningful tradeoff\:

- OH gains more overall Attributes.
- OP gains more control over their distribution.

A broadly competent OH could have better aggregate physical capability, while a focused OP could still have significantly higher values in its chosen specialties.

The cost is that OH gets more total stat value every level, so the value of broad Attributes must be sufficiently distributed across gameplay for that not to become excessive.

## A second consideration: the three guided specialists

MB, S, and L gain +2 in their main Attribute, which is more focused than OH's growth but less flexible than OP's.

This makes sense, although it creates interesting alternative-build possibilities.

An MB could invest all three discretionary points into Power each level, producing\:

- \+2 END
- \+3 POW

That player becomes both physically resilient and offensively threatening.

An L could invest +3 POW each level while automatically gaining +2 SPD, producing a mobile offensive Libero who retains the position's defensive HP/Stamina efficiency.

These are desirable consequences of the system.

## Recommendation

I'd start with **5 points per level for MB/S/L/OP, and 6 for OH**\.

That makes the five positions meaningfully different without creating complicated progression formulas.

The only qualification is that OH's extra point should be evaluated against its starting profile and positional Innate. If OH already has significant advantages elsewhere, equal totals may be sufficient.

But if the level-up profile *is* OH's primary Innate, the additional point seems justified. Otherwise, its defining characteristic is something an OP can reproduce while retaining greater freedom.

Start with a universal level-up award of **2 freely allocated AP**\. Then apply positional Innates.

| Position | Universal gain | Innate progression | Total |
|--------|--------|--------|--------|
| MB | \+2 free AP | \+2 END | 4 |
| S | \+2 free AP | \+2 ACC | 4 |
| L | \+2 free AP | \+2 SPD | 4 |

This makes the OP's progression Innate equivalent to **2.5× the ordinary discretionary AP award**, but I would describe it as a flat bonus rather than a multiplier.

A multiplier creates complications if the universal AP award ever changes. A flat bonus is easier to balance and understand.

### Why the unequal totals are reasonable

These totals aren't directly equivalent because freely allocated points are generally more valuable than forced allocations.

An OH gains six points, but four must be distributed across all Attributes.

An OP gains five points, but all five can be invested wherever the player wants.

The OH has greater aggregate growth, while the OP has greater concentration potential.

For example, over ten level-ups\:

The OH remains capable across all four dimensions, even when deliberately specializing in Power. The OP can achieve a much higher Power value, but sacrifices development elsewhere.

This creates a meaningful tradeoff without requiring either position to have artificially higher contact penalties.

## One important correction to the Innate structure

For MB/S/L, I would distinguish **contact Innates** from **progression Innates**\.

Under the model above\:

| Position | Contact Innate | Progression Innate |
|--------|--------|--------|
| MB | Minimal Block HP cost; faster Block Jump charge | \+2 END |
| S | Minimal Set HP cost, including aim adjustment | \+2 ACC |
| L | Minimal Receive HP and Stamina cost | \+2 SPD |
| OH | — | \+1 all Attributes |
| OP | — | \+3 free AP |

This means MB/S/L have two distinct Innate benefits while OH/OP have one progression-focused benefit.

That might be entirely appropriate because their progression bonuses are more constrained, but it is something to account for when balancing.

## Would I use double or triple AP for OP?

With a universal +2 AP\:

- **Double\:** 4 free AP total.
- **2.5×\:** 5 free AP total.
- **Triple\:** 6 free AP total.

I would start at **5**, not 6.

At 6, OP receives the same total Attribute growth as OH, with complete freedom to allocate it. In pure Attribute terms, OP would strictly dominate OH's progression.

At 4, OP has the same total growth as MB/S/L, but those positions also possess substantial contact Innates.

Five is a reasonable middle ground.

There is one larger balance question, however: if an OP can put five points into one Attribute every level, **do Attributes have diminishing returns or investment limits?** Without either, extreme specialization might become disproportionately powerful.

I wouldn't impose a cap automatically. The cost of neglecting other Attributes could be sufficient, particularly with END governing Stamina-intensive jumping and defensive play.

**My provisional recommendation is a universal +2 freely allocated AP per level, modified by positional Innates: MB +2 END, S +2 ACC, L +2 SPD, OH +1 to all four, and OP +3 additional free AP.**

This gives each position a clear developmental identity while keeping the underlying progression system simple.

At 65 starting points, an OP investing all five AP into one Attribute reaches 99 at Level 8. An MB focusing its automatic main Attribute and both discretionary points reaches 99 at Level 10. An OH focusing one Attribute reaches 99 at Level 13.

## Is early capping necessarily bad?

Not entirely. It creates a tradeoff: a player who reaches 99 in Power at Level 8 has an enormous early offensive specialty but must subsequently invest elsewhere.

That can produce interesting build trajectories: early specialization followed by gradual diversification.

However, the danger is that the OP's defining Innate becomes less valuable once its preferred Attributes reach their caps. It has greater allocation freedom, but that freedom matters less when all relevant Attributes are already near maximum.

The OH, meanwhile, continues receiving broad automatic increases, though these can also become wasted at high levels.

## My preferred adjustment

I would retain **Level 20 and 99 as provisional limits**, but reconsider the starting average before changing AP growth.

A 60-rated start provides five additional points of headroom per Attribute compared with 65, although it only delays the fastest specialized OP cap by approximately one level.

That suggests the issue isn't primarily 60 versus 65. It's the rate of growth relative to the 99 cap.

I would test a **soft specialization limit** before implementing diminishing returns: players can invest freely up to 99, but the opportunity cost of neglecting other Attributes becomes increasingly significant as match demands grow.

The decisive question is whether the player who reaches 99 Power at Level 8 is actually stronger overall than one with a more balanced spread. With END affecting jumping sustainability and Stamina, the answer may well be no.

**For now, I'd provisionally establish Level 20 maximum and 99 maximum Attribute rating, without changing the agreed Innate progression.** The next step should be sample builds at Levels 1, 5, 10, 15, and 20, including extreme specialists and balanced characters, to see whether the resulting profiles make sense before calibrating the action economy.

Yes. **Increasing the AP cost of higher Attribute ratings is probably the cleanest solution** to early specialization caps. It preserves the 0–99 sports-card scale, rewards specialization, and prevents an OP from reaching 99 in a single Attribute by Level 8.

I'd start with your proposed thresholds:

| Attribute rating | Cost to increase by +1 |
|---|---:|
| 0–85 | 1 AP |
| 86–95 | 2 AP |
| 96–99 | 3 AP |

This makes the upper ratings progressively more expensive without introducing a complicated diminishing-returns formula.

### What this changes

Starting at 65, the cost of reaching various ratings becomes:

| Target rating | Total AP required |
|---|---:|
| 75 | 10 |
| 85 | 20 |
| 90 | 30 |
| 95 | 40 |
| 99 | 52 |

An OP investing all 5 AP per level into one Attribute would therefore reach:

| Level | Attribute rating |
|---|---:|
| 1 | 65 |
| 5 | 85 |
| 10 | 97 |
| 12 | 99 |
| 20 | 99 |

This is substantially better than reaching 99 at Level 8. It also makes 90+ feel like a genuine specialization rather than an ordinary midgame stat.

### The interaction with positional Innates matters

There are two ways to handle automatic Attribute increases.

**Option A: Automatic increases ignore escalating AP costs.**

An MB always gains +2 END per level, even above 85.

This makes positional Innates increasingly valuable at high ratings. It also means an MB can reach 99 END substantially earlier than an OP investing five discretionary AP per level.

**Option B: Automatic increases obey the same escalating costs.**

An MB's +2 END represents two points of Attribute investment, not necessarily two full rating increases.

This is more consistent economically, but it weakens the intuitive promise of gaining +2 END every level.

I favor **Option A**, with one qualification: automatic gains should stop at 99 rather than overflow into other Attributes.

That makes positional specialization genuinely distinctive. A Setter can develop exceptional Accuracy efficiently because Setting is their innate specialty, whereas an OP must invest increasingly expensive discretionary points to match it.

However, this would make the OH's +1 to every Attribute particularly powerful at high levels. An OH receiving four automatic increases per level can bypass escalating costs across all four Attributes.

That may be desirable, given that OH is supposed to become a broadly competent all-rounder, but it needs testing.

### One refinement I'd consider

Rather than treating the thresholds as arbitrary numerical penalties, they can represent three recognizable tiers:

- **65–85: Development.** Ordinary improvement through training.
- **86–95: Specialization.** Significant investment to approach elite performance.
- **96–99: Mastery.** Exceptional investment for marginal numerical improvements.

The upper tiers can still provide meaningful mechanical improvements. They simply require more resources.

### My recommendation

Keep **85 and 95 as the thresholds**, with costs of **1 / 2 / 3 AP**.

For the first simulation, I'd use automatic positional gains at full value, exempt from escalating costs. This gives MB/S/L/OH a genuine progression advantage in their prescribed Attributes, while OP retains the ability to specialize in any Attribute.

The next thing worth examining is whether this makes OH too powerful at Level 20. Its automatic +1 to every Attribute could be more valuable than the OP's additional free AP once most Attributes exceed 85.

That comparison should determine whether automatic gains need to obey the same cost curve, rather than deciding the exception purely on intuition.

I think **80/90 thresholds are better suited to a 20-level game**, especially if every position receives at least 3 discretionary AP per level. They make 80 a meaningful transition from general competence to specialization, and 90 the beginning of exceptional performance.

The proposed automatic-growth ceilings are also sensible. They create a distinction between **Innate development** and **deliberate specialization**:

- OH automatically develops all four Attributes toward 80.
- MB/S/L automatically develop their primary Attribute toward 90.
- OP can concentrate discretionary AP wherever desired.

However, **OH could be significantly disadvantaged if its automatic growth simply disappears at 80 without compensation**.

## 1. A revised progression model

I'd initially test this:

| Position | Free AP / level | Innate growth | Automatic ceiling |
|---|---:|---|---:|
| MB | 3 | +2 END | 90 |
| S | 3 | +2 ACC | 90 |
| L | 3 | +2 SPD | 90 |
| OH | 3 | +1 to all Attributes | 80 |
| OP | 6 | Additional discretionary AP | — |

The discretionary costs would be:

| Rating increase | AP cost |
|---|---:|
| Up to 79 → 80 | 1 |
| 80 → 81 through 89 → 90 | 2 |
| 90 → 91 through 98 → 99 | 3 |

An Attribute starting at 65 would require:

- **15 AP** to reach 80.
- **20 more AP** to reach 90.
- **27 more AP** to reach 99.
- **62 AP total** to reach 99.

An OP investing all 6 AP per level could reach 99 at approximately Level 12, while sacrificing nearly all other development.

That's a more appropriate specialization curve.

## 2. The problem with OH's ceiling

Suppose an OH starts with 65 in every Attribute.

Its Innate grants +1 to each Attribute until they reach 80.

That means the Innate provides its full benefit for only **15 level-ups**, assuming no discretionary investment in those Attributes.

At Level 16, the OH reaches:

**80 POW / 80 ACC / 80 SPD / 80 END**

From Level 17 onward, its Innate provides nothing.

Meanwhile, an OP continues receiving 6 discretionary AP per level, and MB/S/L can continue receiving automatic growth in their main Attribute until 90.

There is a second, subtler problem: **OH is discouraged from investing its free AP in any Attribute before reaching 80**, because doing so accelerates the point at which that Attribute stops receiving free growth.

An OH who spends AP on Power early effectively sacrifices future automatic Power increases.

That's a bad incentive. It punishes players for investing in their own strengths.

## 3. I would change how the ceiling works

Instead of making OH's Innate stop functioning when an Attribute reaches 80, I'd make its automatic growth **convert into discretionary AP** once it can no longer increase an Attribute.

For example:

**OH Innate — All-Rounder**

*On leveling up, gain +1 to each Attribute below 80. For each Attribute already at 80 or higher, gain +1 additional discretionary AP instead.*

Under this model:

| OH Attribute situation | Automatic gains | Total discretionary AP |
|---|---|---:|
| All four below 80 | +1 to all four | 3 |
| One at 80+ | +1 to other three | 4 |
| Two at 80+ | +1 to other two | 5 |
| Three at 80+ | +1 to remaining one | 6 |
| All four at 80+ | None | 7 |

The Innate never becomes worthless.

However, the converted AP must obey the 2× and 3× investment costs above 80 and 90. Thus, OH still loses the efficiency of automatic growth once it reaches 80.

This creates a natural developmental transition:

**Early game:** OH grows broadly and efficiently.

**Midgame:** OH begins specializing using increasingly discretionary progression.

**Late game:** OH has a strong general foundation but must pay the same premium for elite Attributes as everyone else.

That seems particularly appropriate for an Outside Hitter.

## 4. MB/S/L need similar treatment

The same problem exists for the guided specialists, although their 90 ceiling makes it less severe.

I'd apply the same conversion principle:

**MB:** Gain +2 END per level, up to 90. Any automatic increases that cannot be applied become discretionary AP.

Likewise for S and L.

One complication is that a +2 automatic increase can cross the ceiling. For example, an MB at 89 END should gain +1 END and convert the other point into +1 discretionary AP.

This is straightforward.

## 5. Does this preserve the OP's identity?

Mostly, but I'd reconsider OP's discretionary AP advantage.

Under this model:

| Position | Total nominal growth before ceilings |
|---|---:|
| MB/S/L | 5 |
| OH | 7 |
| OP | 6 |

OH gains the most total value, but much of it is constrained to broad development.

OP has six freely allocated points from Level 2 onward.

At Level 20, the total discretionary awards are:

- MB/S/L: at least 57 AP, plus converted automatic gains.
- OH: at least 57 AP, plus converted automatic gains.
- OP: **114 AP**, entirely discretionary.

That gives OP an enormous degree of flexibility, while OH retains superior broad efficiency.

I think this is a reasonable initial balance hypothesis.

## 6. One remaining concern: automatic growth bypasses the cost curve

An MB gaining +2 END above 80 is effectively receiving **4 AP worth of END improvement**, while an OP must pay 4 AP to gain the same two ratings.

Between 80 and 90, the MB's total nominal level-up value is therefore:

- 3 discretionary AP.
- +2 END, worth 4 AP.

**Effective value: 7 AP per level.**

OH, while its four Attributes remain below 80, also receives 7 AP worth of growth.

This produces an interesting symmetry: MB/S/L and OH have roughly equivalent effective progression value in their respective development ranges, while OP receives 6 AP with maximum freedom.

Once their Innates hit their ceilings, they lose some efficiency but gain flexibility.

That seems like a promising balance structure.

## Recommendation

I'd provisionally adopt:

**Universal progression:** +3 discretionary AP per level.

**Positional progression Innates:**
- MB: +2 END per level, automatically up to 90.
- S: +2 ACC per level, automatically up to 90.
- L: +2 SPD per level, automatically up to 90.
- OH: +1 to all four Attributes per level, automatically up to 80.
- OP: +3 additional discretionary AP per level.

**Automatic increases that cannot be applied convert into discretionary AP at a 1:1 rate.**

**Discretionary investment costs:** 1 AP per rating through 80, 2 AP through 90, 3 AP through 99.

This gives OH an identifiable advantage without letting it automatically develop four 99-rated Attributes, while allowing MB/S/L to develop elite positional specialties and OP to pursue any build.

The next simulation should specifically compare an OH investing heavily in Power against an OP doing the same. That will reveal whether OH's broad automatic growth is sufficiently valuable to compensate for its slower specialization.

## OH vs. OP: Power-specialist progression

Using our latest proposed model:

| Rule | Outside Hitter (OH) | Opposite (OP) |
|---|---|---|
| Starting Attributes | 65 across all four | 65 across all four |
| Discretionary AP per level | 3 | 6 |
| Innate | +1 to every Attribute below 80 | +3 additional discretionary AP |
| Automatic-growth ceiling | 80 | None |
| Unused automatic increases | Convert to discretionary AP | — |

Both players invest as aggressively as possible into **Power**, subject to the escalating costs:

- 65–80: 1 AP per rating
- 80–90: 2 AP per rating
- 90–99: 3 AP per rating

The key distinction is that the OH's automatic Power increase counts toward its development, whereas the OP must purchase every increase.

An OH also accumulates automatic Accuracy, Speed, and Endurance, so we need to compare **Power concentration and overall competence**, not just who reaches 99 first.

## 1. Numerical comparison

Assuming all available discretionary AP goes into Power until it reaches 99:

| Level | OH Power | OP Power | OH ACC/SPD/END | OP ACC/SPD/END |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| 1 | 65 | 65 | 65 | 65 |
| 5 | 80 | 84 | 69 | 65 |
| 10 | 90 | 96 | 74 | 65 |
| 12 | 93 | **99** | 76 | 65 |
| 15 | 97 | **99** | 79 | 65 |
| 20 | **99** | **99** | 80 | 65 |

At Level 20, after reaching 99 Power, the OH has **26 unspent AP** and the OP has **52 unspent AP**. Those points could be invested in other Attributes; the table deliberately holds them unspent to isolate the Power comparison.

### What this reveals

**OP has a substantial specialization advantage, but OH is not far behind.**

At Level 10:

- OP has 96 Power.
- OH has 90 Power.
- OH also has nine additional points each in Accuracy, Speed, and Endurance.

That is a meaningful distinction. OP reaches exceptional hitting capability earlier, while OH remains substantially more capable across other actions.

By Level 12, OP reaches the Power cap. OH doesn't reach it until Level 17.

Importantly, OH's additional Attributes aren't necessarily wasted. Accuracy improves attacking precision, Speed supports positioning, and Endurance supports repeated jumps.

This is exactly the sort of tradeoff we want.

## 2. But there's a problem with the OH Innate

Consider what happens if OH invests its discretionary AP in an Attribute other than Power.

Suppose OH allocates everything to Endurance.

Because Power still receives its automatic +1 per level, the OH eventually develops:

- Excellent Endurance.
- Competent Power.
- Competent Accuracy.
- Competent Speed.

That is the intended all-rounder identity.

However, the **conversion rule creates an unusual optimization opportunity**.

If OH pushes one Attribute above 80 early, its automatic growth in that Attribute converts into discretionary AP. This can be useful because the player can redirect those points elsewhere.

Consequently, the optimal OH build might deliberately rush an Attribute to 80, not because the player wants that Attribute, but to unlock more discretionary AP.

I don't think that's necessarily desirable. It makes the Innate more about manipulating progression than naturally developing broad competence.

## 3. Should OH be stronger?

I don't think it needs additional Attribute growth.

At Level 10, an OH with 90 Power and 74 in everything else is already an extremely capable all-rounder.

An OP with 96 Power and 65 everywhere else is considerably more specialized, but also much more vulnerable to the consequences of low Endurance, Accuracy, and Speed.

And the OH's automatic development is especially valuable because the game rewards competence in multiple volleyball actions.

An OH must Receive, attack, reposition, and occasionally Set. All four Attributes contribute to those responsibilities.

An OP can choose to sacrifice those capabilities for extreme offensive specialization.

That is a legitimate strategic choice.

## 4. The more important comparison: realistic builds

The previous comparison deliberately maximizes Power. Actual players may not want to do that.

Consider a Level 10 OH that distributes its discretionary investment between Power and Endurance.

Its automatic growth already provides:

**74 POW / 74 ACC / 74 SPD / 74 END**

It also has 27 discretionary AP to invest, subject to escalating costs.

That could produce a character with approximately:

**85 POW / 74 ACC / 74 SPD / 82 END**

Meanwhile, an OP has 54 discretionary AP and could produce something like:

**90 POW / 65 ACC / 65 SPD / 84 END**

The OP remains more specialized offensively, but the OH has better Accuracy and Speed.

These are genuinely different athletes, rather than one being an objectively superior version of the other.

## 5. My assessment

I would **retain the proposed progression values**.

The numbers appear to produce the desired relationship:

| Position | Progression advantage | Limitation |
|---|---|---|
| OH | Broad, efficient Attribute development | Slower concentration into exceptional ratings |
| OP | Rapid, unconstrained specialization | Must sacrifice development elsewhere |
| MB/S/L | Efficient development toward a positional specialty | Less freedom than OP; less breadth than OH |

There is one adjustment I would consider before canonizing:

**Make the OH's automatic growth stop at 80 without converting into discretionary AP, but increase its baseline discretionary award if necessary.**

The conversion rule is mechanically elegant, but it may undermine the point of an Innate that rewards broad development.

Alternatively, keep conversion and accept that OH eventually transitions from generalist development into freely allocated specialization.

Between those two, I currently prefer **keeping conversion**, because otherwise OH could effectively lose its entire progression Innate in the late game.

**Conclusion:** OH is not underpowered on paper. At Level 10, its combination of high Power and broad secondary competence is a substantial advantage. The bigger question is whether the underlying gameplay systems make those secondary Attributes sufficiently valuable to offset OP's earlier access to 90–99 Power.

For calibration, I'd examine **Levels 1–5**, with Level 10 as a later reference point. These are the levels where starting Attributes, positional Innates, and the first few discretionary investments determine whether players can sustain rallies or complete jungle Drills.

We should distinguish three things:

1. **Starting capability:** What every player can do at Level 1, before specialization.
2. **Positional development:** What each position gains automatically.
3. **Player investment:** How discretionary AP produces different builds within the same position.

I would provisionally retain **65 as the universal Level 1 baseline**. Starting everyone at identical Attributes makes it easier to identify which differences arise from Innates and which arise from player decisions. We can introduce starting distributions later if testing demonstrates a need.

The agreed progression rules are:

| Position | Free AP/level | Automatic growth |
|---|---:|---|
| MB | 3 | +2 END, up to 90 |
| S | 3 | +2 ACC, up to 90 |
| L | 3 | +2 SPD, up to 90 |
| OH | 3 | +1 to each Attribute, up to 80 |
| OP | 6 | None |

Automatic growth beyond its ceiling converts to discretionary AP. Discretionary increases cost 1 AP through 80, 2 AP from 81–90, and 3 AP from 91–99.

For early calibration, this means the **first 15 points above 65 are especially important**. Until an Attribute reaches 80, every discretionary point buys one full rating increase.

## 1. Representative early builds

The following are **illustrative builds, not mandatory positional stat profiles**. Every character begins at 65 in each Attribute, then develops according to its Innate and chosen investments.

Values are ordered **POW / ACC / SPD / END**.

| Position and build | Level 1 | Level 3 | Level 5 |
|---|---|---|---|
| MB — balanced blocker | 65 / 65 / 65 / 65 | 67 / 65 / 67 / 71 | 69 / 65 / 69 / 77 |
| MB — END specialist | 65 / 65 / 65 / 65 | 65 / 65 / 65 / 75 | 65 / 65 / 65 / 83 |
| S — balanced setter | 65 / 65 / 65 / 65 | 65 / 71 / 67 / 67 | 65 / 77 / 69 / 69 |
| S — ACC specialist | 65 / 65 / 65 / 65 | 65 / 75 / 65 / 65 | 65 / 83 / 65 / 65 |
| L — defensive specialist | 65 / 65 / 65 / 65 | 65 / 65 / 71 / 69 | 65 / 65 / 77 / 73 |
| L — SPD specialist | 65 / 65 / 65 / 65 | 65 / 65 / 75 / 65 | 65 / 65 / 83 / 65 |
| OH — balanced attacker | 65 / 65 / 65 / 65 | 69 / 69 / 67 / 69 | 73 / 73 / 69 / 73 |
| OH — POW specialist | 65 / 65 / 65 / 65 | 73 / 67 / 67 / 67 | 80 / 69 / 69 / 69 |
| OP — POW/END attacker | 65 / 65 / 65 / 65 | 73 / 65 / 65 / 69 | 81 / 65 / 65 / 72 |
| OP — defensive specialist | 65 / 65 / 65 / 65 | 65 / 65 / 71 / 71 | 65 / 65 / 77 / 77 |

*Calculation note:* These examples apply automatic increases first, then discretionary spending, with unspent AP carried forward. Some builds have leftover AP at Level 5 because their next increase costs 2 AP.

### What stands out

**MB can reach 75 END by Level 3**, or 83 by Level 5, through concentrated investment. That gives us a useful range for testing repeated jumps.

**L can reach 75 SPD by Level 3**, or 83 by Level 5. But its Receive Innate also reduces HP and Stamina costs, meaning its defensive performance cannot be inferred from SPD alone.

**OH develops substantial secondary competence.** Even an OH investing exclusively in Power reaches 69 in its other three Attributes by Level 5.

**OP can specialize in almost any direction.** The POW/END attacker and defensive specialist are mechanically very different despite sharing the same position.

These spreads give us a workable early-game range of roughly **65–83**, with the strongest specialists beginning to enter the expensive 80+ tier around Level 4–5.

## 2. What this means for jumping calibration

We now have several distinct Endurance benchmarks:

| END | Representative player |
|---:|---|
| 65 | Level 1 player; early low-END specialist |
| 69 | Level 3 defensive L; Level 5 OH |
| 71 | Level 3 balanced MB |
| 73 | Level 5 defensive L or balanced OH |
| 75 | Level 3 END-specialist MB |
| 77 | Level 5 balanced MB or defensive OP |
| 83 | Level 5 END-specialist MB |

I would use **65, 75, and 83 END** as the initial jumping-economy test points.

The important distinction is that END should influence both **Stamina capacity** and **the sustainability of repeated jumps**. We haven't established exactly how much it affects each.

For example, a Level 1 MB at 65 END should be capable of participating in an opening Rally, including multiple Blocks. A Level 3 MB at 75 END should noticeably outperform that baseline in repeated jumping, but not become effectively inexhaustible.

The gap between 65 and 75 therefore needs to be meaningful without making a ten-point Attribute investment overwhelmingly powerful.

I'd also test 65 END with different jump demands: a Libero may jump infrequently, whereas an MB may attempt several Blocks in a single Rally. Identical Stamina pools can produce radically different practical endurance.

## 3. What this means for jungle Drills

Drills should be calibrated against **the actual range of early Attributes**, rather than an assumed positional stat profile.

| Drill | Level 1 baseline | Early specialist benchmark |
|---|---|---|
| Serve Receive | 65 SPD / 65 END, with L Innate | 75 SPD / 65–69 END |
| Box Jump Parkour | 65 SPD / 65 END | 71–75 SPD or END |
| Spike Trainer | 65 POW / 65 ACC | 73–80 POW |
| Transition Set | 65 ACC / 65 SPD | 71–75 ACC |
| Repeated Blocking challenge, if introduced | 65 END / 65 POW | 75–83 END |

The **Level 1 Libero requirement** remains particularly useful as a calibration anchor: a 65 SPD / 65 END Libero must be able to complete the first Serve Receive Drill without leveling up.

That means the first Drill cannot require SPD above 65 or END above 65 as a hard mechanical threshold. It can reward higher ratings through easier timing, lower Stamina consumption, or greater consistency.

Likewise, an early Spike Trainer should be feasible for a 65 POW / 65 ACC player, even if a 75 POW specialist completes it much more efficiently.

## 4. One issue to settle before assigning physical values

The examples reveal that **automatic Attribute growth is extremely influential during the opening levels**.

A Level 3 END-specialist MB already has 75 END, while an OP concentrating on Power might still have 65.

That's a ten-point difference after only two level-ups.

If every END point substantially increases both Stamina capacity and jump efficiency, this gap could create a very large difference in jumping sustainability.

That isn't necessarily undesirable. It may be exactly what makes an MB feel different from an OP.

But it suggests that we should calibrate jumping around three reference cases:

**65 END — Baseline athlete.** Can execute normal volleyball actions, including several jumps, but sustained jumping is expensive.

**75 END — Developed jumping specialist.** Can sustain noticeably more repeated jumping, enough to justify investing heavily in END.

**85 END — Advanced endurance specialist.** Can withstand prolonged jumping demands but must still manage Stamina during extended rallies.

The next step is to define **how many jumps each of these athletes should sustain**, including ordinary Attack Jumps versus Block Jumps, and how quickly they recover between exchanges.

That gives us concrete targets from which to derive Stamina capacity, regeneration, and jump costs, rather than choosing those numbers independently.

#### Assistant

Yes, with one important distinction: **the MB's ability to sustain seven jumps should come primarily from its Endurance development and jumping technique, not from its HP-efficient Blocking Innate alone.**

That gives us a useful separation between positional identity and resource economy.

## 1. Level 1 Libero: investment should matter

I agree with changing the Serve Receive Drill requirement.

A Level 1 Libero should be capable of completing the first Drill without investing, but doing so should be difficult or risky. Investing correctly should make the first clear reasonably reliable.

This creates the equivalent of a jungler selecting the correct opening ability in League.

There is one mechanical detail to resolve: **does Level 1 include an initial discretionary AP allocation?** Our current progression model grants AP *on leveling up*, so a Level 1 Libero has no points to invest unless we explicitly provide starting AP.

I would give every player a small starting allocation, perhaps **3 AP at Level 1**, separate from subsequent level-up rewards.

Then:

- A Libero investing +3 SPD starts at 68 SPD and can clear its opening Receive Drill with relatively low risk.
- A Libero investing +3 END has greater Stamina capacity but may struggle with the Drill's positioning demands.
- A Libero investing elsewhere can still attempt the Drill, but the opening becomes substantially less efficient.

That produces a genuine opening build decision without hard-locking the Libero into SPD.

## 2. Fresh-player jumping targets

Your targets establish a clear physical baseline:

| Player | Fresh jump capacity | Illustrative usage |
|---|---:|---|
| Ordinary Level 1 athlete | 4–5 jumps | 1–2 Blocks + 3 Attacks |
| Fresh MB | ~7 jumps | 3–4 Blocks + 3 Attacks |

These are reasonable targets, provided **Exhaustion occurs when Stamina is depleted**, rather than when the player merely crosses a moderate-fatigue threshold.

But we need to distinguish the number of jumps a player can afford from the number they can perform before their next jump would trigger Exhaustion.

If a player has enough Stamina for exactly five jumps, the fifth may consume their remaining Stamina and trigger Exhaustion. That is a very different practical experience from performing five jumps and retaining enough Stamina to move defensively afterward.

I'd calibrate the numbers as **jumps before Exhaustion**, meaning the player can complete the stated sequence without entering the Exhausted state until attempting another costly action.

## 3. How this interacts with the MB Innate

The MB currently has:

- Minimal HP expenditure on Blocks.
- Faster Block Jump charging.
- +2 END per level, up to 90.
- 3 discretionary AP per level.

At Level 1, however, the MB's progression Innate has not yet activated.

If every player starts at 65 END, a Level 1 MB has the same underlying Stamina capacity and jump economy as every other position.

Its Block Innate reduces **HP expenditure**, not Stamina expenditure.

Its faster Block Jump charge improves **timing**, not necessarily the physical cost of jumping.

Therefore, **the existing MB Innate does not explain why a Level 1 MB can sustain seven jumps while another player sustains five.**

That's the main inconsistency.

### Three ways to resolve it

**A. Give MB higher starting END.**

For example, MB begins at 75 END while other positions begin at 65.

This immediately produces the desired Stamina advantage, but reintroduces the positional starting-stat profiles we were trying to avoid.

**B. Give MB an Innate Stamina discount on Block Jumps.**

The MB's faster Block Jump charging could also reduce its Stamina expenditure.

This would produce a difference between:

- Blocking repeatedly as an MB.
- Attacking repeatedly as an MB.

That is a useful distinction because the MB's specialty is repeated net interceptions, not universally superior jumping.

**C. Give MB more starting AP or apply its automatic END growth at Level 1.**

For example, MB begins with +2 END from its progression Innate, then continues gaining +2 END on subsequent level-ups.

This is simpler than creating a separate Block Jump discount, but a two-point END difference is unlikely to account for two additional jumps unless END scaling is extremely steep.

## 4. My preferred solution

I'd combine **B with the existing progression system**.

Keep the universal 65 baseline and give every player an initial discretionary allocation.

Then revise the MB Innate slightly:

**MB Innate — Blocking Specialist**

*Blocks consume minimal HP. Block Jumps charge more quickly and consume less Stamina.*

This is an extension of the existing Innate rather than a new system.

A Level 1 MB could consequently sustain approximately:

- 3 Attack Jumps at normal cost.
- 4 Block Jumps at reduced cost.

While another player could sustain:

- 3 Attack Jumps.
- 1–2 Block Jumps at normal cost.

The MB gains roughly two additional jumps specifically through its Blocking efficiency.

Its +2 END per level then improves general jumping sustainability as the match progresses.

This seems more desirable than simply granting MB a larger universal Stamina pool.

## 5. Entering a Rally with depleted Stamina

This is arguably more important than the maximum jump count.

If full Stamina is a privilege, we should calibrate around multiple entry conditions.

For illustration, suppose a normal player can afford five jumps when fresh.

| Entry Stamina | Approximate jump budget |
|---|---:|
| 100% | 5 |
| 80% | 4 |
| 60% | 3 |
| 40% | 2 |
| 20% | 1 |

This is only a first approximation. Actual costs will differ between Attack Jumps, Block Jumps, and other actions.

For MB, reduced Block Jump costs would let it sustain more interceptions at the same entry Stamina.

That means an MB entering at 60% might still be able to Block multiple times and attack, whereas another player might need to conserve Stamina for a single decisive jump.

This reinforces MB's role without making it universally superior.

## 6. One issue: Stamina regeneration during rallies

We haven't established how much Stamina regenerates during a Rally.

If regeneration is substantial, a player with enough capacity for five jumps might actually perform seven or eight over an extended Rally.

If regeneration is negligible, jump count closely tracks initial Stamina.

I would start with **low but nonzero regeneration during active rallies**, and higher regeneration outside them.

That allows players to recover modestly between contacts without trivializing Stamina management.

However, I'd avoid a categorical in-Rally regeneration penalty unless the existing regeneration system proves insufficient. The natural frequency of jumping and moving during rallies may already keep Stamina under pressure.

## Recommendation

Your target numbers are sound:

**Ordinary Level 1:** approximately 4–5 jumps when fresh.

**Level 1 MB:** approximately 7 jumps when fresh, specifically because Block Jumps are cheaper.

**Typical Rally entry:** less than full Stamina, depending on prior Drills, movement, and Energy Drink access.

I would provisionally add **reduced Block Jump Stamina expenditure** to the MB Innate and provide a small discretionary AP allocation at Level 1.

That gives us enough structure to derive actual Stamina capacity and jumping costs next, while preserving the distinction between general Endurance investment and MB-specific Blocking efficiency.

| Attacker | 70 | 65 | 60 | 65 | None |
| Defensive | 60 | 65 | 70 | 65 | None |
| Workhorse | 63 | 63 | 63 | 63 | Workhorse costing 8 AP |

These are illustrative allocations, not finalized costs.

There is an important choice here: **does buying an Innate reduce the Athlete's starting OVR?**

Under this example, yes. Workhorse exchanges eight Attribute points for a persistent Stamina advantage.

I think that is desirable, provided 65 OVR is understood as the *reference build*, not a mandatory final rating. Otherwise, an Athlete purchasing an Innate would need to receive compensating OVR credit, which makes the rating much harder to interpret.

## 3. Physical characteristics should be tradeoffs

I'd be careful about treating height, arm length, and leg length as ordinary AP purchases.

If taller Athletes simply gain reach, and reach is overwhelmingly valuable, every competitive Athlete will buy maximum height.

Instead, morphology should create advantages and disadvantages within existing mechanics.

| Characteristic | Potential advantage | Potential disadvantage |
|---|---|---|
| Height | Standing reach, Blocking coverage | Greater physical cost of certain movements |
| Arm length | Contact reach, wider interception range | Potentially slower arm repositioning |
| Leg length | Stride length, approach coverage | Different acceleration and jumping characteristics |
| Compact build | Faster repositioning, potentially efficient movement | Reduced standing reach |

These effects should be grounded in actual movement and contact calculations.

For example, an Athlete with longer arms should have a genuinely larger reachable contact volume, not merely receive a hidden Blocking bonus.

A shorter Athlete might require more vertical jump height to achieve the same contact height, but that doesn't automatically mean jumping costs more Stamina. The relationship should emerge from the eventual jumping model.

**I would not assign exact AP costs to morphology until those physical relationships are established.**

The VWM work provides a useful conceptual starting point—particularly separating body position, contact reach, and limb geometry—but I haven't recovered a verified numerical VWM morphology model to reuse directly.

## 4. Early Ability Traits

The Base Athlete Build also gives us a natural way to distinguish basic abilities from advanced ones.

An Athlete with 70 SPD at Level 1 might qualify for an introductory movement Trait. Another with 70 ACC might qualify for a basic Setting adjustment.

However, I would establish two requirements for early Traits:

**Attribute qualification:** The Athlete meets the relevant statistical threshold.

**Complexity restriction:** The Trait belongs to an introductory tier available at Level 1.

This prevents a player from sacrificing all their other Attributes to qualify for an exceptionally powerful ability before the match begins.

Higher-level Traits could require both more substantial Attribute investment and a minimum Athlete level.

The result is that two Level 1 Liberos can play differently without either having access to a fully developed late-game kit.

## 5. The opening Libero test

This system makes the opening Serve Receive Drill a particularly useful test of Base Athlete Build balance.

Consider three Level 1 Liberos:

| Athlete | Starting SPD | Starting END | Additional Innate | Expected opening |
|---|---:|---:|---|---|
| Balanced | 65 | 65 | None | Challenging but possible |
| Receive specialist | 72 | 63 | None | Reliable first clear |
| Workhorse | 63 | 63 | Workhorse | Better sustained activity, but harder initial positioning |

The Receive specialist has invested in the Attribute most relevant to reaching difficult balls.

The Workhorse can potentially attempt more repetitions before running out of Stamina, but that doesn't necessarily compensate for inadequate Speed.

This is the kind of opening tradeoff that makes pre-match Athlete construction meaningful.

## 6. The biggest design question: what does 65 OVR measure?

I would distinguish:

**Attribute OVR:** The average or weighted combination of POW, ACC, SPD, and END.

**Athlete capability:** Attributes combined with morphology, Innates, Traits, and position.

A 63-rated Workhorse might be more effective in a long Rally than a 65-rated Athlete without Workhorse. That is fine. OVR doesn't need to capture every advantage.

Otherwise, we risk constructing a complicated formula in which a particular height, Innate, or body proportion adds an arbitrary number of OVR points.

The sports-card rating should remain a readable summary of Attributes, not an exhaustive prediction of performance.

---

### Recommended provisional structure

I'd establish **Athlete** as the character term and **Base Athlete Build** as the pre-match customization system.

Use a provisional **60-point starting baseline in each Attribute, with 20 AP to spend**, producing a neutral 65 OVR when fully invested in Attributes. Additional Innates consume that same budget, while the chosen position grants its core Innate for free.

Physical proportions should affect the conversion of Attributes into actual movement and contact performance. Whether unusual proportions also cost AP can be determined once their mechanical advantages are quantified.

Finally, Level 1 Traits should be available through modest Attribute thresholds, with stronger and more complex Traits remaining inaccessible until later levels.

**The next design task I'd prioritize is defining the physical profile itself:** height, arm length, leg length, and any other VWM-derived variables that genuinely change volleyball performance. We should do that before finalizing the pre-match AP budget, because a 65 END Athlete's jumping economy could differ materially depending on their body geometry.

### Exchange 053

Conversation turn ID: `6fec3724-9bbe-4310-ac01-07ebfce13eb8`


#### User

let's take 6'0 or 181cm as an "average" athlete height for the in game universe. heights in inches around that, from 5'10 to 6'2, are freely selectable without AP investment, but still do change the more granular stat profile - speed, stamina, power, are all affected. [so should players be able to granularly adjust their individual statistics? or just the overall attributes? leaning towards only attributes in gameplay, but while building the initial athlete then individual statistics within the range allocated by the attribute value.]


#### Assistant

I agree with that distinction. **Players should allocate individual statistics during Base Athlete creation, but only the four overarching Attributes during a match.**

That gives us two different kinds of customization:

- **Base Athlete Build:** Defines what kind of athlete the character physically is, including height, proportions, and specific athletic strengths.
- **In-match development:** Improves the athlete's broader capabilities through POW, ACC, SPD, and END.

This also gives VWM's granular statistical model a meaningful purpose without making in-match leveling excessively complicated.

## 1. Height as a free physical tradeoff

I'd establish:

**Reference height: 6'0" (182.9 cm).**

A small correction: 181 cm is approximately 5'11¼", so I'd use 183 cm as the reference if we want exactly 6'0".

| Height | Metric | AP cost |
|---|---:|---:|
| 5'10" | 178 cm | Free |
| 5'11" | 180 cm | Free |
| **6'0"** | **183 cm** | **Free** |
| 6'1" | 185 cm | Free |
| 6'2" | 188 cm | Free |

All five heights should be competitively viable, but they need not be equally suited to every role.

The crucial point is that **height shouldn't directly change POW, SPD, or END ratings**. Instead, it should modify the underlying physical statistics those Attributes govern.

For example:

| Physical characteristic | Likely benefit | Likely tradeoff |
|---|---|---|
| Taller | Standing reach, interception height | Potentially slower acceleration, higher movement energy expenditure |
| Shorter | Lower center of mass, easier directional changes | Lower standing reach, potentially greater jumping demands |
| Longer arms | Contact reach, Blocking coverage | Potential arm-speed or repositioning tradeoff |
| Longer legs | Stride length, approach coverage | Potentially different acceleration and jumping characteristics |

These should be relatively modest effects within the free height range.

Otherwise, a nominally free choice becomes a disguised mandatory optimization.

## 2. Four Attributes, multiple underlying statistics

I would treat the four Attributes as **development categories**, not as indivisible physical properties.

A possible initial decomposition:

| Attribute | Underlying statistics |
|---|---|
| **POW** | Jump explosiveness, hitting force, upper-body strength |
| **ACC** | Contact precision, ball control, targeting precision |
| **SPD** | Acceleration, maximum running speed, agility, reaction speed |
| **END** | Stamina capacity, Stamina recovery, exertion efficiency, impact resistance |

These are candidate categories rather than a finalized VWM-derived list.

They immediately explain why two Athletes with identical Attributes could perform differently.

For instance, two Athletes with 70 SPD might have:

**Athlete A — Explosive**
- Acceleration: 76
- Maximum speed: 66
- Agility: 72
- Reaction speed: 66

**Athlete B — Mobile**
- Acceleration: 66
- Maximum speed: 76
- Agility: 68
- Reaction speed: 70

Both average 70 SPD, but one excels at short repositioning while the other covers longer distances.

That is particularly interesting for volleyball because court movement is not simply running speed.

## 3. How granular customization should work

I'd avoid giving players two completely independent sets of AP.

Instead, use a **hierarchical allocation system**.

### Step A — Allocate broad Attributes

The player spends Base Athlete AP on POW, ACC, SPD, and END.

For example:

| Attribute | Rating |
|---|---:|
| POW | 65 |
| ACC | 63 |
| SPD | 70 |
| END | 62 |

Average: 65 OVR.

### Step B — Distribute each Attribute internally

The player can redistribute a limited amount of that Attribute's value among its underlying statistics.

For example, with 70 SPD:

| Statistic | Balanced | Acceleration-focused |
|---|---:|---:|
| Acceleration | 70 | 76 |
| Maximum speed | 70 | 66 |
| Agility | 70 | 72 |
| Reaction speed | 70 | 66 |
| **Average** | **70** | **70** |

This preserves the total Attribute budget while allowing different expressions of the same rating.

I'd initially limit these redistributions to approximately **±5–8 points**, preventing an Athlete with 65 SPD from obtaining a 95-rated acceleration statistic through extreme min-maxing.

The exact limits can be calibrated later.

### Step C — Apply physical morphology

Height, arm length, and leg length then influence actual performance.

A 6'2" Athlete with 70 SPD might have the same underlying acceleration statistic as a 5'10" Athlete, but slightly different realized acceleration because of body geometry.

This creates three distinct quantities:

**Attribute rating → underlying statistic → realized physical performance**

That separation is useful for simulation and balancing.

## 4. What happens when the Athlete levels up?

This is where I'd make the system particularly simple.

**In-match Attribute gains should preserve the Athlete's underlying statistical profile.**

Suppose an Athlete starts with:

- SPD: 70
- Acceleration: 76
- Maximum speed: 66
- Agility: 72
- Reaction speed: 66

The player invests +5 SPD during the match.

The resulting profile becomes approximately:

- SPD: 75
- Acceleration: 81
- Maximum speed: 71
- Agility: 77
- Reaction speed: 71

In other words, the underlying statistics retain their relative offsets.

This means a pre-match acceleration specialist remains an acceleration specialist as they develop.

I would not let players redistribute those offsets during a match. Doing so would introduce an entirely separate optimization system on top of Attributes, Innates, Traits, and position-specific growth.

One refinement: at high ratings, offsets should compress near the 99 cap rather than allowing underlying statistics to exceed it.

## 5. An important issue: how granular statistics interact with Traits

This is where I'd draw a firm boundary.

**Ability Traits should primarily require overarching Attributes, not individual underlying statistics.**

For example:

- A movement Trait requires 70 SPD.
- A jumping Trait requires 75 POW.
- A defensive endurance Trait requires 75 END.

Two Athletes with identical SPD but different acceleration distributions can qualify for the same movement Trait.

Their physical profiles may cause them to use it differently, but neither gets access to an entirely different ability tree solely because they adjusted a hidden substat.

Otherwise, Base Athlete creation becomes a complicated exercise in optimizing dozens of thresholds.

There could eventually be exceptions for particularly distinctive physical Traits, but I'd avoid them initially.

## 6. How this affects jumping economy

This is perhaps the strongest reason to use granular statistics.

Consider two Level 1 Athletes, both with 65 POW and 65 END.

**Athlete A — Explosive jumper**
- Higher jump explosiveness.
- Lower exertion efficiency.
- Excellent initial vertical performance.
- Repeated jumping becomes expensive.

**Athlete B — Sustainable jumper**
- Lower jump explosiveness.
- Higher exertion efficiency.
- Slightly lower maximum vertical performance.
- Can sustain repeated jumps more comfortably.

Neither has higher overall POW or END.

Their differences emerge from how they distributed the underlying statistics.

Now introduce height.

A taller Athlete might need less vertical displacement to reach a particular contact height, but could incur different physical costs when jumping.

A shorter Athlete might have superior acceleration and directional responsiveness, yet need a more demanding jump to contest the same ball.

These are meaningful differences even before players begin investing AP during the match.

**This does introduce one calibration requirement:** our previous target of 4–5 fresh jumps for an ordinary Level 1 Athlete should apply to a *reference physical profile*, not every possible Athlete.

A particularly efficient jumper might sustain six, while an explosive but inefficient Athlete might sustain four.

The MB's reduced Block Jump cost would then apply on top of that physical variation.

## 7. One caution: the risk of false complexity

The biggest danger is giving players many underlying statistics that don't produce perceptible differences.

For example, if acceleration, agility, and reaction speed all effectively determine how quickly a player reaches a ball, separating them might not add much.

I'd only include a granular statistic when it influences a distinguishable part of gameplay.

For SPD, that might mean:

- **Acceleration:** How quickly movement speed builds.
- **Top Speed:** Maximum sustained movement velocity.
- **Agility:** How quickly movement direction changes.
- **Reaction:** How quickly the Athlete responds to a ball or initiates movement.

Each has a separate mechanical responsibility.

Similarly, END's capacity, recovery, and exertion efficiency need to affect different aspects of the Stamina economy rather than all functioning as interchangeable Stamina multipliers.

---

## My recommendation

I'd provisionally establish the following:

**Base Athlete**
- Four overarching Attributes determine the sports-card rating.
- The player allocates Base Athlete AP among those Attributes.
- The player can redistribute a limited amount of each Attribute's value among its underlying statistics.
- Height from 5'10" to 6'2" is free.
- Height and body proportions influence physical performance without directly changing displayed Attribute ratings.
- Additional Innates consume Base Athlete AP.
- Position grants its own Innate for free.

**During matches**
- Players allocate AP only to POW, ACC, SPD, and END.
- Underlying statistics improve alongside their parent Attribute, preserving the pre-match distribution.
- Ability Traits are primarily gated by overarching Attributes and Athlete level.
- Morphology and underlying statistical specialization remain fixed.

The next thing I'd establish is the **actual granular statistic roster**, beginning with POW and END, because those determine jumping height, jump Stamina costs, and recovery.

One additional design question matters here: **should height and limb proportions be independently selectable, or should selecting height generate a plausible default body geometry that players can adjust within a limited range?**

I'd favor the latter. It allows meaningful body-type customization without producing implausible combinations or turning character creation into an unrestricted reach-maximization problem.

#### Assistant

**The proposed progression system is stronger than automatically distributing granular statistics.** It gives players two meaningful decisions at each level: which broad capability to improve, and how to express that improvement.

I would retain both decisions, with automatic allocation as the default and manual allocation as an optional expansion.

The main risk isn't balance complexity alone. It's **decision frequency**: a player should not have to navigate two allocation screens every time they level up while managing a rally, a Drill, or an approaching opponent.

Your tree-style UI is a good solution because it can make the second decision available without making it compulsory.

## 1. The progression structure

I'd use three layers:

| Layer | Player decision | Purpose |
|---|---|---|
| Attribute Points (AP) | Invest in POW, SPD, ACC, END | Determines overall development and Trait eligibility |
| Statistical Points (SP) | Distribute within the upgraded Attribute | Determines the specific physical or technical specialization |
| Trait claims | Choose an available Trait | Adds new mechanics or changes existing ones |

The distinction between AP and SP is important: **SP should be generated by investing AP, not earned as an independent resource.**

For example, a player receives 3 AP and invests one in END. Their END rating increases by one, and they receive a corresponding increment to distribute among END's underlying statistics.

They can accept the recommended distribution immediately or expand END to customize it.

That preserves the simplicity of the four-Attribute system while supporting athletes who specialize in different kinds of endurance.

## 2. What VWM contributes

I accessed [volley-world-manager](https://github.com/iracarranza/volley-world-manager) and found several directly relevant design documents.

The most important finding is that **VWM already distinguishes physique from trainable skill**. Its attribute-first generation design explicitly separates body geometry from the skill budget. Height influences mass, wingspan, and stride, while skill specialization comes from distributing a finite budget.

That supports the Base Athlete approach we're developing.

VWM also has a much more granular vocabulary than the provisional four-stat breakdown we were using.

| MOBA Attribute | Relevant VWM statistics | Possible MOBA interpretation |
|---|---|---|
| **Power** | `jump_reach`, `explosiveness`, `arm_speed`, `attack_power` | Jump height, takeoff speed, striking force |
| **Speed** | `acceleration`, `lateral_speed`, `transition_speed` | Initial acceleration, lateral coverage, forward/backward movement |
| **Accuracy** | `hand_control`, `ball_control`, `set_accuracy`, `attack_accuracy`, `reception_stability` | Contact precision and consistency across actions |
| **Endurance** | `stamina`, plus recovery and fatigue mechanics | Stamina capacity, repeated-exertion efficiency, recovery |

VWM's [Granular Attribute Breakdown](https://github.com/iracarranza/volley-world-manager/blob/main/docs/textbook/GRANULAR_ATTRIBUTE_BREAKDOWN.md) also distinguishes jump displacement from takeoff time and models approach quality separately.

That distinction is valuable. An Athlete can be capable of jumping high without necessarily being capable of jumping *quickly*.

One caution: VWM's documentation contains both implemented mechanics and proposed refinements. We should reuse its underlying distinctions, not assume every documented formula is established or suitable for the MOBA.

Sources: [Attribute-First Generation](https://github.com/iracarranza/volley-world-manager/blob/main/docs/design/ATTRIBUTE_FIRST_GENERATION.md), [Player Attributes Ledger](https://github.com/iracarranza/volley-world-manager/blob/main/docs/textbook/PLAYER_ATTRIBUTES_LEDGER.md).

## 3. I would slightly change how Statistical Points work

The potential weakness in your proposal is that an Attribute increase and its granular distribution could become mathematically redundant.

Suppose SPD has three equally weighted statistics:

- Acceleration
- Lateral Speed
- Transition Speed

If SPD increases from 65 to 66, and the player receives one statistical point, raising Acceleration from 65 to 66 doesn't actually produce an underlying average of 66.

The simplest solution is to define an Attribute rating as the **budget governing its statistics**, rather than requiring it to equal their literal arithmetic mean at every moment.

For example, increasing SPD by one could grant **three Statistical Points**:

| Statistic | Before | Automatic | Manual |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| Acceleration | 65 | 66 | 68 |
| Lateral Speed | 65 | 66 | 65 |
| Transition Speed | 65 | 66 | 65 |
| **SPD** | **65** | **66** | **66** |

Both distributions consume three SP. The parent SPD rating rises by one in either case.

This is an illustrative allocation rule, not yet a calibrated one. If the underlying statistics have unequal gameplay value, their SP costs or permitted ranges may need adjustment.

**Most importantly, spending SP should never change the parent Attribute's value or Trait eligibility.** Otherwise players are effectively being charged twice for specialization.

### Recommended allocation behavior

I'd make each Attribute investment generate a small SP budget, then offer three options:

**Automatic:** Distribute SP according to the Athlete's existing profile.

**Balanced:** Distribute SP evenly among the Attribute's statistics.

**Manual:** Let the player choose where each SP goes.

Automatic should preserve the specialization selected during Base Athlete creation. Balanced would be an explicit choice to move toward a more rounded profile.

This is a meaningful distinction: a player who created an explosive jumper can continue developing explosiveness automatically, without repeatedly opening the granular allocation interface.

## 4. The UI is conceptually sound

Your proposed layout could work as follows:

```text
MB  |  LEVEL 5  |  2 AP AVAILABLE

POWER       67  [-] [+]
SPEED       63  [-] [+]
ACCURACY    61  [-] [+]
ENDURANCE   72  [-] [+]

TRAIT TREE
```

Selecting or expanding END could reveal:

```text
ENDURANCE 72 → 73

3 STATISTICAL POINTS

  Stamina Capacity       76  [+]
  Exertion Efficiency    70  [+]
  Stamina Recovery       70  [+]

[AUTO]  [BALANCED]  [CONFIRM]
```

And the Trait Tree:

```text
TRAIT TREE

POWER       67
SPEED       63
ACCURACY    61
ENDURANCE   73

Available Traits
  ● Trait A — Requirements met
  ● Trait B — Requirements met

Locked Traits
  ○ Trait C — Requires END 80
  ○ Trait D — Requires POW 75
```

The key UI improvement I'd make is **keeping Attribute allocation and Trait selection separate, but accessible from the same screen**.

The Trait Tree should always show current Attribute ratings, available claims, and the effects of any pending allocations.

For example, hovering over +1 END could highlight newly available Traits before the player commits.

That makes leveling a strategic decision rather than a sequence of disconnected menus.

## 5. The bigger concern: specialization may undermine Attribute identity

Consider two Athletes:

| | Athlete A | Athlete B |
|---|---:|---:|
| END | 80 | 80 |
| Stamina Capacity | 90 | 70 |
| Exertion Efficiency | 70 | 90 |
| Stamina Recovery | 80 | 80 |

These athletes have the same END rating but different Stamina economies.

That's desirable.

However, suppose Athlete B can jump significantly more often because Exertion Efficiency is overwhelmingly more valuable than Capacity.

Then END becomes a misleading indicator of jumping sustainability. The actual relevant statistic is Exertion Efficiency, and rational players may allocate nearly everything into it.

This is the principal balancing risk of granular allocation.

I would address it in two ways.

**First, use bounded specialization.** Individual statistics can deviate from their parent Attribute, but only within a defined range. For example, ±10 points initially.

**Second, make the statistics solve genuinely different problems.**

For END:

| Statistic | Main gameplay responsibility |
|---|---|
| Stamina Capacity | How much exertion the Athlete can perform before Exhaustion |
| Exertion Efficiency | How much Stamina each physical action consumes |
| Stamina Recovery | How quickly Stamina returns between actions |

This creates a distinction between burst capacity, sustained efficiency, and recovery.

An MB who frequently jumps several times in rapid succession might value Capacity and Efficiency. A roaming L who repeatedly performs Drills between Rallies might value Recovery.

The precise relationship needs testing because Capacity and Efficiency can easily become mathematically interchangeable over short time horizons.

That is exactly the kind of problem our jumping-economy calibration should investigate.

## 6. How this interacts with positional Innates

The proposed system also strengthens our earlier decision to make **Innates drive positional development**.

An MB's Innate can grant END-oriented growth, while the player determines whether that growth becomes Capacity, Efficiency, or Recovery.

Likewise:

- **S:** ACC growth, customized toward Setting precision, tempo control, or other technical specializations.
- **L:** SPD growth, customized toward acceleration, lateral coverage, or transition movement.
- **OH:** Broad Attribute growth from its Innate, with flexible statistical development.
- **OP:** Greater discretionary AP, allowing unusual Attribute combinations and their corresponding granular specializations.

This creates two independent forms of specialization.

A player chooses **which Attributes develop**, subject to their position's growth profile, and then chooses **how those Attributes manifest**.

It also makes the distinction between OH and OP more substantial.

OH receives broad, relatively reliable development. OP has more freedom to concentrate growth in particular capabilities, including unconventional defensive or utility builds.

Neither needs to be explicitly forbidden from developing in another position's specialty.

## 7. My preferred final structure

I would adopt your proposal with one significant simplification:

**Do not require players to spend Statistical Points individually after every level-up.**

Instead:

1. Leveling grants AP according to the position and Innate.
2. Players allocate AP among the four Attributes.
3. Each Attribute increase automatically generates and distributes SP according to the Athlete's current statistical development preference.
4. Players can expand any Attribute and manually adjust that distribution before confirming.
5. The Trait Tree updates to reflect the resulting Attribute ratings.

This retains the full depth of your proposal but makes the normal leveling interaction only a few clicks.

There is one additional option worth considering: **let players define an automatic statistical growth preference for each Attribute during Base Athlete creation**. That preference can then be changed between rallies.

An Athlete's initial physical profile and their subsequent training emphasis would consequently be distinct. A naturally explosive jumper could choose to develop Stamina Efficiency rather than continuing to maximize Explosiveness.

### Verdict

**Strong design direction, with moderate UI and balancing risks.**

The granular layer adds genuine strategic depth because volleyball performance depends on specific physical capabilities, not just four generic ratings. VWM provides a useful foundation for modeling those distinctions.

I would now establish the complete granular statistics governed by each Attribute, using VWM's existing vocabulary as the starting point, before deciding how many SP each AP should generate.

In particular, we should settle whether **jump height belongs primarily to POW while jump economy belongs to END**, and how those two Attributes interact with the Athlete's fixed height, wingspan, and body proportions. That will determine whether the system produces meaningful physical specialization or merely different numerical routes to the same optimal build.
