### Exchange 055

Conversation turn ID: `a96d539a-c0fe-4a14-85a9-b2b7b6033cab`


#### User

yes. also, limiting the number of granular options and making some of them Traits instead can assist. for example, making Serve Power a Trait instead that requires Power and Accuracy for something like Serve Power +x, instead of making Serve Power a subtrait of Power, reduces the amount of required specialization


#### Assistant

Agreed. This suggests a useful distinction between **fundamental athletic statistics** and **specialized volleyball techniques**.

The granular statistics should describe what an Athlete is physically or technically capable of doing across multiple situations. Traits should describe particular ways an Athlete exploits those capabilities.

That gives us a way to preserve VWM's depth without importing its entire attribute roster into the MOBA.

## 1. Three levels of specialization

| Layer | Function | Example |
|---|---|---|
| **Attribute** | Broad development investment | Power |
| **Granular Statistic** | General capability affected by that investment | Arm Speed |
| **Trait** | Specialized application of one or more capabilities | Powerful Serve |

For example, **Serve Power** doesn't necessarily need to be a separate statistic.

An Athlete's ordinary Serve strength can emerge from their Power, Accuracy, Arm Speed, and other relevant mechanics.

A Trait could then provide a specialized bonus:

**Powerful Serve**  
*Requires POW 75 and ACC 70.*

Increases Serve velocity by X%, potentially at an increased HP or Stamina cost.

That is much more economical than making Serve Power a permanent statistical category that every Athlete must develop.

Importantly, an Athlete without the Trait should still be able to deliver a powerful Serve. The Trait provides a specialized advantage rather than granting access to an otherwise unavailable basic action.

## 2. Proposed granular Attribute roster

I'd initially aim for **three granular statistics per Attribute**, giving us twelve total.

That seems like a manageable compromise between VWM's detailed simulation and the MOBA's faster progression.

| Attribute | Granular statistic | Governs |
|---|---|---|
| **POW** | Explosiveness | Jump height, takeoff force, explosive movements |
| | Arm Speed | Velocity generated through arm actions |
| | Strength | Physical force, resistance to contact disruption |
| **SPD** | Acceleration | How quickly the Athlete reaches movement speed |
| | Lateral Speed | Sideways movement and defensive coverage |
| | Transition Speed | Forward/backward movement and approaches |
| **ACC** | Ball Control | General contact precision and directional control |
| | Timing | Precision of contact windows |
| | Stability | Quality of actions from imperfect body positions |
| **END** | Stamina Capacity | Maximum available Stamina |
| | Exertion Efficiency | Stamina consumed by physical actions |
| | Recovery | Stamina regeneration and recovery from exertion |

This is a provisional roster, not a claim that VWM groups these statistics in exactly this way.

### Why three each?

Twelve statistics are enough to produce meaningfully different Athletes without making every level-up an exercise in distributing points among forty variables.

They also create reasonably intuitive distinctions.

Two Athletes with 75 POW might specialize differently: one in Explosiveness for jumping, another in Arm Speed for striking.

Two Athletes with 75 END might specialize in Capacity versus Efficiency.

And two Athletes with 75 ACC might differ in contact precision versus their ability to execute under difficult positioning.

## 3. What should become Traits?

I would move **action-specific specialization** into Traits rather than granular statistics.

| VWM-style specialization | MOBA representation |
|---|---|
| Serve Power | Power + Accuracy Trait |
| Serve Placement | Accuracy Trait |
| Serve Variation | Accuracy + Speed Trait |
| Set Disguise | Accuracy Trait |
| Tempo Control | Accuracy + Speed Trait |
| Attack Tooling | Power + Accuracy Trait |
| Shot Variety | Accuracy Trait |
| Reception Stability | Accuracy + Endurance Trait |
| Block Anticipation | Speed + Accuracy Trait |
| Repeated Jumping Technique | Power + Endurance Trait |

These are examples of how to categorize capabilities, not finalized Trait designs.

Some might eventually become upgrades to existing Traits rather than separate choices.

I would also distinguish **numerical Traits** from **mechanical Traits**.

A numerical Trait might increase Serve velocity or reduce the HP cost of a particular action.

A mechanical Trait might change the available Serve trajectory, introduce a new attack technique, or allow a different kind of Block.

Both have a place, but the more complex mechanical Traits should generally require higher Attributes or Athlete levels.

## 4. Traits can connect Attributes that otherwise remain separate

This is one of the stronger consequences of your suggestion.

Without Traits, an Athlete investing in Power and Accuracy gains two independent sets of improvements.

With Traits, particular combinations can unlock specialized applications.

For example:

| Attribute combination | Potential Trait specialization |
|---|---|
| POW + ACC | Powerful Serve, precision attacking |
| POW + SPD | Explosive approaches, aggressive transitions |
| POW + END | Repeated jumping, sustained hitting |
| ACC + SPD | Quick Setting, reactive ball control |
| ACC + END | Stable Receiving, reliable extended exchanges |
| SPD + END | Persistent coverage, sustained movement |

These pairings create opportunities for hybrid builds.

An OP investing in POW and ACC might develop into a dangerous Server. An OH investing in SPD and END might become unusually effective at sustained defense.

The Trait Tree makes these possibilities visible without requiring position-specific ability restrictions.

**However, I wouldn't require two Attributes for every Trait.** Single-Attribute Traits should remain common, especially at lower levels. Otherwise, specialization becomes excessively expensive and players are pressured into similar balanced builds.

## 5. One important constraint: avoid turning Traits into mandatory stat corrections

Suppose Powerful Serve simply grants +20% Serve velocity with no drawback.

If Serving is important enough, virtually every POW/ACC Athlete will take it.

That isn't necessarily a problem for an introductory Trait, but it creates little strategic differentiation.

I'd prefer Traits that emphasize particular applications or tradeoffs.

For example:

**Powerful Serve — introductory numerical Trait**

Requires POW 70, ACC 65.

Increases Serve velocity moderately, but increases its HP expenditure.

**Jump Serve — advanced mechanical Trait**

Requires POW 80, ACC 75, Level 9.

Allows a more aggressive Serve technique with additional vertical and directional possibilities, at substantial Stamina cost.

The first Trait is simple and accessible during early development. The second changes the available gameplay options.

These examples also demonstrate why **Trait complexity and Trait power should be evaluated separately**. A straightforward numerical Trait can be very powerful, while a mechanically complex Trait might offer flexibility rather than greater raw effectiveness.

## 6. The resulting development model

The complete progression structure now looks like this:

**Base Athlete Build**
- Choose height and body proportions.
- Allocate starting Attribute AP.
- Customize the underlying granular statistical distribution.
- Purchase optional Innates.
- Select eligible introductory Traits.

**In-match progression**
- Gain AP through leveling.
- Invest AP in POW, SPD, ACC, or END.
- Automatically or manually distribute the resulting Statistical Points.
- Unlock additional Traits through Attribute thresholds and level requirements.
- Claim Traits using the existing limited Trait slots.

**Physical performance**
- Body geometry determines reach and influences movement mechanics.
- Granular statistics determine general athletic capabilities.
- Traits specialize how those capabilities are used.
- Positional Innates establish distinctive efficiencies and development patterns.

### The principle I'd establish

**A granular statistic should exist only when it affects multiple meaningful actions or situations. A Trait should exist when the benefit is specific to a technique, action, or playstyle.**

Serve Power is a good example: it is too narrow to justify a permanent substat when Arm Speed and other general statistics already explain much of its underlying performance.

This also means we should be willing to remove granular statistics that fail the same test. For example, if Transition Speed only meaningfully affects approaching the Net, it might be better represented by a movement Trait or derived from Acceleration and general Speed.

I would use this criterion to audit the proposed twelve-stat roster before treating it as established.

#### User

yes agree with this and your stat roster, although i'd like to find something other than arm speed for power's 2nd slot. maybe Explosiveness, Jump Power, Swing Power? to slightly mirror SPD's layout of acceleration and two different facets for it?


#### Assistant

I prefer **Explosiveness / Jump Power / Swing Power**. That creates a cleaner division within POW, although we'd need to define Explosiveness carefully to prevent overlap with Jump Power.

It also establishes a useful parallel with SPD:

| Power | Speed |
|---|---|
| **Explosiveness** | **Acceleration** |
| Jump Power | Lateral Speed |
| Swing Power | Transition Speed |

Both Attributes have one general-purpose statistic and two more specialized expressions.

### Proposed POW statistics

| Statistic | Governs | Does not govern |
|---|---|---|
| **Explosiveness** | How rapidly an Athlete generates force during explosive actions | Maximum jump height or maximum hitting force independently |
| **Jump Power** | Vertical displacement and maximum jumping capability | Takeoff speed independently |
| **Swing Power** | Force transferred to the ball through attacking and serving motions | Contact precision or shot placement |

This creates three distinguishable Athletes even when all have 75 POW.

**Athlete A — Explosive (85 / 70 / 70)**

Reaches takeoff more quickly, enabling reactive Blocks and late approaches. Their maximum jump height and hitting force are comparatively modest.

**Athlete B — Vertical (70 / 85 / 70)**

Jumps higher, giving them a larger vertical contact envelope. However, they require more preparation to reach that height.

**Athlete C — Powerful hitter (70 / 70 / 85)**

Produces greater ball velocity through Attacks and Serves but lacks exceptional jumping ability.

These profiles illustrate how the granular system can create specialization without requiring a Trait for every physical distinction.

### Two mechanical considerations

**1. Explosiveness should not duplicate Acceleration.**

I'd distinguish them by the type of movement:

- **Acceleration (SPD):** How quickly an Athlete builds horizontal movement velocity.
- **Explosiveness (POW):** How quickly an Athlete generates force during jumping and striking actions.

That makes Explosiveness particularly useful for Block timing, quick Attacks, and potentially certain defensive movements.

One complication is Swing Power: striking force also depends on how quickly the arm accelerates. I'd therefore let Explosiveness contribute modestly to the time required to execute a swing, while Swing Power determines its resulting force.

**2. Jump Power should not automatically improve jumping economy.**

Jump Power determines the Athlete's jumping capability. END determines how sustainably they can use it.

An Athlete with exceptional Jump Power but poor END can jump very high, but may struggle to repeat those jumps.

An Athlete with moderate Jump Power and exceptional END can jump repeatedly without necessarily reaching the same height.

There may be some physical interaction between Jump Power, body geometry, and Stamina expenditure, but I wouldn't make high Jump Power inherently more efficient.

### Updated roster

| Attribute | Statistic 1 | Statistic 2 | Statistic 3 |
|---|---|---|---|
| **POW** | Explosiveness | Jump Power | Swing Power |
| **SPD** | Acceleration | Lateral Speed | Transition Speed |
| **ACC** | Ball Control | Timing | Stability |
| **END** | Stamina Capacity | Exertion Efficiency | Recovery |

I think this is a stronger twelve-stat roster than the previous version.

There is one possible refinement worth considering: **Explosiveness might eventually deserve the name *Quickness***, provided we define it specifically as explosive-action execution speed rather than general movement. However, *Explosiveness* better conveys the physical mechanism, and I would retain it provisionally.

The resulting POW structure is particularly useful for our jumping calibration: **Explosiveness determines how quickly an Athlete gets airborne, Jump Power determines how high they can jump, and END determines how often they can afford to do it.**

#### Assistant

I think the distinction between **Shoot Set, Quick Step, and Back Set** is defensible, but I would change how you're framing their availability.

The strongest part of the proposal is that these three Traits address different problems:

| Trait | Problem it solves | Natural timing |
|---|---|---|
| **Quick Step** | Getting into position and converting between actions | Early |
| **Shoot Set** | Delivering a fast offensive opportunity before defenders can organize | Early, but specialized |
| **Back Set** | Concealing offensive direction and distributing attacks among multiple threats | Midgame |

This is preferable to a conventional progression where Quick Step is simply a weaker Setting Trait that eventually upgrades into Shoot Set or Back Set.

They aren't really competing versions of the same ability. They support different playstyles.

## 1. Quick Step should probably be available to everyone

I'd interpret Quick Step as an improvement to the transition between movement and a volleyball action, rather than a Setting-specific mechanic.

For example:

**Quick Step — Introductory Trait**

*After completing a volleyball action, briefly accelerate toward the next movement input, with reduced transition delay.*

Its applications differ by position:

- **Setter:** Receive → reposition → Set, or Set → defensive coverage.
- **Outside Hitter:** Receive → approach → Attack.
- **Middle Blocker:** Block → reposition → Block.
- **Libero:** Receive → transition toward another defensive opportunity.
- **Opposite:** Attack → recover → defensive position.

This makes Quick Step a general-purpose early Trait with particularly valuable Setting applications.

However, I would avoid making it a universal improvement to movement speed. That risks turning it into a mandatory selection.

It should specifically reward **action transitions**, with an effect that depends on timing and player input.

A possible requirement would be SPD 65, with higher SPD improving its effectiveness naturally.

That is accessible to most Athletes without requiring significant specialization.

## 2. Shoot Set can justify a high early Accuracy requirement

Shoot Set has a different role.

It allows an Athlete to deliver a faster, flatter Set that creates an immediate offensive opportunity.

That is particularly valuable in early Rallies because it compresses the opponent's reaction window.

I would make it available at Level 1, but require substantial ACC investment.

For example:

**Shoot Set — Introductory Trait**

*Requires ACC 75.*

Allows a fast, low-arc Set with a shorter travel time, but a narrower acceptable contact window for the intended attacker.

The benefit is offensive tempo. The tradeoff is reduced forgiveness.

This also creates a meaningful Base Athlete choice:

An Athlete could enter the match with 75 ACC and Shoot Set, but would necessarily have fewer points invested elsewhere.

A more balanced Athlete might instead take Quick Step and develop their Accuracy over time.

One important caveat: a high ACC threshold only makes sense if the Base Athlete budget actually permits reaching it. Under our provisional 60-per-Attribute baseline and 20 AP budget, ACC 75 would consume 15 of the 20 AP, leaving little room for other improvements.

That is severe specialization, which may be appropriate, but it needs to be intentional.

## 3. Back Set should be a midgame option, but not necessarily a high-tier Trait

I agree with the underlying observation: Back Set becomes more valuable as Rallies involve more Athletes and attackers can threaten from multiple directions.

However, I would distinguish **when a Trait becomes useful** from **when it becomes available**.

Back Set is mechanically straightforward. It doesn't necessarily need a very high Attribute requirement. Its value comes from the offensive formations it enables.

That suggests something like:

**Back Set — Intermediate Trait**

*Requires ACC 70 and Level 5.*

Allows the Setter to direct the ball behind their facing direction, opening additional attacking angles and concealing the intended recipient.

This is a provisional threshold. The important part is that the Trait is available relatively early but not immediately.

I would also question whether Back Set should be indispensable. If every competitive Setter eventually needs it, we're effectively charging a Trait slot for a mandatory piece of the position's basic functionality.

There are two ways to avoid that:

**Option A — Back Set as a specialized technique.** All Athletes can make backward-directed Sets, but the basic version is slow, telegraphed, or difficult. The Trait improves their execution and deception.

**Option B — Back Set as a genuinely new action.** The Trait grants the ability to Set backward, but other offensive strategies remain competitive without it.

I prefer **Option A**.

It preserves the freedom of the underlying volleyball system. An Athlete doesn't suddenly become physically incapable of redirecting a ball backward because they haven't purchased a Trait.

Instead, Back Set represents mastery of that technique.

That is also consistent with the distinction we've established between fundamental capabilities and specialized applications.

---

## 4. The more important question: should Base Athlete Traits compete with starting Attributes?

**Yes. I think this is worth implementing.**

It creates a meaningful distinction between an Athlete who begins with specialized techniques and one who begins with superior general capabilities.

However, I would reject the specific idea of making a Trait intrinsically more expensive when acquired during a match.

That introduces two different prices for the same capability, which can be difficult to explain and balance.

More importantly, it doesn't necessarily solve the problem you're concerned about.

### The problem

Suppose two Athletes have 20 Base Athlete AP.

| | Athlete A | Athlete B |
|---|---|---|
| Base Attribute investment | 15 AP | 20 AP |
| Base Trait investment | 5 AP | 0 AP |
| Starting Trait | Shoot Set | None |
| Starting advantage | Specialized Setting technique | Higher Attributes |

Athlete A has sacrificed general performance for immediate access to Shoot Set.

Athlete B begins with stronger Attributes.

If Athlete B can acquire Shoot Set after one level, Athlete A's sacrifice may feel pointless.

But the correct response isn't necessarily to impose an arbitrary surcharge on Athlete B.

We should ask **what the early acquisition actually buys**.

Athlete A has Shoot Set during the opening period, potentially enabling a strategy that Athlete B cannot execute.

That is a real advantage, provided the opening period is long enough and Shoot Set is useful enough.

Athlete B has better Attributes throughout that same period and retains them afterward.

This is already a legitimate tradeoff.

The concern arises if Athlete A's initial advantage lasts only a few seconds before Athlete B obtains the same Trait.

### My preferred solution: acquisition timing

I would separate **starting Trait eligibility** from **in-match Trait eligibility**.

For example:

| Trait | Base Athlete acquisition | In-match acquisition |
|---|---|---|
| Quick Step | Available at creation | Level 3+ |
| Shoot Set | Available at creation with ACC 75 | Level 5+ with ACC 75 |
| Back Set | Unavailable | Level 5+ with ACC 70 |

The numbers are illustrative.

This creates an **early-access premium** without requiring the same Trait to have two different AP prices.

The player choosing Shoot Set at creation purchases access to the technique during the opening phase.

The player choosing superior Attributes can eventually obtain Shoot Set, but cannot reproduce that opening advantage.

That seems cleaner.

There is one potential objection: players might perceive an arbitrary level restriction as artificial. I think that is acceptable if we explain it through the distinction between **pretrained techniques** and **techniques developed during the match**.

The Base Athlete can enter with a technique already learned. An Athlete who didn't train it beforehand needs time to develop it.

---

## 5. But there's a stronger alternative: Traits as an investment of AP

We should consider whether Trait acquisition needs a separate currency at all.

Our current framework already contains:

- Attribute Points
- Statistical Points generated by Attribute growth
- Trait claims
- Base Athlete Innates

Adding another system of Trait costs could make progression unnecessarily complicated.

Instead, perhaps **Traits consume AP directly**.

That would produce the tradeoff you're describing without introducing a new resource.

Suppose:

**Quick Step**
- Requires SPD 65.
- Costs 3 AP.

**Shoot Set**
- Requires ACC 75.
- Costs 5 AP.

**Back Set**
- Requires ACC 70.
- Costs 4 AP.

Now the decision is straightforward.

A player can improve their Attributes or purchase a Trait.

Both options compete for the same development budget.

The advantage is that the cost remains meaningful throughout the match. A player acquiring Shoot Set at Level 5 must sacrifice five AP that could otherwise improve their Attributes.

There is no need for a special in-match surcharge.

### One issue with this approach

Our current Attribute progression has escalating costs above 80 and 90.

Consequently, the opportunity cost of spending five AP on a Trait changes depending on the Athlete's current Attributes.

At low ratings, five AP could purchase five Attribute increases.

At high ratings, five AP might purchase only one or two increases.

This could make Traits increasingly attractive later in the match, which may actually be desirable.

However, it also means the system needs careful calibration to prevent players from delaying every Trait purchase until Attribute growth becomes expensive.

I wouldn't settle that yet.

---

## 6. I would not require every Athlete to start with a Trait

This is a particularly important part of your proposal.

An Athlete who invests their entire starting budget into Attributes should be a legitimate build.

Not a beginner build. Not an incomplete build.

A legitimate strategy.

We could establish three broad opening approaches:

| Opening strategy | Investment | Strength | Weakness |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Generalist** | Primarily Attributes | Strong baseline performance | Few specialized techniques |
| **Specialist** | Attributes + early Trait | Immediate tactical identity | Lower general capability |
| **Developmental** | Attributes chosen to approach future Trait thresholds | Strong progression potential | Limited immediate specialization |

The third category is interesting because it rewards planning.

An Athlete might enter with ACC 72, intending to reach ACC 75 and acquire Shoot Set at Level 5.

Another might enter with ACC 75 and Shoot Set already available, sacrificing broader Attributes.

Both have coherent progression plans.

The key balancing question becomes whether the specialist's early advantage compensates for the Attribute investment they sacrificed.

---

## 7. My proposed initial Setting Trait structure

| Trait | Early availability | Primary requirement | Strategic identity |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Quick Step** | Base Athlete or early level | Low SPD | Faster action transitions |
| **Shoot Set** | Base Athlete, high specialization | High ACC | Fast offensive tempo |
| **Back Set** | Midgame | Moderate ACC | Offensive direction and deception |

I would keep these as **independent Traits**, not a single branching progression.

That allows a Setter to combine Quick Step with Shoot Set, Quick Step with Back Set, or Shoot Set with Back Set.

The combinations are potentially more interesting than a linear upgrade tree.

### One concern about Shoot Set

I would not make Shoot Set so powerful that a team with an early specialized Setter can consistently win Rallies before the opposition has meaningful defensive options.

It should be a strong harassment tool, but one requiring coordination.

A fast Set is only valuable if another Athlete can reach and convert it.

That creates a natural early limitation: even if Shoot Set is available at Level 1, the team may not yet possess the movement, jumping, or offensive tools needed to exploit it consistently.

As the match develops, Shoot Set becomes easier to capitalize on.

This is a better balancing mechanism than simply making the Trait weak at low levels.

---

## Recommendation

I would provisionally adopt three principles:

**1. Base Athlete Traits compete with starting Attribute investment.** An Athlete can choose to enter the match with no Traits and superior Attributes.

**2. Pre-match acquisition can grant earlier access than in-match acquisition.** This protects the strategic value of investing in a Trait before the match without requiring arbitrary price differences.

**3. Traits should generally remain independently selectable.** Quick Step, Shoot Set, and Back Set support different strategies rather than representing three levels of the same technique.

The remaining question is whether **Traits should consume AP directly or use separate Trait claims**.

I lean toward **AP costs**, because that makes your proposed choice between starting Attributes and starting Traits a fundamental part of Athlete construction rather than an exception.

But I would not finalize that until we examine the existing Trait claim structure. A separate claim limit may still be necessary to prevent Athletes from eventually accumulating every useful technique.

i think i need to just reframe the issue once again. at Athlete creation, the decision dichotomy is whether to take Innate Traits, OR, Attribute growth. if Innate Traits are taken, then the Athlete gains benefits that cannot be reproduced by other non-investing Athletes *whatsoever*. the tradeoff is that another Athlete could have already invested into key Traits BECAUSE they took those attributes. whether or not a player has access to quick step is not the issue at hand.


#### Assistant

That changes the framework substantially. I was conflating **Innate Traits** with **Ability Traits**, when they serve different purposes and belong to different progression systems.

The actual Base Athlete decision is:

**Do I sacrifice starting Attribute development to acquire permanent, exclusive properties of my Athlete, or do I invest those points into Attributes and gain earlier access to Ability Traits?**

This is a stronger distinction than the early-access model I proposed.

## 1. The corrected distinction

| | Innate Traits | Ability Traits |
|---|---|---|
| Acquired | During Athlete creation | Through Attribute requirements and progression |
| Cost | Base Athlete AP | Trait selection/claim, subject to eligibility |
| Available to an Athlete who didn't select them at creation? | **No** | Yes, upon meeting requirements |
| Purpose | Define unique properties of the Athlete | Specialize how the Athlete plays volleyball |
| Tradeoff | Sacrifice starting Attribute investment | Invest in the Attributes necessary to unlock them |

Crucially, **Innate Traits cannot be purchased later**.

An Athlete who begins without an Innate might eventually develop stronger Attributes and obtain more Ability Traits, but they will never acquire that Innate's unique capability.

Conversely, an Athlete who purchases an Innate has permanently sacrificed some initial Attribute development. They can still pursue Ability Traits, but may reach their requirements later.

## 2. The meaningful opening tradeoff

Consider two Athletes built from the same starting AP budget.

**Athlete A — Innate specialist**

Spends part of the budget on an Innate that fundamentally changes their capabilities.

As a consequence, they begin with lower Attributes and might not qualify for Shoot Set or another early Ability Trait.

**Athlete B — Attribute specialist**

Spends the entire budget developing Attributes.

They cannot reproduce Athlete A's Innate, but they may begin with enough ACC to qualify for Shoot Set, or enough SPD to access another early Ability Trait.

The distinction is not that Athlete B eventually catches up to Athlete A.

**They develop along different axes, and neither can fully reproduce the other's opening configuration.**

Athlete A possesses something exclusive.

Athlete B has a developmental head start.

That is the actual design tension.

## 3. What this means for the Setting Traits

We can now evaluate Quick Step, Shoot Set, and Back Set without involving Innate acquisition costs.

| Ability Trait | Intended availability | Attribute requirements |
|---|---|---|
| **Quick Step** | Early, broadly accessible | Modest SPD |
| **Shoot Set** | Early, specialized | High ACC |
| **Back Set** | Midgame, broadly approachable | Moderate ACC and a level requirement |

These are **Ability Traits**, not Innates.

An Athlete who spends heavily on Innates may be unable to qualify for Shoot Set at Level 1.

An Athlete who skips Innates can concentrate their starting AP into ACC and potentially unlock Shoot Set immediately.

Neither Athlete is necessarily stronger. One has unique capabilities, while the other can begin with more specialized volleyball techniques.

Importantly, there's no need to make Shoot Set more expensive during a match, nor to impose an artificial delay on acquiring it.

Its Attribute requirement already creates the relevant opportunity cost.

## 4. One remaining distinction worth preserving

I would separate three concepts:

**Innate Traits:** Exclusive Athlete properties, purchased during creation.

**Ability Traits:** Volleyball techniques and gameplay modifications unlocked through Attribute investment.

**Positional Innates:** Free, mandatory properties of the Athlete's selected position, including its established Attribute growth pattern.

This keeps the three systems from competing for the same conceptual space.

It also clarifies the role of the granular statistics: they customize how an Athlete performs, but they do not substitute for an Innate's exclusive mechanic.

### The resulting progression structure

At Athlete creation, players distribute a finite AP budget between **Innate Traits and starting Attributes**. Their Attributes determine which introductory Ability Traits they qualify for.

During the match, they gain AP, improve Attributes and granular statistics, and qualify for additional Ability Traits. They cannot acquire new Innate Traits.

That is the framework I would use going forward.

The next useful design question is therefore not how much Quick Step costs. It's **how much starting Attribute development an Innate should sacrifice**, and whether that sacrifice creates a meaningful difference in the introductory Ability Traits available to each Athlete.

### Exchange 059

Conversation turn ID: `cef59a16-af51-4070-952e-45dafed5d55c`


#### User

yes. so we were first discussing what early Traits should be unlockable, but now we have to determine that in tandem with what Innate Traits can be invested into that reasonably cost the opportunity to have those early Traits at an early stage?


#### Assistant

Yes. **Early Ability Traits and purchasable Innate Traits should be designed as a single balancing problem**, because they compete indirectly through the Athlete's starting AP budget.

The goal is not to make Innates and Ability Traits equally powerful in isolation. It's to ensure that the starting Attribute investment sacrificed for an Innate would otherwise have enabled a meaningful early-game advantage.

This suggests designing **opening build packages**, rather than independently inventing two Trait rosters and assigning costs afterward.

## 1. Establish the opportunity-cost relationship

Consider three possible Level 1 Athletes:

| Build | Starting investment | Opening advantage |
|---|---|---|
| **Innate-focused** | Expensive Innate, fewer Attributes | Exclusive mechanic unavailable to other Athletes |
| **Ability-focused** | High investment in one Attribute | Immediate access to a specialized Ability Trait |
| **Balanced** | Modest Innate or no Innate, distributed Attributes | General effectiveness and flexible progression |

The third build matters. We shouldn't inadvertently make choosing either an Innate or a specialized Ability Trait mandatory.

The ideal relationship is that an Innate-focused Athlete can still perform their position's basic responsibilities, while an Ability-focused Athlete can exploit their early specialization without possessing the Innate's exclusive advantages.

## 2. Design the early Ability Trait roster first—but evaluate Innates alongside it

I'd establish a modest introductory Ability Trait portfolio across the four Attributes.

Rather than assigning exact requirements immediately, we can identify the kinds of advantages available to Athletes who prioritize each Attribute.

| Attribute investment | Candidate early Ability Traits | Opening advantage |
|---|---|---|
| **POW** | Powerful Serve, Quick Attack | Stronger offensive pressure |
| **SPD** | Quick Step, Rapid Recovery | Better transitions and positioning |
| **ACC** | Shoot Set, Precision Receive | More reliable or specialized ball handling |
| **END** | Second Wind, Sustained Block | Greater endurance through repeated exchanges |

These are candidates, not finalized Traits. In particular, Rapid Recovery and Second Wind may overlap too much with the underlying granular statistics and need stronger mechanical identities.

I'd also include a few low-threshold hybrid Traits, but avoid making every early option depend on two Attributes.

For Setting, we already have a useful distinction:

**Quick Step** should be relatively accessible and support movement-to-action transitions.

**Shoot Set** should demand substantial ACC investment and reward early offensive coordination.

**Back Set** should remain outside the introductory roster, becoming accessible as larger Rallies develop.

That gives us two early Setting-adjacent choices with different Attribute demands, plus a later tactical expansion.

## 3. Innates need exclusive mechanics, not superior statistics

This is the more difficult side of the problem.

If an Innate simply grants +5 Jump Power, it isn't truly exclusive. Another Athlete can reproduce that benefit through ordinary Attribute development.

Even if the bonus exceeds a normal cap, it's still primarily a numerical advantage.

I'd instead look for Innates that **change the rules under which an Athlete operates**.

For example:

| Candidate Innate | Exclusive property | Potential opening tradeoff |
|---|---|---|
| **Ambidextrous** | Can execute attacks from either hand with equivalent proficiency | Sacrifices early POW/ACC specialization |
| **Natural Reach** | Unusually long arms relative to height, beyond the normal body-adjustment range | Sacrifices early Jump Power or other Attribute investment |
| **Spring-Loaded** | Can perform a distinct rapid second jump under specified conditions | Sacrifices early END/POW development |
| **Soft Hands** | Can perform a specialized controlled touch unavailable to other Athletes | Sacrifices early ACC specialization |
| **Instinctive Recovery** | Gains a unique recovery opportunity after an otherwise committed action | Sacrifices early SPD/END development |

These are conceptual examples. They need much tighter mechanical definitions before adoption.

I particularly like **Ambidextrous** and **Natural Reach** as starting points because they are easy to understand and create enduring differences between Athletes.

Spring-Loaded and Instinctive Recovery are more dangerous: they might invalidate fundamental action-commitment or Stamina constraints.

Soft Hands also risks becoming an Ability Trait rather than an Innate unless its exclusive property is clearly defined.

### An important distinction for physical Innates

Since we've agreed that height and proportions are configurable during Athlete creation, an Innate such as Natural Reach should extend the normal morphology rules rather than duplicate a free body customization option.

For instance, ordinary Athletes could select plausible wingspan variations within their height's normal range.

Natural Reach could permit a longer wingspan than the ordinary range, with the corresponding changes to contact geometry.

That is genuinely exclusive without introducing a completely separate movement system.

## 4. Balance the pairings, not the individual Traits

Take an illustrative opening choice:

**Athlete A: Natural Reach**

Invests starting AP in an Innate that extends their standing reach.

They might begin with moderate POW, ACC, SPD, and END.

**Athlete B: Shoot Set**

Forgoes Innates, invests heavily in ACC, and qualifies for Shoot Set immediately.

Athlete A can access contacts Athlete B cannot physically reach.

Athlete B can execute a specialized Setting technique Athlete A may not yet qualify for.

Neither advantage directly replaces the other.

This is the kind of tradeoff we should seek.

But we must also test **same-position comparisons**, not just different roles.

A Natural Reach Setter and a Shoot Set Setter should represent genuinely different opening strategies.

Likewise, Natural Reach should have useful but different implications for an MB, OH, OP, and L.

An Innate that is overwhelmingly valuable for only one position may effectively become a mandatory positional selection.

## 5. The numerical economy should come after identifying these alternatives

We currently have several provisional numerical assumptions, including the 65 OVR starting target and escalating Attribute costs.

Before setting Innate prices, I would define a small set of opening build comparisons.

For example:

| Opening build | Intended defining advantage |
|---|---|
| No Innate, balanced Attributes | General competence |
| No Innate, high ACC | Shoot Set |
| No Innate, high SPD | Stronger Quick Step access or superior transitions |
| Ambidextrous, balanced Attributes | Unique attacking flexibility |
| Natural Reach, balanced Attributes | Expanded contact geometry |
| Lower-cost Innate, moderate specialization | Some exclusivity plus an introductory Ability Trait |

Then we can determine the minimum starting AP budget and the Innate costs needed to make all six builds possible without allowing every Athlete to take everything.

One particularly useful balancing target would be:

**An expensive Innate should generally cost enough starting AP to prevent immediate access to at least one high-investment introductory Ability Trait, but not enough to make the Athlete broadly ineffective.**

That doesn't mean every Innate needs the same price. Smaller, more situational Innates could coexist with low-requirement Ability Traits.

## 6. The most important design test

For each proposed Innate, ask:

> Would an Athlete who declined this Innate, invested the saved AP into Attributes, and acquired an introductory Ability Trait have a comparably interesting opening strategy?

If the answer is no, we should question the Innate's design or cost.

And the reverse matters equally:

> Does the Innate offer something sufficiently distinctive that an Athlete might willingly delay Shoot Set, Quick Step, or another early Ability Trait to obtain it?

If not, the Innate probably isn't worth purchasing.

### Where I'd start

I'd begin with a **small provisional Innate roster of approximately six to eight options**, alongside perhaps eight to twelve introductory Ability Traits spanning POW, SPD, ACC, and END.

But I wouldn't try to fill those quotas immediately.

Instead, I'd start with **Ambidextrous, an extended-reach Innate, Quick Step, and Shoot Set** as our first comparison group. Those four already represent different kinds of advantages: exclusive technique flexibility, exclusive physical geometry, accessible mobility, and specialized Setting.

Once we establish how much starting Attribute investment each should reasonably compete with, we can use that relationship to design the rest of the opening roster.

The important shift is that we're no longer asking whether an Innate is powerful enough on its own. We're asking whether **its permanent exclusivity is worth the early developmental opportunities it displaces**.

### Exchange 060

#### User

Innate Roster:
Giant/Tiny: Sacrifice the most AP in exchange for exceptional physical qualities, intrinsically modifying calculations about power, stamina, reach, and speed. This suggests that physical capabilities must not be determined by attribute profile alone - the physical build of the athlete MUST be taken into consideration as well


#### Assistant

**Giant/Tiny is a strong candidate for the highest-cost Innate category.** More importantly, it establishes a fundamental rule for the Athlete system:

**Attributes describe athletic capability, while physique determines how that capability manifests physically.**

Two Athletes with identical POW, SPD, ACC, and END should not necessarily produce identical movement, jumping, or contact outcomes if their bodies are substantially different.

This should apply to *all* Athletes, not just Giant and Tiny. Those Innates extend the physical variation beyond the ordinary range.

## 1. Giant and Tiny as exceptional physique Innates

I'd treat these as two mutually exclusive Innates that modify the Athlete's available body geometry.

| Property | Giant | Tiny |
|---|---|---|
| Height | Exceptionally tall | Exceptionally short |
| Standing reach | Substantially greater | Substantially smaller |
| Contact coverage | Greater vertical and potentially horizontal envelope | Smaller contact envelope |
| Body mass | Generally greater | Generally lower |
| Acceleration | Potential disadvantage | Potential advantage |
| Movement agility | Potential disadvantage | Potential advantage |
| Jumping | Greater standing reach, but potentially less vertical displacement | Lower standing reach, but potentially greater vertical displacement |
| Stamina economy | Potentially higher exertion costs | Potentially lower exertion costs |
| Striking force | Greater potential force transfer | Lower potential force transfer |

These are proposed mechanical tendencies, not universal physiological rules. In particular, body mass should not automatically determine movement speed, and height should not independently dictate stamina efficiency.

The critical requirement is that **Giant and Tiny change the physical equations**, rather than simply granting fixed Attribute bonuses and penalties.

### Giant

A Giant might have exceptional standing reach, allowing them to contact balls and establish Blocks at heights unavailable to ordinary Athletes.

However, that reach comes with consequences for movement, jumping, and exertion.

A Giant with high SPD can still be fast. Their physique should influence how that SPD translates into movement, not prevent them from investing in it.

### Tiny

A Tiny Athlete might have a smaller contact envelope but benefit from quicker changes in direction, lower exertion requirements for certain movements, or faster recovery from particular physical actions.

A Tiny Athlete with high POW can still be a powerful hitter. Their physique changes how they generate and deliver that power.

This is important because otherwise Giant and Tiny become disguised positional restrictions: Giant for MB, Tiny for L.

We should preserve the possibility of unconventional but effective builds.

---

## 2. The physical calculation model

I would establish three interacting inputs:

**Physical outcome = f(Attribute statistics, body geometry, action circumstances)**

For example:

### Maximum contact height

A simplified model might be:

\[
\text{Contact Height} =
\text{Standing Reach} +
\text{Vertical Displacement}
\]

Standing Reach depends on height, arm length, and posture.

Vertical Displacement depends on Jump Power, Explosiveness, body mass, approach quality, and current Stamina.

Consequently, a Giant with moderate Jump Power might reach higher than a Tiny Athlete with excellent Jump Power.

But the Tiny Athlete could potentially jump more quickly or more frequently.

### Movement

Movement should similarly depend on:

- Acceleration and movement-speed statistics.
- Stride length and body proportions.
- Body mass and changes in direction.
- Current Stamina and movement state.

A Giant and Tiny Athlete with identical SPD could therefore have different acceleration curves and movement characteristics.

### Striking force

Swing Power should determine an Athlete's ability to generate force, but its realized effect should also depend on arm geometry, body mass, contact quality, and approach mechanics.

This gives Giant and Tiny meaningful offensive differences without directly granting either a flat POW modifier.

---

## 3. The important balancing consequence

Giant and Tiny should not be evaluated exclusively against each other.

They should be evaluated against the **ordinary Athlete who spent the same starting AP on Attributes instead**.

For example:

| | Giant | Ordinary Athlete |
|---|---|---|
| Innate investment | Very high | None |
| Starting Attributes | Lower | Higher |
| Standing reach | Exceptional | Normal |
| Early Ability Traits | Fewer likely available | More likely available |
| Movement profile | Physique-dependent | Physique-dependent |
| Long-term advantage | Exclusive body geometry | Earlier Attribute development |

The ordinary Athlete cannot eventually purchase Giant's reach.

The Giant cannot retroactively recover the Attribute development sacrificed during Athlete creation, although subsequent leveling may reduce the numerical difference.

That is precisely the kind of enduring tradeoff we want.

**However, Giant and Tiny need sufficiently consequential disadvantages that they don't become automatic choices for particular positions.** Their high AP costs help, but AP cost alone is not a substitute for physical tradeoffs.

---

## 4. One issue we should resolve: height outside the normal range

We previously established that ordinary Athletes can freely select heights from **5'10" to 6'2"**.

Giant and Tiny naturally extend this range.

I would provisionally structure it as follows:

| Physique | Height range | Cost |
|---|---|---|
| Ordinary | 5'10"–6'2" | Free |
| Giant | Above 6'2", within a defined exceptional range | High Innate AP cost |
| Tiny | Below 5'10", within a defined exceptional range | High Innate AP cost |

I would **not** finalize the exceptional ranges yet.

The distinction between 6'2" and 6'3" is unlikely to justify a substantial Innate cost on its own.

Instead, Giant and Tiny should provide access to genuinely exceptional body configurations. We might ultimately want a minimum magnitude of deviation before either Innate becomes available.

This also raises a possibility worth considering: Giant and Tiny could have adjustable heights within their respective exceptional ranges, with increasingly extreme bodies requiring greater AP investment.

I'm less convinced by that approach, because it could turn the Innate into a continuous physique optimization problem rather than a clear, recognizable choice.

A fixed high Innate cost and a constrained exceptional range would be simpler.

---

## 5. How this changes the twelve-stat roster

I would **keep the roster unchanged**.

| Attribute | Granular statistics |
|---|---|
| POW | Explosiveness, Jump Power, Swing Power |
| SPD | Acceleration, Lateral Speed, Transition Speed |
| ACC | Ball Control, Timing, Stability |
| END | Stamina Capacity, Exertion Efficiency, Recovery |

But we should explicitly distinguish **rated capability** from **realized performance**.

For instance:

- Jump Power is not synonymous with maximum contact height.
- Acceleration is not synonymous with the actual acceleration achieved by every body.
- Swing Power is not synonymous with the ball velocity produced on every hit.
- Stamina Capacity is not necessarily identical to the number of jumps an Athlete can perform.

The statistics contribute to these outcomes. Physique and circumstances modify the results.

This is consistent with the direction of VWM's existing physical simulation, particularly its separation of standing reach, jumping displacement, and approach mechanics.

### Provisional Innate roster

| Innate | Category | Cost tier | Exclusive property |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Giant** | Exceptional physique | Highest | Access to exceptionally large body geometry |
| **Tiny** | Exceptional physique | Highest | Access to exceptionally small body geometry |

I'd establish these as the first two Innates.

They also give us a useful design standard for future physical Innates: **they should change the Athlete's physical possibilities, not simply award Attribute points.**

The next step should be to determine how ordinary physique variations influence performance, because Giant and Tiny cannot be balanced until we know what a normal 5'10"–6'2" Athlete can achieve with the same Attributes.

I would be careful, however, about assigning Giant and Tiny heights symmetrically around 6'0". Equal deviations in centimeters do not necessarily produce equally valuable or equally costly changes in volleyball performance.

### Proposed height structure

Using 6'0" (183 cm) as our reference:

| Height | Deviation | Classification | AP treatment |
|---|---:|---|---|
| 5'6" (168 cm) | −15 cm | Tiny | High-cost Innate |
| 5'7" (170 cm) | −13 cm | Extended | Modest AP cost |

| 6'3" (191 cm) | +8 cm | Extended | Modest AP cost |
| 6'4" (193 cm) | +10 cm | Extended | Modest AP cost |
| 6'5" (196 cm) | +13 cm | Extended | Modest AP cost |
| 6'8" (203 cm) | +20 cm | Giant | High-cost Innate |

There is a problem with this exact structure: the distinction between 5'7" and 5'6" is only one inch, while the distinction between 6'5" and 6'8" is three inches.

If Tiny at 5'6" is supposed to provide a genuinely exceptional physical identity, that boundary may be too close to the ordinary extended range.

That doesn't necessarily invalidate 5'6". It suggests that **Giant and Tiny should be distinguished by more than height alone**.

## 1. Height should be one dimension of physique

Our physical model already includes:

- Height
- Mass
- Wingspan
- Stride length
- Limb proportions

A 6'8" Giant shouldn't necessarily be equivalent to an ordinary Athlete whose height slider happens to reach 6'8".

The Giant Innate could permit a distinct distribution of physical proportions.

For example, a Giant could have unusually long arms relative to height, while a Tiny Athlete could have unusually favorable strength-to-mass characteristics.

But I would avoid making those benefits arbitrary. They should emerge from a coherent body-generation model.

The Innate should unlock a physique region unavailable through ordinary customization.

## 2. Height costs should follow mechanical advantage, not distance alone

We shouldn't assign AP prices using a formula like:

\[
\text{Cost} \propto |\text{Height} - 183|
\]

That would assume every centimeter away from the reference height has an equivalent strategic value.

Instead, height should affect several realized outcomes:

| Outcome | Taller physique | Shorter physique |
|---|---|---|
| Standing reach | Advantage | Disadvantage |
| Block coverage | Advantage | Disadvantage |
| Attacking contact height | Advantage | Disadvantage |
| Acceleration | Potential disadvantage | Potential advantage |
| Lateral transitions | Potential disadvantage | Potential advantage |
| Stamina expenditure | Potential disadvantage | Potential advantage |
| Contact geometry | Larger reach envelope | Smaller reach envelope |

The AP price should reflect the **combined gameplay value** of these changes.

For instance, 6'5" might be particularly valuable for an MB, but less attractive for an L.

Conversely, 5'7" might be valuable for a defensive specialist but impose significant offensive limitations.

We should not automatically assume the costs should be identical.

## 3. A preliminary AP cost curve

I would start with a simple tiered system rather than a continuous equation.

| Height band | Suggested AP cost |
|---|---:|
| 5'10"–6'2" | 0 |
| 5'8"–5'9" or 6'3"–6'4" | 2 AP |
| 5'7" or 6'5" | 4 AP |
| Giant/Tiny | Substantially higher, TBD |

These are calibration placeholders, not final costs.

Their purpose is to create meaningful opportunity costs without making every inch of height require its own balancing decision.

There is also a reasonable alternative: charge AP only for the most extreme ordinary heights, keeping 5'8"–6'4" free.

That might be preferable if ordinary height variation proves relatively small in mechanical impact.

## 4. Giant and Tiny should be treated as physique packages

I would provisionally define:

**Giant**
- Height approximately 6'8" (203 cm).
- Exceptional reach and contact geometry.
- Distinctive mass, acceleration, and exertion profile.
- High starting AP cost.
- Cannot be acquired after Athlete creation.

**Tiny**
- Height approximately 5'6" (168 cm), subject to revision.
- Exceptionally compact movement geometry.
- Distinctive acceleration, directional movement, and exertion profile.
- High starting AP cost.
- Cannot be acquired after Athlete creation.

The main issue is that Tiny's proposed height is only slightly outside the extended range.

One possibility is to move Tiny closer to 5'2"–5'4", creating a more visibly exceptional body.

But I wouldn't do that purely for numerical symmetry. A 5'6" Athlete could still be mechanically exceptional if the associated body proportions are sufficiently unusual.

### One additional concern

If Giant and Tiny are fixed-height Innates, they may become predictable enough that players immediately identify the opponent's physical strengths and weaknesses.

That is not necessarily bad. In fact, **visual readability is probably a significant benefit**.

However, I'd preserve some customization within each physique category so that not every Giant or Tiny Athlete has identical physical performance.

---

### My recommendation

Keep the ordinary free range at **5'10"–6'2"**, introduce modest AP costs for **5'7"–5'9" and 6'3"–6'5"**, and reserve Giant/Tiny for exceptional body configurations around **6'8" and 5'6"**, respectively.

But before canonizing those Innates, I would establish one mechanical baseline:

**How much does changing height by one inch affect standing reach, jumping, acceleration, and Stamina expenditure at identical Attributes?**

Once those relationships are defined, we can determine whether 6'8" and 5'6" are genuinely exceptional, whether the AP costs make sense, and whether Giant/Tiny need additional physiological distinctions beyond height.

I'd establish **three height bands**, using 6'0" (183 cm) as the center of the distribution. The boundaries should reflect increasingly unusual physiques, rather than equal increments in AP cost.

### Proposed height ranges

| Category | Height range | AP cost | Interpretation |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Ordinary** | **5'10"–6'2"** | Free | Standard Athlete customization |
| **Extended — Short** | **5'5"–5'9"** | Modest, increasing toward extremes | Unusually short but not exceptional |
| **Extended — Tall** | **6'3"–6'7"** | Modest, increasing toward extremes | Unusually tall but not exceptional |
| **Tiny** | **4'10"–5'4"** | High-cost Innate | Exceptionally small physique |
| **Giant** | **6'8"–7'2"** | High-cost Innate | Exceptionally large physique |

These are proposed **gameplay bands**, not statistical percentiles from a real-world height distribution. Real volleyball player heights are not normally distributed around 6'0", and the distribution varies substantially by sex and position.

### Why these boundaries?

**1. The ordinary range remains narrow.**

A four-inch spread around the reference height provides noticeable physical customization without requiring players to engage with the physique economy.

The player can choose a slightly shorter, quicker profile or a slightly taller, longer-reaching profile without sacrificing Attribute investment.

**2. The extended ranges cover another five inches in either direction.**

An Athlete at 5'5" or 6'7" is meaningfully different from one at 6'0", particularly in contact geometry.

These builds should carry a real AP cost, but not one comparable to purchasing an Innate.

I'd use a shallow, increasing cost curve rather than charging the same amount throughout the extended range.

**3. Giant and Tiny begin where the physical differences become defining characteristics.**

At 6'8", an Athlete has a substantially different standing reach and blocking envelope from the ordinary range.

At 5'4", an Athlete has a substantially smaller reach envelope and potentially very different movement characteristics.

These are large enough departures that they could reasonably justify exclusive physique Innates.

The important detail is that the Innate grants **access to the entire exceptional range**, not necessarily a fixed height.

A Giant might choose 6'9" rather than 7'2", depending on their desired balance between reach, movement, and exertion.

Similarly, a Tiny Athlete might choose 5'3" rather than 4'10".

### A concern about the bell curve

If we're using a bell curve as a conceptual guide, I wouldn't force the Giant and Tiny boundaries to be symmetrical in gameplay value.

The benefit of gaining another inch of height in volleyball is not necessarily equal to the benefit of losing one inch.

A taller Athlete may gain substantial reach, but a shorter Athlete's movement advantages might be smaller or harder to exploit.

Consequently, **symmetric height ranges do not imply symmetric AP prices or mechanical value**.

We may ultimately need different prices for Giant and Tiny, even if their height bands are equally distant from the 6'0" reference.

### Recommended starting point

I'd provisionally adopt:

**Free:** 5'10"–6'2"  
**Height investment:** 5'5"–5'9" and 6'3"–6'7"  
**Tiny Innate:** 4'10"–5'4"  
**Giant Innate:** 6'8"–7'2"

This gives us a continuous, readable height spectrum with two clear thresholds: ordinary customization ends at 5'10"/6'2", and exceptional physique begins below 5'5" or above 6'7".

The next design task would be to calibrate the underlying height-to-performance functions so that those thresholds represent meaningful changes in gameplay rather than arbitrary visual categories.

### Revised height ranges

| Category | Height range | Cost |
|---|---|---|
| **Tiny** | 5'0"–5'4" | High-cost Innate |
| **Short height investment** | 5'5"–5'11" | Increasing AP cost |
| **Default** | **6'0"–6'2"** | Free |
| **Tall height investment** | 6'3"–6'7" | Increasing AP cost |
| **Giant** | 6'8"–7'2" | High-cost Innate |

I'd retain 7'2" as a provisional maximum.

This is deliberately asymmetric around the free range. That's acceptable: we don't need to pretend the default Athlete height is the mathematical center of every possible physique.

## What calibrating height-to-performance functions means

Essentially, we need to determine **exactly how an Athlete's physical dimensions affect their performance**, independently of their Attributes.

We've established that an Athlete has two separate sources of physical capability:

1. **Attributes:** POW, SPD, ACC, END and their twelve underlying statistics.
2. **Physique:** Height, mass, wingspan, stride length, and other physical proportions.

But we haven't established the mathematical relationship between them.

For example, suppose two Athletes have identical Attributes:

- 75 POW
- 75 SPD
- 75 ACC
- 75 END

One is 5'6", the other is 6'6".

We know they should perform differently. We haven't determined **how differently**.

That's what calibration addresses.

### 1. Standing reach

This is the most straightforward relationship.

A taller Athlete generally has a higher standing reach, but height isn't the only relevant measurement.

We could use the standing-reach model already explored in VWM:

\[
R = 1.215H + 0.32(W-H)
\]

Where:
- \(R\) = standing reach
- \(H\) = height
- \(W\) = wingspan

Suppose wingspan equals height.

| Athlete height | Approximate standing reach |
|---|---|
| 5'6" (168 cm) | 204 cm |
| 6'1" (185 cm) | 225 cm |
| 6'6" (198 cm) | 241 cm |

That's a **37 cm standing-reach difference** between the shortest and tallest Athletes in this example.

That difference matters enormously in volleyball.

A taller Athlete can potentially contact balls that a shorter Athlete cannot reach, even if the shorter Athlete has superior Jump Power.

But we still need to decide whether this full difference should translate directly into the MOBA's collision and contact mechanics.

### 2. Jumping

Jumping introduces the interaction between physique and POW.

We currently have:

- **Explosiveness:** How quickly an Athlete can initiate explosive actions.
- **Jump Power:** Their vertical jumping capability.
- **Swing Power:** Their striking force.

We need a function that converts Jump Power and physique into actual vertical displacement.

For example:

\[
\text{Jump Height} = f(\text{Jump Power},\text{Mass},\text{Approach})
\]

This function determines whether a 5'6" Athlete with 90 Jump Power can outjump a 6'6" Athlete with 70 Jump Power.

It also determines whether the taller Athlete still has the higher maximum contact point.

Those are two different questions.

An Athlete can jump higher while still reaching a lower point.

**Calibration means choosing the numbers that produce the desired relationship.**

### 3. Movement

Height should also influence movement, but not through a simplistic rule that shorter always means faster.

Consider two Athletes with identical SPD.

A taller Athlete might have:

- Longer strides.
- Greater distance covered at sustained speed.
- More inertia when changing direction.

A shorter Athlete might have:

- Faster directional changes.
- Shorter acceleration phases.
- Better movement in confined spaces.

We would need to decide how those properties interact with Acceleration, Lateral Speed, and Transition Speed.

For instance, a 6'6" Athlete might cover a long straight distance efficiently but struggle to reverse direction as quickly as a 5'6" Athlete.

This could make the shorter Athlete better at certain defensive movements without granting them universally higher movement speed.

Importantly, these differences should remain noticeable even when both Athletes have the same SPD.

### 4. Stamina

Physique should also influence the cost of physical actions.

For example, jumping involves moving an Athlete's body mass vertically.

A heavier Athlete might spend more Stamina per jump, all else equal.

But we need to be careful: real-world athletic ability already reflects an individual's capacity to move their own body.

If Jump Power determines jumping ability, and mass independently imposes a large penalty, we could accidentally penalize heavier Athletes twice.

I'd therefore distinguish:

**Jump Power determines achievable jump performance.**

**END and physique determine the exertion required to produce that performance.**

That keeps the Attributes meaningful without making body size irrelevant.

### 5. Striking force

Swing Power should interact with body geometry.

A taller Athlete might benefit from longer arms and a larger effective striking radius.

But greater arm length doesn't automatically mean a more powerful strike.

The model might account for:

- Swing Power.
- Arm length.
- Effective body mass.
- Contact quality.
- Approach momentum.

A smaller Athlete with high Swing Power should still be capable of powerful attacks.

The larger Athlete's advantage might emerge from a combination of reach, favorable contact angle, and force transfer rather than an unconditional damage bonus.

---

## Why this matters for AP costs

Once those relationships are defined, we can compare physical builds against Attribute investments.

For example, imagine that increasing height from 6'2" to 6'5" grants:

- 9 cm additional standing reach.
- Slightly better attack contact geometry.
- Slightly worse directional acceleration.
- Slightly higher jump exertion.

We could then test whether those effects are worth 2 AP, 4 AP, or another amount.

Likewise, we could compare a 6'8" Giant against an ordinary 6'2" Athlete who invested the saved AP into POW and END.

The question becomes:

**Does the Giant's exclusive reach and physical profile compensate for having weaker Attributes and potentially fewer introductory Ability Traits?**

That is the calibration problem.

### One important recommendation

I would avoid making height directly modify the twelve underlying statistical ratings.

For example, a Giant shouldn't automatically receive −10 Acceleration or +10 Swing Power.

Instead, the Athlete should retain their actual ratings, and their physique should influence the **physical outcomes calculated from those ratings**.

That distinction preserves the purpose of both systems.

An Athlete with 90 Acceleration remains exceptionally capable of accelerating *for their physique*, even if a smaller Athlete with 90 Acceleration achieves a different movement curve.

It also means the Attribute screen remains intelligible: the ratings describe the Athlete's trained capabilities, while their body determines how those capabilities manifest.

**In short:** We have decided *which physical differences exist*. Calibration is the process of deciding *how large their effects are*, expressing them mathematically, and testing whether the resulting Athlete builds are balanced against the AP and Trait opportunities they sacrifice.

### Exchange 064

Conversation turn ID: `430dcf5c-cbd9-4db9-a18b-7d746305c477`


#### User

does VWM already do this effectively? we have the standing reach model which should be retained - what else?


#### Assistant

**VWM already provides much of the physical modeling we need, but it does not yet provide a complete, calibrated physique-to-performance system.** We should reuse its implemented mechanics wherever possible rather than construct a second physical model for the MOBA.

The distinction is between mechanics VWM already implements, relationships it has partially modeled, and proposals documented but not yet implemented.

### What VWM already establishes

| Physical relationship | VWM status | Recommendation for MOBA |
|---|---|---|
| Height + wingspan → standing reach | Implemented | **Retain** |
| Height → mass, wingspan, stride correlations | Established in athlete generation | Retain the relationships, adapt generation to player customization |
| Attributes → horizontal movement | Implemented in approach/locomotion modeling | Adapt |
| Jump rating → vertical displacement | Implemented | Retain the conceptual model |
| Approach speed and alignment → jump effectiveness | Implemented | Adapt |
| Body posture → contact reach | Implemented | Adapt |
| Mass + physical abilities → attack power | Partially represented in derived calculations | Rework for Swing Power |
| Physique → Stamina expenditure | Not established as a complete relationship | Design |
| Physique → directional agility | Partially represented | Extend |
| Physique → repeated-jump performance | Not established as a complete relationship | Design |

VWM's existing model is particularly useful for **reach, approach mechanics, and jumping**. Those three systems already establish how physical characteristics can change an Athlete's available actions.

Source: [VWM — Granular Attribute Breakdown](https://github.com/iracarranza/volley-world-manager/blob/main/docs/textbook/GRANULAR_ATTRIBUTE_BREAKDOWN.md).

## 1. The strongest reusable system: contact geometry

VWM already separates two quantities that the MOBA must keep distinct:

**Standing reach**

\[
R = 1.215H + 0.32(W-H)
\]

Where \(H\) is height and \(W\) is wingspan, both in centimeters.

**Maximum jumping contact height**

\[
C = R + J \times M
\]

Where:
- \(J\) is vertical displacement derived from jumping capability.
- \(M\) represents modifiers from approach quality and body state.

VWM's documented jump-displacement range is approximately **0.12–0.78 meters**, while approach quality can modify available jump height.

This is a much better foundation than directly deriving contact height from POW.

It gives us meaningful differences between:

- A tall Athlete with modest Jump Power.
- A short Athlete with exceptional Jump Power.
- An Athlete with a strong approach.
- An Athlete forced to jump from an awkward position.

**Recommendation:** Preserve this architecture, including approach quality and posture. Replace VWM's `jump_reach` rating with the MOBA's Jump Power statistic.

## 2. Horizontal movement: useful, but physique integration needs work

VWM documents these approximate movement relationships:

| Variable | VWM range |
|---|---|
| Maximum approach speed | 1.35–5.25 m/s |
| Acceleration | 2.2–6.8 m/s² |
| Approach jump multiplier | 0.90–1.08× |
| Approach balance multiplier | 0.58–1.04× |

Its movement system also distinguishes acceleration, lateral movement, and transitions.

That maps well onto our three SPD statistics.

However, **VWM does not yet establish a complete, explicit relationship between height, stride, mass, and movement performance**.

Its Athlete generator derives stride from actual height, and its locomotion design recognizes body dimensions. But we should not assume that every movement calculation already accounts for those dimensions appropriately.

For the MOBA, I would preserve VWM's movement structure while investigating three specific relationships:

1. Does stride length affect sustained movement speed?
2. Does mass affect directional changes and acceleration?
3. Does body size affect the time required to transition between movement and volleyball actions?

Those are particularly important for balancing Giant and Tiny.

## 3. Attack power: VWM already uses physique

VWM's existing `usable_attack_power()` calculation blends:

| Contributor | Weight |
|---|---:|
| Attack Power | 25% |
| Arm Speed | 20% |
| Explosiveness | 18% |
| Approach Timing | 15% |
| Transition Speed | 12% |
| Body Mass | 10% |

This is evidence that VWM already treats physical performance as an interaction between physique and athletic skills.

However, I would **not copy these weights directly**.

Our MOBA has consolidated Attack Power and Arm Speed into Swing Power, while retaining Explosiveness as a separate statistic.

We also want actual contact geometry and approach conditions to matter.

The VWM calculation is therefore a useful precedent, not a finished MOBA formula.

## 4. Stamina: this needs the most new work

VWM has a fatigue model, but it is not equivalent to our MOBA's Stamina economy.

Its documented fatigue model can reduce movement speed and jumping capability as fatigue accumulates.

It also proposes distinguishing anaerobic, aerobic, and neuromuscular fatigue, but those more granular systems are not all implemented.

Our MOBA needs something more specific:

- Explicit STA costs for jumps and actions.
- END-derived capacity, efficiency, and recovery.
- Positional modifiers, such as the MB's reduced Block Jump cost.
- Physique-dependent exertion.
- Exhaustion thresholds.
- Recovery between Rallies.

I would reuse VWM's principle that fatigue affects different capabilities differently, but **not import its entire fatigue model**.

The MOBA already has a clearer resource economy. Adding three separate fatigue meters would probably be unnecessary complexity.

## 5. Athlete generation: retain the principle, change the economy

VWM's implemented Athlete generation separates physique from skill development.

Height influences correlated measurements such as mass, wingspan, and stride. Skills are generated through a separate development budget.

That separation is useful, but the MOBA introduces an intentional exception: players can spend creation AP to access exceptional physiques.

I'd interpret this as:

**VWM determines how a chosen physique is constructed; the MOBA determines how expensive access to that physique is.**

For example, selecting 6'6" should produce a plausible associated body profile, not merely change the height value while leaving mass and stride unchanged.

The player can then customize proportions within permitted limits.

Giant and Tiny expand those limits.

Source: [VWM — Attribute-First Generation](https://github.com/iracarranza/volley-world-manager/blob/main/docs/design/ATTRIBUTE_FIRST_GENERATION.md).

---

## What I'd retain versus redesign

| System | Decision |
|---|---|
| Standing reach formula | **Retain unchanged initially** |
| Height-correlated mass, wingspan, stride | Retain |
| Jump displacement model | Retain structure; remap to Jump Power |
| Approach quality affecting jump height | Retain |
| Posture affecting contact envelope | Retain |
| Horizontal movement curves | Adapt to MOBA controls and pace |
| Attack power composition | Redesign around Swing Power |
| Fatigue degradation | Adapt |
| STA expenditure and regeneration | Design for MOBA |
| Height-based AP pricing | Design for MOBA |
| Giant/Tiny physique limits | Design for MOBA |

### One important caveat

VWM's [Granular Attribute Breakdown](https://github.com/iracarranza/volley-world-manager/blob/main/docs/textbook/GRANULAR_ATTRIBUTE_BREAKDOWN.md) explicitly contains both implemented mechanics and proposed extensions. I've distinguished those where the documentation identifies their status, but I haven't traced every physical calculation through its runtime callers. Consequently, I wouldn't yet certify the entire physique model as fully implemented or quantitatively balanced.

### My recommendation

**Use VWM's contact-envelope and approach systems as the starting physical specification.** They're substantially more developed than what we've independently established for the MOBA.

The next investigation should focus narrowly on whether VWM's actual locomotion and jumping implementations already account for **height, mass, and stride**, or whether those variables exist primarily in Athlete generation.

That determines how much additional engineering Giant and Tiny actually require.

More fundamentally, we should avoid building a second, incompatible set of physical equations. The MOBA can simplify VWM's simulation, but wherever the two games model the same physical phenomenon, they should ideally share the same underlying logic.
