#### Assistant

Agreed. The MOBA should retain VWM's **contact-envelope and approach systems**, but incorporate the MOBA's action-charging mechanics rather than determining performance entirely from Attributes.

The intended relationship is:

**Physique establishes physical geometry; Attributes establish athletic capability; charging and positioning determine how much of that capability an Athlete realizes during an action.**

### 1. Locomotion and jumping: verification status

VWM's documentation establishes several relevant relationships:

| Component | Documented VWM behavior | Verification status |
|---|---|---|
| Height and wingspan | Determine standing reach | Formula established |
| Height and stride | Stride is derived from actual height | Generation documented as implemented |
| Acceleration | Determines how quickly useful movement speed is reached | Movement model documented |
| Lateral and transition speed | Influence movement and approaches | Movement model documented |
| Approach quality | Influences available jump height and balance | Documented as implemented |
| Jump capability | Determines vertical displacement | Documented as implemented |
| Mass | Contributes to derived attacking power | Documented |
| Mass and stride in locomotion | May influence movement outcomes | **Runtime integration not verified** |
| Mass in jumping | May influence realized vertical displacement | **Runtime integration not verified** |

I haven't verified the last two relationships against their runtime implementations. The VWM documents establish that height, mass, and stride exist in the physical model, but that alone doesn't prove they influence locomotion or jumping.

I attempted to initiate a deeper repository inspection, but that handoff wasn't accepted, so I won't represent the implementation as verified.

### 2. Incorporating action charging

I would distinguish three stages of an action.

**Physical potential** determines what the Athlete could achieve under favorable conditions.

**Preparation** determines how much of that potential becomes available. This includes approach quality, positioning, and charging.

**Execution** determines the final result, accounting for timing, direction, Stamina, and ball contact.

This produces more interesting interactions than simply converting Jump Power into a fixed jump height.

For example:

| Situation | Expected result |
|---|---|
| High Jump Power, complete charge, good approach | Near-maximum jumping performance |
| High Jump Power, incomplete charge | Reduced jumping performance |
| Moderate Jump Power, complete charge, good approach | Strong execution within a lower physical ceiling |
| High Jump Power, complete charge, poor approach | Strong jumping ability, but reduced approach benefit |
| Low Stamina | Reduced available performance or inability to execute, depending on exhaustion rules |

This is especially relevant because **Explosiveness and Jump Power should influence different parts of the charging process**.

Explosiveness could determine how quickly an Athlete prepares an explosive action.

Jump Power determines the vertical displacement available once that action is executed.

An Athlete with high Explosiveness and moderate Jump Power could therefore execute effective quick Blocks without reaching the same maximum height as an Athlete specializing in Jump Power.

### 3. Preserve distinct charging behavior by action

I wouldn't apply the same charging formula to every action.

| Action | Primary charging consideration |
|---|---|
| **Attack** | Approach preparation, contact timing, and Swing Power |
| **Block** | Jump preparation and timing relative to the attacker |
| **Set** | Ball control and release timing; charging may improve precision or trajectory control |
| **Receive** | Preparation and platform stability; charging may improve controlled reception |

For jumping specifically, the existing charging system should distinguish **how quickly an Athlete becomes ready to jump** from **how much vertical displacement the jump produces**.

That distinction also preserves the MB's positional advantage: faster Block Jump charging and reduced STA expenditure remain separate benefits.

### 4. How physique should interact with charging

Giant and Tiny become more interesting if their physical differences affect the execution of charged actions.

A Giant might have exceptional maximum contact height but require more preparation to exploit it fully.

A Tiny Athlete might reach their available jumping capability more quickly, but have a substantially lower standing reach.

However, these should not be automatic rules merely because an Athlete is tall or short. They should emerge from the actual relationships between mass, limb geometry, Explosiveness, and jumping mechanics.

We also need to avoid double-counting physique penalties. If greater mass already slows an Athlete's approach, it should not automatically impose an additional arbitrary penalty on jump charging.

### 5. Recommended architecture

I'd use this general structure:

\[
\text{Realized Performance}
=
f(\text{Physique},\text{Attributes},\text{Charge},\text{Approach},\text{STA})
\]

But each component should have a clearly defined responsibility.

- **Physique:** Reach, mass, proportions, stride, and contact geometry.
- **Attributes:** Physical capability and technical execution.
- **Charge:** Degree of preparation and commitment to an action.
- **Approach:** Movement trajectory, speed, alignment, and available runway.
- **STA:** Exertion costs and fatigue-related performance limitations.

One important design constraint: **charging should not always be beneficial without consequence**. Otherwise, every Athlete simply maximizes charge before every action.

A longer charge might provide greater power or control while delaying execution, limiting movement, or increasing vulnerability to deception.

This is particularly important for Blocks, where committing too early should create opportunities for the opposing attacker.

**The remaining technical question is narrow:** Does VWM's runtime locomotion and jumping code already use height-derived stride and body mass to modify realized movement and jumping, or are those relationships only partially implemented?

That needs code-level verification before we decide which calculations can be transferred directly.

The four principal volleyball actions are Receive, Set, Attack,

The meaning and effectiveness of that charge depend on the

JUMP / APPROACH INTERACTION

Charging Jump initiates an approach sequence that transforms
the execution of the currently charged volleyball action.

The transformation depends on the prepared action:

- Receive: The beginning of the approach sequence becomes

- Set: The middle of the approach sequence becomes a

- Block: The middle of the approach sequence becomes a
  Block Jump. Middle Blockers have a positional Innate
  allowing them to change this timing to the Dive timing.

- Attack: The penultimate step of the approach sequence
  becomes a Spike.

This input can again be tapped or charged.

During preparation for the secondary input:

- Movement commands temporarily become directional influence

- A tapped secondary input executes using the established

- A charged secondary input acts as an expensive modifier,
  replacing the initial charge with a new value selected
  within a narrower range.

### Two mechanical questions this exposes

**First, what does charging Jump actually control?**

There are at least two plausible interpretations:

- **Approach preparation:** Charging Jump determines the quality or intensity of the approach sequence.
- **Approach execution:** Charging Jump determines how quickly the sequence advances or how much physical performance is available at its execution point.

These are not equivalent. I would provisionally favor the first interpretation, allowing Explosiveness and the Athlete's physical profile to influence execution speed.

**Second, the secondary charge needs a distinct purpose.**

If it simply replaces the initial charge with a better value, players may learn to undercharge initially and rely on the secondary input.

The narrower range and additional expense help, but we should eventually establish whether secondary charging is primarily intended for **last-moment correction, deliberate overexertion, or both**.

I would leave both questions open during the VWM audit. The repository's existing approach mechanics may help determine which interpretation integrates more naturally.

#### Assistant

Recovered. We had already settled this on **October 17**, including the distinction between initial commitment, secondary correction, and cancellation.

## Established secondary-charge mechanics

The secondary charge is an **optional, expensive corrective adjustment** to an already prepared action. It is not a second opportunity to freely choose the action's power.

| Mechanic | Established rule |
|---|---|
| **Initial charge** | Establishes the action's intended power level. |
| **Initial HP cost** | Charging consumes Hitting Power (HP) equal to the charge. |
| **Secondary tap** | Executes the prepared action without changing its charge. |
| **Secondary charge** | Adjusts the prepared action within a limited range. |
| **Adjustment effectiveness** | Secondary charging is less effective than charging initially. |
| **Additional HP cost** | Secondary charging is disproportionately more expensive. |
| **Initial commitment** | The initial charge remains committed; secondary charging does not refund or erase its original investment. |
| **Late correction** | Permits adjustments such as changing a Set or attempting a late feint, but at reduced effectiveness and increased expense. |

We had specifically selected **Model B: the initial charge remains committed**.

This is important because secondary charging does not simply replace the original expenditure.

### Example: late feint

An Athlete prepares a powerful Attack, committing HP to its initial charge.

During execution, they recognize that the opposing Block is well positioned and attempt to change the Attack into a softer contact.

They can use secondary charging to reduce the effective power, but:

- The original HP expenditure remains spent.
- The adjustment has a limited range.
- The adjustment costs additional HP.
- The resulting action is less effective than if they had prepared the softer contact initially.

Thus, **secondary charging rewards adaptation without eliminating the value of anticipation and preparation**.

## Related rules we also established

Two additional decisions from the same discussion are relevant.

**Grounded cancellation refunds invested HP.** An Athlete who cancels their prepared action before committing to the airborne sequence recovers the HP spent preparing it.

**Airborne action types are locked.** Once airborne, an Athlete cannot select a completely different primary action unless an Ability Trait explicitly permits it.

The airborne input is therefore a secondary tap or charge, not a new primary action selection.

## Updated interpretation of Jump charging

You've now confirmed that **charging Jump determines approach preparation**.

That gives us a consistent sequence:

1. **Initial action charge:** Establishes the intended action and commits HP.
2. **Jump charge:** Prepares and initiates the approach sequence.
3. **Approach:** Movement, timing, and physical capabilities determine the available execution conditions.
4. **Secondary tap:** Executes the prepared action.
5. **Secondary charge (optional):** Makes a limited, inefficient, HP-expensive adjustment before execution.

The initial charge determines the intended action. The approach determines the physical conditions under which it can be executed. The secondary charge provides a costly opportunity to adapt.

### Copyable correction for Work

```text
RECOVERED SECONDARY-CHARGE RULES

We previously established Model B: initial charge remains committed.

- Charging an action consumes Hitting Power (HP) equal to its
  initial charge.

- A secondary tap executes the prepared action without
  modifying its charge.

- A secondary charge permits a limited adjustment to the
  prepared action's effective charge.

- Secondary charging is less effective than initial charging.

- Secondary charging incurs a disproportionately greater
  additional HP cost.

- The original HP expenditure remains committed. Secondary
  charging does not refund or replace that expenditure.

- This permits late corrections, such as adjusting a Set
  or attempting a late feint, but these corrections are
  weaker and more expensive than preparing appropriately
  from the beginning.

- Canceling a grounded prepared action refunds its
  invested HP.

- Once airborne, the action type is locked unless an
  Ability Trait explicitly permits changing it.

- Airborne inputs are secondary tap/charge inputs, not
  new primary action selections.

JUMP CHARGING

Charging Jump determines approach preparation.

It should not be interpreted as directly setting maximum
vertical displacement.

Physique and Jump Power establish available jumping capability;
Explosiveness influences access to explosive performance;
the prepared approach and its execution conditions determine
how much of that capability is realized.

Exact secondary-adjustment ranges, efficiency penalties,
and HP multipliers remain subject to numerical calibration.
```

**The key distinction:** The secondary charge changes the *execution*, not the *original commitment*. That was the central reason for choosing Model B.

Yes. We can continue the design discussion here using the completed VWM audit.

We'll adopt the following distinction:

- **Explosiveness:** Determines how quickly an Athlete can prepare and initiate explosive actions.
- **Jump Power:** Determines the maximum vertical displacement an Athlete can achieve.
- **Jump Charge:** Determines approach preparation.
- **Approach quality:** Determines how effectively the Athlete realizes their available jumping capability.
- **Physique:** Determines standing reach, movement geometry, and physical exertion.

This replaces VWM's direct Explosiveness multiplier on jump displacement. It also avoids counting incomplete preparation multiple times.

### Where I'd continue

The audit identifies three related problems worth resolving in order.

**1. Jump and approach integration**

Determine how Jump Charge, approach progress, and actual movement produce a realized jump. We should preserve VWM's runway, alignment, balance, and momentum calculations without creating redundant preparation requirements.

**2. Action-specific execution**

Determine how the resulting approach interacts with Receive, Set, Attack, and Block, particularly their different execution points. The MB's alternative Block Jump timing is an important exception.

**3. Physique balance**

Once the physical model is established, compare Athletes of different heights with identical Attributes, then compare them at equal creation-AP expenditure. This will help establish meaningful height costs and eventually price Giant and Tiny.

**I'd start with the first problem.** One distinction needs particular attention: whether a fully charged Jump guarantees a fully prepared approach, or whether the Athlete must still execute the approach successfully through movement and positioning.

I favor the latter. Jump Charge should establish the intended preparation, while the actual approach determines how much of that preparation is successfully realized. Otherwise, VWM's approach mechanics would become largely redundant.

### Exchange 072

Conversation turn ID: `e6f40ba9-1844-4927-8d35-425c0f598d08`


#### User

question: should net faults exist? leaning towards yes as a reason to create a reason for effective versus stunted approaches - a hitter must gauge whether a full approach is attainable given the time made available by receive, set, etc. and choose between a full approach (which is caused by charging a jump fully) or a partial approach (by releasing a jump charge early)?


#### Assistant

**Yes. I think net faults should exist, and your reasoning identifies a particularly good reason for including them: they create a physical consequence for committing to an approach that the preceding play cannot support.**

More importantly, I think this gives us a way to distinguish **full versus partial approaches without making partial approaches simply inferior versions of full approaches**.

There is one distinction I'd make, though: **net faults should punish poor approach geometry and timing, not insufficient preparation itself.**

## 1. Full versus partial approaches

Under your proposed model, charging Jump determines the length of the intended approach sequence.

A fully charged Jump initiates a full approach. Releasing Jump early initiates a shortened approach, sacrificing some of the performance benefits of a complete approach.

Consider three situations:

| Situation | Decision | Result |
|---|---|---|
| High, controlled Set | Full Jump Charge | Sufficient time for a complete approach and maximum jumping opportunity |
| Fast, low Set | Partial Jump Charge | Shorter approach, reduced potential, but better synchronization |
| Fast, low Set | Full Jump Charge | Athlete risks arriving late, contacting poorly, or overrunning the net |

The third situation is where net faults become useful.

The Athlete has chosen an approach that demands more time or space than the play provides.

However, I wouldn't make the net fault automatic. Depending on positioning, the Athlete might instead be forced into an awkward contact, miss the ball, or abandon the attack.

That creates several distinct failure outcomes rather than a single punishment.

## 2. Net faults should emerge from physical movement

I'd strongly prefer **actual net collision detection** over a rule like:

> Full approach + insufficient time = net fault.

We already have VWM's locomotion, approach, and contact-envelope systems. Those provide the foundation for determining whether an Athlete physically reaches or crosses the net.

A net fault should occur when an Athlete makes prohibited contact with the net during play.

That means an Athlete's movement trajectory, body position, and action animation should matter.

For example:

**A 6'8" Giant**

- Has a higher standing reach.
- May require more room to complete certain approaches.
- Has a larger physical contact envelope.
- Might struggle to arrest forward momentum near the net.

**A 5'5" Athlete**

- Has lower standing reach.
- May benefit from shorter approach geometry.
- Might recover or redirect more effectively near the net.
- May need greater jumping displacement to achieve the same attacking contact height.

These differences could emerge from physique without giving either Athlete an arbitrary net-fault probability.

One caution: actual volleyball net faults aren't triggered by every possible net contact. For the MOBA, we should define a clear, simplified collision rule rather than attempting to reproduce every officiating exception.

## 3. A full approach shouldn't necessarily be the best approach

This is the most important design consequence.

Previously, we discussed approach quality as something that determines how much of an Athlete's jumping capability they can realize.

But there are two different concepts:

- **Approach completion:** How much of the intended sequence was performed?
- **Approach suitability:** How well did that sequence fit the available time, space, and ball trajectory?

A full approach can have excellent completion but poor suitability.

A partial approach can have lower maximum physical output but excellent suitability.

For example:

| | Full approach | Partial approach |
|---|---|---|
| Maximum jump potential | Higher | Lower |
| Preparation time | Longer | Shorter |
| Required runway | Longer | Shorter |
| Suitability for fast Sets | Lower | Higher |
| Ability to adjust late | Potentially lower | Potentially higher |
| Risk of overcommitting | Greater | Lower |

These are proposed tendencies, not guaranteed results.

**The optimal approach should be the one that best fits the play, not necessarily the longest one the Athlete can perform.**

This would also make setting decisions much more interesting.

## 4. This creates a direct relationship between Set quality and Attack options

Consider how a setter influences the hitter's available approach.

A high Set creates more time for the hitter to prepare.

A fast Set creates less time, but potentially reduces the opponent's ability to establish a Block.

That gives the setter a meaningful choice:

**High Set:** More preparation for the hitter, more preparation for the defense.

**Fast Set:** Less preparation for both the hitter and the defense.

The hitter must then decide whether to attempt a complete approach or shorten it.

This is particularly interesting because the setter's decision may not perfectly match the hitter's expectations.

An unexpectedly fast Set could force a hitter to abandon their intended full approach.

Conversely, a hitter who anticipates the fast Set could begin preparing early and achieve an effective approach despite the reduced time.

That rewards coordination and anticipation rather than just high Attributes.

### An important implication for Ability Traits

This also gives **Shoot Set** a much clearer identity.

Its advantage isn't merely that the ball travels faster.

It creates a different preparation problem for both attackers and defenders.

A hitter who anticipates the Shoot Set may execute a well-timed abbreviated approach. A defender who anticipates it may commit to an earlier Block.

The technique becomes strategically valuable because it changes the timing relationship between players.

---

## 5. I would reconsider one part of the Jump Charge proposal

You suggested:

> Full approach is caused by charging Jump fully; partial approach by releasing Jump Charge early.

I agree with this as the control model.

But I would avoid making **charge duration equal approach duration** in a strictly linear relationship.

Instead, I'd distinguish:

**Jump Charge establishes the intended approach length.**

**Explosiveness determines how quickly the Athlete can prepare and execute that approach.**

**Movement and physique determine whether the approach can physically fit the available space.**

This means two Athletes could charge Jump to the same level but execute their approaches differently.

A highly explosive Athlete might complete a full approach in less time.

A taller Athlete with a longer stride might cover the necessary runway in fewer steps, but still have different braking and directional-control requirements.

The approach should therefore be a physical sequence, not simply a timer.

### One potential problem: automatic approach movement

We should also clarify whether charging Jump causes the Athlete to move automatically through the approach sequence or merely establishes a sequence that the player executes through movement inputs.

Your current description suggests the former.

If the approach is automatic, the player must have sufficient control over its direction and initiation point to avoid the system producing unavoidable net faults.

That doesn't mean automatic approaches are undesirable. It means their physical commitments need to be predictable.

---

## 6. Net faults also create a useful skill distinction

Without net faults, an Athlete approaching too aggressively might suffer only a reduced-quality contact.

That can make reckless approaches worthwhile, especially if the reward for a successful Spike is high.

With net faults, overcommitting has a more substantial downside.

But I'd distinguish three consequences:

| Mistake | Consequence |
|---|---|
| Incomplete approach | Reduced available jumping or attacking performance |
| Poorly synchronized approach | Bad contact timing, unstable strike, or missed opportunity |
| Approach physically carries Athlete into net | Net fault |

This is preferable to treating all three as equivalent.

It also means a short approach is not inherently unsafe or low quality. It is simply a different physical commitment.

## 7. What I'd establish

I would provisionally adopt the following:

**Net faults exist.** They are determined by prohibited physical contact with the net, not by an abstract approach-quality roll.

**Jump Charge selects intended approach preparation.** Releasing early permits a shorter approach with reduced potential but lower time and space requirements.

**Full approaches offer greater maximum performance, not guaranteed superior performance.** Their success depends on runway, positioning, timing, and the preceding Set.

**Explosiveness affects approach preparation speed.** Jump Power determines maximum vertical displacement. Physique affects movement and contact geometry.

**The setter and hitter jointly determine approach viability.** Set trajectory and timing establish the opportunity; the hitter chooses how much preparation to commit.

### One additional consideration

I think this points toward **approach commitment being one of the principal sources of mechanical skill in the MOBA**.

It gives players a recurring judgment:

*How much of my maximum physical performance can I safely commit to this particular ball?*

That judgment applies to Attack, Block, Jump Set, and Dive, albeit with different timing requirements.

It also helps solve a broader design problem: high Attributes should increase an Athlete's capabilities without removing the need for good decisions.

A player with 95 Jump Power can still make a terrible approach decision. A player with 70 Jump Power can execute an excellent one.

**My only substantial reservation is that net faults must remain legible and avoidable.** If players cannot reasonably predict whether their chosen approach will carry them into the net, the mechanic becomes frustrating rather than skill-expressive.

### Exchange 073

Conversation turn ID: `b86b8276-9017-4358-9d13-dd892f274b33`


#### User

Charging a Jump initiates the approach sequence - we already established as such. The approach sequence carries the player towards their movement direction. (Also important: camera controls. During general gameplay the established from-behind perspective persists, but during Rally and Pepper sequences, the player can toggle between Athlete View and Ball View.) Additionally, once the player is in the air, their facing direction is preserved, but they have a brief moment before Moment of Contact to also reorient the camera itself, to examine other perspectives while still maintaining the inputted aiming direction.


#### Assistant

That clarifies the control model. **The approach sequence is an automatic movement commitment initiated by charging Jump, with its trajectory determined by the Athlete's movement direction.** The player does not manually execute each step.

The camera rules also introduce an important distinction between **where the Athlete is facing, where the camera is looking, and where the action is aimed**.

## 1. Updated movement and camera model

| State | Movement | Camera | Facing and aiming |
|---|---|---|---|
| General gameplay | Ordinary player-controlled locomotion | From-behind perspective | Normal movement-relative orientation |
| Rally / Pepper | Ordinary movement or action preparation | Toggle between Athlete View and Ball View | Normal controls until action commitment |
| Jump approach | Automatic sequence traveling in the selected movement direction | Athlete View or Ball View | Approach direction establishes the movement trajectory |
| Airborne | Committed airborne trajectory | Camera can be reoriented before contact | Athlete facing remains locked; previously established aim is preserved |
| Moment of Contact | Action executes | Current camera perspective | Action resolves using the committed aim and secondary input |

The last two rows are particularly important: **camera rotation during the airborne observation window must not unintentionally change the Athlete's aiming direction.**

This means the camera can become an independent observational tool without requiring the Athlete to physically turn.

## 2. Implications for approach commitment

Your clarification strengthens the case for net faults.

Because charging Jump initiates an automatic approach, the player makes three related decisions:

1. **Where to approach:** Established through movement direction.
2. **How much approach to commit:** Established through Jump Charge.
3. **When to initiate:** Determined by the player's release of Jump Charge.

Once initiated, the approach carries the Athlete along the selected trajectory.

A full approach near the net can therefore be dangerous, even if the Athlete has sufficient time to complete it.

Conversely, a partial approach may be preferable because the Athlete needs less forward displacement.

This creates two independent constraints:

- **Temporal feasibility:** Can the approach reach its execution point before the ball arrives?
- **Spatial feasibility:** Can the Athlete complete the approach without overrunning the intended contact position or colliding with the net?

A successful approach must satisfy both.

### A subtle consequence

The longest approach is not necessarily the one that produces the highest contact point.

A full approach that carries an Athlete too close to the net might leave them with worse contact geometry than a shorter, correctly positioned approach.

This is consistent with retaining VWM's approach-quality calculations rather than treating charge percentage as a direct jump-height multiplier.

## 3. Camera reorientation and aiming

I would formalize three separate directional states:

**Movement direction:** Determines the trajectory of the approach.

**Athlete facing:** Determines the Athlete's physical orientation and becomes locked once airborne.

**Camera orientation:** Determines what the player sees and can temporarily move independently of Athlete facing.

The existing directional-influence input provides a fourth relevant variable: **action aim**.

That should remain distinct from camera orientation during the airborne observation window.

For example, an attacker could:

- Approach toward the net.
- Commit to a Spike aimed cross-court.
- Become airborne, locking their facing direction.
- Rotate the camera to inspect the opposing Block.
- Execute the Spike without accidentally redirecting it toward the new camera orientation.

This is especially valuable for high-level play because it lets players gather information after committing physically.

However, the camera should not grant an unlimited opportunity to change the action itself.

The previously established secondary-charge system already provides a constrained method of late correction.

## 4. One interaction worth resolving

There is a potential conflict between two established controls:

- During secondary-input preparation, movement commands become directional influence over the action.
- The approach sequence travels in the player's movement direction.

These can coexist if **approach direction is committed when Jump initiates the sequence**.

After that commitment, movement inputs can influence the resulting action without redirecting the approach trajectory.

That would produce a clean distinction:

| Input phase | Movement input controls |
|---|---|
| Before Jump initiation | Athlete locomotion and intended approach direction |
| During automatic approach | Action direction/aim, rather than ordinary locomotion |
| Airborne before contact | Action direction/aim within permitted limits |
| Camera reorientation window | Camera observation independently of committed facing and aim |

I would treat that separation as the default interpretation, subject to any existing rule allowing limited mid-approach steering.

## 5. Consequence for net faults

With this control scheme, I favor **net faults arising from the Athlete's actual approach and airborne collision geometry**.

That means players can learn to judge safe approach distances.

A full approach from too close to the net carries a predictable risk. A shorter approach can preserve enough distance to complete the action safely.

One caveat: a locked facing direction should not mean a completely rigid body. The Athlete still needs the normal arm and torso motion required to perform an Attack or Block. Those motions should be represented in the contact envelope without granting unrestricted midair rotation.

### Updated design principle

**Jump Charge commits the Athlete to a physical approach; movement direction establishes its trajectory; the selected Action determines its execution point; and camera orientation can temporarily separate from Athlete facing and action aim.**

That gives us a coherent basis for implementing approach length, net faults, airborne commitment, and late visual decision-making.

The next important question is whether **the automatic approach permits any directional correction after initiation**, or whether its trajectory is completely fixed. That decision will substantially affect how punishing full approaches are near the net.

This scenario resolves several of the questions about approach steering, camera control, and airborne commitment. I'd interpret the sequence as follows.

### Scenario: Full approach, cross-court preparation, down-the-line attack

| Phase | Player action | Mechanical consequence |
|---|---|---|
| **1. Attack preparation** | Fully charges Attack | Commits HP to the intended attacking power |
| **2. Approach initiation** | Begins charging Jump toward a high Set | Initiates the approach sequence |
| **3. Curved approach** | Combines movement inputs and camera rotation in Ball View | Steers the approach along a curved trajectory while maintaining visual awareness of the ball |
| **4. Final preparation** | Switches to Athlete View and designates a cross-court angle | Establishes the Athlete's facing direction for takeoff |
| **5. Takeoff** | Reaches the Spike execution point | Athlete facing locks; the available attack trajectories become visible |
| **6. Airborne observation** | Uses an ACC-gated Trait to adjust the camera slightly | Gains additional visual information without changing the committed facing direction |
| **7. Attack execution** | Taps Attack while inputting a lateral direction | Executes at the original full Attack Charge, selecting a down-the-line trajectory |
| **8. Defensive response** | Defender receives the Attack | The chosen trajectory was physically viable but tactically unsuccessful |

Three implications stand out.

### 1. Approach direction and camera direction cannot be permanently coupled

The curved approach demonstrates that the player can use movement and camera rotation together to steer the Athlete.

This corrects my earlier suggestion that the approach trajectory should become fixed immediately upon Jump initiation.

Instead, **the approach is committed as a sequence, but its trajectory remains steerable**.

That is an important distinction. The player commits to approaching, not necessarily to a perfectly straight path.

It also means approach quality must account for the actual path traveled, including curvature, acceleration, alignment, and the final plant.

### 2. Locked facing does not mean locked attack direction

This scenario establishes three separate concepts:

- **Facing direction:** Locked at takeoff.
- **Attack trajectory:** Selected or influenced by the secondary action input.
- **Camera orientation:** Can be adjusted within the limits of the Athlete's available capabilities.

The hitter initially faces cross-court but can still attack down the line.

Therefore, facing should constrain the **available trajectory envelope**, rather than determine a single attack direction.

A cross-court-facing hitter might have access to several down-the-line trajectories, but not necessarily with the same power, precision, or stability as an attack aligned with their body.

This creates a useful role for ACC, particularly Timing and Stability.

Importantly, the secondary tap does not change the original Attack Charge. The hitter changes the intended direction, not the committed power.

### 3. Information is a meaningful part of attacking skill

The hitter's failure is not necessarily attributable to poor Attack Charge, approach quality, or contact execution.

They were unable to determine the defender's position because:

- Their airborne camera adjustment was limited.
- The opposing Block obstructed their view.
- They committed to a trajectory without complete information.

The defender successfully received the attack.

This is a desirable outcome because it creates a distinction between **mechanical execution and tactical information**.

An Athlete with greater camera freedom might identify the defender and select another trajectory. Alternatively, a player with better anticipation might make the correct decision without seeing the defender at all.

Neither requires an artificial accuracy penalty.

## One mechanical distinction remains

Your scenario describes the player switching to Athlete View to designate a cross-court aim angle, then using lateral movement input to attack down the line.

I would interpret the cross-court angle as the **body's committed orientation**, while the lateral input selects a trajectory within the available hitting envelope.

This interpretation preserves the value of facing direction without making late directional input redundant.

It also suggests that the visible airborne trajectory options should reflect the Athlete's actual physical capabilities, rather than simply presenting every direction as equally available.

For example, a sharply redirected attack might require greater Stability or a particular Ability Trait, whereas a naturally aligned attack might be accessible to almost everyone.

**The key design principle emerging from this scenario is that commitment should restrict options without eliminating decisions.**

The hitter commits to an approach, then to a facing direction, but retains meaningful choices about observation and final attack trajectory. Each commitment progressively narrows their available options.

And critically, even a well-executed sequence does not guarantee a successful point.

These clarifications establish a more precise distinction between **trajectory availability, trajectory information, and tactical effectiveness**.

### 1. Facing determines the available attack angles

The hitter's cross-court facing commitment does not prevent them from redirecting the ball toward the sideline. However, it constrains how far they can redirect it.

In the scenario:

- The hitter commits to a cross-court body orientation at takeoff.
- The airborne trajectory display presents several available Spike directions.
- A lateral input selects the trajectory closest to down-the-line.
- That trajectory is still angled somewhat cross-court because of the Athlete's committed facing.

This means the directional input is **relative to the Athlete's facing**, not an instruction to hit toward an absolute court location.

It also means the displayed trajectories should represent actual achievable outcomes, accounting for facing, Attributes, and relevant Traits.

### 2. Trajectory previews communicate physical possibilities, not defensive outcomes

The trajectory display is not a prediction of whether an Attack will score.

It communicates where the Athlete can send the ball.

The player must still interpret the opposing team's positioning.

In your example, the hitter might see:

| Available trajectory | Information available |
|---|---|
| Cross-court | Trajectory visibly intersects an opposing blocker |
| Intermediate angle | Trajectory is physically available; defensive outcome uncertain |
| Closest-to-line | Trajectory is available, but its landing area may be defended |
| Another trajectory into the Block | Clearly presents an opportunity to attack the blocker's hands |

The distinction is that **a visible trajectory intersecting a blocker does not necessarily indicate a bad attack**.

It may be exactly what the hitter wants.

### 3. Hitting off the hands becomes a deliberate attacking specialization

This is particularly interesting because it creates an attacking strategy that doesn't depend on finding open floor.

Consider two Athletes facing the same Block.

**Athlete A — Conventional power hitter**

The player sees that their preferred cross-court trajectory intersects the Block.

They redirect toward the sideline, accepting a less favorable attacking angle because their committed facing limits the adjustment.

The defender receives the ball.

**Athlete B — Hitting-off-the-hands specialist**

The player sees the same blocked cross-court trajectory.

Rather than redirecting away, they deliberately select a trajectory intersecting the outside of the blocker's hands.

Their build allows them to execute this contact more effectively, increasing the likelihood of a deflection that is difficult or impossible to recover.

The Block becomes an opportunity rather than merely an obstacle.

Crucially, **both Athletes can attempt the same attack**. The specialist is better at producing the desired outcome, rather than having exclusive access to the technique.

That seems like a strong candidate for an ACC-based Ability Trait, potentially involving Ball Control and Timing.

### 4. The resulting decision structure

We now have three distinct stages of attacking judgment:

**Before takeoff:** Choose an approach and facing direction that establish favorable attacking possibilities.

**While airborne:** Observe the defense and assess the available trajectories.

**At contact:** Select a trajectory, potentially exploiting the Block rather than avoiding it.

The trajectory display communicates the Athlete's capabilities, while the player's observation and anticipation determine which option is strategically preferable.

This also means we shouldn't automatically color trajectories as *good* or *bad* based on whether they intersect a blocker. A blocked trajectory could be desirable for one Athlete and undesirable for another.

### One design question this raises

Should hitting off the hands be resolved primarily through **the actual point of contact with the blocker's hands**, or should a specialist Trait modify the resulting deflection when the attacker deliberately targets a Block?

I lean toward the former as the foundation, with Traits improving the Athlete's ability to control contact location and deflection. That preserves physical consistency while allowing specialization.

### Exchange 076

Conversation turn ID: `c409eaa8-4517-486b-b705-d6b84915187f`


#### User

the former, yes. any other open questions?


#### Assistant

Yes. I think the core attacking interaction is now sufficiently established that we can move toward implementation, but there are **six remaining questions** that could materially affect the underlying physics or controls.

I'd distinguish these from numerical calibration, which can wait.

### 1. What happens when an approach cannot finish before the ball arrives?

**Scenario:** A hitter begins a full approach toward a fast Set, but the ball reaches their intended contact location while they're still two steps away from takeoff.

Does the Athlete:

- Continue the approach and miss the attacking opportunity?
- Automatically transition into an abbreviated jump?
- Have an opportunity to interrupt the approach and execute a weaker, improvised Attack?

**My preference:** The approach should not automatically rescue the player from poor timing. However, an intentional interruption could be an interesting mechanic, potentially accessible through a Trait.

This is probably the most important unresolved question.

### 2. How much can the player steer during an approach?

**Scenario:** A hitter begins a full approach diagonally toward the net. Halfway through, they recognize that the Set is drifting farther outside than anticipated.

They hold a lateral movement input to curve their approach.

We know this is permitted. What remains unclear is whether a sharp correction should:

- Simply change the Athlete's trajectory according to their movement capabilities.
- Reduce approach quality through the resulting loss of momentum, balance, or alignment.
- Become physically impossible when the required turn exceeds the Athlete's capabilities.

**My preference:** The second and third should emerge naturally from locomotion. There shouldn't be an additional arbitrary penalty for steering.

This is where Acceleration, Lateral Speed, Transition Speed, and Stability could interact.

### 3. What exactly determines the airborne trajectory options?

**Scenario:** Two hitters have identical facing directions and contact positions, but one has substantially greater Ball Control and Stability.

Do they see:

- The same trajectories, with different probabilities of executing them accurately?
- Different trajectory envelopes, with the more accurate Athlete able to redirect farther?
- The same general envelope, but different numbers or distributions of precise trajectory options?

**My preference:** A combination of the latter two.

Physical contact geometry should establish the broad envelope. Accuracy should determine how precisely the Athlete can exploit it.

I would avoid a system where the player chooses a perfectly valid trajectory and then a hidden accuracy roll sends the ball somewhere unrelated.

### 4. How are trajectory previews affected by the Block?

**Scenario:** A hitter sees three trajectories, two intersecting blockers.

The hitter selects one that grazes the outside hand.

The blocker simultaneously adjusts their positioning.

Should the displayed trajectory:

- Update continuously as the blocker moves?
- Show only the predicted ball trajectory, leaving the player to observe the Block independently?
- Include an intersection indicator based on the blocker's current physical position?

**My preference:** Display the ball's predicted trajectory, including its current intersection with visible geometry, but not the blocker's predicted future movement.

The hitter should be able to identify a possible hands-out attack without receiving privileged information about the defender's intentions.

This also preserves the importance of Timing.

### 5. What happens after a net collision?

**Scenario:** A hitter completes an aggressive approach, spikes successfully, but their forward momentum carries them into the net during landing.

Is the point immediately awarded to the defending team?

I'd say yes, assuming the contact qualifies as a net fault under our chosen rules.

More importantly, **the Athlete's physical commitment should persist through landing**.

Otherwise, players could exploit a powerful approach by immediately canceling their momentum after contact.

The same principle should apply to blockers.

One implementation question remains: whether we simulate the full body collision envelope or use a simplified representation of the torso, arms, and relevant limbs.

### 6. How does airborne camera freedom interact with Traits?

**Scenario:** Two hitters jump with identical facing commitments.

One has no relevant Trait. The other has the ACC-gated Trait that permits limited camera reorientation.

The defender moves behind the Block after takeoff.

The Trait-equipped hitter can rotate their camera sufficiently to see the movement. The other cannot.

The question is whether the Trait should affect:

- Only camera freedom.
- Camera freedom and the time available to inspect the defense.
- Camera freedom and the ability to make late directional corrections.

**My preference:** Only camera freedom.

The Trait should grant additional information, not automatically improve the Athlete's physical ability to redirect a Spike. Those should be separate specializations.

---

## Which questions matter most?

I'd prioritize them as follows:

| Priority | Question | Why |
|---|---|---|
| **1** | Interrupted approaches | Determines the commitment and failure model |
| **2** | Airborne trajectory generation | Determines how Attack inputs translate into physical outcomes |
| **3** | Approach steering | Determines how movement Attributes affect execution |
| 4 | Net collision and landing | Determines fault resolution |
| 5 | Block interaction with previews | Determines information available to attackers |
| 6 | Camera Trait boundaries | Determines specialization without changing core physics |

I think questions 3–6 already have fairly natural answers given the systems we've established.

**The genuinely consequential unresolved issue is the first: whether an Athlete can deliberately abandon a full approach to make an improvised contact.**

That decision will determine how forgiving the game is of poor anticipation and how much value players can extract from fast Sets, deceptive Sets, and unusual ball trajectories.

The current approach trajectory is projected to the player on the floor based on current movement inputs and camera angle. Adjusting this trajectory by strafing inputs and camera steering should be relatively forgiving, and the system is more that the Athlete follows the projection based on the player's movement inputs, not the player creating a projection via movement inputs. The latter would technically allow for and then necessitate a system that prevents illogical or impossible movement, whereas the former simply designates the projected movement that will occur, and any adjustment is based on the Athlete's capability to physically move.


#### Assistant

That distinction substantially simplifies the approach system. **The player controls a projected trajectory, and the Athlete follows that trajectory according to their physical capabilities.** The player isn't directly steering the Athlete's body and then waiting for the game to determine whether those inputs were physically plausible.

This also resolves most of my earlier questions about approach steering.

### 1. The projected trajectory is the authoritative movement preview

Consider a hitter beginning a full approach toward a high Set.

As they charge Jump, a trajectory appears on the floor, showing where the Athlete will travel under the current movement inputs and camera orientation.

The player rotates their camera slightly and holds a strafe input.

Rather than immediately redirecting the Athlete, these inputs modify the projected trajectory. The Athlete follows the updated path as their Acceleration, Lateral Speed, Transition Speed, and physical movement constraints permit.

The player sees the result of those constraints directly.

For example:

| Athlete | Same attempted trajectory adjustment | Projected result |
|---|---|---|
| High Acceleration, high Lateral Speed | Sharp redirection toward the outside antenna | Relatively tight, responsive curve |
| High Transition Speed, low Lateral Speed | Same adjustment | Wider curve preserving more forward momentum |
| Low movement Attributes | Same adjustment | Gradual redirection requiring more distance |
| High Stability | Same movement capabilities | Similar path, potentially better control at the eventual plant |

The key is that the projected path already accounts for what the Athlete can do.

There is no need to permit an impossible trajectory and subsequently reject it.

### 2. Approach quality follows from the executed trajectory

This changes how I'd interpret VWM's approach-quality calculations.

Previously, we considered penalizing sharp turns, poor alignment, and incomplete runway.

Under this model, the physical movement system already determines whether those conditions occur.

**The approach-quality system should evaluate the actual resulting movement, not independently penalize the player's steering commands.**

For example, a hitter makes a sharp trajectory adjustment just before takeoff.

If their movement capabilities allow them to complete the adjustment while maintaining sufficient momentum and balance, the approach remains effective.

If they cannot, the resulting trajectory might leave them poorly positioned or with insufficient momentum.

The penalty is a consequence of what physically happened, not the fact that they attempted to steer.

### 3. Full and partial approaches become spatial commitments

Consider two hitters approaching the same Set.

**Hitter A charges Jump fully.** Their projected trajectory shows a longer approach, ending near the net.

**Hitter B releases Jump Charge earlier.** Their projected trajectory shows a shorter approach, ending farther from the net.

The second hitter sacrifices some potential performance but avoids the risk of overrunning the intended contact position.

Crucially, the first hitter can see the danger before completing the approach.

If the projected path intersects the net, they have an opportunity to shorten the approach or adjust its trajectory.

This makes net faults substantially more defensible as a mechanic because the player receives meaningful information about the risk.

The projection should show the Athlete's expected movement, though not necessarily guarantee that subsequent changes in the environment or ball trajectory will preserve the same outcome.

### 4. Camera steering becomes an intuitive control mechanism

The player can remain in Ball View while shaping an approach around the Set.

They don't need to constantly switch back to Athlete View to correct their movement.

The camera establishes a directional reference, and movement inputs modify the projected path relative to it.

Then, near takeoff, they can switch to Athlete View to establish the desired facing direction.

This preserves the scenario you described:

**Ball View → curved approach → Athlete View → facing commitment → airborne trajectory selection → Spike.**

It also means the camera has two distinct functions depending on the action phase:

- **During approach:** Camera orientation helps determine the projected movement trajectory.
- **After takeoff:** Camera orientation primarily determines observation, while the Athlete's facing remains committed.

### 5. One implementation distinction matters

I would avoid treating the projection as a completely separate object that the Athlete tries to chase.

Instead, the projection should be a **forward simulation of the Athlete's movement under the current inputs**.

That ensures the projected trajectory and the actual trajectory obey the same movement rules.

Conceptually:

`Current Athlete state + movement inputs + camera orientation + movement capabilities → projected approach trajectory`

The Athlete then advances through that same movement model.

When the player changes inputs, the projection updates from the Athlete's current state.

This avoids a situation where the projected path is physically possible in isolation but impossible to reach from the Athlete's present position and momentum.

It also avoids the need for a second movement model dedicated to trajectory previews.

---

### What this resolves

We can now establish:

**Approach steering is forgiving because the player adjusts a physically feasible trajectory rather than directly commanding instantaneous changes in movement.**

The Athlete's capabilities determine the available curvature, acceleration, and displacement. The projected path communicates those limits before they become consequences.

That gives movement Attributes meaningful effects without requiring players to learn complicated movement restrictions.

It also creates a useful distinction between **mechanical difficulty and decision difficulty**: steering an approach can be relatively easy, while choosing the correct approach length, timing, position, and facing direction remains challenging.

**The remaining question I'd prioritize is whether the floor projection includes the anticipated takeoff point and contact position, or only the Athlete's ground movement.** Showing both would make the full-versus-partial approach decision considerably more legible, particularly when approaching the net.

Understood. This establishes a simpler relationship between the projection, Jump Charge, and takeoff.

**The floor projection represents the full ground approach available to the Athlete. Jump Charge determines how far along that approach the Athlete travels before taking off.**

The projection does not represent the Athlete's airborne trajectory or eventual landing position.

### Scenario: Full versus partial approach

A hitter prepares an Attack and begins charging Jump toward a high Set.

A projected approach appears on the floor, extending to the position where the Athlete would take off if Jump were fully charged.

As the Athlete advances, the player can steer the projected path through movement inputs and camera rotation. The projection updates according to the Athlete's movement capabilities.

The player has two options:

**Full approach:** Continue charging Jump until the Athlete reaches the projected endpoint, then take off.

**Partial approach:** Release Jump before reaching the endpoint, immediately transitioning into takeoff from the Athlete's current position.

This means the player isn't selecting an approach length in advance. **They are continuously deciding when to end an approach already in progress.**

That is a meaningful distinction.

### Scenario: Approaching too close to the net

A hitter begins charging Jump toward a Set near the net.

The full projection extends dangerously close to the net. The player recognizes that completing the approach may create an unfavorable takeoff position.

They release Jump early.

The Athlete takes off before reaching the projected endpoint, sacrificing some approach preparation but preserving a safer contact position.

Alternatively, the player continues charging, reaches the full endpoint, and jumps. Their forward momentum may carry them into the net.

The projection communicates the ground approach, but **the player must still account for airborne movement and contact geometry**.

This preserves the judgment involved in avoiding net faults without requiring the interface to predict every consequence.

### One important consequence

I would no longer describe Jump Charge as selecting an *intended approach length*.

More precisely:

> Charging Jump initiates an approach sequence and displays its full projected ground trajectory. The Athlete advances along this trajectory as the player maintains the charge, with movement inputs and camera steering continuously adjusting its course within the Athlete's physical capabilities. Releasing Jump initiates takeoff at the Athlete's current position, allowing the player to perform a partial approach rather than completing the full projected sequence. The projection does not include airborne displacement or landing position.

This also clarifies the relationship between Explosiveness and Jump Power.

Explosiveness can govern how quickly the Athlete progresses through the approach and prepares for takeoff. Jump Power determines their available vertical displacement, while the completed portion and quality of the approach determine how much of that capability they realize.

**One detail remains worth establishing:** If a player continues holding Jump after reaching the full projected endpoint, should takeoff occur automatically, or should the Athlete remain at the endpoint until Jump is released?

I favor automatic takeoff. Otherwise, players could exploit holding the charge indefinitely, and the approach would no longer function as a predictable movement commitment.

Agreed. **Automatic takeoff at the end of the projected approach** is now established.

The Jump sequence has three possible outcomes:

1. **Early release:** Releasing Jump before completing the approach immediately initiates takeoff from the Athlete's current position.
2. **Full charge:** Reaching the end of the projected approach automatically initiates takeoff, even if Jump remains held.
3. **Interrupted approach:** If the Athlete's approach is disrupted before takeoff, the resulting behavior remains to be defined.

The floor projection shows the full available ground approach, updates with steering inputs, and excludes airborne displacement and landing position.

This gives Jump Charge a clear function: **the player controls when to take off, up to the maximum approach length permitted by the Athlete's physical capabilities.**

The next useful question is what happens when an approach is interrupted—for example, by contact with another Athlete or an obstruction—before takeoff.

### Exchange 080

Conversation turn ID: `e66213f0-6f29-449b-8102-9d722bcb497a`


#### User

Firstly must also clarify that a Jump's Charge can be initiated without moving for the purposes of things like a standing jump for Jump Sets, Block Jumps, or even the Box Jump Parkour Drill; inputting movement during the charge still affects the resulting trajectory. Secondly, interruption ends the Jump entirely and does not refund the HP it costs to charge.


#### Assistant

These clarifications establish two important rules: **Jump Charge does not require movement**, and **interruption is distinct from voluntary cancellation**.

### 1. Jump Charge can begin from a stationary position

Charging Jump initiates the preparation sequence even when the Athlete is stationary. Movement inputs during the charge can still alter the projected trajectory.

This accommodates several scenarios:

| Scenario | Behavior |
|---|---|
| **Standing Jump Set** | Athlete charges Jump without moving, then releases to take off vertically. |
| **Standing Block Jump** | Athlete prepares a jump in place, without needing an approach. |
| **Box Jump Parkour Drill** | Athlete charges Jump while stationary, then jumps toward or onto the obstacle. |
| **Running Spike** | Athlete charges Jump while moving, following the projected approach until release or automatic takeoff. |
| **Stationary-to-moving approach** | Athlete begins charging while stationary, then introduces movement to develop an approach trajectory. |

This means we should distinguish **Jump preparation** from **approach displacement**.

Both can occur during the same charge, but horizontal movement is not a prerequisite for jumping.

### 2. Interruption terminates the Jump

An interruption immediately ends the Jump sequence.

- No takeoff occurs.
- The Jump Charge is lost.
- **HP spent charging Jump is not refunded.**
- The Athlete must initiate another Jump Charge to attempt a subsequent jump.

This differs from voluntarily canceling a grounded prepared Action, which refunds its initial HP investment.

An important distinction remains: interrupting Jump should not automatically cancel an independently prepared Attack, Set, Receive, or Block. Whether that Action remains prepared should depend on the interruption's effects, rather than being an implicit consequence of losing Jump.

### 3. Updated Jump rules

> **Jump Charge:** Charging Jump initiates jump preparation, with or without movement. A floor projection displays the full available ground approach, excluding airborne displacement and landing position. Movement inputs and camera steering adjust the projected trajectory according to the Athlete's physical capabilities. Releasing Jump initiates takeoff immediately, allowing a standing jump or partial approach. Completing the full projected approach causes automatic takeoff. If the Jump sequence is interrupted, it terminates without takeoff and without refunding the HP expended on Jump Charge.

### One remaining mechanical distinction

For stationary jumps, we should establish whether **holding Jump longer increases the available vertical displacement even without horizontal movement**.

My recommendation is yes, up to the Athlete's Jump Power ceiling.

That would make a fully prepared standing Block Jump more effective than an immediately released one, while allowing an approach to improve realized jumping performance through momentum, positioning, and execution quality.

It would also preserve Explosiveness as preparation speed rather than maximum jump height.

#### Assistant

These scenarios establish a more complete model of **trajectory selection, information sharing, and Moment of Contact (MoC)**. They also clarify that trajectory options are not necessarily a fixed directional menu: the available options depend on the Action, the Athlete's physical state, their Attributes, their Traits, and their previously committed Charge.

The Setter and Libero examples are particularly useful because they demonstrate opposite ends of the same system.

## 1. Scenario A: The Setter's Jump Set

The sequence establishes several distinct stages.

| Stage | Mechanic |
|---|---|
| Receive | The receiving Athlete selects a trajectory, visible to teammates participating in the rally. |
| Setter positioning | The Setter uses Ball View to move toward the incoming ball, informed by the Receive trajectory. |
| Set preparation | The Setter charges Set, establishing the intended power level and spending HP. |
| Jump preparation | The Setter switches to Athlete View, charges Jump briefly, and aims toward the left pin. |
| Takeoff | The Athlete's facing direction locks approximately perpendicular to the net. |
| Airborne preparation | The Setter sees the available Set trajectories, determined partly by the stored Set Charge. |
| Setter View | The Setter switches to a perpendicular camera perspective, looking lengthwise along the net. |
| Back Set Trait | Additional trajectories become available through M2 commands. |
| Moment of Contact | Bullet-time occurs immediately before contact, exposing available trajectories to teammates. |
| Execution | The Setter selects D+M2, sending the ball toward the right pin. |

Two important principles emerge.

### Facing determines the trajectory envelope, not the intended recipient

The Setter initially faces the left pin.

That orientation naturally supports Sets in that direction. However, the Back Set Trait provides access to additional trajectories behind or across the Athlete's facing.

The Setter chooses a right-pin Set, but the available geometry constrains the result.

Rather than freely delivering the ball to any position near the right antenna, the Setter must select a tighter trajectory or one positioned farther from the net.

**The Back Set Trait expands the available repertoire without eliminating the consequences of body orientation.**

This is consistent with the earlier Spike scenario.

### Setter View is an information advantage, not a physical advantage

Setter View provides a perpendicular view relative to the Athlete's facing direction.

In this scenario, because the Setter faces the left pin, switching to Setter View produces a lengthwise perspective of the net.

That helps the Setter inspect the distribution of attackers and blockers before choosing a trajectory.

It does not inherently improve the Set's power, precision, or physical reach.

This gives Setters a distinctive Innate without making their physical execution operate under different rules.

---

## 2. Trajectory options are action-specific

The contrast between the Setter and Libero is important.

**Setter:** Multiple trajectories are available, each associated with an input command and HP cost.

**Libero:** A diving Receive may offer only one available trajectory, regardless of movement input.

This suggests that trajectory selection should be understood as a variable set of physically achievable outcomes:

\[
\mathcal{T} =
f(\text{Action},\text{Charge},\text{Facing},\text{Contact State},\text{Attributes},\text{Traits})
\]

Here, \(\mathcal{T}\) is the set of trajectories the Athlete can execute from their current situation.

The formula is conceptual, not a proposed numerical implementation.

### Scenario: A difficult diving Receive

A Libero dives toward a rapidly descending Attack.

Their body is nearly horizontal, and the ball is approaching the limit of their contact envelope.

At Moment of Contact, only one viable Receive trajectory appears.

The player cannot select an alternative simply by holding another direction.

This is not necessarily because the Libero lacks Ball Control. Their current physical circumstances may leave only one feasible way to redirect the ball.

A more favorable contact position might have produced additional options.

That creates a useful distinction:

**Attributes and Traits expand what an Athlete can accomplish, but physical circumstances determine which capabilities are presently usable.**

---

## 3. Moment of Contact is a shared information event

This is the largest new system established by the example.

Moment of Contact is not exclusive to attacking.

**Every ball contact triggers a brief bullet-time interval.**

During that interval, the acting Athlete sees their available trajectories, associated input commands, and HP costs.

Teammates participating in the rally can also see the available trajectory options.

The acting Athlete then commits to one of them.

This creates an interesting distinction between **pre-contact information** and **post-selection information**.

### Scenario: Setter deception

The Setter reaches Moment of Contact with six available trajectories.

Three go toward the left pin. Three go toward the right.

Their teammates can see all six options, but they do not yet know which will be selected.

The Setter chooses D+M2.

The team then sees the selected right-pin trajectory, allowing the right-side hitter to respond.

The opposing team should not automatically receive the same information.

That would preserve the value of disguising Sets through body orientation and Traits.

One caveat: although teammates can see the available trajectories, they still need enough time to act on that information. The brief bullet-time should assist coordination without making every offensive sequence perfectly predictable.

---

## 4. HP costs belong to individual trajectory options

The Setter's options have distinct HP costs.

That suggests the stored initial Set Charge does not uniquely determine the final HP expenditure.

Instead, it establishes a baseline commitment, while the selected trajectory may require additional expenditure.

For example, a difficult Back Set could cost more than a straightforward forward Set at the same initial Charge.

However, we should preserve the previously established secondary-charge rules:

- Initial Charge expenditure remains committed.
- A secondary tap does not change the stored Charge.
- A secondary charge modifies the effective Charge within a narrower range and incurs additional HP cost.

**The new question is whether a trajectory's displayed HP cost includes the initial expenditure or only the additional cost of executing that trajectory.**

I'd favor displaying the additional cost prominently, with total expenditure available separately. The initial expenditure has already occurred and cannot be recovered through the selection.

Importantly, a difficult trajectory should not automatically count as secondary charging. Choosing D+M2 with a tap can still preserve the original Set Charge.

---

## 5. Camera views now have three distinct functions

| Camera | Function |
|---|---|
| **Athlete View** | General orientation and deliberate facing commitment |
| **Ball View** | Track the ball and coordinate movement relative to its trajectory |
| **Setter View** | Perpendicular perspective relative to Athlete facing, available Innately to Setters |

The Setter View example establishes that camera perspective can be position-specific.

This is separate from ACC-gated camera Traits, which can grant limited additional observational freedom.

A Setter might therefore have access to Setter View by virtue of their position, while another Athlete might need a Trait to gain greater camera flexibility.

These mechanics should remain independent of the Athlete's physical facing.

---

## 6. Two questions remain especially important

### A. Does Moment of Contact pause the ball for everyone?

**Scenario:** A Setter enters MoC while two hitters are approaching and three defenders are repositioning.

Does bullet-time slow the entire rally, including movement and the ball, or does it only give the acting player additional decision time?

I favor globally synchronized bullet-time. Otherwise, the Setter's additional decision time could produce synchronization problems between what different players observe.

However, we should establish whether teammates can still make meaningful movement inputs during this interval.

### B. Are trajectories discrete choices or continuous possibilities?

**Scenario:** A Setter has five visible Set trajectories, labeled W+M1, A+M1, S+M1, D+M1, and D+M2.

Does each command correspond to one discrete trajectory, or does holding the input permit continuous adjustment around that trajectory?

Your examples suggest **discrete, physically generated options**.

I think that's preferable because it makes the interface readable, especially during a short MoC interval.

The Athlete's Attributes, Charge, facing, and contact geometry determine which options are available. The player selects among them rather than manually adjusting the ball's exact flight path.

This also gives Traits a natural design space: they can introduce additional trajectory options, change their geometry, or improve the Athlete's execution of particular contacts.

---

### Current design principle

**Every contact produces a set of physically achievable trajectories, determined by the Athlete's preparation, orientation, contact geometry, Attributes, and Traits. Moment of Contact briefly slows play, displays these options and their input commands, and allows the acting player to select an outcome. Teammates can observe the options and subsequently see the selected trajectory, while position-specific camera views support informed decisions.**

The remaining questions concern how bullet-time affects other players and whether trajectory selection is strictly discrete. Those are worth resolving before designing the underlying trajectory-generation system.
