#### Assistant

This establishes a substantially different model of **Moment of Contact (MoC)** from the one I described previously. Most importantly, **MoC is an impact event, not a decision window**. Players make their decisions before contact, and MoC briefly emphasizes the resulting physical interaction.

It also establishes a distinction between three kinds of trajectory information:

1. **Intended trajectories:** What an Athlete is attempting to produce.
2. **Actual trajectories:** What the ball physically does after contact.
3. **Contact opportunities:** Where an Athlete can physically intercept the ball.

These should remain separate throughout the system.

## 1. Moment of Contact: impact, not preparation

Your rally illustrates that MoC duration and timing can vary by Action, Charge, and Attributes.

| Contact | What happens | MoC behavior |
|---|---|---|
| Weak Serve | Ball enters play with relatively little force | Minimal or potentially imperceptible MoC |
| Powerful Serve | Ball is struck with greater force | Slightly longer MoC, despite the faster resulting ball |
| Neutral Receive | OH double-taps Receive without directional input | Brief MoC; one intended trajectory |
| Set | Setter confirms a trajectory before contact | MoC occurs at the actual Set contact, with Timing influencing when that contact can occur |
| Spike | Attacker swings and physically contacts the ball | MoC occurs at impact, not when the attacker selects their trajectory |
| Block | Ball contacts the blocker's hands | A separate MoC occurs, potentially immediately after the Spike's MoC |

The important consequence is that **players do not receive extra time to choose an action simply because MoC occurs**.

They can continue orienting themselves during the impact frames, but the relevant contact decision has already been made.

This corrects my earlier interpretation of MoC as a globally synchronized trajectory-selection interval.

### Scenario: A deceptive Set

A Setter has several available trajectories.

Their teammates can see these possibilities before contact. The Setter chooses one and confirms it.

A high-Timing Setter may be able to commit slightly earlier or later relative to the incoming ball.

An earlier commitment might preserve a deceptive body animation. A later commitment might occur after an opposing blocker has already committed to moving.

The MoC then occurs when the Set physically contacts the ball.

**Timing therefore influences the temporal placement of contact, not simply the duration of bullet-time.**

That is a more interesting responsibility for the Attribute.

---

## 2. Set trajectories and attack contact envelopes are different objects

Your attacker example clarifies something particularly important.

The Set trajectory establishes the ball's predicted path through three-dimensional space.

Each Athlete interprets that path relative to their own physical capabilities.

A tall OH might see a viable attacking contact location above the net.

A short Libero might see the corresponding opportunity as physically unreachable, obstructed by the net, or otherwise unsuitable.

The ball trajectory itself hasn't changed.

**The Athlete's physical contact envelope determines whether and where that trajectory can be intercepted.**

This suggests two distinct visualizations.

| Visualization | Represents |
|---|---|
| **Ball trajectory** | The expected path of the ball after the preceding contact |
| **Contact opportunity** | The portion of that path accessible to the observing Athlete's physical envelope |

For attacking, the relevant contact opportunity is more restrictive than simply being able to touch the ball.

An Athlete might be capable of contacting the ball but unable to perform an effective Spike from that position.

Their ideal spiking envelope should therefore reflect reach, jumping capability, orientation, and relevant contact geometry.

### Scenario: Two hitters reading the same Set

A Set travels toward the left pin.

A 6'7" hitter sees an accessible contact opportunity above the net.

A 5'5" hitter sees a substantially different opportunity, potentially one that would require an exceptional jump or an unsuitable contact position.

Both see the same ball trajectory.

They do not see the same feasible attacking opportunity.

This gives physique a direct tactical consequence without introducing arbitrary positional restrictions.

---

## 3. Attack trajectories are determined by the hitter's physical envelope

This is another important correction.

I previously treated attack trajectories as being generated relative to the ball's current position.

Your example establishes that the Athlete's **physical hitting envelope** is the primary determinant of the available attacking trajectories, assuming the ball reaches an appropriate contact position.

The player must arrange for those two things to coincide.

### Scenario: Late cross-court Spike

A right-handed OH approaches a Set at the left pin.

They position themselves for a cross-court Attack.

Their available trajectory options reflect their facing, reach, Jump Power, and other relevant capabilities.

They choose the cross-court option.

However, their confirmation input is slightly late.

The ball has moved beyond the ideal point of contact.

The intended cross-court trajectory is no longer physically achievable in exactly the same way.

Instead, the resulting Spike travels more centrally.

The important distinction is:

**The player's chosen trajectory remains cross-court, but the ball's actual trajectory differs because contact occurred outside the ideal hitting envelope.**

This should not necessarily be treated as a random Accuracy failure.

It is a deterministic or physically derived consequence of contact timing and geometry.

Accuracy Attributes may influence the Athlete's tolerance for imperfect contact, but they should not erase the physical distinction.

---

## 4. Intended trajectories are not guaranteed trajectories

This is central to your information model.

The attacking team sees the hitter's chosen trajectory.

They do **not** automatically see the truthful resulting trajectory after an imperfect contact.

In the example:

- The hitter intends to attack cross-court.
- The attack actually travels toward central court.
- The attacking team has access to the intended cross-court trajectory.
- The defending team must interpret the resulting ball movement visually.

This creates a distinction between **team communication** and **perfect information**.

Allied trajectory sharing communicates intentions.

It does not guarantee that the intended action will succeed.

That is particularly important because otherwise a trajectory interface could unintentionally eliminate much of the perceptual skill involved in volleyball.

---

## 5. Blocking is fundamentally different from setting or attacking

The MB example establishes that a Block does not involve selecting the ball's outgoing trajectory.

Instead, the blocker influences the physical conditions of the collision.

### Scenario: Leftward Block adjustment

The MB anticipates the Set and moves into position.

They charge Block and initiate a Block Jump, benefiting from their positional Innate.

Before the attacker's contact, they input a leftward influence.

That changes the orientation or positioning of their blocking hands.

The hitter attempts a cross-court Spike, but imperfect timing sends the ball more centrally.

Because of the MB's leftward adjustment, the ball contacts their hands.

The collision produces a deflection back toward the attacking team's court.

A new MoC occurs at that collision.

**The blocker did not choose the deflection trajectory. They chose a blocking posture that made that deflection possible.**

This distinction is essential.

It also supports the earlier decision that hitting off the hands should depend on actual contact geometry rather than a special trajectory-selection rule.

### Why this matters for Traits

A blocking Trait might improve:

- The range of available hand orientations.
- The speed of adjusting hand position.
- Stability during contact.
- Control over the angle of the blocking surface.

But it shouldn't simply allow the blocker to select an arbitrary outgoing ball trajectory.

Likewise, a hitting-off-the-hands Trait should improve the attacker's ability to create favorable hand contact, not directly prescribe the resulting deflection.

---

## 6. Trajectory visibility is asymmetric

Your example establishes an important information asymmetry.

| Event | Attacking team | Defending team |
|---|---|---|
| Setter prepares Set | Can see allied trajectory options | Must interpret the Setter's actions visually |
| Setter confirms Set | Sees intended trajectory | Observes the resulting ball |
| Hitter prepares Spike | Can see allied intended trajectory | Must read the approach, facing, and swing |
| Hitter contacts ball imperfectly | Still has intended trajectory information, not guaranteed actual trajectory | Must react to actual ball motion |
| Blocker deflects Spike | Must visually interpret the deflection | Sees allied block's resulting trajectory |

This is a strong basis for team coordination.

**Trajectory information belongs to the team responsible for the contact, but that information is not universally truthful.**

There is one nuance worth preserving: the blocker does not select the deflection. The displayed trajectory is generated by the physical collision.

That makes a Block different from a Set or Spike even though all three can produce MoC events.

---

## 7. What this changes about the underlying architecture

I would now separate the system into four components.

**A. Action preparation**

The Athlete selects and charges an Action, establishes a physical commitment, and prepares for contact.

**B. Contact opportunity**

The system evaluates the incoming ball's trajectory against the Athlete's physical envelope, producing a viable interception region.

**C. Intended execution**

The Athlete selects an available trajectory or, for a Block, a contact posture. These choices are communicated to allies where appropriate.

**D. Physical resolution**

The actual contact occurs. The system resolves the ball's resulting trajectory from the physical state, timing, and contact geometry.

MoC emphasizes this final event.

This separation is especially important for adapting VWM. We should not make the trajectory preview itself the authority on the ball's resulting movement.

The physical contact calculation must remain authoritative.

---

## 8. Remaining questions

I think this resolves most of the conceptual uncertainty around trajectory selection. Three questions are still consequential.

**First: How does MoC affect the simulation clock?**

Your description establishes that players can continue orienting themselves during MoC. Does the entire simulation slow down for those frames, including the ball and Athlete movement, or does MoC primarily affect presentation?

I'd favor a short synchronized slowdown, but not a complete pause.

**Second: When does a trajectory become visible to teammates?**

The Setter example establishes that allied options can be visible before MoC.

Should teammates see the complete option set whenever an allied Athlete is preparing contact, or only once that Athlete is sufficiently close to an actionable contact opportunity?

I would lean toward the latter to avoid cluttering the court with trajectory previews.

**Third: How is the truthful ball trajectory revealed after contact?**

The Block example establishes that the defending team sees its resulting deflection trajectory, while the attacking team does not.

Does the trajectory remain visible to the defending team throughout the ball's flight, or only briefly following MoC?

That matters because it determines how much trajectory sharing assists subsequent Receives.

### Most important conclusion

The design now has a coherent distinction between **intent, capability, and physical outcome**:

- **Intent:** The trajectory or contact posture selected by the player.
- **Capability:** What the Athlete's Attributes, physique, preparation, and Traits allow.
- **Outcome:** What physically happens when the Athlete contacts the ball.

MoC marks the transition from intent to outcome. It does not replace the player's judgment beforehand.

That distinction should remain foundational as we move into the implementation model.


---

#### Assistant

I think this revision produces a stronger system. In particular, **trajectory options should be information that takes time to become available, rather than a complete menu presented whenever an Athlete prepares an action.**

It also establishes that different contacts communicate different information to teammates.

## 1. Revised trajectory visibility rules

| Action | Acting Athlete sees | Allies see |
|---|---|---|
| **Serve** | Available Serve trajectories, subject to preparation | Chosen Serve trajectory |
| **Receive** | Available Receive trajectories, limited by preparation time | Only the chosen Receive trajectory |
| **Set** | Available Set trajectories once preparing the Set | All available Set trajectories, then the chosen trajectory |
| **Spike** | Available Spike trajectories, limited by preparation time | Only the chosen Spike trajectory |
| **Block** | No selectable outgoing trajectory; controls blocking posture | Only the resulting deflection trajectory |

The Serve row is a provisional extension of the other rules; its visibility behavior hasn't been explicitly established.

The important distinction is that **Set is uniquely collaborative**. Teammates can see the Setter's options before the Setter commits, whereas Receives and Spikes communicate only the selected intention.

This gives setting a distinctive information role beyond simply transferring the ball.

## 2. Scenario: Hard-driven Spike versus lofted free ball

### Hard-driven Spike

An opposing hitter drives the ball toward the receiving OH.

The OH has already prepared Receive, but the incoming ball is traveling quickly.

The Athlete has very little time to identify possible outgoing trajectories.

Perhaps only the neutral Receive trajectory becomes available before contact.

The OH double-taps Receive.

A brief MoC occurs, and teammates see the chosen neutral trajectory.

**The Athlete may have been physically capable of producing other trajectories, but there wasn't enough time to make those options available.**

This is an important distinction between physical capability and available decision-making time.

### Lofted free ball

The opposing team sends a slow, high ball over the net.

The receiving Libero prepares Receive well before the ball arrives.

As the ball approaches, additional Receive trajectories become available.

The Libero can inspect the options and select a controlled pass toward the Setter.

Teammates see only the selected Receive trajectory, not the alternatives considered.

The Libero benefits from the additional preparation time without requiring the system to artificially improve their Ball Control.

### Implication

The same Athlete, with identical Attributes and Receive Charge, can have different trajectory options available depending on the incoming ball.

That creates a meaningful distinction between **what an Athlete can physically execute** and **what they have time to prepare and select**.

I would preserve this distinction in the implementation.

---

## 3. Scenario: High Set versus quick Set

The same principle applies to attacking.

### High Set

The Setter delivers a high ball toward the left pin.

The OH has time to prepare a full approach.

As the Athlete approaches their anticipated contact point, several Spike trajectories become available.

They can evaluate cross-court, down-the-line, and potential hands-out opportunities before committing.

The OH chooses a trajectory.

Their teammates see that selected intention.

The actual Spike may still differ because of imperfect contact.

### Quick Set

The Setter delivers a fast Set toward the MB.

The MB begins a shortened approach.

Because the ball reaches the attacking window quickly, fewer Spike trajectories become available.

The MB might have only a central or slightly angled attack available by the time contact occurs.

However, the opposing Block also has less time to respond.

This creates a direct tradeoff:

| | High Set | Quick Set |
|---|---|---|
| Attacker preparation time | More | Less |
| Potential trajectory options | More | Fewer |
| Defensive preparation time | More | Less |
| Opportunity for late decisions | Greater | Smaller |
| Dependence on anticipation | Lower | Higher |

There is an important qualification: a highly prepared MB should not necessarily lose options simply because the Set is fast.

If they correctly anticipate the quick Set, they may have enough time to prepare trajectories before the ball reaches them.

**Preparation should depend on the Athlete's actual state and available time, not simply on whether the Set is categorized as quick or high.**

---

## 4. How trajectories become available

I would separate three conditions.

**Physical feasibility:** The trajectory must be achievable from the Athlete's body orientation, contact envelope, Charge, and Attributes.

**Preparation:** The Athlete must have progressed sufficiently through the relevant action to access the trajectory.

**Time remaining:** The trajectory must become available before the Athlete commits to contact.

For example, a hitter might be physically capable of five attacking trajectories but have time to prepare only two.

The other three aren't necessarily physically impossible. They simply haven't become available.

This creates a useful potential responsibility for **Explosiveness and Timing**, but I would distinguish their effects.

Explosiveness primarily governs physical preparation.

Timing governs the Athlete's ability to coordinate action execution with the incoming ball.

We should avoid turning both into interchangeable bonuses to trajectory availability.

---

## 5. Moment of Contact is now more precisely defined

We can establish:

**MoC is a synchronized, brief slowdown triggered by actual ball contact.**

Its duration varies according to the nature and intensity of the contact.

Players retain the ability to orient themselves during the slowdown.

MoC does not automatically grant additional trajectory choices or delay the acting Athlete's decision until contact.

This means a powerful Spike followed almost immediately by a Block can produce two successive MoCs.

The second contact may radically change the ball's trajectory, and only the blocking team receives the resulting deflection projection.

The attacking team must interpret the deflection visually.

That preserves the asymmetry you described.

---

## 6. One issue worth examining: how options appear

There are two possible interpretations of your revised rule.

**Model A — Progressive revelation**

An Athlete begins with a small number of trajectories. More options become visible as preparation progresses.

For example, a hitter might first see a central Spike, then cross-court, then down-the-line, then more specialized trajectories.

**Model B — Preparation-dependent availability**

All physically feasible trajectories are generated, but only those the Athlete has sufficient time to prepare become selectable. The available options might change with movement, facing, or contact timing rather than following a fixed sequence.

I favor **Model B as the underlying mechanic**, with progressive revelation as its visual presentation.

That would prevent a rigid rule such as "cross-court always unlocks before down-the-line."

Instead, a hitter facing cross-court might immediately have access to a cross-court attack, while a down-the-line redirection requires additional preparation.

A hitter facing down-the-line could experience the reverse.

This preserves the importance of facing commitment and allows Traits to change how quickly or effectively particular options become available.

### The next question

**Should an Athlete's preparation of a particular trajectory persist if the ball's arrival is delayed or its path changes?**

For example, an OH prepares a cross-court Spike, but the Set drifts farther outside than expected.

The Athlete adjusts their approach and remains within their viable hitting envelope.

Does the previously available cross-court option remain prepared, or must the system recalculate its availability based on the new contact geometry?

My preference is to **preserve preparation but recalculate physical feasibility**. The Athlete shouldn't lose preparation simply because the ball moves, but they also shouldn't retain an impossible trajectory after their contact position changes.

I'd distinguish three things:

1. **Trajectory feasibility:** What the Athlete can physically attempt.
2. **Trajectory prediction:** How precisely the Athlete can anticipate the resulting ball flight.
3. **Execution difficulty:** How demanding the selected contact is, given the Athlete's position, timing, and physical capabilities.

These should be related but not interchangeable.

## Scenario 1: Two hitters, identical physical capabilities, different Accuracy

Both hitters approach the same Set, jump from the same position, and face down the line.

They have identical Jump Power, Swing Power, and physical contact geometry.

| | Low-ACC hitter | High-ACC hitter |
|---|---|---|
| Available input | D+M1 | D+M1 |
| Trajectory visualization | General down-the-line direction | Detailed projected trajectory |
| Boundary information | Cannot confidently distinguish in-bounds from out-of-bounds | Can see that the predicted landing point is slightly outside |
| Defensive information | General attacking lane | More precise intersection with visible defenders |
| Execution | Less reliable | More reliable |

The low-ACC hitter isn't prevented from attempting the attack.

They simply have less information about its likely outcome.

This also means that **a low-ACC player can learn to compensate through experience**. They might recognize that a particular approach angle frequently sends the ball long, even without receiving precise visual feedback.

That seems desirable.

### One important qualification

The high-ACC preview should represent a more precise *prediction*, not an infallible statement about the future.

If the hitter contacts the ball later than anticipated, the resulting trajectory can still differ.

Likewise, a defender can move after the preview appears.

So a high-ACC hitter might see a trajectory passing directly toward a defender's current position, but that does not mean the defender will necessarily remain there.

---

## Scenario 2: A difficult short-court Attack

A hitter is airborne near the left pin.

They have charged a powerful Attack, but want to place the ball sharply into the opposing front court.

The short-court trajectory is physically available.

However, the Athlete's current body orientation makes that contact difficult.

Both a low-ACC and high-ACC hitter can attempt it.

**Low-ACC hitter:**

The interface displays:

`S+M1 — Short-court Attack — 8 HP`

The player sees the general trajectory but receives limited information about its difficulty.

**High-ACC hitter:**

The interface displays:

`S+M1 — Short-court Attack — 8 HP`

Alongside it, the player sees a more precise trajectory and an indication that the current contact geometry introduces substantial execution difficulty.

For example:

`Contact difficulty: High`

The higher-ACC player might recognize that this option is likely to produce an inaccurate result, despite being physically available.

This creates a useful advantage: **the Athlete can identify a poor option before committing to it.**

But I would be careful about representing this as an additional HP penalty.

### Why I wouldn't automatically increase HP cost

Suppose the contact is difficult because the Athlete is poorly aligned with the ball.

That difficulty should primarily affect the likelihood or magnitude of execution error.

Charging extra HP simply because the contact is awkward risks penalizing the same mistake twice.

It also creates an odd situation where a player with higher Accuracy sees an additional HP cost that a lower-Accuracy player doesn't.

The actual cost must be the same for both Athletes if their physical states and relevant resource efficiencies are identical.

**Higher Accuracy should reveal a cost or difficulty that already exists, not create it.**

There could still be actions that genuinely require additional HP—for example, a particularly demanding redirection or a specialized contact technique. In those cases, the higher-ACC preview could reveal the full cost more precisely.

But physical difficulty and HP expenditure should remain separate quantities.

---

## Scenario 3: Accuracy reveals the difference between a difficult and an expensive Attack

Consider two short-court trajectories.

**Trajectory A:** A relatively gentle, well-aligned tip requiring 5 HP.

**Trajectory B:** A sharp redirection from an awkward body position requiring 8 HP and presenting substantial contact difficulty.

A low-ACC hitter might see both trajectories and their HP costs, but only approximate landing locations.

A high-ACC hitter sees the same options and costs, along with more precise landing predictions and an indication of Trajectory B's execution difficulty.

The higher-ACC hitter can make a more informed decision.

Crucially, Trajectory B isn't necessarily inferior. It might be the only trajectory that avoids a Block.

The system communicates the tradeoff without making the decision for the player.

---

## How the three Accuracy statistics could contribute

I would provisionally divide their responsibilities this way:

| Statistic | Responsibility |
|---|---|
| **Ball Control** | Precision of outgoing ball trajectories and ability to produce demanding redirections |
| **Timing** | Precision of contact timing and tolerance for imperfect synchronization |
| **Stability** | Consistency of execution under awkward posture, movement, and contact geometry |

However, I would be cautious about assigning all trajectory-preview detail to Ball Control.

**The overarching Accuracy Attribute could determine information precision**, while its granular statistics determine the physical reasons particular actions are easier or harder.

For example, a high-Timing Setter might see a more precise indication of when a Set can be contacted successfully, whereas a high-Ball-Control hitter might see more precise outgoing trajectories.

This would make the information advantage relevant to the Athlete's actual specialization.

## One distinction I'd formalize

There are two fundamentally different ways a trajectory can be difficult:

**Geometrically difficult:** The Athlete's body position makes the intended contact hard to execute.

**Technically difficult:** The trajectory requires unusually precise ball control, even from a favorable contact position.

A short-court Attack could be difficult for either reason.

The first might primarily involve Stability and Timing.

The second might primarily involve Ball Control.

A high-ACC Athlete should be better at both, but the system should preserve the distinction because it creates different opportunities for Traits and specialization.

---

### Revised trajectory model

I'd establish the following:

> All physically available trajectory inputs remain selectable regardless of Accuracy. Accuracy determines the precision and detail of the Athlete's trajectory previews, including predicted landing position and, where applicable, contact difficulty. The Athlete's physical state, Charge, and Traits determine the available trajectories and their execution requirements. Accuracy also affects the Athlete's ability to execute the selected trajectory, but does not guarantee that the resulting ball follows the preview.

This replaces my earlier suggestion that trajectories progressively become selectable based on preparation time.

**The remaining question is whether low Accuracy should merely make predictions less detailed, or whether it should also make them visibly less certain—for example, showing a broad landing region rather than a precise line.**

I favor the latter. A broad predicted landing region communicates uncertainty without hiding the fact that the trajectory exists, and it naturally becomes narrower as Accuracy increases.

**Yes, I think ACC is at risk of becoming disproportionately influential—but not because of the mechanics you've just proposed.** The problem is how many distinct advantages we might inadvertently assign to it.

Your revised interpretation is stronger than mine. In particular, I agree that a high-ACC Athlete should be able to see a genuinely higher HP cost that a low-ACC Athlete fails to recognize. The cost is not created by Accuracy; it exists in the physical execution of the action, and Accuracy determines how accurately the Athlete anticipates it.

I also think **both confirmation timing and approach/contact geometry should contribute to difficulty**, but they should do so in different ways.

## 1. Two kinds of difficulty: foreseeable and realized

Consider your short-court Attack.

The hitter is approaching from near the sideline, relatively far from the net. Their body is oriented toward a cross-court attack.

They want to send the ball sharply into the opposing short court.

There are two independent problems.

**Geometric difficulty:** Even with perfect timing, the desired trajectory requires an awkward contact angle. The hitter must redirect the ball more sharply than their position naturally supports.

**Execution difficulty:** The hitter must actually contact the ball at the correct moment to achieve that trajectory.

The first can be evaluated before the swing. The second cannot be known completely until contact.

I'd distinguish them as follows:

| Component | Determined by | Known before contact? |
|---|---|---|
| Base HP cost | Action Charge and selected trajectory | Yes |
| Geometric difficulty | Athlete facing, ball position, contact angle, posture | Predictable |
| Timing difficulty | Required precision of the contact window | Predictable |
| Actual timing error | Difference between intended and actual contact | Only at execution |
| Actual HP expenditure | Realized physical execution | Only fully known at execution |
| Resulting trajectory | Actual contact geometry and timing | Only after contact |

The crucial distinction is between **predicted expenditure** and **actual expenditure**.

A high-ACC hitter can predict that a short-court Attack will cost substantially more HP than the nominal trajectory suggests.

A low-ACC hitter might see only the nominal cost.

Both are subject to the same physical requirements.

This also gives us a reason for predicted costs to change during an approach: the Athlete's position, posture, and expected contact point are changing.

---

## 2. Scenario: The difficult short-court Attack

Let's use illustrative numbers, not proposed balance values.

Two hitters have identical physical capabilities except for Accuracy.

Both have prepared an Attack with an initial investment of 10 HP.

Both approach from an unfavorable position near the sideline.

The desired short-court Spike requires an unusually precise contact angle.

### Athlete A: Low Accuracy

The player sees:

**Short-court Attack — 4 additional HP**

The displayed trajectory appears to reach the intended short-court area.

However, the actual geometry makes the attack significantly more demanding.

The player commits to the trajectory.

Their confirmation input is slightly late.

The ball strikes the net.

The actual additional expenditure is 8 HP, rather than the predicted 4.

**Total HP expenditure: 18.**

### Athlete B: High Accuracy

The player sees:

**Short-court Attack — 8 additional HP**

The trajectory preview also reveals a narrow successful contact window.

The player recognizes the difficulty but commits anyway.

They confirm at the appropriate moment.

The ball clears the net and lands in the intended short-court area.

**Total HP expenditure: 18.**

The two players spent the same amount because they attempted the same demanding action from comparable physical circumstances.

Their different outcomes came from execution.

However, there's a subtle issue here.

If the low-ACC hitter's late confirmation genuinely makes the contact more physically demanding, their actual expenditure could exceed 18 HP.

That leads to an important question: **does a mistake itself increase expenditure, or does it merely waste expenditure already committed to an unsuccessful action?**

I think the answer should depend on the kind of mistake.

A poorly timed but otherwise ordinary Attack might simply waste its committed HP.

A desperate, off-balance correction could incur additional HP because the Athlete actually performs a more demanding movement.

I would not make every flubbed contact automatically more expensive. Otherwise, Accuracy becomes an indirect resource-efficiency statistic, overlapping with Exertion Efficiency.

---

## 3. Scenario: The low-ACC hitter recognizes the problem anyway

This is the strongest part of your proposal.

The low-ACC hitter sees an apparently viable short-court trajectory with an inexpensive HP estimate.

However, the player recognizes from experience that their approach has left them too far from the net.

They decide not to trust the preview.

Instead, they use secondary charging to reduce their effective Attack Charge.

Because secondary charging is expensive and inefficient, this correction incurs its own HP cost.

The resulting Attack is weaker, but its lower striking force makes a safer, higher-arcing trajectory achievable.

The hitter clears the net.

The high-ACC hitter in the same situation might recognize an additional option: a slightly more aggressive improvised Attack that remains within the feasible contact envelope.

Both players can make intelligent decisions.

**Accuracy provides better information and physical execution capability, but it does not replace player judgment.**

This is a particularly desirable relationship for a competitive game.

It means low-ACC Athletes are not condemned to making mistakes. Their players can learn the limitations of their previews.

---

## 4. Where ACC risks becoming too powerful

Consider everything we've discussed assigning to Accuracy.

| ACC benefit | Current design direction | Concern |
|---|---|---|
| More precise trajectory previews | Yes | Appropriate |
| More accurate HP-cost estimates | Yes | Appropriate |
| Better contact timing | Yes | Appropriate |
| More reliable ball placement | Yes | Appropriate |
| Greater tolerance for awkward contact geometry | Potentially | Significant additional advantage |
| Access to otherwise unavailable attack angles | Potentially | Could become excessive |
| Additional airborne camera freedom | Trait-gated | Appropriate if specialized |
| Additional trajectory options | Trait-gated or physical | Appropriate if specialized |
| Reduced HP expenditure on difficult contacts | Potentially | Overlaps END |
| Greater ability to correct mistakes | Potentially | Could overlap Timing, Stability, and secondary charging |

The concern is not any one benefit.

It's that **ACC could improve information, execution, physical versatility, and resource efficiency simultaneously**.

Meanwhile, the other Attributes have narrower responsibilities.

POW governs explosive performance.

SPD governs movement.

END governs resource capacity and sustainability.

ACC could end up governing almost everything that happens at contact—which is the decisive moment in volleyball.

That deserves attention.

### The distinction I'd enforce

**ACC should govern precision, not physical capability.**

That doesn't mean Accuracy cannot expand what an Athlete can successfully accomplish.

It means it should expand their ability to exploit existing physical possibilities, rather than independently granting new physical capabilities.

For example:

- Ball Control improves how precisely an Athlete can direct the ball.
- Timing improves synchronization with the incoming ball.
- Stability improves consistency under difficult body conditions.

But ACC should not directly increase maximum Jump Power, Swing Power, or horizontal reach.

Nor should it simply make every demanding action cheaper.

Those distinctions preserve the other Attributes' importance.

---

## 5. A better way to model difficult geometry

I think the short-court example suggests that we should distinguish **physical feasibility** from **precision requirements**.

Imagine an Athlete's possible contact outcomes as a continuous region.

Some outgoing trajectories occupy broad, forgiving portions of that region.

Others occupy narrow portions.

A straightforward cross-court Spike might tolerate a relatively large range of contact positions and timing errors.

A sharply angled short-court Spike might require contact within a very narrow region.

Both are physically possible.

But one is much harder to execute.

### Scenario: Three attacks from the same position

| Attack | Physical requirement | Precision requirement |
|---|---|---|
| Deep cross-court | Ordinary striking angle | Moderate |
| Sharp short-court | Significant redirection | High |
| Extreme short-court | Outside available contact geometry | Impossible |

A low-ACC Athlete can attempt the first two.

They cannot achieve the third merely by spending more HP.

A high-ACC Athlete can also attempt the first two, but is more likely to execute the second successfully.

The third remains impossible unless another physical capability or Trait changes the available envelope.

This prevents Accuracy from becoming a universal substitute for positioning.

### How HP fits into this

The HP cost should reflect the **physical demand of the attempted action**, not merely the probability of success.

A sharply redirected Attack might require more HP because it involves a more demanding striking motion.

A narrow timing window alone need not increase HP cost.

This gives us two separate sources of difficulty:

- **Mechanical demand:** Can increase HP expenditure.
- **Precision demand:** Increases the consequences of imperfect execution.

A particular attack can have both.

That separation is important because it prevents every difficult action from automatically being expensive.

---

## 6. How I would divide the responsibilities among the twelve statistics

| Attribute | Statistic | Primary responsibility |
|---|---|---|
| POW | Explosiveness | Rate of explosive preparation |
| POW | Jump Power | Maximum jumping displacement |
| POW | Swing Power | Maximum striking capability |
| SPD | Acceleration | Rate of gaining movement speed |
| SPD | Lateral Speed | Lateral movement capability |
| SPD | Transition Speed | Approach and transition movement |
| ACC | Ball Control | Precision of ball redirection |
| ACC | Timing | Precision and flexibility of contact timing |
| ACC | Stability | Maintaining execution quality under imperfect physical conditions |
| END | Stamina Capacity | Available stamina |
| END | Exertion Efficiency | Physical expenditure required for actions |
| END | Recovery | Resource restoration |

Under this division, **the geometric difficulty of an Attack exists independently of ACC**.

ACC determines how effectively the Athlete handles it.

END determines how expensive the physical effort is.

POW determines how much striking performance is available.

SPD determines how effectively the Athlete arrived at the contact position.

This gives every Attribute a contribution to the same play without assigning overlapping responsibilities.

---

## 7. One potential issue with the proposed HP model

We should revisit something established earlier:

> Initial Action Charge consumes HP equal to the initial Charge, and secondary charging incurs additional HP.

Your new proposal introduces **additional expenditure determined by actual contact geometry and execution**.

That can work, but we should explicitly distinguish three quantities:

\[
HP_{\text{total}}
=
HP_{\text{initial}}
+
HP_{\text{secondary}}
+
HP_{\text{execution}}
\]

Where:

- \(HP_{\text{initial}}\) is committed when the Action is first charged.
- \(HP_{\text{secondary}}\) is incurred when modifying that Charge.
- \(HP_{\text{execution}}\) is the additional cost of physically realizing the selected contact.

The interface estimates the third quantity.

Accuracy determines how reliable that estimate is.

This is a proposed extension, not yet an established rule.

It also raises an important balance concern: **can an Athlete accidentally spend more HP than they currently possess?**

I would avoid allowing negative HP.

Instead, if an Athlete cannot afford the actual execution cost, their attempted contact should degrade toward an affordable outcome.

That would make resource management consequential without introducing arbitrary failure.

---

## 8. Does ACC have too much influence?

**Potentially, but I wouldn't reduce its informational role.**

The informational role is one of the most distinctive parts of your system.

It creates a compelling relationship between player expertise and Athlete expertise:

- An inexperienced player controlling a high-ACC Athlete receives better guidance.
- An experienced player controlling a low-ACC Athlete can compensate through learned judgment.
- A skilled player controlling a high-ACC Athlete can exploit difficult opportunities with greater confidence.

That's valuable.

The risk arises if ACC simultaneously provides:

1. Better information.
2. More accurate execution.
3. Larger physical contact envelopes.
4. Lower HP costs.
5. More trajectory choices.
6. More time to decide.

I would preserve the first two as its principal advantages.

The remaining benefits should generally emerge through specific granular statistics, physical interactions, or Traits—not from a universal ACC multiplier.

### My recommendation

Adopt your revised HP interpretation, with one qualification:

**A difficult physical contact can have a genuinely higher execution cost, and Accuracy determines how precisely that cost is predicted. However, inaccurate execution should not automatically cost more HP unless it actually requires additional physical effort.**

This allows the low-ACC hitter to misjudge the expense of a short-court Attack, miss the contact, and still incur the true cost of the attempted maneuver.

It also allows them to recognize the risk independently and deliberately choose a safer secondary-charge correction.

And it preserves an important distinction:

**Accuracy determines how precisely an Athlete can execute and anticipate a difficult action. It should not determine how much physical work that action fundamentally requires.**

The next issue I'd resolve is whether the extra HP cost belongs to the **selected trajectory's physical demand** or the **actual movement performed at contact**. They can differ when a player mistimes the action, and that distinction determines whether a flubbed hit is merely wasted expenditure or actively more expensive.

I think this question exposes a weakness in our current interpretation of ACC. We've been treating Accuracy as though it grants the Athlete an increasingly sophisticated understanding of the simulation's internal calculations. But **being good at volleyball does not necessarily mean being able to predict the exact consequences of a movement, especially its energy expenditure.**

We should distinguish what the simulation *knows*, what the Athlete could plausibly *perceive*, and what information the interface should communicate to the player.

These are three different things.

## 1. What could a high-ACC Athlete actually recognize?

Consider the difficult short-court Spike again.

The hitter is near the sideline, relatively far from the net, and wants to strike a sharp angle.

A skilled hitter could plausibly recognize several things:

- **Body alignment:** Their shoulders and striking arm aren't naturally aligned with the desired shot.
- **Contact position:** The ball will arrive slightly behind or outside their ideal hitting position.
- **Required redirection:** They will need to manipulate the contact more precisely than for a straightforward Spike.
- **Available time:** They have a narrow window in which the ball can be contacted successfully.
- **Likely outcome:** An aggressive swing risks striking the net or sending the ball out.

Those are recognizable physical circumstances.

But would they recognize that the attack costs **8 HP instead of 4 HP**?

Not necessarily.

An experienced hitter might know the shot requires greater effort or an uncomfortable movement. That doesn't imply they can predict the exact additional exertion.

And some difficult shots aren't especially exhausting. A soft tip can require extraordinary precision while consuming relatively little energy.

**The difficulty of executing a shot and the effort required to execute it are not the same quantity.**

This is where I think our previous interpretation became questionable.

---

## 2. The simulation doesn't need ACC to calculate difficulty

Suppose the game has a physical contact model.

For any potential Spike, it knows:

| Variable | Example |
|---|---|
| Athlete position | Near left sideline |
| Athlete orientation | Facing diagonally across court |
| Expected ball contact | Slightly behind striking shoulder |
| Arm reach | Determined by physique |
| Available Swing Power | Determined by POW |
| Desired trajectory | Sharp short-court angle |
| Contact timing | Estimated from ball flight and input |
| Current movement state | Airborne, rotating or stabilizing |

From those variables, the simulation can determine whether the desired trajectory is physically feasible.

It can also estimate the effort required to produce it and how sensitive the result is to contact errors.

**None of this requires Accuracy.**

The same calculations should occur for every Athlete.

ACC should influence execution where appropriate, but it doesn't need to be the source of the underlying physical information.

This suggests a useful architecture:

\[
\text{Physical State}
\longrightarrow
\text{Contact Evaluation}
\longrightarrow
\text{Player Feedback}
\]

The question becomes how much of the contact evaluation the player should be shown.

---

## 3. Three possible interpretations of ACC

### Model A: ACC grants better knowledge of simulation outcomes

This is the model we've implicitly been using.

A low-ACC hitter sees an approximate trajectory.

A high-ACC hitter sees a precise trajectory, its landing position, and its true HP cost.

**Advantages:** Easy to understand mechanically. High ACC provides a clear informational reward.

**Problems:** It makes Accuracy resemble a predictive computer. It also risks granting ACC too many benefits, particularly if it already improves actual execution.

A skilled hitter shouldn't necessarily know exactly where a ball will land before striking it.

### Model B: ACC improves the precision of the Athlete's physical execution

Here, every player receives approximately the same trajectory information.

But high ACC improves the relationship between selected input and resulting contact.

For example, two hitters choose the same cross-court Spike.

The high-ACC hitter can execute the intended contact angle more consistently.

The low-ACC hitter experiences greater deviation when their timing or body alignment is imperfect.

**Advantages:** More physically intuitive. ACC represents athletic technique rather than privileged knowledge.

**Problems:** It underuses the trajectory interface you've been developing. It also makes ACC's effects harder to communicate before contact.

### Model C: ACC improves the Athlete's ability to identify and control a specific contact

This is the interpretation I favor.

A high-ACC Athlete doesn't know the future. They have a more precise sense of **where, when, and how they can contact the ball**.

The interface translates that athletic awareness into useful visual information.

For example:

- A low-ACC hitter sees a broad region representing a possible short-court Spike.
- A high-ACC hitter sees a more precisely defined contact opportunity.
- The high-ACC hitter can recognize that the desired shot requires contacting the ball near the edge of their available hitting envelope.
- Both can attempt the shot, but the high-ACC hitter is better equipped to execute it.

The information advantage emerges from a plausible athletic skill: proprioception, anticipation, and contact technique.

It doesn't require the Athlete to possess an abstract understanding of HP calculations.

---

## 4. Reinterpreting your short-court scenario

Let's revisit it without assuming ACC directly reveals hidden costs.

A hitter prepares a short-court Attack from an unfavorable approach position.

The simulation determines that the desired trajectory is physically achievable, but requires a narrow contact window and substantial redirection.

### Low-ACC hitter

The player sees a relatively broad projected short-court trajectory.

They cannot clearly distinguish which part of their contact envelope would produce the desired result.

The interface displays the nominal HP cost of the selected action.

They attempt the Spike.

Their contact is imperfect, and the ball strikes the net.

They incur the actual HP expenditure associated with the attempted movement.

### High-ACC hitter

The player sees the same broad attacking direction, but their interface resolves the available contact opportunity more precisely.

They can identify that the desired trajectory requires an unusually narrow contact position.

They might also see that the shot is near the boundary of what their Athlete can physically execute.

They have two choices.

They can commit to the demanding Spike, or they can recharge to attempt a safer shot.

**The high-ACC player doesn't need to be told that the shot costs 8 HP. They can recognize that the contact is demanding from the geometry itself.**

That seems more coherent than making HP estimation an inherent function of Accuracy.

However, this would be a change from your most recent proposal, not merely a clarification of it.

---

## 5. What information could the interface actually generate?

We should avoid designing an elaborate prediction system that requires solving the entire physics simulation for every possible contact.

A more practical approach is to generate feedback from quantities the game already needs to calculate.

For each available trajectory, the contact model could evaluate:

| Quantity | How it's derived | Possible visualization |
|---|---|---|
| Expected contact position | Ball trajectory intersecting Athlete's reachable space | Contact marker |
| Contact tolerance | Range of contact positions producing an acceptable outcome | Narrow or broad contact region |
| Directional tolerance | Range of outgoing angles achievable from the current posture | Trajectory cone |
| Required striking effort | Desired ball speed and redirection relative to Athlete capability | HP cost |
| Expected landing area | Predicted outgoing ball velocity and flight | Landing region |
| Net clearance | Predicted trajectory intersecting net geometry | Visible net intersection |
| Boundary clearance | Predicted landing relative to court lines | Landing region crossing a boundary |

The system doesn't necessarily need to calculate a perfect trajectory for every possible movement.

It can evaluate a manageable number of candidate contacts associated with the available directional commands.

For example, when the player selects `D+M1`, the simulation evaluates the expected contact state and generates a candidate outgoing trajectory.

The interface can then display that trajectory at a level of detail appropriate to the Athlete.

This is technically plausible.

The more difficult problem is determining how the contact model produces the trajectory in the first place.

That depends on the physical simulation's authority over ball movement.

---

## 6. A possible visual language for Accuracy

Instead of giving high-ACC Athletes additional numerical information, the interface could progressively clarify the same physical prediction.

| Information | Low ACC | High ACC |
|---|---|---|
| Attack direction | Broad directional indication | Narrow trajectory projection |
| Contact location | Approximate region | Precise contact region |
| Landing position | General landing zone | More precise landing area |
| Net clearance | Unclear unless obviously obstructed | Visible clearance or intersection |
| Timing window | Approximate | More precisely indicated |
| HP expenditure | Nominal action cost | Potentially more precise estimate |

Notice that the HP estimate remains optional.

I wouldn't remove it outright because your original scenario has a useful design purpose: allowing a skilled Athlete to recognize an unexpectedly demanding action.

But we should determine whether that information belongs to ACC specifically.

It might instead be appropriate for **all players to see an estimated HP cost**, with the estimate becoming more accurate as the Athlete's contact position becomes better defined.

In that interpretation, ACC improves the Athlete's ability to achieve the intended contact, not necessarily their ability to read a resource cost.

---

## 7. Does this make ACC too influential?

It depends on which benefits we preserve.

I would distinguish three potential advantages:

**Perception:** Understanding the available contact geometry.

**Execution:** Producing the desired ball trajectory.

**Adaptation:** Adjusting to imperfect contact conditions.

All three are reasonable expressions of Accuracy.

However, they should probably be distributed among its statistics rather than granted wholesale by the overarching Attribute.

For example:

| Statistic | Physical meaning | Information consequence |
|---|---|---|
| **Ball Control** | Ability to manipulate outgoing ball direction and speed | More precise outgoing trajectory predictions |
| **Timing** | Ability to synchronize contact with the ball | More precise contact-window feedback |
| **Stability** | Ability to maintain controlled execution under movement and awkward posture | More reliable predictions under imperfect body alignment |

This is more coherent than simply saying that higher ACC reveals additional information.

It also creates an interesting relationship between the statistics.

A hitter with excellent Ball Control but poor Timing might understand how to direct a Spike while struggling to contact the ball at the intended moment.

A hitter with excellent Timing but poor Ball Control might consistently reach the ball at the right moment but have limited precision in choosing the outgoing trajectory.

Their interfaces could reflect those differences.

---

## 8. One deeper concern: don't make the UI solve volleyball for the player

Consider the following scenario.

A high-ACC hitter sees three possible trajectories:

- Cross-court: lands in bounds.
- Down-the-line: lands out.
- Short-court: strikes the net.

If these predictions are consistently authoritative, the game has effectively told the player which options are legal.

The remaining decision becomes selecting the best highlighted trajectory.

That risks undermining the skill expression you've been building through camera control, approach positioning, facing commitment, and confirmation timing.

A better interface would communicate **physical opportunities and limitations**, without perfectly resolving the outcome.

For example, the hitter might see that the down-the-line attack has a narrow margin relative to the sideline.

They must still decide whether the shot is worth attempting.

That preserves the value of player judgment even at high ACC.

---

## My proposed interpretation

I would revise our current model to this:

> **Accuracy represents the Athlete's ability to perceive, coordinate, and execute ball contact with precision. The simulation independently evaluates the physical feasibility, difficulty, and cost of candidate contacts. Accuracy influences how precisely those contact opportunities are represented to the player, and how reliably the Athlete can execute them. It does not inherently provide exact knowledge of the resulting ball trajectory or HP expenditure.**

The key architectural separation is:

\[
\boxed{\text{Simulation knowledge} \neq \text{Athlete awareness} \neq \text{Player information}}
\]

This is especially important for a game where the player controls an Athlete rather than the ball directly.

### The next design decision

I think we should resolve **what the trajectory visualization actually represents** before deciding how ACC modifies it.

There are two viable interpretations:

**1. Expected outcome:** The trajectory shows the ball's predicted flight if the Athlete executes the selected contact under current conditions.

**2. Intended outcome:** The trajectory shows the ball flight the Athlete is attempting to produce, even if the actual contact is unlikely to achieve it.

Your previous scenarios have leaned toward the second interpretation, particularly the mistimed cross-court Spike.

I think we should preserve that.

But if so, **ACC should probably modify the confidence or precision surrounding the intended trajectory rather than simply revealing an increasingly truthful version of the future.**

That would let a high-ACC Athlete recognize a narrow or difficult contact without receiving a guaranteed prediction of the ball's actual path.


---

I think this simplification resolves most of the problems we were creating for ourselves. It also establishes a much cleaner distinction between **player skill and Athlete skill**.

Previously, we were trying to represent an Athlete's Accuracy by giving the player more information about the simulation. But the player is already responsible for interpreting trajectories, positioning, timing inputs, and making tactical decisions.

**ACC can instead determine how faithfully the Athlete translates those decisions into successful physical actions.**

That is both simpler to implement and easier to balance.

## 1. Accuracy as forgiveness

Under this model, every Athlete has access to the same underlying trajectory interface, subject to their actual physical capabilities and Traits.

Accuracy doesn't reveal hidden information or progressively improve the precision of previews.

Instead, it modifies how an Athlete responds to imperfect circumstances.

| Circumstance | Low ACC | High ACC |
|---|---|---|
| Slightly mistimed contact | Significant trajectory deviation | Smaller deviation |
| Ball outside ideal contact position | Poor contact quality | Athlete compensates physically |
| Unfavorable body orientation | Greater accuracy penalty | Better adaptation |
| High movement velocity | More disruption to contact | Better control |
| Airborne contact | More difficulty maintaining precision | Better execution |
| Awkward redirection | Greater deviation from intended trajectory | More faithful execution |

These aren't necessarily six independent modifiers. They're different physical circumstances that could contribute to a common contact-quality calculation.

That matters because we don't want ACC to become a collection of unrelated bonuses.

## 2. Revisit the Setter scenario

A Setter prepares a Jump Set while facing the left pin.

They use the Back Set Trait to select a right-pin trajectory.

Their chosen trajectory is physically demanding because they're airborne, redirecting the ball behind their facing, and perhaps contacting it slightly outside their ideal setting position.

**Low-ACC Setter**

The player selects the right-pin trajectory and confirms slightly late.

The Athlete makes contact, but the resulting Set drifts farther toward the attack line than intended.

The intended trajectory was legitimate. The Athlete simply couldn't execute it precisely under those circumstances.

**High-ACC Setter**

The player makes the same decision and confirms at the same moment.

The Athlete compensates for the imperfect contact position and maintains a more accurate Set.

Their higher Timing might also permit a larger interval during which the Set can be executed without significant degradation.

The high-ACC Setter doesn't receive additional information about the Set. They simply perform it better.

This preserves the significance of the Setter's original decision.

### Timing and Ball Control remain distinguishable

We can preserve meaningful differences between the granular statistics:

- **Timing:** How far contact timing can deviate from ideal before quality deteriorates.
- **Ball Control:** How faithfully the outgoing trajectory follows the selected trajectory.
- **Stability:** How strongly unfavorable movement and posture affect execution.

These are different components of one contact-resolution system.

---

## 3. Revisit the mistimed Spike

A right-handed OH approaches a high Set at the left pin.

They intend to swing cross-court.

Their confirmation is slightly late, and the ball has moved beyond the ideal spiking position.

Previously, we discussed giving a high-ACC hitter a more precise prediction of the consequences.

We no longer need that.

**Low-ACC hitter:** The Athlete struggles to compensate for the late contact. The Spike travels toward central court rather than the intended cross-court angle.

**High-ACC hitter:** The Athlete adjusts their striking motion to accommodate the imperfect contact position. The resulting Spike remains closer to the intended cross-court trajectory.

However, your qualification is important:

> The ACC trees do not pertain to spiking, so the benefits stop there.

I interpret this as meaning that ACC provides **general contact forgiveness**, but doesn't independently improve specialized attacking capabilities.

A high-ACC Athlete might be better at contacting a slightly misplaced ball, but that doesn't mean they automatically gain:

- More powerful Spikes.
- Sharper attacking angles.
- Additional attacking trajectories.
- Specialized shot placement.
- Better ability to exploit blocker hands.

Those would require other physical capabilities, Traits, or the player's own execution.

This prevents ACC from becoming the dominant attacking Attribute.

There is one subtle distinction I'd retain: a high-ACC hitter may achieve a better resulting trajectory because they made cleaner contact, but they shouldn't gain a superior *intended* attacking trajectory solely from ACC.

---

## 4. Revisit the difficult short-court Attack

This scenario becomes considerably simpler.

The hitter approaches from near the sideline, relatively far from the net.

They select a sharp short-court Attack.

The trajectory is physically available, but the contact geometry is unfavorable.

Both low-ACC and high-ACC hitters see the same trajectory and its estimated HP cost, assuming identical physical circumstances.

The difference occurs at execution.

**Low ACC:** The Athlete struggles to accommodate the awkward contact geometry. The ball strikes the net.

**High ACC:** The Athlete makes a more effective physical correction, allowing the ball to clear the net.

Neither player receives privileged information about the difficulty.

Both can recognize the unfavorable position through ordinary observation.

Both can choose to recharge and attempt a safer, weaker Attack.

The high-ACC Athlete simply has more tolerance for imperfect circumstances.

### What happens to HP?

I would now remove the idea that ACC changes how accurately the interface estimates HP expenditure.

Instead:

- HP costs are determined by the attempted action and its physical requirements.
- The interface displays those costs consistently.
- ACC modifies execution quality, not knowledge of expenditure.
- END's Exertion Efficiency remains responsible for reducing physical resource costs.

A flubbed Attack may still waste its committed HP.

Additional HP expenditure from a physically demanding correction remains possible, but that should be a property of the movement actually performed, not an ACC-specific information mechanic.

This is a much cleaner division.

---

## 5. One concern: how far should forgiveness extend?

There is an important boundary between **correcting an imperfect contact** and **performing an impossible contact**.

Consider three situations.

| Situation | Low ACC | High ACC |
|---|---|---|
| Ball slightly outside ideal contact envelope | Significant penalty | Small penalty |
| Ball substantially outside ideal envelope but still reachable | Very poor contact | Somewhat better contact |
| Ball outside physical reach | No contact | No contact |

The third case is essential.

Accuracy should not extend an Athlete's arm reach or make an unreachable ball physically accessible.

Similarly, it shouldn't allow an Athlete to produce a trajectory that their Swing Power, orientation, or contact geometry makes impossible.

**ACC should reduce the consequences of imperfect execution within the Athlete's physical capabilities, not redefine those capabilities.**

That also provides a natural reason for POW and SPD to remain valuable.

POW determines what the Athlete can physically achieve.

SPD helps the Athlete reach favorable positions.

ACC determines how much execution quality they retain when the situation is imperfect.

END determines how sustainably they can perform.

These responsibilities are much more distinct.

---

## 6. Does this solve ACC's disproportionate influence?

Largely, yes.

The previous model gave ACC two different kinds of advantages:

1. Better information for the player.
2. Better execution by the Athlete.

The revised model removes the first.

It also makes the remaining benefit dependent on the quality of the player's decisions.

A high-ACC Athlete who consistently approaches from poor positions will still underperform an appropriately positioned Athlete with lower ACC.

Conversely, high ACC becomes especially valuable in difficult rallies, where ideal positioning and timing aren't always achievable.

That's an appropriate specialization.

There is still a balancing consideration: volleyball involves contact on almost every meaningful action, so a universally applicable ACC modifier could remain extremely valuable.

I would therefore avoid giving every ACC statistic a universal bonus to every contact.

Instead, apply them where their physical responsibilities are relevant.

For instance, a well-timed Spike should not receive an enormous additional benefit merely because the hitter has high Timing. Timing should primarily protect against mistiming.

Likewise, Stability should matter more during a running or airborne Set than during a stationary, well-positioned one.

This produces **diminishing practical value for Accuracy under ideal conditions**, without requiring an explicit diminishing-returns formula.

## 7. A simplified contact model

We could represent the entire system with four stages:

**1. Player intent**

The player selects an Action, Charge, directional influence, and confirmation timing.

**2. Physical circumstances**

The simulation evaluates the Athlete's actual position, orientation, velocity, posture, and relationship to the ball.

**3. Athlete execution**

The Athlete's relevant Attributes determine how successfully they translate the intended action into physical contact.

**4. Ball outcome**

The simulation resolves the resulting ball trajectory.

Accuracy operates primarily in Stage 3.

It doesn't need to modify the information available in Stage 1.

That is a much simpler architecture than the predictive-information model.

---

### One remaining distinction I'd like to preserve

Your example says a high-ACC hitter will *naturally correct physically* when the ball is farther from their ideal spiking envelope.

That raises a meaningful distinction between two possible implementations.

**A. Contact correction:** The Athlete's actual contact animation adjusts to reach the ball more effectively. Their hand position and striking posture change, and the resulting trajectory follows from that corrected contact.

**B. Outcome forgiveness:** The Athlete performs approximately the same physical contact, but the resulting trajectory receives a smaller accuracy penalty.

I strongly favor **A as the intended behavior**, even if some aspects initially require simplified calculations.

It means Accuracy represents genuine simulated athletic skill rather than a hidden adjustment to the ball's trajectory.

It also ensures the correction remains constrained by the Athlete's physical reach and available movement.

The cleanest resulting principle would be:

**The player chooses what to attempt. The Athlete's Attributes determine how well they physically execute that attempt. The ball responds to what the Athlete actually does.**

That principle seems sufficiently strong to use as the foundation for the entire contact system.
