This scenario is a useful test of the simplified ACC model because **the player makes the same decisions in both cases, yet the Athletes produce different outcomes through their physical execution**.

It also establishes something important about the game more broadly: an Athlete can fail to produce an intended trajectory even when the player has made the best available decisions. Their Attributes impose real limitations, not merely difficulties that sufficiently skilled inputs can always overcome.

## 1. The rescue sequence

I'd break the scenario into three distinct failures or limitations, each governed by different systems.

| Stage | What happens | Governing factors |
|---|---|---|
| **Self-set** | The hitter attempts a neutral Receive to set the ball upward, but cannot achieve the intended trajectory | Receive Charge, END, contact execution |
| **Recovery and approach** | The hitter attempts to reach the ideal attacking position but lacks sufficient physical capacity | SPD, Jump Power, available time, ball trajectory |
| **Attack contact** | The hitter jumps from a compromised position and attempts a neutral Attack | ACC, arm reach, contact geometry, timing |

The important point is that **ACC does not undo the failures of the preceding stages**.

A high-ACC hitter cannot retroactively improve the Receive or teleport to the ideal attacking position. They must deal with the ball and body positions that actually resulted.

Their advantage is in making better physical use of the contact opportunity they have.

### The imperfect self-set

The player attempts a neutral Receive with a particular Charge.

However, the Athlete lacks the necessary END and/or has committed an unsuitable Charge to produce the intended upward trajectory.

The actual ball trajectory deviates.

This is not simply an inaccurate input. The requested outcome exceeded what the Athlete could accomplish under those circumstances.

That is an important distinction from the earlier discussion of Accuracy:

**A physically unattainable trajectory remains unattainable regardless of how precisely the player performs the inputs.**

One implication worth preserving: the trajectory preview represents the player's intended Receive, not a guarantee that the Athlete can realize it.

---

## 2. The ideal contact position can be inaccessible

The poor self-set creates a ball trajectory whose ideal attacking contact point is unreachable or unusable.

That might happen because:

- The ideal point lies too close to or beyond the net.
- The hitter cannot accelerate into the required approach quickly enough.
- The hitter cannot jump high enough to reach the ball at the desired position.
- The necessary approach would require more ground or time than is available.

The player therefore selects a compromised interception point.

This is a significant feature of your approach system.

**The player isn't choosing between an ideal Attack and a slightly worse Attack. They're choosing among the physical opportunities that remain possible.**

A well-designed trajectory visualization should make the ball's path understandable, but it doesn't need to label the ideal contact point or explicitly announce that the Athlete is incapable of reaching it.

As you note, the player may simply recognize that the self-set has gone wrong and react accordingly.

That seems preferable to introducing an additional instructional interface.

---

## 3. Low ACC: the fingertip contact

The low-ACC hitter jumps from the compromised location.

They attempt a neutral Attack as the ball passes near the limit of their arm's reach.

The Athlete's body is poorly aligned with the ball.

Their hand can barely reach it.

They make a fingertip contact, producing a weak Attack.

This is not necessarily an Accuracy roll that downgrades the Attack.

It's the physical consequence of the Athlete's inability to organize an effective striking motion from that position.

The Athlete can touch the ball, but they cannot establish a sufficiently good contact to transfer the intended striking force.

That gives us a useful distinction:

**Being able to reach the ball is not the same as being able to strike it effectively.**

A fingertip brush, an awkward palm contact, and a fully extended attacking swing may all occur within the Athlete's maximum reach, but they should produce very different outcomes.

---

## 4. High ACC: a better physical correction

Now consider a high-ACC hitter making the same inputs.

The Athlete still jumps from the compromised location.

They still have the same physical reach and jumping capacity, assuming those Attributes are otherwise identical.

But their execution differs.

They might:

- Rotate their torso more effectively.
- Adjust their striking shoulder.
- Extend their arm along a more favorable path.
- Reorient their wrist to create a better contact surface.
- Coordinate their swing with the ball's actual position.

The result is a more substantial contact.

Perhaps the low-ACC hitter only brushes the ball with their fingertips, while the high-ACC hitter manages to place more of their palm behind it.

The high-ACC hitter produces a stronger, more controlled Attack.

Crucially, **their arm has not become longer and their jump has not become higher**.

They have simply used the available physical geometry more effectively.

This is exactly the sort of distinction that makes simulated Athlete skill meaningful.

### A necessary physical limit

If the ball is genuinely beyond maximum reach, neither Athlete can contact it.

If the ball is reachable only at the fingertips even with optimal body mechanics, high ACC should not magically produce a full-handed Spike.

The benefit exists within the Athlete's physically achievable range of postures and contact motions.

---

## 5. This suggests three different envelopes

I think your scenario reveals a useful way to conceptualize contact without introducing a new gameplay system.

### Maximum reach envelope

The region in which the Athlete can make any legal contact with the ball.

This is determined primarily by physique, position, jump trajectory, and the body's possible configurations.

### Effective contact envelope

The region in which the Athlete can make a useful contact with the ball.

A ball near the edge of maximum reach might only permit a weak touch.

A ball closer to the body might permit a controlled Receive or Set.

### Ideal action envelope

The region in which the Athlete can execute their intended Action with minimal compromise.

For a Spike, this would generally require favorable ball position relative to the striking arm, torso, and swing path.

These envelopes are not necessarily fixed spheres or boxes.

They depend on action, posture, orientation, and motion.

**ACC primarily affects how effectively the Athlete uses the space between maximum reach and ideal contact.**

It should not simply expand maximum reach.

This is a particularly useful way to formalize forgiveness.

---

## 6. What this means for the three ACC statistics

Your scenario also suggests a cleaner division between them.

| Statistic | Contribution to the rescue |
|---|---|
| **Ball Control** | Determines how precisely the Athlete can manipulate the ball once contact is established |
| **Timing** | Determines how effectively the Athlete coordinates the contact motion with the ball's arrival |
| **Stability** | Determines how much contact quality is preserved under compromised posture and movement |

The physical correction you describe seems especially related to **Stability**.

A high-Stability Athlete could maintain a more effective contact posture despite jumping from an awkward position.

Timing would matter if the ball is passing rapidly through the available contact region.

Ball Control would matter once the Athlete actually makes contact and attempts to direct the ball.

However, since you've specified that ACC's trees do not specialize in spiking, I wouldn't assign the entire rescue advantage to a spiking-specific Ball Control mechanic.

It can emerge from the general contact system.

---

## 7. One distinction worth preserving: Athlete correction versus player correction

This scenario gives us a clean division of responsibility.

**The player controls:**
- Where to move.
- When and how to charge Jump.
- Where to aim the approach.
- When to confirm the Attack.
- Which directional influence to apply.

**The Athlete handles:**
- How to coordinate their body to execute those instructions.
- How effectively to adjust to the ball's actual position.
- How much useful contact can be produced from the resulting posture.

A skilled player can compensate for weak Attributes through better decisions.

But they cannot eliminate the Athlete's physical limitations.

Likewise, a high-ACC Athlete can rescue some imperfect decisions, but cannot eliminate the consequences of poor positioning or insufficient physical capability.

That seems to be the balance you're aiming for.

---

## 8. A possible complication: does high ACC improve the actual contact point?

There's a subtle distinction between two ways the high-ACC Athlete might improve the rescue.

**A. Better body configuration at the same contact time.**  
The Athlete adjusts their torso, shoulder, and arm to make a more effective contact with the ball.

**B. Better microtiming around the player's confirmation input.**  
The Athlete can shift the precise moment of contact slightly earlier or later to find a more favorable point along the ball's trajectory.

Both seem compatible with the scenario.

But B has broader implications because it means **the player's confirmation input is not necessarily the exact instant of contact**.

That would also explain how Timing creates a more forgiving contact window for Sets.

I think this is worth establishing explicitly.

My preference would be to allow a small, Attribute-dependent correction window around the confirmation input, constrained by the Athlete's actual physical motion.

That would make Timing an active component of simulated athletic coordination rather than a hidden multiplier on accuracy.

### Provisional principle

**ACC determines how effectively an Athlete organizes their body and contact timing to execute the player's intended action within the physical opportunities available. It improves the quality of compromised contacts without changing the Athlete's fundamental reach, movement capacity, or striking-power ceiling.**

The rescue scenario supports that interpretation particularly well.

The remaining decision is whether Timing can shift the actual contact moment around the player's input, or whether it only improves the quality of a contact occurring at that exact moment.

## 1. Spike Point versus actual contact

We now have three separate locations:

| Location | Meaning | Determined by |
|---|---|---|
| **Spike Point** | Ideal location for this Athlete to strike the incoming ball | Ball trajectory, Athlete's attributes and physique, ideal preparation |
| **Reachable contact opportunity** | Where the Athlete can realistically intercept the ball from their current state | Current position, movement, available time, jump preparation |
| **Actual contact point** | Where the Athlete ultimately touches the ball | Player inputs, physical motion, ACC-based corrections |

The Spike Point assumes ideal conditions, including a full Jump and sufficient time to reach the required position.

It does **not** account for whether the Athlete can get there from their present location.

This makes it a reference point rather than an instruction the simulation must fulfill.

## 2. Revisiting the botched self-set

Suppose the hitter attempts to bump-set the ball upward.

Their Receive produces a trajectory that is too low and somewhat to the right.

The game calculates the Athlete's ideal Spike Point along that trajectory.

Because the ball never rises very high, **the Spike Point might simply be at the apex of the ball's arc**.

That point is still determined by the Athlete's capabilities. Another Athlete with different height, reach, or Jump Power could have a different ideal point along the same ball trajectory.

However, our hitter cannot reach the calculated Spike Point in time.

Perhaps it requires an approach that is too long, an impractical change in direction, or a jump that would have needed to begin earlier.

The player recognizes the poor Set and attempts a rescue.

They move toward the ball, jump from a compromised position, and confirm Attack as the ball passes near their reachable contact envelope.

The Spike Point remains where it was calculated. The game does not relocate it to accommodate the player's poor position.

### Low-ACC outcome

The Athlete makes a marginal contact near the edge of their reach.

They can only brush the ball with their fingertips, producing a weak Attack.

### High-ACC outcome

With identical movement, Jump, and Attack inputs, the Athlete makes a more effective physical correction.

They adjust their body configuration and, through Timing, can shift the precise contact moment slightly around the player's confirmation input.

This produces a better contact.

**Neither Athlete reaches the ideal Spike Point.** The high-ACC Athlete simply extracts more useful performance from the compromised opportunity.

That is the intended distinction.

---

## 3. Why the ideal Spike Point should remain independent of current positioning

I think this is valuable for three reasons.

**First, it preserves the consequences of earlier actions.** A poor Receive creates a poor Set trajectory. The game does not automatically redefine the ideal Attack around the hitter's current position.

**Second, it makes physical limitations meaningful.** A player might identify the correct Spike Point but lack the SPD, Jump Power, or available time to reach it.

**Third, it gives ACC a clear responsibility.** Accuracy helps the Athlete perform under imperfect circumstances, rather than making those circumstances disappear.

This also prevents an undesirable feedback loop in which the Spike Point continually moves toward the Athlete whenever they fall behind their approach.

The ideal target can remain stable while the actual opportunities for contact change.

## 4. One nuance: the Spike Point should be Athlete-specific, not necessarily action-specific

A Spike Point calculated for a 6'7" OH with exceptional Jump Power could differ substantially from one calculated for a 5'6" Libero.

However, I wouldn't have ACC move the Spike Point toward a more convenient location.

Otherwise, high ACC would effectively improve the Athlete's ideal geometry, rather than their ability to execute within that geometry.

Instead:

- **Physique and physical Attributes** establish the ideal Spike Point.
- **Current movement and preparation** determine whether it can be reached.
- **ACC** determines how well the Athlete performs when the actual contact differs from ideal.

One qualification: if the Spike Point calculation uses the Athlete's ideal striking mechanics, we should decide whether it incorporates their maximum Swing Power or simply assumes an ideal contact position for a standard Attack. Those could produce different ideal points.

---

## 5. A useful consequence for the interface

The Spike Point should not need to indicate whether it is currently reachable.

A player might see it positioned well beyond their realistic approach path.

That's useful information in itself: the Set is poor, and they need to improvise.

The player can then use the ball's visible trajectory, their movement controls, and their understanding of their Athlete to attempt a rescue.

We don't need an additional system that labels the situation *unreachable*, *suboptimal*, or *difficult*.

That keeps the interface consistent with your broader direction of avoiding excessive explanatory feedback.

### Revised rule

> **Spike Point:** An Athlete-specific ideal contact location calculated from the incoming ball trajectory and the Athlete's physical capabilities, assuming a fully prepared Jump and sufficient time to reach the contact location. The Spike Point is independent of the Athlete's current position and does not guarantee reachability. Actual contact is determined by the Athlete's movement, Jump, confirmation timing, and ACC-mediated physical corrections.

This also confirms the earlier Timing decision: **the player's confirmation establishes the intended contact moment, but the Athlete can make a limited physical timing correction around it.**

One question follows naturally: **Should the Spike Point represent the single best point for maximizing attacking power, or the best compromise between power, available attacking angles, and net clearance?**

Those can differ, especially on a low Set.

### Exchange 090

Conversation turn ID: `14d43874-2e15-49b4-806e-2b6ffaf33b47`


#### User

the Spike Point represents the highest point at which a hitter's attributes allow them to make the best contact with the ball. a high set will not have its Spike Point be the peak of the arc; instead, there may be TWO spike points where the trajectory of the ball is at its highest intersection with the Athlete's highest jump and arm reach.


#### Assistant

That clarifies the Spike Point considerably. **It is the highest point along the ball's trajectory at which the Athlete can make an ideal attacking contact, assuming a fully realized Jump and ideal positioning.**

Crucially, **a ball trajectory can have zero, one, or two Spike Points**, depending on its relationship to the Athlete's maximum effective attacking height.

## 1. Spike Points as intersections

Consider two functions:

- **Ball trajectory:** The ball's position in three-dimensional space over time.
- **Maximum ideal attacking height:** The highest ball-contact height at which this Athlete can execute an ideal Spike, given their physique, Jump Power, and arm reach.

For a sufficiently high Set, the ball rises above the Athlete's maximum ideal attacking height.

It therefore intersects that height twice:

1. **Ascending Spike Point:** The ball first reaches the Athlete's maximum ideal attacking height while rising.
2. **Descending Spike Point:** The ball returns to that height while falling.

Both are theoretically ideal contact opportunities.

Neither is necessarily reachable from the Athlete's current position.

### Example: High Set

Suppose an Athlete's maximum ideal attacking contact height is 3.20 m.

The Set reaches a peak of 3.80 m.

| Ball position | Height | Interpretation |
|---|---:|---|
| Ascending intersection | 3.20 m | First Spike Point |
| Apex | 3.80 m | Above the Athlete's ideal attacking reach |
| Descending intersection | 3.20 m | Second Spike Point |

The two Spike Points occupy different positions in space and occur at different times.

That distinction matters because the Athlete may be able to approach one but not the other.

It also means that an early Attack and a delayed Attack can target different positions along the same Set without changing the Athlete's maximum attacking height.

## 2. Low Set: Spike Point at the apex

Now consider the botched self-set.

The ball only rises to 2.85 m, below the Athlete's maximum ideal attacking contact height of 3.20 m.

There is no intersection with the maximum-height boundary.

Instead, the highest possible ideal contact along the trajectory occurs at the ball's apex.

**The apex becomes the single Spike Point.**

This matches your rescue scenario.

Even though the Athlete could theoretically reach higher, the ball never reaches that height.

And even though the Spike Point exists, the Athlete might lack the time or movement capacity to reach it.

## 3. The three cases

| Ball trajectory | Spike Points | Reason |
|---|---|---|
| Apex below maximum ideal attacking height | **One** | The apex is the highest available contact |
| Apex exactly at maximum ideal attacking height | **One** | The apex touches the maximum-height boundary |
| Apex above maximum ideal attacking height | **Two** | Ascending and descending intersections |

There is one physical qualification: a trajectory could provide **zero valid Spike Points** if no portion of it permits an ideal attacking contact—for example, because the relevant contact locations are obstructed or otherwise illegal.

That is distinct from an Athlete simply being unable to reach an otherwise valid Spike Point in time.

---

## 4. Why two Spike Points matter

Consider a high Set traveling toward the left pin.

The ascending Spike Point might be closer to the Setter, while the descending Spike Point is farther toward the antenna.

An OH preparing an approach may prefer the descending point because it provides more time to arrive.

But another Athlete with exceptional movement and preparation might attack at the ascending point.

Importantly, the system shouldn't automatically choose whichever point is easiest to reach.

Both are idealized geometric targets.

**The player decides which contact opportunity to pursue.**

This gives high Sets an additional tactical property: they can create multiple ideal contact opportunities for the same Athlete.

A quick Set, by contrast, may provide only a very narrow opportunity to reach its Spike Point.

## 5. How this affects ACC

This also reinforces the simplified Accuracy model.

ACC does not determine where the Spike Points are.

Instead, it determines how well the Athlete can compensate when actual contact occurs away from them.

For example, a hitter might jump too early to reach the descending Spike Point.

A low-ACC Athlete makes a poor contact.

A high-ACC Athlete adjusts their arm position and contact timing, preserving more of the intended Attack.

Neither Athlete gains a different theoretical maximum attacking height through ACC.

### Formalized rule

> **Spike Point:** The highest ideal attacking contact location or locations along an incoming ball trajectory, determined by the Athlete's physique, Jump Power, and arm reach under ideal Jump and positioning conditions. When the ball's apex exceeds the Athlete's maximum ideal attacking contact height, two Spike Points exist at the ascending and descending intersections. Otherwise, the trajectory's apex is the single Spike Point, provided that an ideal attacking contact is physically valid there. Spike Points do not account for whether the Athlete can reach them from their current position.

One implementation nuance: **these intersections must be calculated in three-dimensional space**, not merely by comparing heights. The ascending and descending Spike Points may have the same height but very different horizontal positions, and those positions are essential to approach planning.

### Exchange 091

Spike Points also do not correct for a player's approach decisions- a Spike Point is always physically reachable given ideal conditions, but oftentimes a player will be slightly out of position to approach perfectly towards the Spike Point, or they may choose an approach path that deviates their own body away from that point.


#### Assistant

Agreed. This establishes that **Spike Points are independent of the player's approach decisions**, including their current positioning, chosen approach trajectory, and eventual takeoff location.

A Spike Point represents what the Athlete could achieve under ideal conditions—not what their current approach will allow them to achieve.

## 1. Three distinct components

| Component | Determined by | Responds to player movement? |
|---|---|---|
| **Spike Point** | Ball trajectory, Athlete physique, Jump Power, and ideal attacking reach | **No** |
| **Approach trajectory** | Movement inputs, camera steering, SPD, physical movement constraints | **Yes** |
| **Actual attacking contact** | Resulting jump, body position, confirmation timing, ACC corrections | **Yes** |

The Spike Point remains fixed relative to the predicted ball trajectory, even when the player makes a poor approach.

It can move if the predicted ball trajectory itself changes, but it does not move to accommodate the Athlete.

## 2. Scenario: Slightly misaligned approach

An OH receives a high Set toward the left pin.

The descending Spike Point is located at a height and position the Athlete could reach with a full Jump and an appropriately aligned approach.

The player begins charging Jump.

Their projected ground approach curves toward the ball, but their steering leaves them slightly too far inside the court.

The Spike Point remains unchanged.

At takeoff, the Athlete is displaced from the ideal location.

They jump and attempt to Attack.

**Low ACC:** The Athlete struggles to accommodate the lateral displacement. Contact occurs farther from the ideal striking position, reducing the quality of the Attack.

**High ACC:** The Athlete adjusts their torso, shoulder, arm, and contact timing to compensate. They achieve a better contact from the same compromised position.

Neither Athlete is automatically repositioned toward the Spike Point.

This preserves the consequences of the player's approach.

## 3. Scenario: Deliberately deviating from the Spike Point

This distinction also matters when the deviation is intentional.

Suppose an attacker sees the ideal Spike Point near the antenna but deliberately chooses an approach that positions them farther inside the court.

They might want to avoid a blocker, preserve a particular facing angle, or reduce the risk of a net fault.

The system should not interpret this as a request to relocate the Spike Point.

Instead, the player accepts a less favorable contact geometry in exchange for another tactical advantage.

Their ACC determines how effectively they can execute from that compromised position.

**The Spike Point is a reference for optimal contact, not a mandatory destination.**

## 4. Spike Points and physical reachability

Your statement that a Spike Point is always physically reachable under ideal conditions is worth formalizing carefully.

It means the calculation assumes:

- The Athlete can begin from an appropriate location.
- Sufficient time exists to prepare and execute a full Jump.
- The Athlete can align their body appropriately for the contact.
- The contact is within their maximum ideal attacking envelope.

It does **not** guarantee that the Athlete can reach the point from their current location, nor that their chosen approach will bring them there.

This distinction prevents the Spike Point system from becoming a dynamic pathfinding or interception assistant.

The game identifies the ideal contact location. **The player is responsible for getting the Athlete there.**

## 5. A consequence for the approach visualization

The projected ground approach and the Spike Point should remain separate visual elements.

The approach projection shows where the Athlete will move along the ground if they continue the current Jump Charge.

The Spike Point shows the ideal location of ball contact.

Neither projection needs to automatically correct the other.

This is particularly important because the approach projection intentionally excludes airborne displacement.

A player might steer their projected approach to end short of the Spike Point's horizontal position, correctly anticipating that their airborne movement will carry them toward it.

Conversely, a projected approach that appears to end directly beneath the Spike Point might produce an overshoot after takeoff.

That relationship is something the player must learn to judge.

### Consolidated definition

> **Spike Point:** An Athlete-specific, idealized location along the incoming ball trajectory at which the Athlete can make their highest ideal attacking contact, assuming sufficient preparation, optimal positioning, and a fully realized Jump. A trajectory may produce one or two Spike Points depending on its maximum height relative to the Athlete's ideal attacking reach. Spike Points are independent of the Athlete's current position, approach trajectory, takeoff location, and actual contact execution. They remain fixed unless the underlying ball trajectory changes.

This creates a clean division of responsibility:

**The Spike Point tells the player where ideal contact is possible. The approach determines where the Athlete actually goes. ACC determines how effectively the Athlete handles any discrepancy.**

Despite the Athlete being capable of reaching the height of the Set at the Spike Point, the player makes their approach to line up with a lower point of the Set's trajectory.


#### Assistant

That is a clearer example because **the player isn't necessarily making a worse approach. They're deliberately choosing a different contact point along the Set's trajectory.**

The Spike Point identifies the highest ideal contact available to the Athlete, but it does not prescribe where they should attack.

### Scenario: Attacking closer to the antenna

A Setter delivers a high Set toward the left pin.

The descending Spike Point occurs somewhat inside the antenna, where the ball crosses the Athlete's maximum ideal attacking height.

However, the ball continues traveling outward and downward toward the antenna.

The hitter has two possible approaches:

**Approach A — Toward the Spike Point**

The player aims their approach to intercept the ball at the Spike Point.

- The Athlete can contact the ball at their maximum ideal attacking height.
- The player can exploit their full vertical attacking potential.
- The contact occurs farther inside the court.

**Approach B — Toward the antenna**

The player deliberately directs their approach farther outside.

- The ball has descended below the Spike Point's height by the time it reaches the intended contact location.
- The Athlete can still make a good attacking contact, but at a lower height.
- The more outward contact position may create different attacking angles relative to the net and opposing blockers.

Neither approach is inherently incorrect.

**Approach B trades contact height for contact location.** That can be tactically advantageous, even though the Athlete could have contacted the ball higher at the earlier Spike Point.

### What this establishes

We should distinguish **maximum-height contact** from **tactically desirable contact**.

| Concept | Meaning |
|---|---|
| **Spike Point** | Highest ideal attacking contact location along the ball trajectory, assuming ideal conditions |
| **Chosen contact location** | Where the player intends to intercept the ball, based on their approach and timing |
| **Actual contact location** | Where the Athlete physically contacts the ball after executing those inputs |

The Spike Point remains unchanged when the player chooses Approach B.

Importantly, **contacting the ball below the Spike Point does not automatically impose an ACC penalty**.

If the hitter deliberately approaches the lower contact location and aligns their body appropriately, the resulting contact can still be clean and controlled.

The lower height changes the physical attacking possibilities—particularly the available downward angles and net clearance—but does not inherently indicate poor execution.

ACC becomes relevant if the Athlete's actual contact differs from the contact they were attempting to make.

### Revised principle

**The Spike Point is a maximum-height reference, not a universal optimum for attacking.**

Players can deliberately target other points along the Set's trajectory to obtain different attacking positions, angles, and timing opportunities. Those contacts may be lower without being less accurate or poorly executed.

This also means that a successful approach should not be evaluated primarily by its proximity to the Spike Point. It should be evaluated by how well the Athlete executes the contact the player actually pursued.

### Exchange 093

Conversation turn ID: `0ddc55fb-dcfc-4fb6-b73b-fd05cd70aca2`


#### User

then additionally, it is the Setter's implicit responsibility to understand their hitters' plausible Spike Points and measure their trajectories and charge levels to idealize these Spike Points versus the offense they want to run


#### Assistant

Exactly. This gives the Setter a more demanding responsibility than simply delivering the ball to a particular hitter or location.

**The Setter is implicitly designing the hitter's contact opportunities through the Set's trajectory and Charge.** To do that effectively, they must understand the receiving hitter's physical capabilities and the offense they intend to run.

Importantly, the Setter isn't directly choosing the hitter's Spike Point. They're choosing a ball trajectory that *produces* particular Spike Points for that Athlete.

## 1. Setting as the construction of attacking opportunities

Consider two hitters:

| | Hitter A | Hitter B |
|---|---|---|
| Height | 6'7" | 6'1" |
| Jump Power | Moderate | Exceptional |
| Arm reach | Long | Shorter |
| SPD | Moderate | High |
| Preferred attack | High outside Attack | Fast, aggressive Attack |

The same Set can produce different Spike Points for these Athletes.

A Set that creates an excellent descending Spike Point for Hitter A might create a poorly positioned or mistimed opportunity for Hitter B.

Conversely, a faster, flatter Set could suit Hitter B's movement and jumping capabilities while giving Hitter A insufficient time to establish a full approach.

Neither Set is universally better.

**Its quality depends on the intended hitter and offensive purpose.**

## 2. Scenario: Setting for maximum contact height

A Setter wants their OH to attack at their maximum available height.

They know the OH has substantial Jump Power and arm reach.

The Setter selects a high Set whose descending trajectory crosses the OH's maximum ideal attacking height at a desirable location near the left pin.

This creates a Spike Point the OH can plausibly approach.

The Setter must consider several things simultaneously:

- **Set Charge:** Sufficient to produce the desired height and distance.
- **Trajectory:** Positions the descending Spike Point at the intended location.
- **Hitter capabilities:** Determines the height at which that intersection occurs.
- **Available preparation time:** Determines whether the hitter can realistically reach the Spike Point.
- **Offensive purpose:** Determines whether maximum contact height is actually desirable.

A Setter who understands these relationships can produce more effective opportunities without receiving an explicit bonus to Set quality.

Their advantage comes partly from the player's understanding of the game.

## 3. Scenario: Setting for a faster offense

Now the Setter wants to run a quick Attack with their MB.

They choose a flatter, faster trajectory.

The ball may never rise above the MB's maximum ideal attacking height.

Consequently, the Spike Point could occur at the apex of the Set.

The Setter is deliberately sacrificing some potential contact height to accelerate the offense.

This can reduce the time available for opposing blockers to position themselves.

However, it also places greater demands on the MB's preparation and coordination.

If the MB is late, their ACC may help them rescue the contact, but it cannot fully compensate for an approach that never brought them into the ball's reachable space.

This creates a meaningful Setter–Hitter relationship: the Setter must understand not just where the hitter *can* attack, but where they can attack **within the intended offensive timing**.

## 4. Scenario: Setting beyond the Spike Point

Your antenna example introduces another dimension.

The Setter wants their OH to attack very close to the antenna.

They deliver a Set whose descending Spike Point occurs somewhat inside the antenna.

The ball continues outward and downward afterward.

The OH deliberately approaches the later, lower contact location.

This can be an entirely successful offensive design.

The Setter has not necessarily made a poor Set simply because the hitter doesn't attack at the Spike Point.

Instead, they have created a trajectory that supports a different attacking location.

**Spike Points are reference information, not the sole measure of Set quality.**

That distinction is essential if the game is to support different offensive styles.

---

## 5. The Setter's skill is partly predictive

This also clarifies what a skilled Setter player must learn.

They need to anticipate the relationship between:

**Set trajectory → hitter-specific Spike Points → feasible approaches → available attacking options.**

Each stage depends on different information.

The Setter controls the first through their Action Charge, directional influence, and execution.

The hitter's physique and Attributes determine the second.

The hitter's positioning, movement capabilities, and available time determine the third.

The hitter's actual approach, facing, contact timing, and physical execution determine the fourth.

The Setter cannot control the entire sequence.

They can only create favorable conditions for the hitter.

This preserves meaningful agency for both players.

## 6. A useful implication for trajectory sharing

The earlier decision that allies can see a Setter's available trajectories becomes more valuable here.

The hitter can observe the Setter's options and begin anticipating which Set they might receive.

Once the Setter commits, the hitter sees the chosen trajectory and can identify their own Spike Point or Points along it.

**Those Spike Points are calculated for the observing hitter, not universally shared coordinates.**

A different teammate could see different Spike Points along the same Set.

This means trajectory sharing communicates the ball's intended movement, while each Athlete interprets its attacking opportunities according to their own capabilities.

We don't need to give the Setter an interface that explicitly calculates every teammate's Spike Points.

Their ability to anticipate those opportunities can remain part of player skill.

---

## 7. This creates two distinct forms of setting excellence

I think this is the most consequential implication.

**Mechanical setting skill** is the ability to execute a chosen trajectory accurately despite imperfect timing, positioning, or ball contact.

This is where the Athlete's ACC matters.

**Tactical setting skill** is the ability to choose trajectories that create useful attacking opportunities for particular teammates within the desired offensive structure.

This belongs primarily to the player.

A high-ACC Setter controlled by an inexperienced player might execute an unsuitable Set with exceptional precision.

A low-ACC Setter controlled by an experienced player might choose an excellent trajectory but struggle to reproduce it consistently.

Both distinctions should remain meaningful.

### Consolidated principle

> **The Setter determines the intended ball trajectory, thereby creating Athlete-specific Spike Points and other possible attacking contact locations. Effective setting requires anticipating the hitter's physique, Attributes, approach capabilities, and intended offensive timing. The Setter's ACC governs how faithfully the Athlete executes the selected Set, while the player's tactical understanding determines whether that Set creates a useful attacking opportunity.**

This also suggests that **a Set should never be evaluated solely by its proximity to a hitter's Spike Point**. A lower contact near the antenna, a fast quick Attack, or a deliberately unusual trajectory may be preferable depending on the offense.

The system should make these possibilities physically meaningful without prescribing which one is optimal.


---

a weak high ball to the middle may be ideal despite the Spike Point being essentially instant, as the MB can still create high contact nearby to the Spike Point, and a rising shoot set to the outside may have a Spike Point that is far outside the court - but the tactically ideal location to spike the ball is at the antenna while it is rising. This can also be circumvented with an absolutely perfect set that rises exactly to the antenna and the spiker's jump power and arm reach.


#### Assistant

These examples establish something important: **the Spike Point is a geometric reference, but the Setter's objective is to create a useful attacking opportunity—not necessarily to place the Spike Point at the intended contact location.**

The distinction becomes especially clear when we consider how much useful attacking height exists *around* a Spike Point.

## 1. Weak, high Set to the middle

Suppose the Setter delivers a relatively weak, high ball toward the MB.

The ball's trajectory barely rises above—or perhaps never reaches—the MB's maximum ideal attacking height.

Its Spike Point occurs near the apex, potentially almost immediately after the Set.

At first glance, this might seem undesirable: the MB cannot realistically complete an approach and Jump in time to reach that exact point.

But the Set may still be excellent.

Because the ball is moving relatively slowly and remains near its apex for some time, the MB can contact it slightly later and slightly lower, at a nearby location.

The difference in contact height might be negligible.

The MB doesn't need to reach the Spike Point precisely to obtain a powerful, high-quality Attack.

**A Spike Point occurring too early does not necessarily mean the Set is too fast.**

The relevant question is how the ball moves through the surrounding region of useful contact opportunities.

## 2. Rising shoot Set to the outside

Now consider a fast Set traveling toward the outside antenna.

The ball is still rising as it passes the antenna.

Its theoretical Spike Point occurs farther along the trajectory, potentially well outside the court.

The Spike Point is physically valid under ideal conditions, but it is tactically undesirable.

The OH instead aims to intercept the ball near the antenna while it is rising.

That contact occurs below the Spike Point's maximum height.

Nevertheless, it may be the best attacking opportunity because it provides the desired location, timing, and attacking geometry.

Here, **the ideal tactical contact occurs before the Spike Point**, whereas in the middle example, it occurs after it.

Neither requires the Spike Point to be recalculated.

## 3. The perfectly calibrated outside Set

Your third example is particularly useful because it describes the limiting case of exceptional setting.

The Setter chooses a trajectory whose ascending intersection with the OH's maximum ideal attacking height occurs **exactly at the antenna**.

The OH can now contact the ball:

- At their maximum ideal attacking height.
- At the intended outside attacking location.
- At the desired moment in the offensive sequence.

The Setter has aligned the geometric Spike Point with the tactically preferred contact location.

That is an exceptionally well-calibrated Set.

However, it is only useful if the OH can actually arrive there with sufficient Jump preparation. A perfectly positioned Spike Point does not guarantee a successful Attack.

Also, the Setter must execute that trajectory accurately. Their ACC determines how consistently they can reproduce the intended Set.

## 4. What these examples establish about Set quality

We can now distinguish three characteristics without introducing new gameplay systems.

| Characteristic | Question |
|---|---|
| **Spike Point placement** | Where does the ball reach the Athlete's maximum ideal attacking height? |
| **Useful contact opportunities** | Where else along the trajectory can the Athlete still make an effective Attack? |
| **Offensive suitability** | Do those opportunities align with the intended location, timing, and hitter? |

A Set can succeed in the second and third categories without achieving perfect Spike Point placement.

Conversely, a Set can place its Spike Point at an excellent height but still be unsuitable because the hitter cannot approach it in time.

### One important geometric consequence

The **Spike Point alone cannot describe the quality of a Set**.

Two trajectories might have identical Spike Points but very different behavior around them.

For example, one ball might pass rapidly through the ideal contact height, while another spends substantially longer near that height.

The second trajectory provides more opportunity for the hitter to make a high contact, even though the Spike Points themselves are identical.

This doesn't require another Attribute or a separate contact-window system. It follows directly from the ball's trajectory and the Athlete's physical contact envelope.

## 5. The Setter's actual optimization problem

The Setter is effectively trying to align three things:

**The hitter's physical capabilities × the ball's trajectory × the intended offense.**

Sometimes that produces a Set whose Spike Point coincides with the desired contact.

Sometimes the Setter deliberately creates a trajectory where the hitter should attack before or after the Spike Point.

And sometimes an imperfect Set still offers a sufficiently generous region of useful contact that the hitter can execute the offense successfully.

This is a strong basis for Setter skill expression because it doesn't require the game to explicitly rate Sets as good or bad.

The player learns to recognize which trajectories create the desired opportunities.

**The Spike Point identifies the maximum-height possibility. The trajectory determines the surrounding possibilities. The Setter's skill is understanding which of those possibilities the offense actually needs.**

## Recovered attachment: Pasted text.txt

The conversation exposes one attached text file with this name. Its body is reproduced verbatim below. This is the Work-mode VWM audit handoff, not a new user decision; its open questions must be read alongside later exchanges. In particular, its statement that six granular statistic names were unavailable is superseded by the roster recovered in Exchange 056.

<!-- BEGIN VERBATIM ATTACHMENT -->

This is the handoff from the read-only code audit of `iracarranza/volley-world-manager` (VWM), followed by clarification of the volleyball MOBA’s charging system.

Repository inspected: `main` at commit [`ed65d401755c79862c02840f25a5b85ad267f303`](https://github.com/iracarranza/volley-world-manager/commit/ed65d401755c79862c02840f25a5b85ad267f303).

The investigation traced source formulas, callers and default feature flags. It was a static audit; simulation benchmarks were not run. No repository files were modified.

## Main findings

VWM already uses height-derived stride and body mass in runtime locomotion. Earlier discussion understated that implementation.

However, VWM does not yet provide one consistent physical authority across movement, approach evaluation, defensive coverage and jump/contact resolution. Retain its geometry and approach structure, but consolidate overlapping calculations before reuse.

Base jump displacement does not directly depend on mass, height, stride or wingspan. Height and wingspan determine standing reach; physique can also affect the approach that makes jumping performance available.

Several comments and documents describe older implementations. Source expressions, callers and feature flags are stronger evidence than those descriptions.

## 1. Physique generation and locomotion

The player model stores height, mass, wingspan and stride independently.

Default stride is:

\[
s=\operatorname{clamp}(0.43H_{\mathrm m},0.55,1.15)
\]

Generation refreshes stride after body variation, so it reflects actual generated height rather than an unchanged role default. Stored stride can subsequently differ from the height-derived default.

References:

- [`scripts/models/volleyball_player.gd:318–345`](https://github.com/iracarranza/volley-world-manager/blob/ed65d401755c79862c02840f25a5b85ad267f303/scripts/models/volleyball_player.gd#L318)
- [`scripts/systems/player_generator.gd:123–130`](https://github.com/iracarranza/volley-world-manager/blob/ed65d401755c79862c02840f25a5b85ad267f303/scripts/systems/player_generator.gd#L123)
- [`scripts/systems/player_generator.gd:546–577`](https://github.com/iracarranza/volley-world-manager/blob/ed65d401755c79862c02840f25a5b85ad267f303/scripts/systems/player_generator.gd#L546)

Body generation correlates height variation with mass and wingspan variation, with role, regional and body-type contributions. It is not one universal deterministic height-to-mass function.

Body types also modify statistics. Do not automatically transfer those statistical bonuses into the MOBA: the established preference is to retain the athlete’s twelve ratings and let physique affect calculated outcomes separately.

Reference: [`scripts/domain/body_type_gameplay.gd`](https://github.com/iracarranza/volley-world-manager/blob/ed65d401755c79862c02840f25a5b85ad267f303/scripts/domain/body_type_gameplay.gd#L9).

### Runtime stride/cadence model

`LocomotionModel` calculates:

\[
v_{\max}=s_{\mathrm{mode}}\times c_{\mathrm{mode}}
\]

Cadence combines the relevant movement rating, fatigue, effort, limb turnover and mass:

\[
c_{\mathrm{mode}}
=\operatorname{lerp}(c_{\min},c_{\max},r)
(1-0.30f)E
\left(\frac{0.8299}{s}\right)^{k_{\mathrm{mode}}}
\operatorname{lerp}\left(1,\frac{M}{0.9793},0.30\right)
\]

where:

\[
M=\operatorname{lerp}\left(1.06,0.90,
\operatorname{clamp}\left(\frac{m-55}{60},0,1\right)\right)
\]

Mode-specific stride and turnover produce these net stride exponents at fixed mass and ratings:

| Movement mode | Net exponent | Effect |
|---|---:|---|
| Approach | +0.40 | Longer stride increases top speed |
| Transition | +0.65 | Larger long-stride benefit |
| Lateral | −0.35 | Shorter stride benefits shuffle speed |
| Block close | −0.10 | Small short-stride benefit |

Direction-change delay is scaled by reference cadence divided by athlete cadence, bounded to `0.72–1.30`.

References:

- [`scripts/simulation/locomotion_model.gd:43–210`](https://github.com/iracarranza/volley-world-manager/blob/ed65d401755c79862c02840f25a5b85ad267f303/scripts/simulation/locomotion_model.gd#L43)
- [`scripts/simulation/locomotion_model.gd:278–307`](https://github.com/iracarranza/volley-world-manager/blob/ed65d401755c79862c02840f25a5b85ad267f303/scripts/simulation/locomotion_model.gd#L278)

### Runtime inconsistencies

`RallyMovementSystem` uses this stride/cadence speed, then multiplies the result by another mass factor. Mass therefore affects speed both inside cadence and outside it.

Acceleration is:

\[
a=\operatorname{lerp}(2.2,6.8,\mathrm{Acceleration}/100)(1-0.30f)
\]

It has no direct mass or stride term.

Reference: [`scripts/simulation/rally_movement_system.gd:583–623`](https://github.com/iracarranza/volley-world-manager/blob/ed65d401755c79862c02840f25a5b85ad267f303/scripts/simulation/rally_movement_system.gd#L583).

Approach takeoff evaluation and defensive coverage still select a legacy speed curve by default:

\[
v_{\mathrm{legacy}}
=\operatorname{lerp}(1.35,V_{\mathrm{ceiling}},r)
M(1-0.30f)E
\]

The ceiling is `5.25 m/s` for approach and `4.65 m/s` for coverage.

`ENABLE_UNIFIED_SPEED_MODEL = false`. Thus, the stride/cadence model is active in movement, while these callers retain different top-speed calculations. Stride still affects preferred runway and coverage turning.

References:

- [`scripts/simulation/approach_mechanics_system.gd:469–501`](https://github.com/iracarranza/volley-world-manager/blob/ed65d401755c79862c02840f25a5b85ad267f303/scripts/simulation/approach_mechanics_system.gd#L469)
- [`scripts/simulation/coverage_calculator.gd:85–115`](https://github.com/iracarranza/volley-world-manager/blob/ed65d401755c79862c02840f25a5b85ad267f303/scripts/simulation/coverage_calculator.gd#L85)
- [`scripts/simulation/rally_feature_flags.gd:37–41`](https://github.com/iracarranza/volley-world-manager/blob/ed65d401755c79862c02840f25a5b85ad267f303/scripts/simulation/rally_feature_flags.gd#L37)

**Reuse recommendation:** retain mode-specific stride/cadence and turnover structure. Establish one speed authority and one mass application. Do not add a new mass-dependent acceleration penalty without deciding how much physical disadvantage the existing movement terms already represent.

## 2. Standing reach, jumping and contact geometry

Standing reach is implemented as:

\[
R_{\mathrm{cm}}=1.215H+0.32(W-H)
\]

Current base leap is:

\[
J_{\mathrm{cm}}
=\operatorname{lerp}(20,110,\mathrm{jump\_reach}/100)
\operatorname{lerp}(0.72,1,\mathrm{explosiveness}/100)
(1-0.35f)
\]

Jumping reach is:

\[
C_{\mathrm{cm}}
=R_{\mathrm{cm}}+J_{\mathrm{cm}}\operatorname{clamp}(\mathrm{effort},0,1)
\]

There is no direct mass, height, stride or wingspan term in base leap. Height and wingspan enter through standing reach.

The earlier documented `12–78 cm` leap range is stale. Runtime constants are `20–110 cm` before modifiers.

Reference: [`scripts/models/volleyball_player.gd:568–665`](https://github.com/iracarranza/volley-world-manager/blob/ed65d401755c79862c02840f25a5b85ad267f303/scripts/models/volleyball_player.gd#L568).

The model also derives shoulder height from height and a shared body ratio, arm length from standing reach minus shoulder height, and forward contact offset from arm geometry.

Reference: [`scripts/models/volleyball_player.gd:572–619`](https://github.com/iracarranza/volley-world-manager/blob/ed65d401755c79862c02840f25a5b85ad267f303/scripts/models/volleyball_player.gd#L572).

### Contact-envelope implementation

`ContactEnvelopeSystem` implements:

- Standing and jumping reach.
- Horizontal reach from wingspan, posture, technical stability and balance.
- Takeoff preparation:
  \[
  T=\operatorname{lerp}(0.3128,0.1196,\mathrm{Explosiveness}/100)
  \]
- Reduced additional takeoff preparation after an attack approach.
- Action-specific standing/approached jump effort:
  - Attack: `0.55 → 1.00`
  - Block: `0.80 → 0.92`
  - Set: `0.34 → 0.72`
- Gates preventing takeoff while airborne, diving or recovering.
- Contact feasibility, vertical margin and recovery information.

Reference: [`scripts/simulation/contact_envelope_system.gd:30–216`](https://github.com/iracarranza/volley-world-manager/blob/ed65d401755c79862c02840f25a5b85ad267f303/scripts/simulation/contact_envelope_system.gd#L30).

However, jump consumers differ. The envelope combines action-specific effort with the attack approach multiplier. The geometric attack path uses standing reach plus full leap multiplied by approach quality’s jump multiplier, minus a contact offset.

Reference: [`scripts/simulation/geometric_attack_promotion.gd:374–402`](https://github.com/iracarranza/volley-world-manager/blob/ed65d401755c79862c02840f25a5b85ad267f303/scripts/simulation/geometric_attack_promotion.gd#L374).

**Reuse recommendation:** preserve geometry and body-state distinctions, but compute realized takeoff and contact geometry once for physics, prediction and animation.

### Block timing model

A ballistic block timing model exists. It calculates hang time:

\[
T_{\mathrm{hang}}=2\sqrt{2J/g}
\]

It also models timing error, height at ball arrival, rising/descending hands and block effectiveness.

Its influence on the geometric block contest is disabled by default: `ENABLE_BLOCK_JUMP_TIMING = false`. This is implemented code behind a closed flag, not active default contest behavior.

References:

- [`scripts/simulation/block_jump_model.gd:132–285`](https://github.com/iracarranza/volley-world-manager/blob/ed65d401755c79862c02840f25a5b85ad267f303/scripts/simulation/block_jump_model.gd#L132)
- [`scripts/simulation/geometric_attack_promotion.gd:243–274`](https://github.com/iracarranza/volley-world-manager/blob/ed65d401755c79862c02840f25a5b85ad267f303/scripts/simulation/geometric_attack_promotion.gd#L243)

For the MOBA, use the player’s actual release timing and airborne trajectory rather than automatically importing VWM’s synthetic timing error.

## 3. Approach system

VWM evaluates approach quality from:

\[
q=\operatorname{clamp}(
0.24\,\mathrm{speedFraction}
+0.22\,\mathrm{timing}
+0.15\,\mathrm{alignment}
+0.12\,\mathrm{lateralControl}
+0.19\,\mathrm{runwayFit}
+0.08\,\mathrm{balance})
\]

It then applies runway completion and a system-fit bonus.

Preferred runway is stride multiplied by an acceleration-dependent step count, with positional modifiers. Approach quality affects:

- Jump multiplier: `0.90–1.08`.
- Balance multiplier.
- Clean power-attack access.
- Availability of a broader attack repertoire.

Reference: [`scripts/simulation/approach_mechanics_system.gd:505–591`](https://github.com/iracarranza/volley-world-manager/blob/ed65d401755c79862c02840f25a5b85ad267f303/scripts/simulation/approach_mechanics_system.gd#L505).

**Reuse recommendation:** preserve runway fit, directional momentum, alignment, lateral control and plant/balance evaluation. Replace AI-controlled recognition and timing assumptions with actual player inputs where appropriate. Replace overlapping jump preparation multipliers with the MOBA’s sequence model described below.

## 4. Attack power and fatigue

`usable_attack_power()` currently blends:

| Input | Weight |
|---|---:|
| Attack Power | 25% |
| Normalized mass | 10% |
| Explosiveness | 18% |
| Transition Speed | 12% |
| Arm Speed | 20% |
| Approach Timing | 15% |

Reference: [`scripts/models/volleyball_player.gd:271–283`](https://github.com/iracarranza/volley-world-manager/blob/ed65d401755c79862c02840f25a5b85ad267f303/scripts/models/volleyball_player.gd#L271).

This is a precedent, not a suitable direct MOBA formula. Swing Power already consolidates part of this capability, while approach and charging will be explicit runtime states. Copying the blend could count those contributions again.

VWM now implements:

- Match fatigue.
- Staged broad, physical-range and error consequences.
- Within-rally windedness.
- Stamina-dependent burst capacity.
- Jump exertion accounting.

The proposed anaerobic/aerobic/neural fatigue meters are not the same as these implemented systems.

Important inconsistencies:

- Movement and geometric jump functions use direct linear fatigue multipliers.
- Other execution calculations use staged fatigue and windedness through `_rating()`.
- Jump exertion uses a fixed `0.0048` cost scaled by action effort, not mass or realized displacement.
- `_charge_jump()` writes directly to exertion and bypasses the winded surcharge used by `_charge_exertion()`.

References:

- [`scripts/simulation/fatigue_model.gd`](https://github.com/iracarranza/volley-world-manager/blob/ed65d401755c79862c02840f25a5b85ad267f303/scripts/simulation/fatigue_model.gd#L65)
- [`scripts/simulation/rally_simulator.gd:12651–12712`](https://github.com/iracarranza/volley-world-manager/blob/ed65d401755c79862c02840f25a5b85ad267f303/scripts/simulation/rally_simulator.gd#L12651)
- [`scripts/simulation/rally_simulator.gd:16381–16428`](https://github.com/iracarranza/volley-world-manager/blob/ed65d401755c79862c02840f25a5b85ad267f303/scripts/simulation/rally_simulator.gd#L16381)
- [`docs/textbook/GRANULAR_ATTRIBUTE_BREAKDOWN.md:251–276`](https://github.com/iracarranza/volley-world-manager/blob/ed65d401755c79862c02840f25a5b85ad267f303/docs/textbook/GRANULAR_ATTRIBUTE_BREAKDOWN.md#L251)

VWM’s `_charge_*` function names mean “book resource expenditure.” They are not evidence of the MOBA’s action-preparation charging mechanic.

## 5. User-confirmed MOBA charging rules

The user clarified the following after the audit. These rules supersede the earlier suggestion that Jump Charge directly scales jump displacement.

- Actions—Receive, Set, Attack and Block—are selected from an Action Wheel.
- Initial Action Charge establishes an initial power level.
- Charge levels behave differently by Action and relative to preceding actions.
- Jumps, Peppers and Serves can also be charged.
- Any action can be tapped to establish a default attribute-based charge.
- Grounded cancellation refunds the initial HP expenditure.
- Charging Jump initiates an approach sequence.
- Jump Charge determines approach preparation, not maximum vertical displacement.
- The currently charged Action modifies the sequence:
  - Receive: the beginning becomes a directional Dive.
  - Set: the middle becomes a Jump Set.
  - Block: the middle becomes a Block Jump.
  - Attack: the penultimate step becomes a Spike.
  - MBs Innately can change the Set/Block transition to Dive timing.
- A second input completes the Action.
- A secondary tap executes at the initial Action Charge.
- Secondary charging replaces the initial charge for execution purposes within a narrower range.
- Secondary charging incurs additional, disproportionately higher HP cost for a less efficient adjustment.
- The original HP expenditure remains committed.
- While preparing the secondary input, movement inputs temporarily become directional influence on the resulting Action.
- Once airborne, the selected Action cannot change unless an Ability Trait explicitly permits it.
- Additional restrictions and inputs exist beyond those described here.

The user also clarified that ACC and END mean Accuracy and Endurance. The available context still does not expose the six agreed granular ACC/END statistic names.

## 6. Revised integration architecture

Use one physical/action authority:

**Movement state → approach sequence + Action Charge state → takeoff/contact → resource expenditure → recovery.**

Store independently:

1. Selected Action.
2. Initial Action Charge \(c_0\).
3. Effective Action Charge.
4. Approach/preparation progress.
5. Physical capability ceilings.
6. Current body/contact state.
7. Committed HP expenditure.
8. STA exertion and recovery state.

### Statistic ownership

| Confirmed statistic | Responsibility |
|---|---|
| Explosiveness | Explosive preparation/charge speed and initiation latency |
| Jump Power | Fully prepared maximum vertical displacement |
| Swing Power | Fully prepared striking-speed/impulse ceiling |
| Acceleration | Rate of gaining movement speed |
| Lateral Speed | Lateral cadence and control |
| Transition Speed | Approach and transition cadence |

Keep the three agreed Accuracy statistics in their existing action-specific precision/control roles. Keep the three agreed Endurance statistics in their existing resource roles. Obtain their exact names before finalizing the twelve-stat mapping; do not invent replacements.

### Jump preparation and displacement

Recommended model:

\[
J_{\mathrm{real}}
=J_{\max}(\mathrm{JumpPower})
\,A(\mathrm{actualPreparation},\mathrm{plant},\mathrm{balance})
\,F_{\mathrm{STA}}
\]

Jump Charge advances approach preparation. It is not an additional direct jump-height multiplier.

Explosiveness determines how quickly explosive preparation progresses. Jump Power determines the displacement ceiling. A rushed or interrupted approach reduces usable performance through preparation and plant quality.

Remove VWM’s separate Explosiveness multiplier on maximum displacement under this division of responsibilities.

Height and wingspan determine reach. Stride shapes movement and preferred runway. Mass can affect movement and exertion without automatically imposing another fresh-jump penalty.

At takeoff:

\[
v_{y0}=\sqrt{2gJ_{\mathrm{real}}}
\]

Thereafter, resolve contact from actual airborne position and posture. Use the same trajectory for collision, block height, attack contact and animation.

### Action-specific sequence

Represent Dive, Jump Set, Block Jump and Spike as transition events within a shared approach sequence. Exact phase timing requires calibration.

Directional input during secondary preparation controls the appropriate action influence: dive direction, set trajectory, attack aim or block orientation. Derive any resulting instability from actual movement and pose.

Allow preparation during compatible approach movement. Do not add another stationary takeoff timer for preparation already performed in the sequence.

### Action Charge interpretation

Use:

\[
O=\operatorname{Resolve}_{a}
(c_{\mathrm{effective}},\mathrm{precedingState},
\mathrm{relevantStatistics},\mathrm{contactState})
\]

Each Action needs its own charge response curve.

Possible interpretations requiring specification:

- Receive: intended response to incoming momentum.
- Set: intended delivery.
- Attack: intended striking output.
- Block: intended commitment or contact response.

These interpretations are proposals, not established rules. Charge must remain relative to preceding actions and ball state.

Geometry first determines whether contact is possible. Charge and technical capability then determine the result.

### Secondary adjustment

A concrete proposed implementation is:

\[
c_1=\operatorname{clamp}
(c_{\mathrm{requested}},c_0-\Delta_a^{-},c_0+\Delta_a^{+})
\]

Execution uses \(c_1\) instead of \(c_0\), not their sum.

\[
HP_{\mathrm{total}}
=HP_{\mathrm{initial}}(c_0)
+HP_{\mathrm{adjustment}}(c_1,c_0)
\]

The adjustment bounds may depend on Action, execution phase and traits. The additional cost should be steep relative to the limited correction.

A secondary tap preserves \(c_0\).

The treatment of secondary expenditure on cancellation still needs an explicit rule.

### HP and STA

The user explicitly named HP as the charge expenditure. Do not silently reinterpret HP as STA or assume they are interchangeable.

Keep charge commitment and physical exertion separately accounted until their relationship is confirmed.

A proposed normalized jump-exertion formula is:

\[
C_j=C_{0,j}
+k_j\frac{m}{m_0}\frac{J_{\mathrm{real}}}{J_0}
\frac{1}{\eta_{\mathrm{END}}}
\]

This is a gameplay cost inspired by vertical work, not a claim that STA measures joules.

Apply positional cost modifiers once. Book takeoff cost once; account separately for locomotion and sustained preparation.

Grounded cancellation refunds initial HP under the confirmed rule. It should not automatically erase exertion already incurred; that is a proposed distinction requiring confirmation if the resource systems are coupled.

## 7. Avoiding double-counting

- Apply mass to movement once.
- Do not penalize jump output separately for low Jump Charge, incomplete approach and short runway when they describe the same missing preparation.
- Do not add a second stationary loading timer after the sequence already loaded the jump.
- Do not reuse VWM’s attribute-blended attack-power calculation unchanged when Swing Power, actual approach and Action Charge already represent those inputs.
- Apply fatigue/low-STA degradation through one authoritative path rather than reducing effective statistics and multiplying the same output again.
- Do not impose an additional generic Giant/Tiny penalty after the same disadvantage has emerged through stride, turnover, preparation and exertion.
- Keep secondary correction’s HP expense separate from instability caused by actual directional adjustment.
- Preserve separate MB benefits for preparation speed, transition timing and exertion cost.

## 8. Decisions and calibration still needed

1. Recover the exact twelve granular statistic names and their agreed definitions.
2. Define the charge-response curve for each Action, including its relationship to preceding actions.
3. Specify charge/tap defaults, preparation progression, hold limits and cancellation boundaries.
4. Confirm the relationship between HP and STA.
5. Define the narrower secondary adjustment range and expensive cost curve.
6. Define the treatment of secondary expenditure on cancellation.
7. Calibrate sequence transition timing, including the MB Innate.
8. Decide whether Jump Power is an achieved-displacement rating or an absolute-force rating. Recommendation: achieved displacement, with mass primarily affecting exertion rather than imposing another fresh-jump penalty.
9. Consolidate movement, prediction and approach evaluation onto one speed model.
10. Consolidate contact-envelope and geometric-attack jump calculations.
11. Calibrate low-STA output loss, affordability and recovery together.
12. Test the agreed physique range: free height 6'0"–6'2", minimum 5'0", with the previously discussed Giant maximum of 7'2" still provisional. Existing VWM clamps and roster-centred coefficients do not establish MOBA balance across that range.
13. Price height/AP and Giant/Tiny only after measuring resulting gameplay value.

Use both equal-stat and equal-AP comparisons. Test short reversals, long transitions, rushed versus full approaches, repeated jumps and secondary corrections. Measure contact height, preparation time, reachable court area, HP commitment, STA cost and sustainable action frequency.

The recommended reuse boundary is: **VWM supplies geometry, mode-specific locomotion and approach evaluation. The MOBA supplies action selection, charge commitments, action-specific sequence transitions, secondary adjustment rules and airborne commitment.**
