# Consolidated summary of the conversation

This section is an editorial synthesis, not a verbatim transcript. Later explicit user corrections take precedence over earlier proposals. Illustrative numerical examples and assistant recommendations remain provisional unless adopted. The transcript above preserves the reasoning and alternatives.

## Project identity and match loop

**Hitting Lanes** is a newly reconstructed volleyball MOBA concept, not a claim that every design detail was recovered from an old project. Teams of five develop across three lanes and a jungle. Volleyball rallies resolve scoring objectives at each lane's Net; Peppers support smaller engagements elsewhere on the map.

The initial role assignments are OP top, MB middle, OH and S bottom, and L jungle. These establish natural responsibilities rather than exclusive permissions. Position, physique, Attributes, granular statistics, Innates and Ability Traits jointly produce a build. Unconventional builds should receive the actual benefits they invest in, while retaining positional tradeoffs.

Recurring Drills provide XP and Hitting Power. XP advances development; AP purchases Attribute growth, which enables Trait eligibility. A Serve successfully crossing a Net starts a Rally; an uncontested Serve awards a point. The final victory condition, score target and match duration are not fully specified.

## HP, Stamina and exhaustion

HP means **Hitting Power**, also sometimes called Hitting Points in early notes. It represents accumulated effectiveness and access to enhanced actions and techniques. It is distinct from Stamina, abbreviated STA, the regenerative capacity to sustain physical output. The comparison “HP like health, STA like mana” is an analogy, not an instruction to turn volleyball contacts into conventional damage combat.

Most exertion consumes STA. An Athlete at zero HP can still use basic tap inputs at diminished efficacy. Exhaustion begins when STA reaches zero and persists until at least 50% recovery, reducing movement, HP acquisition efficiency and rally HP efficiency. Some earlier prose calls the exhausted Athlete effectively unable to act; the exact remaining action permissions and penalty magnitudes need specification.

Leveling increases a protected **HP Minimum**, intended to preserve access to some already-unlocked techniques and basic charged play. Advanced techniques may still exceed that floor. Its exact accounting, expenditure and regeneration rules require numerical implementation rather than assuming an ordinary freely spendable reserve.

Initial position-only contact-cost tables are historical starting points. Later discussion shifts toward attribute-dependent HP efficiency combined with positional Innates. The retained conceptual matrix is:

| Contact | Primary Attribute | Secondary Attribute |
|---|---|---|
| Spike | POW | ACC |
| Set | ACC | SPD |
| Receive | SPD | END |
| Block | END | POW |

This matrix describes principal contact-efficiency relationships, not an exhaustive physics formula. END includes absorbing and resisting incoming impact as well as sustained exertion. Jumping is especially taxing and must depend strongly on END for sustainability.

## Map resources, Drills and Peppers

Drills recur at preset locations, and suitability emerges from capability and efficiency. Examples include Spike Trainer, cooperative Transition Set, demanding jungle Serve Receive and top-lane Box Jump Parkour. Some Drills are exclusive and others shareable. Opponents may invade and interrupt through Peppers.

Energy Drinks are contestable sources of resources. Entering a Rally fully replenished should be a privilege gained through preparation, resource control or foregoing Drills. A Level 1 Libero should need an appropriate initial investment to clear the opening defensive Drill safely and efficiently; the earlier unconditional-clear assumption was revised.

Walls, corners and elevation provide traversal choices and visual obstruction. Few areas fully hide players. The proposed map has diagonal lanes progressing toward a central Net line, with contestable objectives in both jungle halves; the schematic discussion is preserved above, but a text archive does not reproduce its visual artifact.

Outside Nets, Serve is replaced by Pepper. A threatened target receives a proximity-based directional warning affected by distance and visibility. Receiving the initiation starts an adjusted bump–set–spike exchange; an error loses HP and the winner gains XP. Peppers and Serves are **not cancellable**. A missed Pepper initiation has a consequence, but its exact penalty remains open. Ability cooldowns and some HP costs are reduced outside Rallies; specific values remain uncalibrated.

## Rally participation and contact rules

The three-contact limit remains. An Athlete may make successive contacts, including handling all three in a solo Rally, but repeated personal contacts escalate HP cost. This replaces the ordinary consecutive-contact fault without removing the team-contact limit.

Setting creates better attacking geometry and was also proposed to reduce attack HP cost and slightly improve attack output. Exact bonuses and their interaction with physically realized contact need calibration.

The playing area is based on the highest number of participants on either side, making numerical advantage meaningful. It grows during a Rally but does not shrink when participants leave. The serving distance remains fixed. The design allows up to six participants per side, despite teams having five players; the source of a sixth participant is unresolved.

Participation requires being in the team's court area and an opposing ball contact, including a Block. Players standing at a Net are not automatically in a Rally before a successful Serve starts it. The exact first-Serve and subsequent-entry event ordering, block counting under the three-contact rule, and edge-case faults still need a formal rules specification. Net faults are favored as a meaningful consequence of approach and airborne commitment.

## Attributes, statistics and development

The agreed twelve-stat roster recovered in Exchanges 055–057 is:

| Attribute | Statistic 1 | Statistic 2 | Statistic 3 |
|---|---|---|---|
| Power (POW) | Explosiveness | Jump Power | Swing Power |
| Speed (SPD) | Acceleration | Lateral Speed | Transition Speed |
| Accuracy (ACC) | Ball Control | Timing | Stability |
| Endurance (END) | Stamina Capacity | Exertion Efficiency | Recovery |

Explosiveness is explosive-action preparation speed; Jump Power is maximum vertical displacement; Swing Power is striking capability. Acceleration governs building horizontal velocity. END separates reserve capacity, action expenditure and recovery. Exact conversions and bounds remain calibration work.

The later allocation model permits granular customization **during matches**, superseding the early creation-only proposal. AP increases a parent Attribute and generates Statistical Points within that category. Automatic spending is the default convenience; optional manual allocation supports specialization. Three SP per parent increase, deviation limits and automatic-growth preferences are proposals rather than final calibrated rules. Parent Attributes govern eligibility; allocating SP should not separately charge the player for that eligibility.

Athlete creation targets a roughly 65-OVR reference build, with starting AP also available for optional Innates. Spending on unique Innates sacrifices starting Attribute development and potentially early Ability access. Do not assume every Innate-heavy build still receives the same final starting ratings. OVR is derived, not a separately purchased Attribute.

The working limits are Level 20 and 99 per Attribute. The later proposed discretionary costs are 1 AP through 80, 2 AP for 80→81 through 89→90, and 3 AP for 90→91 through 98→99. Earlier 85/95 thresholds are superseded. The working progression calibration is:

| Position | Discretionary AP per level | Automatic Innate growth | Ceiling |
|---|---:|---|---:|
| MB | 3 | +2 END | 90 |
| S | 3 | +2 ACC | 90 |
| L | 3 | +2 SPD | 90 |
| OH | 3 | +1 to all four Attributes | 80 |
| OP | 6 total | Extra discretionary AP rather than automatic POW | — |

Conversion of blocked automatic growth to discretionary AP at 1:1 was recommended and retained in the comparison, but remains a balance hypothesis. Automatic growth bypasses the discretionary price curve until its ceiling. Historical sample-build tables are illustrative calculations, not a verified balancing dataset.

Positional Innates also distinguish contacts: MB has minimal Block HP expenditure and faster Block Jump preparation; S has minimal Set HP expenditure including directional adjustments; L reduces Receive HP **and STA**, replacing the earlier Sprint-efficiency idea. MB reduced Block Jump STA cost is part of the jumping-economy discussion. OH favors breadth and OP freely chosen specialization, including defensive utility.

## Traits, creation and physique

Most Traits are shared across positions. Eligibility makes a Trait available to claim rather than automatically granting it. Only a limited number can be claimed; Deep Bag raises that maximum. Optional Innates are creation-only and should provide distinctive benefits non-investing Athletes cannot reproduce merely by ordinary Attribute growth. Separate Trait-slot rules and eligibility from AP expenditure; not every proposed example establishes a final price or prerequisite.

Technique-specific specialization generally belongs in Traits rather than permanent substatistics. Serve Power is the motivating example. Quick Step is an accessible early conversion/mobility option; Shoot Set is an early option requiring substantial ACC investment; Back Set is intended to become available later, when multiple attackers make deception more valuable. Exact prerequisites and the wider early Trait/Innate roster remain unfinished.

The latest explicitly requested free height range is **6'0"–6'2"**, with an absolute minimum of **5'0"**. The assistant's proposed surrounding bands are short investment 5'5"–5'11", tall investment 6'3"–6'7", Tiny 5'0"–5'4", and Giant 6'8"–7'2". The exceptional bands and maximum remain provisional gameplay boundaries, not measured population percentiles.

Selecting height should generate plausible default proportions, with bounded customization. Physique affects realized geometry and performance rather than automatically rewriting displayed ratings. Giant/Tiny pricing must account for reach, movement, exertion and foregone Attribute/early-Trait investment.

## Action selection, charge and confirmation

Receive, Set, Attack and Block are selected through an Action Wheel. Initial charging establishes intended Action power; tapping establishes a statistic-based default. Charge has action-specific meaning relative to preceding contacts. Jumps, Serves and Peppers can also be charged.

Receive charge represents cushioning/follow-through; direction controls intended redirection, while facing determines which incoming directions can be handled effectively. Attack direction is relative to committed body facing, position and handedness. Low charge can produce a naturally softer attack; Traits specialize techniques beyond those basic possibilities.

The secondary input completes the Action. A tap uses the initial charge. A secondary charge replaces the initial charge within a narrower adjustment range, retains the original committed expenditure, and adds a disproportionately expensive correction. It is an option, not a required second full charge. Movement controls temporarily become directional action influence while preparing confirmation.

Grounded cancellation and interruption must not be conflated. Grounded Action cancellation was discussed as refundable, but Serve/Pepper exceptions remain. The explicit later rule for an **interrupted Jump** is that it ends entirely without refunding its charging HP. Exact secondary-charge cancellation/refund accounting remains to be formalized.

Once airborne, selected Action and body/aim direction are committed unless an Ability Trait permits an exception. Camera examination can occur without rotating the committed body aim.

## Jump, approach and projection

Charging Jump begins preparation immediately; it is not advance selection of a future approach length and is not a direct maximum-jump-height slider. Standing preparation is allowed. Movement inputs and camera steering influence the physically available approach while charging.

The floor projection displays the full available **ground approach**, excluding airborne displacement and landing. Early release initiates takeoff from the current position. Reaching the projected endpoint causes automatic takeoff. Actual steering is bounded by the Athlete's physical capability; the projection describes that capability rather than promising an arbitrary player-drawn path.

The charged Action changes the sequence: Receive becomes a directional Dive near its beginning; Set and Block transition around the middle into Jump Set/Block Jump; Attack becomes a Spike at the penultimate step. MB has an Innate timing exception for the Set/Block transition using Dive timing. Precise animation/state-machine timings and exceptional interrupted states still require specification.

Physique establishes geometry, the statistics establish capability, preparation and plant quality determine how much capability is available, and STA governs sustainability. Explosiveness affects readiness speed; Jump Power affects maximum displacement. These must remain distinct.

## Camera, Moment of Contact and trajectories

General play uses a third-person, behind-the-Athlete camera. Rally and Pepper play support Athlete View and Ball View. Setters Innately have Setter View, perpendicular to the Athlete's facing and useful for seeing along the Net. Airborne view adjustment does not automatically change facing; expanded adjustment can be Trait-dependent.

Moment of Contact (MoC) is a synchronized slowdown/impact-frame interval on most or all contacts. Its expression varies by action and intensity. It permits orientation and decision-making without giving one participant a separate simulation clock. Timing affects usable confirmation windows. Exact durations, networking and input buffering remain open.

Trajectories are previews, not authoritative guarantees of the eventual ball path. They reflect physical feasibility, charge, facing and action/trait options; execution may deviate. Directional options do not promise perfect down-the-line placement from an incompatible body orientation, nor reveal every defender or guarantee an open landing spot.

Allies in the Rally see chosen trajectories. Set preparation may expose multiple available Set options to allies. Receive and Spike reveal only the chosen trajectory to allies; the acting player sees options as available time permits. Block shows the resultant trajectory. A fast incoming ball or quick Set can leave less time for branching options than a free ball or high Set.

## Final Accuracy interpretation

The later explicit simplification supersedes the intermediate idea that higher ACC reveals better information or more truthful HP costs. ACC represents the **Athlete's simulated physical execution and forgiveness**, not the player's intelligence or privileged access to the simulation.

Ball Control, Timing and Stability support faithful redirection, wider useful timing tolerance, lower variance and compensation for unfavorable body/contact geometry. On attacks, general physical correction may improve contact without granting specialized spiking techniques that belong to Traits.

High ACC cannot repair a preceding bad Receive, teleport the Athlete to an ideal approach or create contact outside physical capability. Equally, player skill cannot force an intended trajectory that the Athlete lacks the END, charge or physical capability to produce. A low-ACC hitter may brush a compromised ball while a high-ACC hitter makes better contact from the same attempted action.

## Spike Points and setting tactics

A Spike Point is an Athlete-specific **maximum-height contact reference**, derived from ball trajectory and ideal jumping/contact capability. It assumes optimal starting conditions and preparation, not the Athlete's current position or chosen approach.

For a simple single-arc trajectory above the Athlete's ideal maximum contact height, ascending and descending intersections can produce two Spike Points. If the ball peaks below that height, its apex provides the single maximum-height reference. The later discussion permits a theoretical point outside the court; do not silently restrict the geometric reference to tactically preferred or currently legal contact locations. Real attack legality is evaluated separately.

Spike Points do not relocate to rescue bad approaches. They remain tied to the underlying trajectory and capability assumptions. Current reachability, intended contact and actual contact are separate quantities.

The highest contact is not always the best tactical contact. A hitter may deliberately contact lower near the antenna for better location or timing, without an automatic Accuracy penalty. A weak high middle ball may offer useful nearby contacts after its immediate apex; a rising shoot Set can be attacked at the antenna before its theoretical Spike Point outside the court. A perfectly calibrated Set may align the desired location, timing and maximum contact height.

The Setter chooses charge and trajectory to create useful opportunities for particular hitters. Player tactical understanding selects the offense; Athlete ACC determines fidelity of execution. Set quality cannot be reduced to distance from one Spike Point: the trajectory and useful contacts surrounding it matter.

## VWM audit findings and reuse boundary

The recovered attachment contains the detailed read-only code audit at commit `ed65d401755c79862c02840f25a5b85ad267f303`, including source links. It is evidence about implementation, not proof of MOBA balance.

- Retain standing reach: `R_cm = 1.215 H + 0.32(W − H)`.
- Default stride is `clamp(0.43 × height_m, 0.55, 1.15)` and is refreshed after generated body variation.
- Live movement uses stride/cadence, limb turnover and mass, but applies mass inside cadence and again outside final speed.
- Approach and coverage retain legacy top-speed paths because unified speed is disabled by default. Consolidation is needed.
- Base leap uses a 20–110 cm band before Explosiveness/fatigue/effort modifiers; the older 12–78 cm documentation is stale. It has no direct mass/height/stride/wingspan term beyond separate standing reach and approach effects.
- Contact-envelope and geometric-attack consumers do not fully share one jump calculation. A ballistic Block timing model exists but its effect on the contest is disabled by default.
- Fatigue stages, rally windedness and jump exertion exist. Proposed separate anaerobic/aerobic/neural meters must not be represented as implemented. Fixed jump cost does not yet model mass and realized displacement as the proposed MOBA design would.

Reuse VWM's contact geometry and mode-specific movement/approach evaluation, adapted to actual player inputs. The MOBA supplies its own action sequence, charge commitments, correction costs and airborne lock. Apply physique and fatigue effects once in an authoritative path. Do not copy synthetic AI timing errors, duplicate mass factors or overlapping approach penalties blindly.

## Remaining specification and calibration work

The twelve-stat names are now recovered; the attachment's earlier claim that six names were unavailable is historical. Remaining work is numerical and behavioral specification: AP-to-SP conversion, specialization bounds, all Trait prerequisites and slot limits, HP-Minimum accounting, charge-to-output curves, secondary adjustment costs, cancellation exceptions, exhaustion permissions, camera/MoC timing, event ordering, jump collision/legality and win conditions.

Working jump-economy targets are about 4–5 jumps for a fully fresh ordinary starting Athlete and roughly 7 for a fresh MB across a mixed attack/block sequence. These are calibration targets, not universal guarantees across physiques and builds. Full-resource entry is deliberately not assumed in normal play.

Before assigning final physique/AP prices, compare equal-stat and equal-AP builds across short reversals, long transitions, standing/rushed/full preparations, repeated jumps, imperfect contacts and secondary adjustments. Measure reach, preparation time, actual contact fidelity, resource cost and sustainable frequency. Keep player choice, Athlete capability, geometry and resource commitment distinct while making their joint effects visible in play.
